THE COURT,
composed as above,
after deliberation,
delivers the following Judgment:
Institution of proceedings and written pleadings
1. On 11 November 2019, the Republic of The Gambia (hereinafter “The Gambia”) filed in
the Registry of the Court an Application instituting proceedings against the Republic of the Union of
Myanmar (hereinafter “Myanmar”) concerning alleged violations of the Convention on the
Prevention and Punishment of the Crime of Genocide, adopted by the General Assembly of the
United Nations on 9 December 1948 (hereinafter the “Genocide Convention” or the “Convention”).

-92. In its Application, The Gambia sought to base the Court’s jurisdiction on Article IX of the
Genocide Convention, in conjunction with Article 36, paragraph 1, of the Statute of the Court.
3. The Application contained a Request for the indication of provisional measures, submitted
with reference to Article 41 of the Statute of the Court and to Articles 73, 74 and 75 of the Rules of
Court.
4. The Registrar immediately communicated to the Government of Myanmar the Application
containing the Request for the indication of provisional measures, in accordance with Article 40,
paragraph 2, of the Statute and Article 73, paragraph 2, of the Rules of Court. He also notified the
Secretary-General of the United Nations of the filing by The Gambia of the Application and the
Request for the indication of provisional measures.
5. In addition, by a letter dated 11 November 2019, the Registrar informed all States entitled
to appear before the Court of the filing of the above-mentioned Application and Request.
6. Pursuant to Article 40, paragraph 3, of the Statute, the Registrar notified the Member States
of the United Nations through the Secretary-General, and any other State entitled to appear before
the Court, of the filing of the Application, by transmission of the printed bilingual text.
7. Since the Court included upon the Bench no judge of the nationality of either Party, each
Party proceeded to exercise the right conferred upon it by Article 31, paragraph 3, of the Statute of
the Court to choose a judge ad hoc to sit in the case. The Gambia chose Ms Navanethem Pillay, and
Myanmar chose Mr Claus Kress.
8. By an Order dated 23 January 2020, the Court, having heard the Parties, indicated the
following provisional measures:
“(1) The Republic of the Union of Myanmar shall, in accordance with its
obligations under the Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of the Crime of
Genocide, in relation to the members of the Rohingya group in its territory, take all
measures within its power to prevent the commission of all acts within the scope of
Article II of this Convention, in particular:
(a) killing members of the group;
(b) causing serious bodily or mental harm to the members of the group;
(c) deliberately inflicting on the group conditions of life calculated to bring about its
physical destruction in whole or in part; and
(d) imposing measures intended to prevent births within the group;
(2) The Republic of the Union of Myanmar shall, in relation to the members of
the Rohingya group in its territory, ensure that its military, as well as any irregular armed
units which may be directed or supported by it and any organizations and persons which

- 10 may be subject to its control, direction or influence, do not commit any acts described
in point (1) above, or of conspiracy to commit genocide, of direct and public incitement
to commit genocide, of attempt to commit genocide, or of complicity in genocide;
(3) The Republic of the Union of Myanmar shall take effective measures to
prevent the destruction and ensure the preservation of evidence related to allegations of
acts within the scope of Article II of the Convention on the Prevention and Punishment
of the Crime of Genocide;
(4) The Republic of the Union of Myanmar shall submit a report to the Court on
all measures taken to give effect to this Order within four months, as from the date of
this Order, and thereafter every six months, until a final decision on the case is rendered
by the Court.”
9. By another Order of 23 January 2020, the Court fixed 23 July 2020 and 25 January 2021 as
the respective time-limits for the filing of a Memorial by The Gambia and a Counter-Memorial by
Myanmar.
10. Pursuant to subparagraph 4 of the operative clause of the Order of 23 January 2020
indicating provisional measures, Myanmar submitted reports on the measures taken to give effect to
that Order on 22 May 2020 (First report), 23 November 2020 (Second report), 20 May 2021
(Third report), 23 November 2021 (Fourth report), 23 May 2022 (Fifth report), 23 November 2022
(Sixth report), 23 May 2023 (Seventh report), 23 November 2023 (Eighth report) and 23 May 2024
(Ninth report). The Gambia submitted comments on each of these reports respectively, on
5 June 2020, 7 December 2020, 16 August 2021, 7 December 2021, 7 June 2022, 7 December 2022,
6 June 2023, 7 December 2023 and 6 June 2024, within the time-limits fixed by the Court.
11. By letters dated 14 October 2024, the Registrar informed the Parties that in the report due
in November 2024, Myanmar should, in particular, respond to the following questions posed by the
Court:
“Recalling the Presidential Directive No. 1/2020 on the Compliance with the
Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of the Crime of Genocide, promulgated
by the President of the Republic of the Union of Myanmar on 8 April 2020:
(a) Paragraph 4 of the Presidential Directive refers to the possible establishment of
‘appropriate mechanisms’ to transmit credible information to the Office of the
President about the commission of acts referred to in Articles II and III of the
Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of the Crime of Genocide. Have any
such mechanisms been established and, if so, what form do they take?
(b) What action, if any, may the President take on receipt of any credible information
referred to in paragraph 4?
(c) Are there any other mechanisms in place to enforce the Presidential Directive?”
12. On 22 November 2024, Myanmar submitted its tenth report on the measures taken to give
effect to the Order of 23 January 2020 indicating provisional measures, which included the responses

- 11 to the questions put to it by the Court. Myanmar subsequently submitted reports on 23 May 2025
(Eleventh report), 21 November 2025 (Twelfth report) and 22 May 2026 (Thirteenth report).
The Gambia submitted comments on each of these reports respectively, on 6 December 2024, 6 June
2025, 5 December 2025 and 5 June 2026, within the time-limits fixed by the Court.
13. In accordance with Article 43, paragraph 1, of the Rules of Court, the Registrar addressed
to States parties to the Genocide Convention the notification provided for in Article 63, paragraph 1,
of the Statute of the Court. In addition, in accordance with Article 69, paragraph 3, of the Rules of
Court, the Registrar addressed to the United Nations, through its Secretary-General, the notification
provided for in Article 34, paragraph 3, of the Statute of the Court.
14. By an Order of 18 May 2020, the Court, at the request of the Applicant, extended the
time-limit for the filing of the Memorial to 23 October 2020. By the same Order, the Court also
extended the time-limit for the filing of the Counter-Memorial of the Respondent to 23 July 2021.
The Gambia filed its Memorial within the time-limit as extended.
15. By a joint letter dated 11 November 2020, the Governments of Canada and the Kingdom
of the Netherlands, referring to Article 53, paragraph 1, of the Rules of Court, requested to be
furnished with copies of the written pleadings and documents annexed thereto filed in the case.
Having ascertained the views of the Parties in accordance with the above-mentioned provision, the
Court decided, taking into account the objection raised by one Party, that it would not be appropriate
to grant that request. By letters dated 27 November 2020, the Registrar communicated this decision
to the Governments of Canada and the Kingdom of the Netherlands, and to the Parties.
16. On 20 January 2021, within the time-limit prescribed by Article 79bis, paragraph 1, of the
Rules of Court, Myanmar raised preliminary objections to the jurisdiction of the Court and the
admissibility of the Application.
17. Public hearings on the preliminary objections raised by Myanmar were held on 21, 23, 25
and 28 February 2022. By a Judgment of 22 July 2022, the Court found that it had jurisdiction on the
basis of Article IX of the Genocide Convention to entertain the Application filed by The Gambia on
11 November 2019, and that the said Application was admissible.
18. By an Order of 22 July 2022, the Court fixed 24 April 2023 as the new time-limit for the
filing of the Counter-Memorial of Myanmar. By Orders of 6 April 2023 and 12 May 2023
respectively, the Court, at the request of the Respondent, extended that time-limit first to 24 May
2023 and then to 24 August 2023. The Counter-Memorial of Myanmar was filed within the time-limit
thus extended.
19. By an Order of 16 October 2023, the Court authorized the submission of a Reply by
The Gambia and a Rejoinder by Myanmar, and fixed 16 May 2024 and 16 December 2024 as the
respective time-limits for the filing of those written pleadings. Following a request by The Gambia
to extend the time-limit for the filing of the Reply, the Registrar informed the Parties, by letters dated
14 May 2024, that it had been decided not to extend the time-limit for the filing of the Reply, but that
the Reply would not be considered out of time if it was submitted by 23 May 2024. The Gambia filed
its Reply on 23 May 2024, as authorized by the Court. By an Order of 21 November 2024, the Court,
at the request of the Respondent, extended the time-limit for the filing of the Rejoinder of Myanmar
to 30 December 2024. The Rejoinder of Myanmar was filed within the time-limit thus extended.

- 12 20. By a letter dated 21 January 2025, the Registrar, acting pursuant to Article 69, paragraph 3,
of the Rules of Court, transmitted to the Secretary-General of the United Nations copies of the written
proceedings filed in the merits stage of the case, and asked whether the Organization intended to
present observations in writing under that provision. By a letter dated 29 January 2025, the Acting
Under-Secretary-General for Legal Affairs and United Nations Legal Counsel stated that the
Organization did not intend to submit any such observations.
21. By letters to the Parties dated 16 September 2025, the Registrar recalled that The Gambia
had provided, as annexes to its Memorial filed on 23 October 2020 as well as to its Reply filed on
23 May 2024, witness statements which had been redacted to protect the safety and security of the
witnesses and related persons. Unredacted versions of these annexes had been filed under seal in the
Registry “for the Court’s in camera review only” and were not transmitted to Myanmar; nor were
they distributed by the Registry to the judges sitting in the case. The Registrar further stated that the
Court, having examined the views of the Parties in this respect, and having regard to Articles 43,
paragraph 4, and 46 of its Statute and Articles 49, 50 and 57 of its Rules, as well as to the principles
of due process, equality of the parties and the need to ensure the proper administration of justice,
wished to emphasize that it would not take into account ex parte evidence. Accordingly, the two
sealed envelopes provided by The Gambia with its Memorial and its Reply were returned to
The Gambia.
Documents submitted after the closure of the written proceedings
22. By a letter dated 8 September 2025, the Agent of Myanmar, referring to Article 56,
paragraph 1, of the Rules of Court, requested authorization to produce three new documents. By a
letter dated 22 September 2025, the Agent of The Gambia informed the Court that his Government
had no objection to the production by Myanmar of these three documents. By letters of the same
date, the Registrar informed the Parties that, taking into account the absence of objection by
The Gambia, the documents had been added to the case file.
23. By a letter dated 17 November 2025, the Agent of The Gambia, referring to Article 56,
paragraph 1, of the Rules of Court, requested authorization to produce four new documents, namely
a public summary of investigations dated 29 September 2025, prepared by the Independent
Investigative Mechanism for Myanmar (hereinafter the “IIMM” or the “Mechanism”); a chronology
of major events in Myanmar from 1982 to 2017, prepared by the IIMM; a less redacted version of a
witness statement originally produced by The Gambia as an annex to its Reply, in which the identity
of the witness had been unredacted; and a letter dated 11 November 2025 from the Head of the IIMM
to the Agent of The Gambia.
24. By another letter, dated 19 November 2025, the Agent of The Gambia, referring to the
same provision, further requested that the Court authorize The Gambia to produce new versions of
14 witness statements which had previously been annexed to its Memorial and Reply “with
personally identifiable information redacted”. In these new versions, the witnesses’ identities had
been unredacted. The Agent of The Gambia also requested that the identities of these witnesses
remain confidential between the Parties and the Court, and that any mention of their statements
during the oral proceedings refer either to the witness number provided or to the relevant annex
number of The Gambia’s Memorial or Reply.
25. By a letter dated 28 November 2025, Myanmar objected to The Gambia’s two
above-mentioned requests.

- 13 26. By letters dated 10 December 2025, the Registrar informed the Parties that the Court had
decided, in accordance with Article 56, paragraph 2, of the Rules of Court, to authorize the
production of (i) the less redacted version of a witness statement, in which the identity of the witness
had been unredacted, and the letter from the Head of the IIMM to the Agent of The Gambia, attached
to The Gambia’s letter dated 17 November 2025 (see paragraph 23 above); and (ii) the less redacted
versions of 14 witness statements attached to The Gambia’s letter dated 19 November 2025 (see
paragraph 24 above). These documents were thus added to the case file.
By the same letters, the Parties were further advised that the identity of these witnesses should
be kept confidential and that any mention of their statements during the oral proceedings should be
made by reference either to the witness statement number or to the annex number in the written
pleadings. Finally, it was indicated to the Parties that, should Myanmar wish to comment in writing
on the new documents of The Gambia and submit documents in support of its comments, pursuant
to Article 56, paragraph 3, of the Rules of Court, it was invited to do so no later than 5 January 2026.
27. By a letter dated 6 December 2025 and received in the Registry on 8 December 2025, the
Agent of The Gambia, referring to Article 56, paragraph 1, of the Rules of Court, requested
authorization to produce new confidential documents related to a witness statement provided on
5 December 2025 to both Parties by the IIMM. Myanmar objected to The Gambia’s request by a
letter dated 15 December 2025, stating that, if the Court were to authorize the production of the new
documents, then Myanmar should be entitled to respond to the new material.
28. By letters dated 17 December 2025, the Registrar informed the Parties that the Court,
pursuant to Article 56, paragraph 2, of its Rules, had decided to authorize the production of the
confidential documents attached to The Gambia’s letter dated 6 December 2025 (see paragraph 27
above) and that those documents had been added to the case file. The Registrar further stated that,
should the Government of Myanmar wish to comment in writing on the new documents of
The Gambia and to submit documents in support of its comments, pursuant to Article 56,
paragraph 3, of the Rules of Court, it was invited to do so no later than 5 January 2026.
29. By a letter dated 5 January 2026, the Agent of Myanmar referred to Myanmar’s previous
comments on the new documents produced by The Gambia (see paragraph 27 above), and
communicated new documents, namely two witness statements, in support of those comments,
pursuant to Article 56, paragraph 3, of the Rules of Court.
30. By a letter dated 9 January 2026, the Agent of Myanmar, referring to Article 56,
paragraph 1, of the Rules of Court, requested the Court to authorize Myanmar to produce seven new
documents. By a letter dated 11 January 2026, the Agent of The Gambia indicated that his
Government considered that the documents submitted by Myanmar were part of publications readily
available and, accordingly, might be referred to during the oral proceedings under Article 56,
paragraph 4, of the Rules of Court.
31. By letters dated 13 January 2026, the Registrar informed the Parties, inter alia, that, having
considered the request of Myanmar and the views of The Gambia thereon (see paragraph 30 above),
the Court had decided that, in accordance with Article 56, paragraph 4, of the Rules of Court,
Myanmar might refer to the seven documents during the oral proceedings.

- 14 Proceedings related to the declarations of intervention
32. On 15 November 2023, the Republic of Maldives (hereinafter “the Maldives”) filed a
Declaration of intervention in the case, with reference to Article 63 of the Statute of the Court. On
the same date, a joint Declaration of intervention was filed, pursuant to the same provision, by
Canada, the Kingdom of Denmark, the French Republic, the Federal Republic of Germany, the
Kingdom of the Netherlands and the United Kingdom of Great Britain and Northern Ireland
(hereinafter the “Joint Declaration”). In accordance with Article 83, paragraph 1, of the Rules of
Court, the Registrar immediately transmitted certified copies of the Declaration of the Maldives and
of the Joint Declaration to The Gambia and Myanmar, which were informed that 15 January 2024
had been fixed as the time-limit for the submission of written observations on those two Declarations.
In accordance with paragraph 2 of the same Article, the Registrar also transmitted copies of the
Declarations of intervention to the Secretary-General of the United Nations and to the States entitled
to appear before the Court.
33. By letters dated 15 January 2024, Myanmar and The Gambia each filed written
observations on the Declaration of the Maldives and on the Joint Declaration. While Myanmar
objected to the admissibility of both Declarations, The Gambia contended that they were admissible.
34. By an Order of 3 July 2024, the Court decided that the Declaration of intervention of the
Maldives and the Joint Declaration were admissible in so far as they concerned the construction of
provisions of the Genocide Convention.
35. Further to the Order of 3 July 2024 and in accordance with Article 86, paragraph 1, of the
Rules of Court, the intervening States were furnished with copies of the written pleadings of the
Parties. On 30 December 2024, copies of the Rejoinder of Myanmar were transmitted to the
intervening States, which were informed that the President of the Court had fixed 3 March 2025 as
the time-limit within which they could file written observations on the subject-matter of their
intervention under Article 86, paragraph 1, of the Rules of Court.
36. On 28 February and 3 March 2025, the Maldives and the joint intervening States (Canada,
the Kingdom of Denmark, the French Republic, the Federal Republic of Germany, the Kingdom of
the Netherlands and the United Kingdom of Great Britain and Northern Ireland), respectively, filed
written observations on the subject-matter of their interventions. Copies of these written observations
were transmitted to the Parties and to the intervening States.
37. Declarations of intervention under Article 63 of the Statute of the Court were also filed by
the Republic of Slovenia (hereinafter “Slovenia”) on 29 November 2024, by the Democratic
Republic of the Congo (hereinafter the “DRC”) on 10 December 2024, by the Kingdom of Belgium
(hereinafter “Belgium”) on 12 December 2024 and by Ireland on 20 December 2024. In accordance
with Article 83, paragraph 1, of the Rules of Court, the Registrar immediately transmitted certified
copies of each Declaration of intervention to The Gambia and Myanmar, which were informed that
29 January 2025, 10 February 2025, 12 February 2025 and 20 February 2025, respectively, had been
fixed as the time-limits for the submission of their written observations on those Declarations. In
accordance with paragraph 2 of the same Article, the Registrar also transmitted copies of the
Declarations to the Secretary-General of the United Nations and to the States entitled to appear before
the Court.

- 15 38. Within the time-limits fixed by the Court, The Gambia and Myanmar each filed written
observations on the Declarations of intervention submitted by Slovenia, the DRC, Belgium and
Ireland. While Myanmar objected to the admissibility of all these Declarations, The Gambia
contended that they were admissible.
39. By an Order of 25 July 2025, the Court decided that the Declarations of intervention
submitted by Slovenia, the DRC, Belgium and Ireland were admissible in so far as they concerned
the construction of provisions of the Genocide Convention. By the same Order, the Court fixed
25 September 2025 as the time-limit for the filing, by these intervening States, of the written observations
referred to in Article 86, paragraph 1, of the Rules of Court.
40. Further to the Order of 25 July 2025 and in accordance with Article 86, paragraph 1, of the
Rules of Court, Slovenia, the DRC, Belgium and Ireland were furnished with copies of the written
pleadings of the Parties.
41. The DRC, Slovenia, Belgium and Ireland filed written observations on the subject-matter
of their interventions on 22, 23, 24 and 25 September 2025, respectively. Copies of these written
observations were transmitted to the Parties and to all intervening States.
42. By letters dated 17 and 21 October 2025 respectively, the Parties and the intervening States
were informed that the Court considered that it had been sufficiently informed of the positions of the
intervening States on the subject-matter of their intervention by way of written observations, and that
it did not deem it necessary for them to submit observations in the course of the oral proceedings, in
accordance with Article 86, paragraph 2, of the Rules of Court.
Organization of the oral proceedings
43. By letters dated 17 June 2025, the Parties were invited to provide in writing, by 2 July
2025, their views regarding the organization of the oral proceedings on the merits of the case,
including with respect to the presentation of evidence. Within the time-limit thus fixed, the Parties
communicated to the Court their views on the length and structure of the oral proceedings and on the
organization of the testimonies of any witnesses or experts they might call.
44. By letters dated 17 July 2025, the Registrar requested the Parties to provide, by
8 September 2025, the names of any witnesses and experts they intended to call during the oral
proceedings, as well as the other information required by Article 57 of the Rules of Court. In
response, The Gambia indicated that it wished to call four witnesses and one expert, while Myanmar
stated that it intended to call six witnesses. The Parties were furnished with copies of each other’s
response and given the opportunity to comment on the other’s communication by 23 September
2025. In addition, by letters dated 16 September 2025, the Parties were invited to provide, also by
23 September 2025, their views on various points concerning the procedure for the examination of
the witnesses and the expert during the hearings, including whether they considered protective
measures to be necessary for any of the individuals they proposed to call to testify and, if so, which
measures and on what grounds.

- 16 45. By a letter dated 23 September 2025, Myanmar clarified that it intended to call five
witnesses and indicated that some protective measures would be necessary for the witnesses that it
proposed to call.
46. By a letter dated 23 September 2025, The Gambia stated that, at that time, it did not
consider protective measures to be necessary for any of the witnesses or the expert that it intended to
call to testify, but that it would notify the Court as soon as possible if it considered that any such
measures would be required owing to new information or developments.
47. By letters dated 17 October 2025, the Registrar informed the Parties of decisions taken by
the Court relating to the hearing of the witnesses and the expert during the oral proceedings. The
Parties were informed in particular that the Court had decided that the Parties’ witnesses and the
expert to be called by The Gambia would be heard in the order requested by each Party. The Parties
were required to submit, by 18 November 2025, a written affidavit for each of the individuals they
intended to call as a witness at the hearings. The Gambia was also required to submit a written
statement by the expert it intended to call. The affidavits and statement (i) had to be dated and signed
by the witness or expert and by any interpreter or counsel involved in their preparation; (ii) could not
include any redactions or other forms of anonymization; and (iii) had to be in one of the two official
languages of the Court or accompanied by a certified translation in one of these languages. The
written statement of the expert of The Gambia had to be limited to a summary of the expert’s findings
in his two reports previously submitted as annexes to the Memorial and Reply of The Gambia.
Pursuant to the same letters, the Parties were also required to provide, by 18 November 2025,
unredacted copies of any previous statements or accounts given by the witnesses or expert called to
testify during the oral proceedings that had been annexed to their pleadings in redacted form.
48. By letters dated 13 November 2025, the Deputy-Registrar informed the Parties of,
inter alia, further decisions taken by the Court concerning the procedure for the examination of the
witnesses and the expert called by them.
The Parties were thus advised that, after making the solemn declaration provided for in
Article 64 (a) of the Rules of Court, each witness would be asked to confirm his or her written
affidavit and, if applicable, any prior written statements or accounts annexed to the written pleadings,
subject to any correction the witness might wish to make. The calling Party would then proceed to
the examination-in-chief, which would take the form of replies by the relevant witness to questions
put to him or her by counsel. This would be followed by a cross-examination of the witness by the
other Party, which would be confined in scope to written and oral statements already made as part of
the witness’s evidence-in-chief. Re-examination by the calling Party would then be possible, limited
to matters having arisen as part of the cross-examination.
The expert called by The Gambia would be asked, after making the appropriate declaration in
accordance with Article 64 (b) of the Rules of Court, to endorse his written statement, as well as his
earlier reports annexed to The Gambia’s written pleadings, subject to any correction he might wish
to make. The written statement and reports would constitute the expert’s evidence-in-chief and serve
in lieu of his examination-in-chief at the hearings. This would be followed by a cross-examination
of the expert by Myanmar, which would be confined in scope to statements already made by the
expert as part of his written statement or reports. A re-examination would then be conducted by
The Gambia, which would in turn be confined to matters having arisen as part of the
cross-examination.
Following re-examination, questions could be put to the witnesses and expert by the President
on behalf of the Court or by individual judges. The witnesses and expert would respond orally to any

- 17 such questions, except in particular circumstances, for example when specific information was
requested that needed to be verified.
The Parties were also informed that the Court had authorized three of the witnesses called by
The Gambia to testify in the Rohingya language, and the witnesses called by Myanmar to testify in
the Myanmar language (also referred to as the Burmese language). The Parties were informed that if
a witness was to give evidence in a language other than French or English, the necessary
arrangements for interpretation into one of the two official languages of the Court had to be made by
the Party calling the witness, while the Court would make arrangements for the verification of that
interpretation.
49. By a letter dated 18 November 2025, The Gambia confirmed that it intended to call four
witnesses and one expert (see paragraph 44 above) and communicated the order in which it intended
to call them. Together with the written statement of the expert, it also provided the written affidavits
of the four witnesses, in which they expressed concerns regarding their security and the security of
their families and stated that they understood that their identity would remain confidential. By a letter
dated the same day, Myanmar informed the Court that it intended to call only one witness and
reserved the right to call a different witness “if for any reason the former should be unavailable to
testify”. Myanmar also provided confidential written affidavits for both witnesses in the Myanmar
language, accompanied by certified translations into English.
50. With regard to protective measures (see paragraphs 45, 46 and 49 above), the Parties were
informed of the following decisions taken by the Court: (i) the witnesses called by the Parties would
be heard in closed sessions, with only judges, Registry staff, interpreters, technical personnel and
members of the Parties’ delegations allowed in the courtroom; (ii) pseudonyms would be used when
addressing the witnesses or referring to them at the hearings; (iii) a verbatim record of each closed
session scheduled for the examination of the witnesses would be prepared for the use of the Court
and the Parties, but anything which could lead to the identification of the protected witnesses would
be kept confidential; and (iv) the Parties would be permitted to refer to the testimony given in closed
session during their second round of oral argument, but would have to do so in a manner that would
ensure the protection of the identity of the relevant witness.
51. With regard to measures to ensure the integrity of the testimony of the witnesses, the
Parties were informed that the Court had prescribed the following: (i) each witness was to remain
outside the Great Hall of Justice both before and after his or her testimony; (ii) the Parties’ respective
witnesses were not to be informed of each other’s testimonies (or of those of witnesses whose
statements had been annexed to the written pleadings but who had not been called to testify at the
hearings) and they were not to have any contact which could compromise their independence or
breach the terms of their solemn declaration; (iii) in the event that the Court decided that the written
pleadings and documents annexed thereto, which contained certain written statements and accounts
given by the witnesses called by the Parties, were to be made available to the public, either in full or
in a redacted form provided by the Parties, these would be posted on the website of the Court only
after the end of the oral proceedings; (iv) in the event that the Court decided that the affidavits of the
witnesses were to be made available to the public, either in full or in a redacted form provided by the
Parties, these would be posted on the website of the Court only after the end of the oral proceedings;
(v) verbatim records of the sittings during which the witnesses were heard would be prepared by the
Registry and circulated to the Parties as soon as available after each sitting, but the Parties had to
ensure that their witnesses did not have access to the records concerning the testimonies of the other
witnesses. If the Court were to decide that the verbatim records should be made available to the
public, either in full or in redacted form, these would be posted on the website of the Court only after
the end of the oral proceedings.

- 18 52. By a letter dated 10 December 2025, the Agent of The Gambia informed the Court that the
Office of Legal Affairs of the United Nations had determined that Mr Nicholas Koumjian, Head of
the IIMM, one of the four witnesses whom The Gambia intended to call, should not appear “on behalf
of a party” to provide oral testimony in the case. For this reason, the Agent requested that the Court,
in exercise of its authority under Article 62, paragraph 2, of the Rules of Court, invite Mr Koumjian
to appear as a witness at the oral proceedings.
53. By a letter dated 11 December 2025, the Agent of Myanmar informed the Court that
Myanmar objected to the request of The Gambia that the Court invite the Head of the IIMM to appear
as a witness at the oral proceedings. The Agent of Myanmar further stated that, if The Gambia’s
request were not granted, the affidavit of Mr Koumjian should be removed from the case file.
54. By letters dated 15 December 2025, the Registrar informed the Parties that, having
considered the request of The Gambia and the views of Myanmar thereon (see paragraphs 49 and
52-53 above), the Court had decided not to arrange for the attendance of Mr Koumjian as a witness.
The Registrar further informed the Parties that the Court had decided that the written affidavit of
Mr Koumjian, submitted by The Gambia on 18 November 2025, would remain part of the case file.
55. By a letter dated 5 January 2026, the Agent of Myanmar sought the Court’s authorization,
pursuant to Practice Direction IXquater, to present at the hearing, including during the examination
of witnesses, audiovisual material consisting of 14 maps and 2 videos that were not previously
included in the case file. By a letter dated 8 January 2026, the Agent of The Gambia informed the
Court that The Gambia opposed Myanmar’s request. Additional information on the videos was
communicated by Myanmar by a letter dated 12 January 2026.
56. By letters dated 13 January 2026, the Registrar informed the Parties that the Court had
decided, inter alia, to authorize Myanmar to present the maps and videos at the hearings, including
during the examination of witnesses.
Accessibility to the public of the pleadings and documents annexed
57. After ascertaining the views of the Parties, the Court decided, pursuant to Article 53,
paragraph 2, of its Rules, that copies of the pleadings and documents annexed thereto (in their
redacted versions, as appropriate) would be made accessible to the public on the opening of the oral
proceedings, except for certain annexes containing internal government documents or witness
testimony which, at the request of a Party, would remain confidential with respect to the public.
Where the present Judgment refers to information contained in those annexes, their confidential
character is indicated in the same manner as in the written pleadings. In addition, with regard to the
written pleadings and documents annexed thereto which contained certain written statements and
accounts given by the witnesses called by the Parties, as well as the redacted versions of the affidavits
of witnesses and the verbatim records of their testimonies, it was decided that these materials would
be posted on the Court’s website only after the end of the oral proceedings. The Court also decided
to make accessible to the public on the opening of the oral proceedings the reports submitted by
Myanmar on the measures taken pursuant to subparagraph 4 of the operative clause of the Order on
provisional measures of 23 January 2020 and The Gambia’s comments thereon (see paragraphs 10
and 12 above). In this respect, the Court noted that the Parties had been consulted on this matter and

- 19 did not object to such publication. Further, after ascertaining the views of the Parties and the
intervening States, the Court decided to make accessible to the public on the opening of the oral
proceedings the written observations filed by the Parties on the Declarations of intervention, pursuant
to Article 83, paragraph 1, of the Rules of Court; the written observations of the States seeking to
intervene and those of the Parties on the admissibility of the Declarations of intervention in
accordance with Article 84, paragraph 2, of the Rules of Court; and the written observations of the
intervening States, referred to in Article 86, paragraph 1, of the Rules of Court, on the subject-matter
of their intervention.

Requests made in the Application and submissions presented by the Parties
61. In the Application, the following requests were made by The Gambia:
“While reserving the right to revise, supplement or amend this Application, and
subject to the presentation to the Court of the relevant evidence and legal arguments,
The Gambia respectfully requests the Court to adjudge and declare that Myanmar:
⎯ has breached and continues to breach its obligations under the Genocide
Convention, in particular the obligations provided under Articles I, III (a), III (b),
III (c), III (d), III (e), IV, V and VI;
⎯ must cease forthwith any such ongoing internationally wrongful act and fully
respect its obligations under the Genocide Convention, in particular the obligations
provided under Articles I, III (a), III (b), III (c), III (d), III (e), IV, V and VI;
⎯ must ensure that persons committing genocide are punished by a competent
tribunal, including before an international penal tribunal, as required by Articles I
and VI;
⎯ must perform the obligations of reparation in the interest of the victims of genocidal
acts who are members of the Rohingya group, including but not limited to allowing
the safe and dignified return of forcibly displaced Rohingya and respect for their
full citizenship and human rights and protection against discrimination, persecution,
and other related acts, consistent with the obligation to prevent genocide under
Article I; and
⎯ must offer assurances and guarantees of non-repetition of violations of the Genocide
Convention, in particular the obligations provided under Articles I, III (a), III (b),
III (c), III (d), III (e), IV, V and VI.”
62. In the written proceedings on the merits, the following submissions were presented by the
Parties:
On behalf of the Government of The Gambia,
in the Memorial:
“For the reasons given in this Memorial, and reserving the right to supplement,
amplify or amend the present Submissions, the Republic of The Gambia respectfully
requests the International Court of Justice to adjudge and declare:
(1) That the Republic of the Union of Myanmar is responsible for violations of the
Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of the Crime of Genocide:

- 21 (a) in that members of its armed forces, police and other security forces, and also
persons for whose conduct it is responsible, committed genocide against
members of the Rohingya group on its territory by:
(i) killing members of the group;
(ii) causing deliberate bodily or mental harm to members of the group;
(iii) deliberately inflicting on the group conditions of life calculated to bring
about its physical destruction in whole or in part; and
(iv) imposing measures intended to prevent births within the group;
with the intent to destroy that group in whole or in part, contrary to Article II
of the Convention;
(b) in that persons for whose conduct it is responsible conspired to commit the acts
of genocide referred to in paragraph (a), were complicit in respect of those acts,
attempted to commit further such acts of genocide and incited others to commit
such acts, contrary to Article III of the Convention;
(c) in that, aware that the acts of genocide referred to in paragraph (a) were being
or would be committed, it failed to take any steps to prevent those acts, contrary
to Article I of the Convention;
(d) in that it has failed to bring to trial persons within its jurisdiction who are
suspected on probable grounds of involvement in the acts of genocide referred
to in paragraph (a), or in the other acts referred to in paragraph (b), and is thus
in continuing breach of Articles I and IV of the Convention;
(e) in that it has failed to enact the necessary legislation to give effect to the
provisions of the Convention, and, in particular, to provide effective penalties
for persons guilty of genocide or any of the other acts enumerated in Article III,
and is thus in continuing breach of Article V of the Convention.
(2) That, as a consequence of its responsibility for these breaches of the Convention,
the Republic of the Union of Myanmar:
(a) must cease forthwith any ongoing internationally wrongful act referred to in
paragraph (1), in particular:
(i) take immediate and effective steps to enact the specific genocide criminal
legislation referred to in paragraph (1) (e);
(ii) take immediate and effective steps to submit to trial before an
independent and effective tribunal, including before an international
penal tribunal, those members of its armed forces, police and other
security forces or any other persons within its jurisdiction who are
suspected on probable grounds of having committed acts of genocide as
referred to in paragraph (1) (a), or any of the other acts referred to in
paragraph (1) (b), notably Senior-General Aung Min Hlaing, Vice
Senior-General Soe Win, Lieutenant-General Aung Kyaw Zaw,

- 22 Major-General Maung Maung Soe, Brigadier-General Aung Aung and
Brigadier-General Than Oo, and to ensure that those persons, if
convicted, are duly punished for their crimes;
(iii) take immediate and effective steps to suppress and prevent any direct and
public incitement to commit genocide;
(b) must perform the obligation of reparation in the interest of the victims of
genocidal acts who are members of the Rohingya group, and must:
(i) by way of restitution:
(1) allow the safe and dignified return to their place of residence of
displaced members of the Rohingya group, whether they are
displaced within Myanmar or abroad;
(2) return to the Rohingya their individual and collective property,
including their land, houses, places of worship and communal life,
fields, livestock and crops, or replace them in kind;
(3) allow and facilitate the safe and dignified reunification of families;
(4) provide for the rehabilitation of the physically or mentally injured
members of the Rohingya group; such rehabilitation must include
adequate medical and psychological care as well as legal and social
services;
(5) facilitate the search for the disappeared and assist in the recovery,
identification and reburial of the bodies of those killed in accordance
with the expressed or presumed wishes of the victims and in
accordance with the cultural and religious practices of the Rohingya;
(6) ensure the protection of the Rohingya against discrimination and
persecution;
(7) ensure the right of the Rohingya to identify as such;
(8) ensure the liberty and freedom of movement of the Rohingya within
Myanmar and remove any restriction on their place of residence;
(9) remove any restriction or discrimination on the employment or
access to livelihoods of the Rohingya.
(ii) compensate, and provide any additional forms of reparation, for any
harm, loss or injury suffered by the Rohingya victims that is not capable
of full reparation by restitution.
(c) must offer assurances and guarantees of non-repetition by notably providing
full and equal citizenship to all members of the Rohingya group who are
present in Myanmar or have been displaced due to the events for which
Myanmar bears responsibility under the Convention.

- 23 (3) That the Republic of the Union of Myanmar has failed to fully and adequately
implement the Provisional Measures Order and must:
(a) by way of restitution as referred to in paragraph (2) (b) (i) above, make good
any bodily or mental injury, including death, suffered by members of the
Rohingya group, or any material injury caused to their property, as a result of
Myanmar’s violations of paragraph 86 (1), (2) or (3) of the Order;
(b) compensate, and provide any additional forms of reparation, for any harm, loss
or injury referred to in paragraph (3) (a) above that is not capable of full
reparation by restitution.
(4) That, failing agreement between the Parties on the amount of compensation and any
additional forms of reparation referred to in paragraph (2) (b) (ii) and
paragraph (3) (b) above, the question will be decided by the Court in a subsequent
phase of the proceedings in this case.”
in the Reply:
“For the reasons given in the Memorial and this Reply, and reserving the right to
supplement, amplify or amend the present Submissions, the Republic of The Gambia
respectfully requests the International Court of Justice to adjudge and declare:
(1) That the Republic of the Union of Myanmar is responsible for violations of the
Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of the Crime of Genocide:
(a) in that members of its armed forces, police and other security forces, and also
persons for whose conduct it is responsible, committed genocide against
members of the Rohingya group on its territory by:
(i) killing members of the group;
(ii) causing deliberate bodily or mental harm to members of the group;
(iii) deliberately inflicting on the group conditions of life calculated to bring
about its physical destruction in whole or in part; and
(iv) imposing measures intended to prevent births within the group;
with the intent to destroy that group in whole or in part, contrary to Article II
of the Convention;
(b) in that persons for whose conduct it is responsible conspired to commit the acts
of genocide referred to in paragraph (a), were complicit in respect of those acts,
attempted to commit further such acts of genocide and incited others to commit
such acts, contrary to Article III of the Convention;
(c) in that, aware that the acts of genocide referred to in paragraph (a) were being
or would be committed, it failed to take any steps to prevent those acts, contrary
to Article I of the Convention;

- 24 (d) in that it has failed to bring to trial persons within its jurisdiction who are
suspected on probable grounds of involvement in the acts of genocide referred
to in paragraph (a), or in the other acts referred to in paragraph (b), and is thus
in continuing breach of Articles I and IV of the Convention;
(e) in that it has failed to enact the necessary legislation to give effect to the
provisions of the Convention, and, in particular, to provide effective penalties
for persons guilty of genocide or any of the other acts enumerated in Article III,
and is thus in continuing breach of Article V of the Convention.
(2) That, as a consequence of its responsibility for these breaches of the Convention,
the Republic of the Union of Myanmar:
(a) must cease forthwith any ongoing internationally wrongful act referred to in
paragraph (1), in particular:
(i) take immediate and effective steps to enact the specific genocide criminal
legislation referred to in paragraph (1) (e);
(ii) take immediate and effective steps to submit to trial before an
independent and effective tribunal, including before an international
penal tribunal, those members of its armed forces, police and other
security forces or any other persons within its jurisdiction who are
suspected on probable grounds of having committed acts of genocide as
referred to in paragraph (1) (a), or any of the other acts referred to in
paragraph (1) (b), notably Senior-General Aung Min Hlaing, Vice
Senior-General Soe Win, Lieutenant-General Aung Kyaw Zaw,
Major-General Maung Maung Soe, Brigadier-General Aung Aung and
Brigadier-General Than Oo, and to ensure that those persons, if
convicted, are duly punished for their crimes;
(iii) take immediate and effective steps to suppress and prevent any direct and
public incitement to commit genocide;
(b) must perform the obligation of reparation in the interest of the victims of
genocidal acts who are members of the Rohingya group, and must:
(i) by way of restitution:
(1) allow the safe and dignified return to their place of residence of
displaced members of the Rohingya group, whether they are
displaced within Myanmar or abroad;
(2) return to the Rohingya their individual and collective property,
including their land, houses, places of worship and communal life,
fields, livestock and crops, or replace them in kind;
(3) allow and facilitate the safe and dignified reunification of families;
(4) provide for the rehabilitation of the physically or mentally injured
members of the Rohingya group; such rehabilitation must include
adequate medical and psychological care as well as legal and social
services;

- 25 (5) facilitate the search for the disappeared and assist in the recovery,
identification and reburial of the bodies of those killed in accordance
with the expressed or presumed wishes of the victims and in
accordance with the cultural and religious practices of the Rohingya;
(6) ensure the protection of the Rohingya against discrimination and
persecution;
(7) ensure the right of the Rohingya to identify as such;
(8) ensure the liberty and freedom of movement of the Rohingya within
Myanmar and remove any restriction on their place of residence;
(9) remove any restriction or discrimination on the employment or
access to livelihoods of the Rohingya.
(ii) compensate, and provide any additional forms of reparation, for any
harm, loss or injury suffered by the Rohingya victims that is not capable
of full reparation by restitution.
(c) must offer assurances and guarantees of non-repetition by notably providing
full and equal citizenship to all members of the Rohingya group who are
present in Myanmar or have been displaced due to the events for which
Myanmar bears responsibility under the Convention.
(3) That the Republic of the Union of Myanmar has failed to fully and adequately
implement the Provisional Measures Order and must:
(a) by way of restitution as referred to in paragraph (2) (b) (i) above, make good
any bodily or mental injury, including death, suffered by members of the
Rohingya group, or any material injury caused to their property, as a result of
Myanmar’s violations of paragraph 86 (1), (2) or (3) of the Order;
(b) compensate, and provide any additional forms of reparation, for any harm, loss
or injury referred to in paragraph (3) (a) above that is not capable of full
reparation by restitution.
(4) That, failing agreement between the Parties on the amount of compensation and any
additional forms of reparation referred to in paragraph (2) (b) (ii) and
paragraph (3) (b) above, the question will be decided by the Court in a subsequent
phase of the proceedings in this case.
The Gambia reserves the right to supplement or amend these submissions in light
of further pleadings and as necessary.”

*

I. GENERAL BACKGROUND
64. Myanmar, known as Burma until 1989, is located in mainland South East Asia. It is
bordered by the People’s Republic of China to the north and north-east, by the Lao People’s
Democratic Republic and the Kingdom of Thailand to the east, and by the Republic of India and the
People’s Republic of Bangladesh to the west. To the south and south-west of Myanmar lie the Bay
of Bengal and the Andaman Sea.
65. The territory of Myanmar is divided into 14 administrative units: 7 regions and 7 states,
including Rakhine State, a coastal strip of land in the westernmost part of the country, facing the Bay
of Bengal. States and regions of Myanmar are divided into districts, which are subdivided into
townships. Townships comprise towns and villages. Towns are further subdivided into wards, and
villages are grouped together in village tracts.
66. Myanmar’s population is ethnically diverse and, according to the Respondent, estimated
to be over 54 million. The Myanmar Government officially recognizes 8 “major ethnic national
races”, which are further subdivided into 135 “national ethnic groups” (see paragraph 137 below).
The largest “major ethnic national race” in Myanmar is the Bamar, accounting for approximately
65 per cent of the country’s total population. The Bamar are predominantly Buddhist and speak the
Myanmar (or Burmese) language, which is part of the Sino-Tibetan language family and is the sole
official language of Myanmar.
67. According to Myanmar’s 2014 census, Rakhine State had a total population of
approximately 3.2 million. Ethnic Rakhine (also known as Arakanese) ⎯ a group that is
predominantly Buddhist and speaks a dialect closely related to the Burmese language ⎯ account for
the majority of the population of Rakhine State. Rakhine State is also inhabited by a sizeable
population designated by the Applicant as “Rohingya” and by the Respondent as “Bengali”. The
Court’s references to the “Rohingya” in the present Judgment should be understood as references to
the group that is commonly called the Rohingya, which self-identifies as such and claims a
long-standing connection to Rakhine State (Application of the Convention on the Prevention and
Punishment of the Crime of Genocide (The Gambia v. Myanmar), Preliminary Objections, Judgment
(hereinafter the “2022 Judgment on preliminary objections”), I.C.J. Reports 2022 (II), p. 491,
para. 29; see paragraphs 308-310 below).
68. The Rohingya are predominantly Muslim and speak a language known as Rohingya, an
Indo-Aryan tongue that is primarily oral and is closely related to the Chittagonian language spoken
in Bangladesh. The Rohingya are not included in the 8 “major ethnic national races” or 135 “national

- 30 ethnic groups” officially recognized by the Government of Myanmar (see paragraphs 66 above and
137 below).
69. At the time of Myanmar’s 2014 census, the Rohingya population of Rakhine State was
estimated to comprise over one million people. Members of the Rohingya community lived mainly
in northern Rakhine State, in the townships of Maungdaw, Buthidaung and Rathedaung, two of which
border Bangladesh (see sketch-map No. 1 for locations). Prior to the “clearance operations” (a term
used to refer to counter-insurgency operations) launched by the Myanmar authorities in 2017, the
Rohingya population of northern Rakhine State comprised approximately 815,000 persons, 492,000
of whom were located in Maungdaw Township, 286,000 in Buthidaung Township and 37,000 in
Rathedaung Township. At that time, the Rohingya accounted for approximately 93 per cent of the
total population of Maungdaw Township, 80 per cent of the total population of Buthidaung Township
and 22 per cent of the total population of Rathedaung Township.

*

70. Most of the territory of present-day Myanmar was first unified under the Kingdom of
Pagan (which spanned the ninth to the thirteenth centuries), marking the beginning of the Burmese
Empire. Rakhine State, historically known as Arakan, was not part of this Kingdom and developed
independently from the central Burmese regions, from which it was geographically separated by the
Arakan mountain range. Between the fifteenth and the eighteenth centuries, much of the territory of
present-day Rakhine State was part of the independent Kingdom of Mrauk U, which also extended
over large coastal areas that are now located within the Chittagong Division of Bangladesh. In 1784,
the Konbaung Dynasty of Burma conquered Arakan, which was then incorporated into the Burmese
Empire.
71. In 1826, as a result of the First Anglo-Burmese War (1824-1826), the Burmese Empire
ceded Arakan to the British East India Company. Following further colonial wars, by 1886 the British
Empire had conquered the entirety of present-day Myanmar. Having been subject to British colonial
rule since 1826, Burma became an independent State on 4 January 1948 and joined the
United Nations later that year. On 18 June 1989, the Union of Burma informed the United Nations
that it had changed its name to the Union of Myanmar. On 30 March 2011, the Union of Myanmar
changed its name to the Republic of the Union of Myanmar.
72. Sectarian tensions and armed conflict occurred at various points before independence. A
significant outbreak of violence between Buddhists and Muslims took place in the village of
Alethankyaw in Rakhine State in 1942, during the Second World War, resulting in thousands of
deaths on both sides. Violence erupted again in northern Rakhine State not long after independence.
In April 1948, insurgents sought to separate northern Rakhine State from Burma and to join what
was then East Pakistan (later to become an independent State, the People’s Republic of Bangladesh,
in 1971). The Burmese military quashed the insurrection and launched several further armed
operations in subsequent years, until the last insurgents surrendered in 1961.
73. In 1962, the armed forces of Burma, known as the Tatmadaw, removed the civilian
government from power in a coup d’état, and ruled the country for over five decades until

- 31 March 2016, when a new civilian government was formed following a general election held in
November 2015. During the period of military rule, the Tatmadaw twice undertook operations that
led to the displacement of large sections of northern Rakhine State’s Rohingya population. In 1978,
a registration operation, accompanied by acts of violence, resulted in the flight to Bangladesh of an
estimated 200,000 Rohingya, nearly all of whom were subsequently repatriated pursuant to an
agreement between Myanmar and Bangladesh. In late 1991 and early 1992, the Tatmadaw launched
a further operation, similar to that of 1978. This operation led to the displacement to Bangladesh of
an estimated 200,000 Rohingya, the great majority of whom were later repatriated to Myanmar.
74. Sectarian and intercommunal violence resurfaced in Rakhine State on several occasions.
Armed confrontations and acts of violence took place in 2012, involving members of Rakhine State’s
Buddhist and Muslim communities, as well as members of the Tatmadaw, the Border Guard Police
(hereinafter the “BGP”) and the Police (referred to collectively as “security forces”). These events
resulted in casualties, extensive destruction and massive internal and cross-border displacement,
primarily affecting the Rohingya, over 100,000 of whom remain in internally displaced persons
(“IDP”) camps in Rakhine State (see paragraph 151 below).
75. Following these events, in 2013 Rohingya insurgents formed an armed group, initially
known as Harakah al-Yaqin, and subsequently known as the Arakan Rohingya Salvation Army or
ARSA, which engaged in the recruitment and training of insurgents in northern Rakhine State. In
October 2016, and again in August 2017, ARSA members launched attacks against certain police
and military installations and personnel in northern Rakhine State. Thereafter, Myanmar security
forces conducted a number of clearance operations at various localities in the townships of
Maungdaw, Buthidaung and Rathedaung, with the stated purpose of responding to ARSA armed
activities. These operations, according to Myanmar, were carried out between 9 October 2016 and
1 February 2017, and again between 25 August 2017 and 5 September 2017, with sporadic incidents
occurring thereafter. The Gambia contends that the 2017 clearance operations extended into 2018.
The United Nations Independent International Fact-Finding Mission on Myanmar (hereinafter the
“FFM” or the “Mission”) stated that “[a]lthough the Government officially declared 5 September
2017 to be the end of the offensive, Tatmadaw ‘clearance operations’ continued for some time after
this date”, referring to Myanmar’s Office of the Commander-in-Chief, according to which
“engagements were on the decrease after 5 September” (United Nations Human Rights Council,
“Report of the detailed findings of the Independent International Fact-Finding Mission on
Myanmar”, UN doc. A/HRC/39/CRP.2 (17 September 2018) (hereinafter “FFM, Report of the
detailed findings (2018)”), para. 1039).
As a result of these operations, approximately 87,000 Rohingya were displaced and fled to
Bangladesh in 2016 (see paragraph 175 below), and over 700,000 fled in 2017 (see paragraph 262
below).
76. On 24 March 2017, the United Nations Human Rights Council adopted resolution 34/22
establishing the FFM. On 27 September 2018, by its resolution 39/2, the Human Rights Council
established the IIMM (see paragraph 23 above). Myanmar did not allow either the FFM or the IIMM
to enter its territory to access evidence or witnesses and did not respond to requests for information.

- 32 77. On 1 February 2021, the Tatmadaw again seized power in Myanmar, deposing the
previously elected government and detaining many of its members. The acting head of the military
government was the Commander-in-Chief of the military, Senior General Min Aung Hlaing.
Following general elections held in December 2025 and January 2026, Mr Min Aung Hlaing was
declared President in April 2026.
II. QUESTIONS OF PROOF
78. The Court observes that questions of proof assume particular significance in the present
case because the Parties disagree on the relevant facts. Myanmar largely denies The Gambia’s
account of those facts, arguing that the Court should find that most of the evidence relied on by
The Gambia has little or no evidential weight. The Court will consider in turn the questions of burden
of proof, standard of proof and the weight of the evidence relied on by the Parties.
A. Burden of proof
79. The Court’s established jurisprudence confirms that, “as a general rule, it is for the party
which alleges a fact in support of its claims to prove the existence of that fact” (Armed Activities on
the Territory of the Congo (Democratic Republic of the Congo v. Uganda), Reparations, Judgment,
I.C.J. Reports 2022 (I), p. 54, para. 115; Ahmadou Sadio Diallo (Republic of Guinea v. Democratic
Republic of the Congo), Merits, Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2010 (II), p. 660, para. 54). For this reason,
The Gambia bears the burden of identifying and proving facts to establish the alleged breaches of the
Genocide Convention by Myanmar. However, in so far as Myanmar has invoked certain facts in
support of its own contentions, it carries the burden of establishing those facts. The Court has stated
that the rule that a party alleging a fact bears the burden of proof in respect of that fact “is not an
absolute rule applicable in all circumstances. There are situations where ‘this general rule would have
to be applied flexibly . . . and, in particular, [where] the Respondent may be in a better position to
establish certain facts’” (Armed Activities on the Territory of the Congo (Democratic Republic of the
Congo v. Uganda), Reparations, Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2022 (I), p. 54, para. 116, citing Ahmadou
Sadio Diallo (Republic of Guinea v. Democratic Republic of the Congo), Compensation, Judgment,
I.C.J. Reports 2012 (I), p. 332, para. 15). While the exercise of such flexibility does not reverse the
burden of proof (see Application of the Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of the Crime
of Genocide (Croatia v. Serbia), Judgment (hereinafter the “2015 Croatia Judgment”), I.C.J. Reports
2015 (I), pp. 73-74, paras. 172-174), it may have an effect on the assessment of the evidence, for
example, in circumstances where the applicant State lacks access to evidence located in the
respondent State’s territory owing to the latter’s lack of co-operation (see paragraphs 128-131
below).
B. Standard of proof
80. Claims alleging the commission of the acts enumerated in Article III of the Genocide
Convention, including genocide, must be proved by “fully conclusive” evidence (Application of the
Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of the Crime of Genocide (Bosnia and Herzegovina v.
Serbia and Montenegro), Judgment (hereinafter the “2007 Bosnia Judgment”), I.C.J. Reports
2007 (I), p. 129, para. 209). The Court must “be fully convinced that allegations made in the
proceedings, that the crime of genocide or the other acts enumerated in Article III [of the Genocide
Convention] have been committed, have been clearly established” (ibid.; 2015 Croatia Judgment,
I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 74, para. 178). As regards claims of violations of the obligations to prevent
and punish genocide, the Court requires “proof at a high level of certainty appropriate to the
seriousness of the allegation” (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 130, para. 210).

- 33 This standard of proof also applies to the alleged violations of obligations under Articles I, IV and
VI of the Convention at issue in this case. With respect to the obligation under Article V to enact the
necessary legislation to give effect to the provisions of the Genocide Convention, the question for
the Court concerns the interpretation to be given to national legislation and, specifically, whether the
legislation in force gives effect to the requirements of the Convention.
C. Evidence relied on by the Parties
81. In the course of the oral proceedings, The Gambia called three Rohingya witnesses and an
expert on counter-terrorism, all of whom gave oral testimony. The Gambia annexed to its written
pleadings the written statements of 63 individuals, 53 of whom were Rohingya, drawn from a variety
of sources, which will be discussed below (see paragraphs 89-96). The Gambia also introduced into
the record the transcripts of video testimonies of Rohingya witnesses and of persons self-identifying
as former Tatmadaw soldiers. It further relied on a document produced by the non-governmental
organization Legal Action Worldwide (hereinafter “LAW”) which collated short witness statements
from 80 alleged victims, which were unsigned and in which, in some cases, the witnesses’ identifying
details had been redacted. Additionally, The Gambia submitted a collection of 49 witness statements
by Rohingya that had been gathered by the non-governmental organization Fortify Rights, in which
witnesses’ identities were redacted. Other evidence relied on by The Gambia includes the reports of
the FFM and the IIMM, reports from other United Nations bodies including the Office of the High
Commissioner for Human Rights and the Special Rapporteur on the situation of human rights in
Myanmar, as well as satellite images and satellite-based fire detection data from the United Nations
Institute for Training and Research (UNITAR) and the United Nations Satellite Centre (UNOSAT).
The Gambia also introduced more than 50 reports by non-governmental organizations.
82. Myanmar, for its part, called one witness during the oral proceedings to give testimony
regarding an alleged attack by ARSA resulting in the deaths of civilians. It further submitted
45 witness statements by individuals, 20 of whom were Rohingya and the remainder security forces
personnel and non-Rohingya villagers. Myanmar also relied on official documents including
population lists, military operational reports and the military’s rules of engagement, as well as
Myanmar media reports. It also relied, to some extent, on the FFM reports and reports of
non-governmental organizations to contradict The Gambia’s allegations, while maintaining its
reservations concerning the evidentiary value of these reports.
D. Evidential weight
83. The Court must make its own determination of the facts that are relevant to the legal
obligations at issue (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 130, para. 212). In the 2007
Bosnia Judgment, the Court noted the “unusual feature” of that dispute, in so far as many of the
allegations before the Court had already been the subject of processes and decisions of the
International Criminal Tribunal for the former Yugoslavia (ICTY) (ibid.). The ICTY had physical
access to crime scenes, including mass graves, and collected forensic evidence that “corroborate[d]
much of the testimony of the survivors” (ibid., p. 137, para. 229). The Croatia case shared this
feature. In both of these cases, the Court was thus able to rely on the decisions of the ICTY as a basis
for its own findings on alleged breaches of obligations under the Genocide Convention. The Court
described the factual findings of the ICTY as “highly persuasive” (ibid., p. 134, para. 223) and

- 34 accorded them the “greatest weight” (2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 136,
para. 469). In this case, by contrast, the Court does not have comparable judicial decisions available
to support its factual findings and can only rely on the evidence proffered by the Parties to make its
own determination of the facts.
84. In the case concerning Armed Activities on the Territory of the Congo (Democratic
Republic of the Congo v. Uganda), the Court stated that it would
“treat with caution evidentiary materials specially prepared for this case and also
materials emanating from a single source. It will prefer contemporaneous evidence from
persons with direct knowledge. It will give particular attention to reliable evidence
acknowledging facts or conduct unfavourable to the State represented by the person
making them (Military and Paramilitary Activities in and against Nicaragua
(Nicaragua v. United States of America), Merits, Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 1986, p. 41,
para. 64). The Court will also give weight to evidence that has not, even before this
litigation, been challenged by impartial persons for the correctness of what it contains.
The Court moreover notes that evidence obtained by examination of persons directly
involved, and who were subsequently cross-examined by judges skilled in examination
and experienced in assessing large amounts of factual information, some of it of a
technical nature, merits special attention.” (Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2005, p. 201,
para. 61.)
Below, the Court will identify the evidential weight that it attributes to the different types of evidence
submitted by the Parties.
1. Witness testimony and other statements
85. The Court recalls that “[i]n determining the evidential weight of any statement by an
individual, the Court necessarily takes into account its form and the circumstances in which it was
made” (2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 78, para. 196). These considerations
include whether the witness was an interested State official or a disinterested private individual,
whether the statement was by a person with direct knowledge of the events and whether it was
contemporaneous with the relevant events (ibid., p. 78, para. 197). The Court will accord evidential
weight to statements that are not eyewitness accounts “only where they have been . . . corroborated
by credible evidence” (ibid., pp. 78-79, para. 199). The Court has also indicated that the absence of
the signatures of the persons who made or took the statements does not always exclude those
documents, but the Court must ensure that the statements “faithfully record the evidence actually
given by those individuals” (ibid., p. 77, para. 196).
86. Myanmar has provided evidence that criminal and armed groups, including ARSA, have a
presence in the camps in Bangladesh, and suggests that The Gambia’s witnesses may have been
reticent about ARSA for fear of reprisals when giving oral testimony and in their written testimony
or other statements submitted by The Gambia. The Court observes that numerous Rohingya
witnesses, in statements submitted to the Court, also expressed concern for their own safety if they
returned to Myanmar, and for the safety of family members still present there, if it became known
that they had co-operated with international investigations. While the Court will take fear of reprisal
into account when evaluating witness evidence, it does not consider that this factor impugns the
credibility of The Gambia’s witnesses generally.

- 35 (a) Oral witness testimony
87. During the oral proceedings, the Court heard from four witnesses who had direct
experience of violence in the last week of August 2017, three of whom were called by The Gambia
and one by Myanmar. These witnesses were heard in closed session for reasons of witness protection.
As explained above (see paragraph 48), each witness first confirmed the written affidavit and any
prior written statement setting out their testimony to the Court. The witnesses were then examined
by the Party bringing their evidence, with the possibility of cross-examination and re-examination;
this was followed by an opportunity for the judges to put questions to the witnesses.
88. The Court found The Gambia’s witnesses credible in their accounts of the particular events
to which they testified. Many of the witnesses’ key statements were unchallenged in
cross-examination. The Gambia’s three witnesses each came from a village in the three main
townships in northern Rakhine State ⎯ from the villages of Min Gyi, Chut Pyin and Maung Nu
respectively. The descriptions offered by The Gambia’s witnesses are consistent with other evidence
before the Court regarding the events in these locations. Myanmar’s witness testified to the alleged
attack on 25 August 2017 at Kha Maung Seik, in which ARSA allegedly killed around 100 civilians
of the Hindu faith. Her account of the attack is consistent with the evidence in the record (see
paragraphs 177 and 546 below).
(b) Written witness testimony and other written statements
89. As regards the written testimony and other written statements of Rohingya persons,
The Gambia submitted 11 signed statements prepared by the IIMM in 2022 and 2023, 42 signed
statements prepared by LAW in 2020 and 2024, 2 statements ⎯ one unsigned and one signed ⎯ by
the same witness prepared by the non-governmental organization Fortify Rights, and 2 signed
statements the origin of which is unclear. Myanmar also introduced a number of written testimonies
and other statements by Rohingya persons: 18 signed witness statements prepared by the IIMM, an
IIMM interview screening note and 2 signed statements taken from Rohingya held in Myanmar
prisons.
90. For reasons of witness protection, many but not all of the signed statements have had
identifying details redacted (see paragraphs 21 above and 91-92 below), and some are unsigned (see
paragraph 93 below). Myanmar argues that no evidential weight can be given to any of the witness
statements produced by The Gambia for a variety of reasons, including that the information contained
in those statements is likely to emanate from the same sources and that the witnesses contradict
themselves.
91. In the 2015 Croatia Judgment, the Court considered the issue of unsigned witness
statements (I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), pp. 77-78, paras. 196-198; see paragraph 94 below). There is,
however, a substantial difference between the evidential weight to be accorded to unsigned witness
statements and that to be accorded to witness statements that were originally signed but have since
had those signatures and other identifying details redacted to protect the witnesses’ identity. With
respect to such witness statements, the Court recognizes that, while redactions seek to protect the
identity of victims, they may nevertheless limit the ability of the opposing party to contest the

- 36 evidence and of the Court to assess it. These redactions may accordingly reduce the evidential weight
of a statement, although they do not preclude its consideration altogether.
92. In this connection, the Court notes that Myanmar identified a limited number of instances
where it has been shown that the same witness gave statements to more than one body. Based on this,
Myanmar argues that, because many witnesses remain unidentified, the true extent of such
duplication cannot be known. Given the large number of interviews conducted by the various
organizations and the fact that identifying details were often redacted, the Court recognizes the
possibility that certain accounts may have originated from the same witness. However, having
carefully assessed the evidence, it does not consider this to be a widespread characteristic of the
witness statements before it. The Court has taken particular care to ascertain, based on the substance
of the statements, that where facts have been corroborated by witness statements obtained from
different sources, the statements came from different witnesses.
93. With respect to unsigned witness statements in the record, the Court notes that they
represent only a very small portion of the witness statements on which the Court relies. In fact, the
only unsigned statements that were submitted to the Court are those presented as part of the collated
witness statements collected from 80 victims of sexual violence, prepared and submitted by LAW to
the International Criminal Court in 2019 through a victim participation procedure (see paragraph 81
above).
94. The Court recalls that the absence of the signatures of the persons who made or took
statements does not always exclude such documents, but the Court must ensure that they are faithful
records of witness evidence (see paragraph 85 above). In its 2015 Croatia Judgment, the Court
considered “records of interviews by the Croatian police of one or sometimes several individuals
which [were] not signed by those persons and contain[ed] no indication that those individuals were
aware of the content” to be “deficient”, and the Court did not accord any evidential weight to them
(I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 78, paras. 197-198). The Court notes that, in the present case, unsigned
witness statements were collected by a non-governmental organization, rather than by an organ of
either Party, a circumstance relevant to the assessment of the weight to be accorded to such evidence.
While they were unsigned, the witness statements were identified by individual codes, and in the
majority of cases indicated the first names of the witness. The Court further recognizes that some of
the alleged acts referred to in the unsigned witness statements, such as sexual violence and mutilation,
generally pose challenges for evidence collection (see Armed Activities on the Territory of the Congo
(Democratic Republic of the Congo v. Uganda), Reparations, Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2022 (I),
pp. 77-78, paras. 189-191). Witnesses may be reluctant to reveal their identity because of the shame,
social stigma and ostracization associated with being victims of sexual violence (see paragraphs 258
and 368 below; see also Prosecutor v. Bosco Ntaganda, ICC-01/04-02/06, Trial Chamber VI,
Judgment of 8 July 2019, para. 88). Accordingly, the Court finds that such unsigned witness
statements are not without evidential weight. The Court is nevertheless cautious in relying on them,
only doing so rarely and where they are corroborated by other, more reliable, evidence (see
paragraph 100 below).
95. The Court has also carefully reviewed the signed written witness statements, assessing
their overall credibility and whether they are based on direct knowledge of events. The Court
concludes that, to a large extent, the written statements are consistent with the oral witness testimony,

- 37 the factual findings of the FFM’s reports and other information before the Court
(see paragraphs 132-282 below). The Court finds the detailed witness statements that were collected
by the IIMM to have considerable evidential weight in light of the IIMM’s careful methodology (see
Affidavit of Nicholas Koumjian, Head of the IIMM (15 November 2025), paras. 18-40). The IIMM’s
