witness interviews were preceded by the preparation of detailed interview plans and they typically
lasted for several days (ibid., paras. 29-30). An important aspect of the Mechanism’s methodology
involved understanding what the witnesses’ knowledge of the information shared was based on,
“particularly, whether the information they disclose[d] [was] something they saw or experienced
themselves or learned about through other sources” (ibid., para. 33). Following the interview, the
statements were read back to the witnesses to confirm their content and then signed (ibid., para. 38).
The Court also notes that both Parties rely on witness statements collected by the IIMM to
substantiate their claims.
96. According to LAW, the signed witness statements it collected were gathered through
remote and in-person interviews by qualified experts, including criminal and police investigators,
and in line with LAW’s standard operating procedures. The statements were also read back to the
witnesses to confirm their content before they were signed. As the witness statements are less
detailed, the evidential weight of these statements is lower than in the case of the IIMM’s witness
statements. Nevertheless, the Court considers that they generally constitute reliable evidence. The
Court further notes that the written statements or affidavits of the three witnesses called by
The Gambia to give oral testimony were collected by LAW and were corroborated by the witnesses’
oral testimony.
2. Third-party fact-finding
(a) General considerations
97. In evaluating evidence from third-party fact-finding, the Court has generally considered
the independence and qualifications of the author, the methodology used in the preparation of the
evidence, and the comprehensive nature and overall quality of the evidence. In particular, when
assessing reports from official or independent bodies, the Court has clarified that
“[t]heir value depends, among other things, on (1) the source of the item of evidence
(for instance partisan, or neutral), (2) the process by which it has been generated (for
instance an anonymous press report or the product of a careful court or court-like
process), and (3) the quality or character of the item (such as statements against interest,
and agreed or uncontested facts)” (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I),
p. 135, para. 227; see also 2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 76,
para. 190).
98. In both the 2007 Bosnia Judgment and the 2015 Croatia Judgment, the Court made
extensive use of reports prepared by independent United Nations bodies. For example, in the 2007
Bosnia Judgment, the Court stated that it had “gained substantial assistance” from the report entitled
“The Fall of Srebrenica”, prepared by the United Nations Secretary-General at the request of the
General Assembly. The Court observed that “[t]he care taken in preparing the report, its
comprehensive sources and the independence of those responsible for its preparation all lend
considerable authority to it” (I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 137, para. 230). In the 2015 Croatia
Judgment, the Court relied on a report on the situation of human rights in the territory of the former

- 38 Yugoslavia of 7 November 1995 prepared by the Special Rapporteur of the Commission on Human
Rights. It noted that “it must give evidential weight to [this] document[], by reason both of the
independent status of its author” ⎯ which Croatia acknowledged even though it disputed some of
the report’s factual findings ⎯ and “of the fact that it was prepared at the request of organs of the
United Nations, for purposes of the exercise of their functions” (I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 133,
para. 459; see also p. 141, para. 484, and p. 144, para. 495). The Court also stated in the Armed
Activities on the Territory of the Congo (Democratic Republic of the Congo v. Uganda) case that it
would “take into consideration evidence contained in certain United Nations documents to the extent
that they are of probative value and are corroborated, if necessary, by other credible sources”
(Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2005, p. 239, para. 205).
99. More recently, in its Judgment in the case concerning Application of the International
Convention for the Suppression of the Financing of Terrorism and of the International Convention
on the Elimination of All Forms of Racial Discrimination (Ukraine v. Russian Federation), the Court
relied on a series of reports prepared by the Office of the United Nations High Commissioner for
Human Rights (I.C.J. Reports 2024 (I), p. 165, paras. 215-216; see also p. 171, para. 238). The Court
noted in this regard that it “generally ascribes particular weight to reports by international
organizations that are specifically mandated to monitor the situation in a given area” (ibid., p. 165,
para. 215). While it observed in this context that “it must also take into consideration the lack of
access to Crimea of the Human Rights Monitoring Mission in Ukraine on whose observations the
relevant reports are based”, the Court ultimately relied on the reports to confirm the events in Crimea
(ibid., paras. 215-216).
100. The standard of proof in the present case requires a thorough assessment of the evidence.
Thus, the Court will carefully assess the evidential weight to be given to third-party fact-finding
reports in accordance with the criteria set out in its jurisprudence. Where such evidence is
corroborated by other evidence, the Court will accord greater weight to it. Corroboration does not
automatically establish weight or credibility, but the absence of corroboration is a factor to be
considered when weighing the evidence and assessing credibility (Dragomir Milošević,
IT-98-29/1-A, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 12 November 2009, para. 248; Kupreškić et al.,
IT-95-16-A, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 23 October 2001, para. 220). This Court has held that
evidence that has limited weight on its own may nevertheless be used to corroborate other evidence
that emanates from a stronger source (see 2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), pp. 78-79,
para. 199; p. 87, para. 239; p. 107, para. 344; p. 132, para. 457; and p. 143, para. 489). Moreover, the
fact that the Court determines that a particular report is entitled to evidential weight does not mean
that all its contents have evidential value. The Court will apply its standard criteria in assessing the
factual findings of the reports. However, it will not ascribe any probative value to the legal
assessments contained therein.
(b) Reports of the FFM
101. The FFM’s mandate, under resolution 34/22 of the Human Rights Council, was
“to establish the facts and circumstances of the alleged recent human rights violations
by military and security forces, and abuses, in Myanmar, in particular in Rakhine State,
including but not limited to arbitrary detention, torture and inhuman treatment, rape and

- 39 other forms of sexual violence, extrajudicial, summary or arbitrary killings, enforced
disappearances, forced displacement and unlawful destruction of property, with a view
to ensuring full accountability for perpetrators and justice for victims” (FFM, Report of
the detailed findings (2018), para. 4).
102. The President of the Human Rights Council appointed three members of the Mission:
Marzuki Darusman (Indonesia), who served as its chairperson, Radhika Coomaraswamy (Sri Lanka)
and Christopher Sidoti (Australia). The Secretariat of the FFM included specialists and advisers in
fact-finding, human rights law, military affairs and sexual violence (FFM, Report of the detailed
findings (2018), para. 1).
103. The FFM investigated, inter alia, the 2016 and 2017 events in northern Rakhine State
and produced two major reports, one in September 2018 and the other in September 2019
(United Nations Human Rights Council, “Detailed findings of the Independent International
Fact-Finding Mission on Myanmar”, UN doc. A/HRC/42/CRP.5 (16 September 2019) (hereinafter
“FFM, Detailed findings (2019)”)). The FFM further produced thematic reports on sexual and
gender-based violence and on the economic interests of the Myanmar military in August 2019. The
FFM’s mandate ended in September 2019 and the FFM transferred its evidential material to the
IIMM, which became operational in August 2019.
104. The FFM described the methodology used in the preparation of its reports in the following
manner:
“[T]he Mission strictly adhered to the principles of ‘do no harm’, independence,
impartiality, objectivity, transparency and integrity.
[T]he Mission followed the best practices established for commissions of inquiry
and fact-finding missions, outlined in the 2015 [Office of the High Commissioner for
Human Rights] publication, International Commissions of Inquiry and Fact-Finding
Missions on International Human Rights Law and International Humanitarian Law ⎯
Guidance and Practice.” (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 8-9; see
also FFM, Detailed findings (2019), paras. 31 and 35.)
105. The FFM conducted “875 in-depth interviews with victims and eyewitnesses” for its first
report (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 19) and 419 further interviews for its
second report (FFM, Detailed findings (2019), para. 32). The FFM assessed the reliability and
credibility of each source, considering, among other things, the political and personal interests of
each witness and any potential biases, their apparent capacity to recall events in light of their age,
their exposure to traumatic events and the time period between the relevant events and the interview
(FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 16). Interviews were generally conducted in
person in a safe and private place, and, when required, in the presence of a professional interpreter
(ibid., para. 21). As the FFM was unable to operate inside Myanmar, most of the interviews were
conducted in displaced persons camps in Cox’s Bazar in Bangladesh (see ibid., para. 23). In
identifying interviewees, the Mission used random selection ⎯ interviewing persons in different

- 40 areas of the refugee camps and in different refugee centres without prearrangement ⎯ as well as
interviewing persons selected on the basis of their specific experiences, including people from
different ethnic and religious backgrounds (ibid., para. 20). The Mission endeavoured to speak only
with persons who confirmed that they had not previously spoken with any other organization or
media outlet (ibid.). The Court observes, however, that only one of the underlying interview records
was presented to the Court, in the form of an interview note, and the Court has not been able to study
the remaining witness records referred to in the FFM reports (see paragraph 110 below). The FFM
also examined satellite imagery and satellite-based fire detection data analysed by
UNITAR-UNOSAT, authenticated photographs and videos, publicly available accounts of relevant
facts by Myanmar authorities, laws, policies and directives of Myanmar, and statistics, surveys and
other quantitative information generated by Myanmar or the United Nations (FFM, Report of the
detailed findings (2018), para. 13).
106. In making its factual determinations, the FFM adopted a “reasonable grounds” standard
of proof (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 10; FFM, Detailed findings (2019),
para. 31), which according to the FFM, is “[c]onsistent with the practice of United Nations
fact-finding bodies” (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 10). The FFM considered
this standard to be met “when a sufficient and reliable body of primary information, consistent with
other information, would allow an ordinarily prudent person to reasonably conclude that a case,
incident or pattern of conduct occurred” (ibid.). The FFM acknowledged that this standard of proof
is “lower than that required in criminal proceedings” (ibid.).

*

*

107. The Parties’ assessment of the evidentiary value of the FFM reports differs sharply.
The Gambia characterizes the FFM’s reports as “authoritative”, “convincing” and “reliab[le]”. In its
view, the FFM’s reports merit “significant weight”, based on the Mission’s “detailed methodology”
and “the care taken in obtaining and analysing evidence”. The Gambia also contends that the FFM’s
findings, the witness testimony, the IIMM’s reports and reports of other United Nations and
non-governmental organizations are all reliable and mutually corroborative.
108. Myanmar, for its part, maintains that “no evidential weight” should be given to the FFM’s
reports. It draws attention to the fact that Myanmar did not recognize or co-operate with the FFM
and submits that the reports are therefore one-sided and that the Mission was biased against
Myanmar. Myanmar expresses concern that the FFM applied a standard of proof of “reasonable
grounds”, noting that this differs from the standard adopted by criminal courts, and that the FFM did
not have a mandate to collect evidence for use in judicial proceedings. Myanmar points to the
interview note that was prepared by the FFM, and made available to the Parties by the IIMM, arguing
that this note demonstrates the methodological deficiencies in the FFM’s process (see paragraphs 105

- 41 above and 110 below). Myanmar contrasts the evidential weight of the reports of the FFM with that
of the ICTY’s findings, on which the Court relied in the 2007 Bosnia Judgment and the 2015 Croatia
Judgment, noting that the FFM’s process was non-adversarial and the FFM was not a judicial body.
Myanmar further argues that, as the FFM’s reports are based on records of interviews, they constitute
hearsay evidence at best or, since the members of the FFM were not directly involved in preparing
the interviews, “double hearsay” evidence.

*

*

109. Considering that, in making factual determinations, the FFM applied a standard of proof
that is lower than the standard of proof that the Court has identified as applicable in this case (see
paragraph 80 above), the Court has been cautious in assessing the evidential weight of the reports
prepared by the Mission. Nevertheless, the Court finds the two major reports prepared by the FFM
in general to be a reliable source of factual evidence in light of the following considerations: (1) the
FFM employed a rigorous methodology, conducting 1,294 interviews altogether, gathering a broad
range of other information and applying appropriate factors to the assessment of the reliability and
credibility of each source; (2) the reports were prepared “at the request of organs of the
United Nations, for purposes of the exercise of their functions” (2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J.
Reports 2015 (I), p. 133, para. 459; see also p. 141, para. 484), and are “reports by international
organizations . . . specifically mandated to monitor the situation in a given area” (Application of the
International Convention for the Suppression of the Financing of Terrorism and of the International
Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Racial Discrimination (Ukraine v. Russian
Federation), Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2024 (I), p. 165, para. 215); (3) many of the factual findings
of the reports are extensively based on or corroborated by other ⎯ in some cases uncontested ⎯
evidence, such as satellite imagery and witness statements; and (4) Myanmar, despite alleging that
the reports are unreliable, has not produced evidence of its own that has led the Court to question the
reliability of the reports or identified any fundamental inconsistencies between the reports and other
evidence on record.
110. The Court recalls that only one of the underlying interview records was presented to it,
in the form of an interview note. The Court has not been able to examine any of the remaining witness
records referred to in the FFM reports; nor has it been able to examine most of the other source
materials on which those reports were based. In this regard, the Court observes that third-party reports
rarely share their underlying source materials and the reliability of such reports is not contingent
upon such disclosure, provided that the Court is able to examine the methodology employed in
collecting and assessing the underlying source materials. In the present case, the Court has carefully
examined the FFM’s methodology, as described in its reports, and found it rigorous. With respect to
the one interview note to which the Court had access, in the Court’s view, there is nothing in the
content of the note or in the way the FFM relied on it in its reports that would give rise to doubts
concerning the FFM’s methodology.
111. As noted above (see paragraph 99), the Court considers that the lack of access of
fact-finding and monitoring bodies to the relevant territory is a factor to be taken into account in

- 42 assessing the persuasiveness of the evidence. In this case, the FFM and other fact-finding bodies
were limited in their evidence-gathering by a lack of access to evidence and witnesses in northern
Rakhine State and elsewhere in Myanmar (see paragraph 76 above). However, the Court notes that
a substantial part of the field work undertaken by the FFM ⎯ conducting interviews with victims
and eyewitnesses now located in the Cox’s Bazar refugee camp in Bangladesh (see paragraph 105
above) ⎯ did not depend on the Mission having access to Myanmar.
112. Overall, the Court finds that the FFM’s reports sought to provide a comprehensive
account of the relevant facts, and that they are based on a rigorous methodology that enabled the
FFM to carry out its mandate in an independent and impartial manner. The reports of the FFM
demonstrate a level of detail and coverage that is comparable to, and in certain aspects exceeds, that
of reports produced by independent fact‑finding bodies relied upon by the Court in earlier cases (see
paragraphs 98-99 above).
(c) Reports of the IIMM
113. The IIMM was established in response to reports, including those of the FFM, that “gross
human rights violations amounting to serious international crimes had been committed in Myanmar”
(Affidavit of Nicholas Koumjian, Head of the IIMM (15 November 2025), para. 1; see paragraph 76
above). The Head of the IIMM, Mr Nicholas Koumjian, is an experienced criminal prosecutor. The
IIMM comprises investigators, analysts and legal officers, and occasionally consultants with specific
linguistic, thematic and/or geographic expertise (Affidavit of Nicholas Koumjian, Head of the IIMM
(15 November 2025), paras. 13-14).
114. The IIMM has produced six reports on thematically distinct topics (“Anti-Rohingya hate
speech on Facebook: Content and network analysis” (27 March 2024); “Analysis of the Myanmar
Government’s encouragement of resettlement by Buddhists from Bangladesh to Rakhine State”
(22 March 2024); “Efforts to investigate and punish sexual and gender-based crimes committed
against Rohingya: Evidence analysis” (27 March 2024); “Evidence of ARSA weapons possession
and use” (29 April 2024); “Evidence related to detention of Rohingya in Buthidaung Prison and other
locations” (15 May 2024); and “The destruction and dispossession of Rohingya land and property
during the 2017 clearance operations — public summary” (29 September 2025)).
115. According to the Head of the IIMM, the Mechanism is “not focused on fact-finding”; its
mandate is rather to “collect evidence of serious international crimes and facilitate criminal
prosecution of such crimes in national and international courts” (Affidavit of Nicholas Koumjian,
Head of the IIMM (15 November 2025), para. 12). The IIMM’s Terms of Reference provide that the
Mechanism “shall systematically record, organize, preserve and store all information, documentation
and evidence in its possession, in accordance with international criminal law standards and the good
practices of other comparable international accountability mechanisms”.

- 43 116. As regards sources of evidence, the IIMM relies on
“open-source information including social media, geospatial imagery and analysis,
digital evidence including photographs and videos, primary documentation such as
laws, orders and military documents, and witness testimonies, in the form of screening
notes and witness statements” (Affidavit of Nicholas Koumjian, Head of the IIMM,
para. 18).

*

*

117. The Gambia argues that the IIMM’s reports should be given “great evidentiary weight”
since they emanate from an independent body established by the United Nations Human Rights
Council, draw on comprehensive sources and follow best practices in fact-finding. According to The
Gambia, they thus meet the relevant standard in the Court’s jurisprudence for reliance on evidence
collected by third parties. The Gambia emphasizes that the IIMM, like the FFM before it, attempted
in good faith to obtain evidence from Myanmar and from sources close to Myanmar that might
support Myanmar’s position and that both of those bodies included in their reports Myanmar’s public
statements on the objectives of the clearance operations. The Gambia also points out that the IIMM’s
mandate was to collect evidence to facilitate criminal proceedings and that, in so doing, it applied an
international criminal law standard of proof.
118. Myanmar, for its part, does not recognize and refuses to co-operate with the IIMM. It
maintains that the IIMM’s reports failed to deal with the “core events of 2016 and 2017” and, like
the FFM reports, have no evidential weight since they are the product of a non-adversarial process
in which Myanmar did not participate. Myanmar also argues that, as the reports’ authors did not have
first-hand knowledge of the events, the reports constitute a secondary, rather than a primary, source
and are thus without evidential value (see paragraph 108 above).

*

*

119. In accordance with the IIMM’s mandate, the analytical reports aim to provide evidence
on thematically distinct topics. For this reason, unlike the FFM’s reports, the IIMM’s reports are not
designed to provide a holistic account of the 2017 clearance operations. The Court nevertheless
considers them to have considerable evidential weight because of the demanding standard of proof
and methodology applied by the Mechanism in collecting the evidence contained in the reports.
120. In making its own independent findings of fact, it is for the Court to determine the
evidential weight of the particular factual information in the FFM and IIMM reports. In evaluating
the information in the reports, the Court has taken into account the extent to which the facts therein

- 44 are substantiated. It has also considered the extent to which the information is corroborated by other
credible evidence in the record, including by examining the source of the item of evidence, the
process by which it was generated and its quality or character (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports
2007 (I), p. 135, para. 227; 2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 76, para. 190).
3. Other evidence
121. The Gambia relies extensively on satellite imagery and satellite-based fire detection data
that was obtained by the FFM with the support of UNOSAT to substantiate its claims about the
burning and destruction of Rohingya villages. While Myanmar disputes some of the inferences that
The Gambia seeks to draw from the satellite imagery, noting that the periodic images do not allow
for a precise determination of the date on which events occurred, Myanmar does not question the
authenticity of the images as such. The Court finds the images which were collected periodically
from 16 September 2017, as well as the accompanying satellite-based fire detection data which was
collected at least once every two days (see paragraph 271 below), to be of considerable evidential
weight in themselves. They also corroborate other categories of evidence, such as witness accounts
and facts recorded in the reports prepared by the FFM and the IIMM. Myanmar did not challenge the
use of fire detection data and relied on data from the same sources, alongside satellite imagery, in
support of some of its contentions.
122. The Gambia also relies on other United Nations reports, statements and documents,
including reports of the Secretary-General, the Office of the High Commissioner for Human Rights,
the Special Adviser on the Prevention of Genocide and the Special Rapporteur on the situation of
human rights in Myanmar. The Court has carefully analysed these materials in accordance with the
criteria it applies generally to reports from official or independent bodies (see paragraphs 97-100
above), and it has relied on their contents where appropriate.
123. The Parties both present a wide array of reports prepared by non-governmental
organizations, including the Public International Law & Policy Group (hereinafter “PILPG”),
Amnesty International, Médecins Sans Frontières, Physicians for Human Rights, the International
Crisis Group, LAW and Fortify Rights.
124. In its prior judgments on the application of the Convention, the Court duly considered the
reports of non-governmental organizations although it did not accept them as “conclusive proof of
the facts alleged” (2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 133, para. 459). In the 2015
Croatia Judgment, the Court confirmed that the criteria identified in the 2007 Bosnia Judgment (see
paragraph 97 above) for assessing the evidential weight of reports of official or independent bodies
are also applicable in the context of reports prepared by non-governmental organizations (2015
Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 89, para. 249, and p. 132, para. 457). The Court further
notes that, in the present case, reports by non-governmental organizations were submitted and
referenced by both of the Parties. They were typically prepared on the basis of interviews with
Rohingya survivors, as well as with experts and other relevant sources. For example, PILPG’s report
was prepared based on interviews with 1,024 Rohingya (PILPG, “Documenting atrocity crimes

- 45 committed against the Rohingya in Myanmar’s Rakhine State: Factual findings & legal analysis
report” (December 2018) (hereinafter “PILPG, Factual findings & legal analysis report”), pp. 33, 40,
73 and 82). The Court will thus take these materials into account in this case, recognizing that they
generally have lower evidential weight than other categories of evidence discussed above.
125. Myanmar, for its part, also relies on its own governmental statements, documents and
reports, including statements collected from military personnel and from people currently held in
Myanmar’s custody. In Military and Paramilitary Activities in and against Nicaragua (Nicaragua v.
United States of America), the Court considered that declarations made by the authorities of a State
party must be subject to “critical scrutiny” (Merits, Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 1986, p. 43, para. 69).
In this case, the Court notes that some features of the documents relied on by Myanmar raise concerns
about their reliability as evidence. For example, the military reports do not record the number of
civilian casualties and no explanation is offered for the large number of rounds of ammunition
reported as used in each incident during the clearance operations. These gaps are noteworthy in light
of the number of casualties reported and the description of the shots fired. For these reasons, in the
Court’s view, aspects of Myanmar’s governmental documents and reports are to be considered with
caution, except to the extent that they are independently corroborated by credible evidence or present
evidence “contrary to the interests or contentions of the State . . . or . . . relating to matters not
controverted” (ibid., para. 70).
126. The Parties also rely on the Executive Summary of the final report of the Independent
Commission of Enquiry (hereinafter the “ICOE”), a body established by Myanmar in July 2018 to
investigate allegations of human rights violations during the events in northern Rakhine State in 2016
and 2017 (see paragraphs 536 and 539 below). The detailed findings of the ICOE were not made
public and neither Party annexed them to their written submissions. During the second round of the
oral proceedings, however, in connection to a question from a judge, Myanmar’s Counsel referred to
witness evidence contained in several annexes of the ICOE final report for the purpose of addressing
efforts by the security forces to protect civilians, and provided a web link to where certain annexes
of the report were publicly available. Because of the limited purposes for which Myanmar invoked
these documents and because The Gambia did not have an opportunity to review or comment on
them while raising significant questions regarding the independence and methodology of the ICOE
(see paragraphs 537-538 below), the Court has not considered the annexes of the ICOE final report,
other than for the purposes identified by Myanmar.
127. Finally, the Court turns to the expert evidence provided by The Gambia (see paragraph 60
above). The Gambia submitted two expert reports, in October 2020 and May 2024, on the topic of
whether Myanmar’s clearance operations were consistent with “counter-insurgency doctrine”. The
author of these two reports was also called as an expert to give oral testimony at the hearings, where
he was cross-examined by counsel for Myanmar and questioned by judges. The Court has considered
the evidence given by the expert to the extent that the subject-matter concerned was within both his
mandate and his expertise. It cannot attach any evidential weight to the expert’s legal assessment and
conclusions extending beyond the question whether Myanmar’s actions during the clearance
operations, as portrayed by the FFM, were conducted in a manner consistent with the “standards of
counter-insurgency operations”.

- 46 4. Myanmar’s non-co-operation
128. As noted above (see paragraph 76), Myanmar did not co-operate with the two
investigative bodies established by the United Nations Human Rights Council, refusing to allow
entry into its territory for access to evidence and witnesses there, and failing to respond to requests
for information. While Myanmar allowed access to the United Nations Special Rapporteur on the
situation of human rights in Myanmar in the period between 2014 and 2017, it revoked permission
for such access after what it described as the Rapporteur’s “tactless and undiplomatic” statements in
July 2017, and it continued to deny access to her successor. The IIMM issued 26 requests for
information to the Myanmar authorities, including requests for information that was “potentially
exonerating”, to which Myanmar did not respond (Affidavit of Nicholas Koumjian, Head of the
IIMM (15 November 2025), para. 22).
129. Myanmar justifies its refusal to co-operate with the FFM and the IIMM by citing its
“distrust” of the Human Rights Council and the investigative bodies established by it, noting that
these bodies were created without Myanmar’s consent and arguing that the Human Rights Council
was biased against Myanmar. Myanmar further points out that it was under no obligation “to grant
the FFM and IIMM access to its territory or to provide them with information”.
130. As a general rule, a State is not obliged to grant United Nations mechanisms access to its
territory. The Court has found, however, that in cases where one of the parties exercises exclusive
control over a territory, rendering the other party “unable to furnish direct proof of facts giving rise
to responsibility”, the latter State “should be allowed a more liberal recourse to inferences of fact and
circumstantial evidence” (Corfu Channel (United Kingdom v. Albania), Merits, Judgment, I.C.J.
Reports 1949, p. 18; see also Armed Activities on the Territory of the Congo (Democratic Republic
of the Congo v. Uganda), Reparations, Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2022 (I), p. 67, para. 157). It was,
for example, on this basis that the Court took account of Ukraine’s lack of access to Crimea in its
assessment of the evidence in Application of the International Convention for the Suppression of the
Financing of Terrorism and of the International Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of
Racial Discrimination (Ukraine v. Russian Federation) (Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2024 (I), p. 152,
para. 169).
131. In the present case, neither the Applicant nor the United Nations fact-finding bodies on
which The Gambia substantially relies had access to evidence within the territory where the alleged
acts occurred. The Court observes that The Gambia is thus at a disadvantage with respect to direct
access to certain kinds of evidence. Accordingly, in the assessment of the evidentiary record, the
Court takes into account both Myanmar’s failure to genuinely investigate the alleged atrocities itself
(see paragraphs 511-516 and 553 below) and its refusal to allow access to evidence and witnesses in
its territory and to respond to the requests of the fact-finding bodies established by the United Nations
to investigate the matter. The Court may therefore have more liberal recourse to inferences of fact in
relation to events with respect to which Myanmar has largely or wholly exclusive knowledge and for
which it has not provided a satisfactory and convincing explanation (see paragraph 111 above).

- 47 III. EXAMINATION OF FACTUAL EVIDENCE
132. Much of the evidence in the record is contested between the Parties. The Court
accordingly finds it appropriate to set forth its assessment of the central facts in the case, applying
the criteria identified in the previous section with respect to the probative value and weight to be
given to the evidence and the inferences to be drawn therefrom. The Court does not consider it
necessary to examine every incident reported, nor to compile an exhaustive list of the allegations.
Rather, it is sufficient to examine those facts which, viewed in their context, would elucidate whether
acts prohibited under the Genocide Convention were committed (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J.
Reports 2007 (I), p. 143, para. 242). The Court will therefore focus on the clearance operations of
2017 and related events, in the course of which Myanmar is alleged to have breached its obligations
under the Convention, as well as the relevant context that informs the Court’s understanding of these
events.
133. The Court will begin with an assessment of the historical treatment of the Rohingya in
Myanmar (Section A), paying particular attention to Myanmar’s allegedly discriminatory laws and
policies concerning citizenship, marriages and births, as well as other restrictions. It will also
examine alleged anti-Rohingya hate speech. The Court will then consider the events of 2016 in
northern Rakhine State, including the emergence of ARSA and its military capabilities (Section B).
Finally, the Court will examine the evidence concerning the ARSA attacks of August 2017 and
Myanmar’s conduct during the 2017 clearance operations, first with respect to specific locations and
then with respect to broader evidence (Section C).
A. Historical treatment of the Rohingya in Myanmar
1. Discriminatory laws and policies
(a) Citizenship
134. The Rohingya have been present and have maintained civil, economic, social and political
connections in what is now Myanmar, including Rakhine State, for generations.
135. The Parties dispute when ancestors of the Rohingya first arrived in present-day Myanmar.
Myanmar recognizes that “certain Bengali migrants . . . settled in Arakan even before the British
colonial period”, and that by the end of the Mrauk U dynasty in 1784, a Muslim population
originating from Bengal was present in Arakan (see, for instance, Myanmar Institute of Strategic and
International Studies (MISIS), “Background Paper on Rakhine State” (28 May 2018), pp. 8-9 and
17-18; United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees, Memorandum of Jacques Cuénod and
Angelo Rasanayagam to United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees Headquarters, “Mission
to Dacca: 11 May-14 May 1978” (17 May 1978), para. 16). Myanmar thus acknowledges that
“[t]here was a population of Bengali-speakers in Arakan even before Burma became part of the
British Empire”. In 1799 reference was made to a dialect of Hindi spoken by Muslims “who have
long [been] settled in Arakan, and who call themselves Rooinga” (F. Buchanan (1799), “A
Comparative Vocabulary of Some of the Languages Spoken in the Burma Empire”, republished in
SOAS Bulletin of Burma Research, Vol. 1, No. 1 (Spring 2003), p. 55). In the nineteenth and
twentieth centuries, during the British colonial period, a large number of Muslims from present-day
Bangladesh also immigrated to modern Rakhine State. Myanmar contests, however, whether these
populations self-identified as a single group.

- 48 136. The record shows that in the period after independence in 1948, Rohingya were treated
as part of the polity of the Union of Burma (“Official transcript of the address delivered by Brigadier
General Aung Gyi, Vice Chief of Staff (Army), The Union of Burma Armed Forces Muhajid
Insurgents’ Surrender Ceremony, Maung Daw Town, Rakhine, Burma, 4 July 1961”, published in
Ministry of Defence, Union of Burma, Khit Yay (Current Affairs), Mayu Special Issue, Vol. 12, No. 6
(15 July 1961), pp. 8-10 and 23-26; Advisory Commission on Rakhine State, “Towards a peaceful,
fair and prosperous future for the people of Rakhine: Final report of the Advisory Commission on
Rakhine State” (August 2017) (hereinafter “Final report of the Advisory Commission on Rakhine
State (2017)”), p. 29; F. Wade, “IIMM Rohingya report” (January 2022), p. 5, Sec. 1.3; United States
Holocaust Memorial Museum, Video “Gradual Erasure: Burma’s Path to Genocide” (May 2020)).
The 1947 Constitution and 1948 Union Citizenship Act together recognized citizenship, inter alia,
for members of “indigenous races” that had been permanently settled in the country in 1823, prior to
British colonial rule. Under these instruments, citizenship was automatically recognized based on
either third-generation residence or descent from “indigenous races”, without the need to apply. No
exhaustive list of the groups qualifying as “indigenous races” was created, and the record does not
establish that Rohingya, as a group, were excluded from this category (United Nations High
Commissioner for Refugees, Memorandum of Jacques Cuénod and Angelo Rasanayagam to
United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees Headquarters, “Mission to Dacca: 11 May-14 May
1978” (17 May 1978), paras. 16 and 22-23). Separately, any person could “elect” to hold Burmese
citizenship if they met certain conditions of birthplace and residence; Myanmar accepts that “[t]ens
of thousands” of Rohingya received citizenship through this latter pathway. Like all residents,
Rohingya were issued National Registration Cards, which indicated their type of citizenship and gave
them access to the benefits enjoyed by citizens, although the cards did not constitute formal proof of
citizenship.
137. In 1982, Burma adopted a new citizenship law (Socialist Republic of the Union of Burma,
Burma Citizenship Law (15 October 1982) (hereinafter the “1982 Citizenship Law”)), which
established three types of citizenship, namely “citizen”, “associate citizen” and “naturalized citizen”,
and introduced a hierarchy among them, with full citizenship primarily tied to “national” races (ibid.,
Chap. I, Sec. 2). Section 3 of the 1982 Citizenship Law defined national races in a manner similar to
“indigenous races” under the 1948 Union Citizenship Act — namely, as including “such . . . ethnic
groups as have settled in any of the territories included within the State as their permanent home”
prior to 1823 — and did not expressly exclude the Rohingya (ibid., Chap. II, Sec. 3). However, the
1982 Citizenship Law further provided that it was for the Council of State to “decide whether any
ethnic group is national or not” (ibid., Chap. II, Sec. 4). The State exercised this authority by
identifying 8 specified groups recognized as “major ethnic national races” indigenous to the country,
which it later subdivided into 135 “national ethnic groups” (see paragraph 66 above). These latter
groups included the Kaman Muslim population of Rakhine State, but not the Rohingya. As a result,
the Rohingya were formally excluded for the first time from those entitled to citizenship based on
race or ethnic origin.
138. Myanmar contends that other avenues of citizenship are available to Rohingya under the
1982 Citizenship Law. Onerous and discriminatory citizenship verification procedures, however,
have resulted in the denial of citizenship to even Rohingya who could document their entitlement
(FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 479; United Nations Human Rights Council,
“Oral Update of the High Commissioner for Human Rights on Situation of Human Rights of

- 49 Rohingya People”, UN doc. A/HRC/38/CRP.2 (3 July 2018), para. 32). The Myanmar authorities
have also implemented successive identification régimes for the Rohingya. Following the 1982
Citizenship Law, in place of National Registration Cards, Myanmar began issuing Temporary
Identity Cards, or “white cards”, to the Rohingya population. As of 2015, Myanmar sought to replace
white cards with National Verification Cards (hereinafter “NVCs”), which state that card holders are
non-citizens (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 483-484). At least until 2016,
Rohingya were required to identify as “Bengali” on their application in order to receive a card (ibid.,
paras. 484-485; Final report of the Advisory Commission on Rakhine State (2017), p. 27; see
“Example of a National Verification Card (NVC)”, Counter-Memorial of Myanmar, Vol. IX,
Annex 469, and confidential document submitted as Annex 468 to Myanmar’s Counter-Memorial).
139. It is uncontested that numerous rights are conditioned on the possession of an NVC,
including the right to travel between townships, and the right to have access to employment, other
economic activities and higher education. Witnesses reported efforts by village leaders and the
military to pressure Rohingya to accept NVCs, which are widely perceived by the Rohingya as a
rejection of their Rohingya identity and Myanmar citizenship (FFM, Report of the detailed findings
(2018), para. 488). Witness NJ, for instance, told the Court that “that card says we are not nationals
of this country, that’s why we didn’t accept it” (CR 2026/14, p. 22 (Witness NJ)).
140. In September 2016, Myanmar established an Advisory Commission on Rakhine State,
with a mandate to examine the challenges facing Rakhine State and propose responses. The
Commission, which was chaired by former United Nations Secretary-General Kofi Annan,
comprised a majority of members from Myanmar and reported to the Government. In its final report
of 24 August 2017, the Advisory Commission observed that
“the [1982 Citizenship] law — and the way it was implemented — significantly
narrowed the prospects of citizenship for the Muslims in Rakhine. In 1989, a citizenship
inspection process was carried out across Myanmar . . . The majority of Muslims in
Rakhine with NRCs [National Registration Cards] surrendered their documents, but
were never issued with CSCs [Citizenship Scrutiny Cards], rendering them de facto
stateless.” (Final report of the Advisory Commission on Rakhine State (2017), p. 30.)
The Commission concluded that, as a result, only about 4,000 Muslims in Rakhine State were
recognized as enjoying any form of citizenship, leaving approximately one million Muslims
stateless (ibid., p. 26).
141. In 1992, Myanmar’s Permanent Mission to the United Nations conveyed through a Note
Verbale that “the so-called ‘Rohingyas’ never belonged to the national races or national racial groups
of Myanmar” and that “[t]he Rohingyas do not exist in Myanmar either historically, politically or
legally” (United Nations, Commission on Human Rights, “Implementation of the Declaration on the
Elimination of all Forms of Intolerance and of Discrimination Based on Religion or Belief: Report
submitted by Mr Angelo Vidal d’Almeida Ribeiro, Special Rapporteur appointed in accordance with
Commission on Human Rights resolution 1986/20 of 10 March 1986”, UN doc. E/CN.4/1993/62
(6 January 1993), pp. 76-77, para. 46). Officially and unofficially, “Bengalis” in Myanmar have been
characterized as illegal immigrants or foreigners.

- 50 142. The Court notes that the manner of granting access to citizenship for the Rohingya
through legal and administrative measures was discriminatory and contributed to the progressive
erosion of the political and civil rights of the Rohingya in Rakhine State in the decades leading up to
the 2017 clearance operations, leaving the group marginalized and particularly vulnerable (Final
report of the Advisory Commission on Rakhine State (2017), p. 29; United Nations High
Commissioner for Refugees, “Background note on discrimination in nationality laws and
statelessness” (October 2021), p. 9).
(b) Restrictions on marriage, births and birth certificates
143. The evidence establishes that in 1993 the Maungdaw Township border security force,
noting that “[p]opulation increase in Maung Daw Township is extremely high” and that “the majority
of residents in Maung Daw are Muslims”, adopted a temporary regional order imposing distinct
permission requirements for “Islamic” marriages. The measures were described as “for the good of
the region and the race” (Border Region Immigration Control Headquarters, Maung Daw, Letter
No. Na-Sa-Ka/56/U 4 (31 January 1993)). A subsequent regional order in force from 2005 to 2018
stated that “the birth rate is extremely high” and that “there is likely to be population explosion”
(Maung Daw Township Peace and Development Council, Regional Order 1/2005 (1 May 2005)
(hereinafter “Regional Order 1/2005”), para. 1). The order reiterated separate permission
requirements for Islamic marriages and established additional “Requirements for Bengalis who apply
for Permission to Marry”, including that men be photographed without a beard (ibid., Sec. 1 (c) and
Addendum 3).
144. Regional Order 1/2005 further provided that “those who have permission to marry must
limit the number of children, in order to control the birth rate” (Regional Order 1/2005, Sec. 1 (f)).
The birth restrictions were not limited to Muslims. The regional authorities, however, officially
described the order’s marriage and birth limitations as “Processes for Controlling Bengali
Population” (Border Region Immigration Control Headquarters, “Regional Order and Processes for
Controlling Bengali Population” (11 February 2008)). Regional Order 1/2005 provided for intrusive
verification measures, and witness testimony and evidence gathered by the FFM suggests it entailed
the payment of exorbitant fees or bribes (Affidavit of Witness NJ, para. 22; Affidavit of Witness MN,
para. 12; FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 593).
145. Evidence before the Court also establishes that breaches of the measures prescribed to
control marriages and births were subject to fines or imprisonment. Although such penalties were
not always enforced, Witness NJ recounted before the Court that she and her husband had to sign a
document stating that “we cannot [have] more than two children”. As she had more than two children,
Witness NJ had to hide one of her children from inspections for fear of her husband being sent to jail
or facing fines (CR 2026/14, p. 29 (Witness NJ)). Myanmar did not challenge this account. Other
witness testimony corroborates the existence of restrictions on marriage and children, and the impact
of these measures on individuals and their community (IIMM, Witness Statement
No. IIMM0019629329, para. 47; confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 343 to
The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 4).

- 51 146. Although the birth rate in northern Rakhine State was high, it was not the highest in the
country, and Myanmar did not impose similar restrictions in other regions or on other groups
(Republic of the Union of Myanmar, Ministry of Labour, Immigration and Population, Department
of Population, Census Atlas Myanmar: The 2014 Myanmar Population and Housing Census).
147. With respect to the registration of births and the issuance of birth certificates, evidence
in the record indicates that Myanmar stopped issuing birth certificates for Rohingya in the 1990s
(FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 463; United Nations Human Rights Council,
“Situation on human rights in Myanmar since 1 February 2022: Report of the United Nations High
Commissioner for Human Rights”, UN doc. A/HRC/52/21 (2 March 2023), para. 39). Myanmar
acknowledges that registration of “Bengali” newborns in northern Rakhine State ended after the 2012
violence. In 2012, Myanmar established a government Inquiry Commission on Sectarian Violence
in Rakhine State which estimated that by 2013, approximately 60,000 Rohingya children were
unregistered (Republic of the Union of Myanmar, “Final report of Inquiry Commission on Sectarian
Violence in Rakhine State” (8 July 2013), para. 12.28). The Commission attributed this to the
requirement that Bengalis obtain permission from local authorities to marry and to the lack of birth
records for children of unregistered relationships (ibid.). Although Myanmar submits that over
15,000 birth certificates were issued in Buthidaung and Maungdaw Townships in 2017 as a result of
renewed registration efforts, it does not represent that these were issued to Rohingya children. The
evidence before the Court also establishes the difficulty of having Rohingya children included on
household lists, the main form of documentation for Rohingya families in Rakhine State. Such
inclusion was a prerequisite for the issuance of identity documents, access to education, and
permission to travel within townships and to marry (Affidavit of Witness NJ, paras. 22-23; FFM,
Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 583-585; United Nations High Commissioner for
Refugees, “Study on community perceptions of citizenship, documentation and rights in Rakhine
State” (August 2016), p. 9).
(c) Other restrictions
148. Rohingya were subjected to other restrictions affecting numerous aspects of their daily
lives. These included severe limitations on movement for Rohingya both between and within
townships in northern Rakhine State (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027995869,
paras. 24-25, 28-29 and 31-32; IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019915259, paras. 35-36;
FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 500-511; United Nations Human Rights Council,
“Situation of human rights of Rohingya Muslims and other minorities in Myanmar: Report of the
United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights”, UN doc. A/HRC/32/18 (29 June 2016),
paras. 28-31). Access to livelihoods, food, healthcare and other services for the Rohingya population
was correspondingly constrained (ibid.; see also Final report of the Advisory Commission on
Rakhine State (2017), p. 33; FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 509 and 534-539).
The resulting delays in access to emergency healthcare could prove life threatening, including for
babies and women experiencing complications during pregnancy and childbirth (United Nations
Human Rights Council, “Situation of human rights of Rohingya Muslims and other minorities in
Myanmar: Report of the United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights”,
UN doc. A/HRC/32/18 (29 June 2016), para. 39).

- 52 149. Rohingya travelling with or without documents faced arbitrary fees and demands for
payment, while those travelling without the required certificates also risked arrest at security
checkpoints (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027992306, paras. 59, 61, 63-64 and 101; FFM,
Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 511 and 525; United Nations Human Rights Council,
“Situation of human rights of Rohingya Muslims and other minorities in Myanmar: Report of the
United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights”, UN doc. A/HRC/32/18 (29 June 2016),
para. 28). The Court notes that restrictions on travel between townships also applied to the Kaman
Muslims, despite their being recognized as Myanmar citizens (see paragraph 137 above), indicating
an application of the policy to Muslims generally (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),
paras. 503-505).
150. Restrictions were also imposed on humanitarian aid to the region following the 2016
events (see paragraph 175 below). In 2017, just prior to the clearance operations, the Government of
Myanmar banned the World Food Programme from operating in northern Rakhine State following a
report finding that nearly one third of the population of northern Rakhine State was “severely food
insecure”, with women and children particularly affected (IIMM, Witness Statement
No. IIMM0001557475, paras. 95-96; World Food Programme, “Food security assessment in the
northern part of Rakhine State: Final report” (July 2017), p. 1). The report, which covered a
geographic area that was 90 per cent populated by Rohingya, found that “[r]estriction of movement
was one of the main constraints” for accessing food (ibid., p. 2), and that “limited access to essential
services including health care, and poor access to safe water and sanitation may have exacerbated . . .
already serious malnutrition” (ibid., p. 1). Médecins Sans Frontières was also required to terminate
its services in northern Rakhine State two weeks prior to the 2017 clearance operations. A year later
it had not been allowed to resume (Médecins Sans Frontières, “International humanitarian access to
Rakhine State must urgently be permitted”, press release (18 September 2017); Médecins Sans
Frontières, “Myanmar continues to block humanitarian access in Rakhine State”, press release
(10 August 2018); see paragraph 124 above).
151. Following the sectarian violence in 2012, over 100,000 internally displaced Rohingya
were confined in camps in central Rakhine State (United Nations Security Council, “Report of the
Secretary-General on children and armed conflict in Myanmar”, UN doc. S/2018/956 (29 October
2018), para. 3; United Nations Human Rights Council, “Situation of human rights of Rohingya
Muslims and other minorities in Myanmar: Report of the United Nations High Commissioner for
Human Rights”, UN doc. A/HRC/32/18 (29 June 2016), para. 30), with another 4,000 in the Aung
Mingalar quarter of Sittwe (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 520; United Nations
Human Rights Council, “Report of the Special Rapporteur on the situation of human rights in
Myanmar”, UN doc. A/HRC/25/64 (2 April 2014), para. 44). IDP camps for ethnic Rakhine and
other non-Rohingya ethnic groups displaced in 2012 were subsequently closed, with the residents
returned to their villages or resettled with government assistance (Final report of the Advisory
Commission on Rakhine State (2017), p. 35; FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),
paras. 689-691; United Nations and Partners, “Humanitarian Response Plan, January-December
2019” (December 2018), p. 10). However, Myanmar reports that, as of November 2025, over
100,000 Rohingya remained in IDP camps.

- 53 152. The Advisory Commission on Rakhine State found in 2017 that living conditions in the
IDP camps “remain poor, with overcrowded shelters and inadequate access to services and livelihood
opportunities”, while “Muslim IDPs are almost entirely deprived of freedom of movement” (Final
report of the Advisory Commission on Rakhine State (2017), pp. 33-35). Rohingya IDPs have been
prevented “from engaging in livelihoods, attending school or accessing health services and they
regularly face exploitation by the authorities” (United Nations General Assembly, “Report of the
Special Rapporteur on the situation of human rights in Myanmar”, UN doc. A/73/332 (20 August
2018), para. 52; see also United Nations and Partners, “Humanitarian Response Plan,
January-December 2019” (December 2018), p. 11; FFM, Detailed findings (2019), paras. 156-159
and 172; Amnesty International, “‘We will destroy everything’: Military responsibility for crimes
against humanity in Rakhine State, Myanmar” (27 June 2018) (hereinafter “Amnesty International,
‘We will destroy everything’”), pp. 107-108; see paragraph 171 below).
2. Hate speech
153. The Court turns next to examining the allegations relating to anti-Rohingya speech,
particularly messages emanating from the Myanmar military, including a military network on
Facebook (see paragraphs 161-163 below). Both Parties refer to hate speech in their submissions,
without defining such speech. While there is no international legal definition for hate speech, for
purposes of analysing the arguments of the Parties and the record in this case, the Court considers
hate speech to involve pejorative language that was derogatory towards or that dehumanized the
Rohingya.
154. Myanmar acknowledges that hate speech within the general population has been a matter
of concern, particularly since the intercommunal violence in 2012. It denies, however, that the
Government of Myanmar or its military engaged in any campaign of hate speech and maintains that
statements of political parties and monks cannot be taken to reflect the views of the Government.
155. The record establishes that following the 2012 violence, discourse that disparaged and
dehumanized the Rohingya increased, including in the media and in the sermons of monks. In varying
ways, the messages portrayed the Rohingya community as deceitful, threatening and a legitimate
target of violence. Many messages used the derogatory slur “kalar”, portraying the Rohingya as
dark-skinned foreigners. Such speech was also propagated by State authorities, as described below.
(a) Senior General Min Aung Hlaing and the Tatmadaw
156. The Commander-in-Chief of the military, Senior General Min Aung Hlaing, delivered a
series of speeches both during and after the 2017 clearance operations. While the Court does not have
a transcript of those speeches, The Gambia submitted English versions of online reports of their
content, the accuracy of which was not disputed by Myanmar.
157. For example, on 1 September 2017, while the clearance operations were under way,
Senior General Hlaing gave a speech entitled “Entire government institutions and people must defend

- 54 the country with strong patriotism”, at a fundraiser for the security forces that had participated in the
clearance operations and for ethnic Rakhine civilians who had fled the ARSA attacks. The audience
comprised high-level military and other government officials and private donors. The Senior General
denounced the “Bengali terrorists” for their “well-planned terrorist acts” of 25 August 2017. He then
stated:
“There were Muslims but there was no problem. But the situation in Buthidaung
and Maungtaw regions is different. Bengalis from those regions were taken into the
country as manpower from Bengal region during the colonial era. Their population
increased through various means, and later they attacked the local ethnics.
During the Alethankyaw crisis in 1942, over 20,000 ethnic Rakhine people were
slaughtered. Bengalis after murdering ethnic Rakhine people seized their land and
villages and lived there. There is no oppression or intimidation against Bengalis in
Rakhine region. The only measure security forces are taking is to ensure everything is
within the framework of the law. The root cause of the recent crisis is the well-planned
terrorist acts that came together with the denial of legal verification while ignoring the
1982 Citizenship Law. The act of using religion as a tool to make instigation and violent
attacks is totally unacceptable. People living in Myanmar must obey its laws. The
Bengali problem was a long-standing one which has become an unfinished job despite
the efforts of the previous governments to solve it. The government in office is taking
great care in solving the problem.
As regards the term Bengali, . . . Bengalis calling themselves Rohingya has
become a national cause. So we openly declare that ‘absolutely, our country has no
Rohingya race.’” (Senior General Min Aung Hlaing, “Entire government institutions
and people must defend the country with strong patriotism” (1 September 2017).)
At a similar fundraiser on 10 September 2017, the Senior General reiterated that “the Bengali
issue in Rakhine State occurred in the colonial era” and that “[e]fforts to solve this problem by the
successive governments went unfinished” (Senior General Min Aung Hlaing, “Lack of
country-loving spirits may lead to disunity and all citizens should have country-loving spirits,
patriotic spirits and Myanmar spirits” (10 September 2017)).
158. At a speech later that month honouring members of the military for their “gallant” and
“brilliant efforts” in the clearance operations, Senior General Hlaing again emphasized the attacks of
the “extremist Bengali terrorists”, and stated:
“[T]he Bengali population exploded and the aliens tried to seize the land of local
ethnics. The 1942 Alethankyaw crisis is the worst in history. Even during the
post-independence era, the Tatmadaw had to crush the Mujahidin insurgents.
................................................................
[C]ollective efforts must be made to protect the minorities of
Buthidaung/Maungtaw region . . . In checking the cases in which Bengalis trying to

- 55 sneak into the country through various means were seized involvement of some officials
[was] found. Race cannot be swallowed by the ground but only by another race. All
must be loyal to the State in serving their duties, so that such cases will never happen
again.” (Senior General Min Aung Hlaing, “Gallant efforts to defend the HQ against
terrorist attacks and brilliant efforts to restore regional peace, security are honoured”
(20 September 2017).)
Finally, while addressing the ARSA attacks of 25 August at a speech to military graduates in
December, he stated that the “Tatmadaw must fully protect the ethnic minorities in Rakhine region
not to have the genocide” (Senior General Min Aung Hlaing, “Try to be good officers who are
reliable for higher officials, given respect by lower ranks, be trustworthy of the populace”
(2 December 2017)).
159. The Court considers that the Senior General’s messages, as reported, were capable of
fuelling hatred against the Rohingya. The speeches were not limited to condemning ARSA
“terrorists” involved in the August 2017 attacks, but repeatedly portrayed the Rohingya in general as
a violent threat to other ethnic groups that needed to be addressed. The Senior General described a
“long-standing” “Bengali problem”, accused the “Bengalis” of threatening to “swallow up” the
legitimate races, and emphasized their non-recognition as a “national race”. The speeches were
widely disseminated through official Facebook Pages (“Pages”), including that of the Senior General,
as well as other Pages of the Facebook military network (see paragraphs 161-162 below; IIMM,
“Anti-Rohingya hate speech on Facebook: Content and network analysis” (27 March 2024), para. 79;
FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 1329 and 1336).
160. The Senior General’s message echoed a 2012 Tatmadaw training presentation entitled
“Fear of Extinction of Race”, which was devoted to warning of the “danger of [Buddhism] being
swallowed up by Bangladeshi Chittagonian Indian Muslims (TRANSLATOR’S NOTE Using
derogatory phrase kowtow kalars)” and that “[n]o race faces extinction from being swallowed up by
the earth, but by human beings” (Tatmadaw, Nyi Pyi Taw Divisional Military Headquarters,
Organizing Public Lecture prepared by No. 13 Combatants Organizing School: “Fear of Extinction
of Race” (Combatants organizing (officer) Training Batch No. 102) (26 October 2012), pp. 4-9;
FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 716).
(b) Facebook military network
161. Facebook was Myanmar’s most widely used social media network at the time of the
clearance operations, with approximately 14 million resident users in 2018 (IIMM, “Anti-Rohingya
hate speech on Facebook: Content and network analysis” (27 March 2024), para. 3). In August 2018,
Facebook Inc. (known as Meta since 2021) removed 43 Pages containing public content from the
platform. Six of the Pages were linked to persons or entities that were banned from Facebook for
“human rights abuses” in Myanmar, including Pages in the name of Senior General Min Aung Hlaing
and the Office of the Commander-in-Chief of the Defence Services (ibid., paras. 6-10), which had
1.4 and 2.9 million followers, respectively. The other 37 were removed for “engaging in coordinated
inauthentic behaviour” to “covertly push the messages of the Myanmar military” (ibid., para. 6; see
also Affidavit of Nathaniel Gleicher, Head of Security Policy at Meta Platforms, Inc. (2 November
2022)).

- 56 162. According to an independent investigation conducted by the IIMM, 36 of the removed
Pages included a total of 10,485 pieces of content containing hate speech against the Rohingya
between July and December 2017 (IIMM, “Anti-Rohingya hate speech on Facebook: Content and
network analysis” (27 March 2024), para. 175). For example, the IIMM found posts containing hate
speech on “‘verified’ Pages, which de facto served as the official presence of the Tatmadaw on
Facebook”, including the Pages of Senior General Min Aung Hlaing and his office (ibid., paras. 8-10
and 176). The IIMM further found extensive confirmation of a “coordinated network” among all
43 Pages “with clear ties to the Myanmar military” (ibid., para. 5). Often, the Pages shared creators,
administrators and/or editors and were posting material using the same IP addresses and the same
shared devices as those used for Pages overtly affiliated with the military. The same material was
frequently posted on multiple Pages, sometimes within minutes (ibid., paras. 88-159 and 177). The
linkages among the Pages “demonstrate[d] the systematic and coordinated nature of the hate speech
content” (ibid., para. 178), which explains why the IIMM used the term Facebook “Military
Network” (ibid., para. 93).
163. Myanmar disputes the existence of a co-ordinated network, contending that while
members of the Tatmadaw or police force may have administered such Pages from their office
internet connection, they operated “outside the scope of their employment” and without instruction.
Myanmar provides no evidence to rebut the findings of the IIMM that there was an interconnected
network of Pages, while the IIMM persuasively establishes a co-ordinated relationship between hate
speech posted on Pages and members of the Tatmadaw.
164. The IIMM considered hate speech in this context to be content “intended or likely to
influence its Facebook audience to fear . . . [or] hate the target group and/or to encourage, support or
excuse discriminatory measures or violence directed against the target group” (IIMM,
“Anti-Rohingya hate speech on Facebook: Content and network analysis” (27 March 2024),
para. 13). The IIMM found that posts on the Facebook military network “often played upon prevalent
discriminatory and derogatory narratives”, including “that the Rohingya pose an existential threat to
Myanmar through violence, terrorism or ‘Islamisation’”, and “pose a threat to Burmese racial purity
through their alleged rampant breeding” (ibid., para. 175). Examples of posts found on the Facebook
military network include the following:
⎯ Portraying the “Kalar” as “murderous, rapists who will root out the entire Buddhism” (ibid.,
para. 189);
⎯ “‘Kalar-soe’ [bad Kalar], like you, relatives of cows, we don’t hesitate to punch you till your
teeth fall out! . . . If you say you love the Myanmar land so much, you can get a six-foot pit [a
grave]” (ibid., para. 228);
⎯ Claiming that India is “eliminating all the Bengalis who enter the [border] area under the
shoot-to-kill policy [and i]t is high time for Myanmar” to do the same (ibid., para. 199);
⎯ Warning of the “reproduction rate of the . . . extremist, terrorist Bengali Muslim people . . .
[and] the brutal killing of our races” (ibid., para. 192);
⎯ “[T]he Bengali turned Rohingya [to] try to become ‘Taing-Yin-Thar’ [ethnic group] . . . There is
no limit on population, marrying as they wish, birthing children as they wish, they draw up plans
to increase their population size” (ibid., para. 218).

- 57 165. A video posted on at least four different sites of the Facebook military network on
28 August 2017, the day after the clearance operations in Chut Pyin and Maung Nu, shows a
uniformed soldier explaining the plan for the clearance operations to a group of people, presumably
non-Rohingya civilians in Rakhine State (IIMM, “Anti-Rohingya hate speech on Facebook: Content
and network analysis” (27 March 2024), paras. 321-329). The soldier states:
“They [the Rohingya] are eating what we give them . . . they will even take over
the whole country if they can. That’s why, these animals, who are not from our country,
infiltrating into our country . . . and making all kinds of threats . . . [W]e will clear the
villages where those animals live . . . We have guns. We have bullets. That’s what we
came with. With ammunitions and the spirit to attack these animals, we have come
here . . . [I]f you can carry a sword, then carry a sword, if you can carry a stick, then
carry a stick. Carry whatever you can and bravely face these animals.” (Ibid., para. 320.)
166. Another message posted on two different Facebook military network Pages on
6 September 2017 asked: “How have you prepared to face Jihad?” (IIMM, “Anti-Rohingya hate
speech on Facebook: Content and network analysis” (27 March 2024), para. 360). The post explained
that “[t]he Muslims” had “systematically laid down [a] tactical plan” of jihad for murder and burning
of homes. “Therefore, all the ethnic Myanmar people need to respond systematically”. The message
then described the analogous fate of others:
“During the Jihad attack of Muslims in Indonesia and Bangladesh, . . . all the
Muslims came out with swords/knives drawn from their houses at the same time. They
forcefully entered the houses of the Buddhist people, and acted as if they were possessed
by demons — old people killed other old people, women killed other women, children
killed children. The Buddhist people who did not keep a sharp blade at home even for
chopping onions suffered under the brilliant pre-planning of the Muslims. Hundreds of
thousands of Buddhist necks were slain by Muslims’ blades. Now, Myanmar, which is
a Buddhist country, in order to prevent the same scenario that happened in Indonesia
and Bangladesh, I want you to sharpen even the knives at home that you do not use.”
(Ibid.)
The post was accompanied by four cartoons portraying masked “terrorists” infiltrating the country
and murdering people (ibid., p. 179).
167. These and other messages dehumanized the Rohingya, portrayed them as foreign
infiltrators and as violent and existential threats to the purity and existence of other ethnic groups
and to Buddhism, and at times encouraged and justified violence against them.
168. On 24 August 2017, the day before the ARSA attacks and the commencement of the
clearance operations, the Facebook Page of the Office of the Commander-in-Chief of the Defence
Services reported that security forces had repelled an attack by 200 “Bengali carrying sticks, swords,
and spears” (IIMM, “Anti-Rohingya hate speech on Facebook: Content and network analysis”
(27 March 2024), para. 64). This post alone, according to the IIMM, “unleashed a torrent of public
vitriol” (ibid., para. 65). Of the 685 comments posted in reaction, over 200 “explicitly called for the
Rohingya villagers, as well as Rohingya and/or Muslims in general, to be shot, killed, exterminated
or permanently removed from Myanmar” (ibid.).

- 58 B. Events of 2016 to 2017
169. The Court now turns to the facts regarding the events of 2016 to 2017 in northern Rakhine
State. It will begin with an examination of the nature and activities of ARSA (see paragraph 75
above), and then consider the evidential record relating to the events of 2016 in northern Rakhine
State and the clearance operations that commenced on 25 August 2017.
1. Arakan Rohingya Salvation Army
170. Myanmar contends that in 2016 and 2017 it conducted counter-terrorism operations in
northern Rakhine State, in response to attacks by ARSA and its supporters (see paragraph 75 above).
The existence of ARSA is not in dispute. However, the Parties differ regarding the size, degree of
organization and sophistication, and military capabilities of the group. Other armed groups also
operated in northern Rakhine State during the relevant period, most notably the Arakan Army, a
Rakhine insurgent group allegedly established in 2015 and whose activities intensified after 2018,
but their activities are not directly relevant to the situation before the Court.
171. In support of their contentions regarding ARSA, both Parties have relied on a report by
Amnesty International introduced into the record by The Gambia (Amnesty International, “We will
destroy everything”). The report is based on interviews with over 300 Rohingya in Bangladesh, as
well as 41 interviews conducted within Rakhine State with members of other ethnic groups, several
members of Myanmar’s security forces and village administrators, and on analysis of satellite
imagery and forensic evidence (ibid., pp. 17-18; see paragraph 124 above). The Amnesty
International report thus constitutes one of the few sources in the record based in part on evidence
collected within Myanmar independently of either Party. Myanmar also produced reports from the
International Crisis Group addressing the development of ARSA, which were also relied upon by
The Gambia in relation to ARSA activities and other matters (International Crisis Group, “Myanmar:
A new Muslim insurgency in Rakhine State”, Asia Report No. 283 (15 December 2016);
International Crisis Group, “Myanmar’s Rohingya crisis enters a dangerous new phase”, Asia Report
No. 292 (7 December 2017), p. 6). These reports by the International Crisis Group are based on
information and assessments gathered in the field by teams of political analysts (see paragraph 124
above).
172. ARSA first emerged in northern Rakhine State in the wake of the sectarian violence in
2012 (see paragraph 75 above), with the stated objective of “defend[ing], salvag[ing] and protect[ing]
[the] Rohingya community in Arakan” (ARSA, press release, ARSA/PR/01/2017 (29 March 2017)).
According to Myanmar, participation in the group was limited to a few hundred members in 2016.
By 25 August 2017, the group appears to have grown to at least “several thousand” (Amnesty
International, “We will destroy everything”, p. 23), including “supporters” — local villagers who
joined in violence initiated by the group ⎯ often spontaneously (FFM, Report of the detailed
findings (2018), para. 1022; Amnesty International, “We will destroy everything”, p. 23). ARSA’s
structure, including its senior leadership, is not well-documented, but evidence shows that ARSA
had a network of village “cells” in northern Rakhine State, as well as a hierarchy of leadership, some
of whom appear to have been based in foreign countries and may have benefited from some amount
of foreign funding (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0028674762, paras. 139-152; FFM, Report
of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 1016-1017; International Crisis Group, “Myanmar: A new

- 59 Muslim insurgency in Rakhine State”, Asia Report No. 283 (15 December 2016), p. 15). ARSA’s
activities in 2016 and 2017 (see paragraphs 175-178 below) also evidence its ability to co-ordinate
its members.
173. The evidence in the record indicates that ARSA had a modest weapons arsenal, estimated
at 40 to 70 firearms, and that “the majority of ARSA members used bladed weapons, sticks and a
small number of locally produced (artisanal) firearms” (IIMM, “Evidence of ARSA weapons
possession and use” (29 April 2024), para. 3; see also FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),
para. 1032). This is consistent with the Tatmadaw’s own records of the weapons seized during the
2017 clearance operations. In Chut Pyin in August 2017, for example, where Myanmar contends its
security forces confronted 200 to 400 fighters, killing 32 of them, its military recovered
“2 homemade guns (not functioning)” (confidential document submitted as Annex 120 to
Myanmar’s Counter-Memorial, p. 7, para. (F)); and in Min Gyi, where Myanmar contends it killed
101 ARSA fighters, no guns were seized (confidential document submitted as Annex 127 to
Myanmar’s Counter-Memorial, pp. 5-6). While ARSA made use of crude improvised explosive
devices (“IEDs”), the evidence is inconclusive regarding the extent of its ability to manufacture and
use them effectively (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019628625, paras. 82-83; see also
IIMM, “Evidence of ARSA weapons possession and use” (29 April 2024), para. 3).
174. Although ARSA possessed limited weapons capabilities and to a significant extent
comprised individuals with little or no military training, by August 2017 it appears to have been
sufficiently organized to be capable of conducting approximately 30 co-ordinated violent attacks and
mobilizing several thousand Rohingya villagers to participate, as discussed below (FFM, Report of
the detailed findings (2018), paras. 56, 58, 1029-1030 and 1038; Amnesty International, “We will
destroy everything”, p. 38). The International Crisis Group reported that ARSA’s 9 October 2016
attack “marked a major escalation of violence in Rakhine and reflected an unprecedented level of
planning” (International Crisis Group, “Myanmar: A new Muslim insurgency in Rakhine State”, Asia
Report No. 283 (15 December 2016), p. 6). The Gambia’s expert also testified orally that, as of
25 August 2017, ARSA constituted a “serious” insurgency. All told, Myanmar submits that a total
of 13 members of its security forces were killed in the 2017 ARSA attacks and the ensuing clearance
operations.
2. Events leading up to the August 2017 clearance operations
175. The Court accepts Myanmar’s contention that on 9 October 2016, ARSA attacked three
Border Guard Police headquarters, with the stated objective of acquiring weapons. Those attacks
resulted in the deaths of nine police officers and eight assailants (FFM, Report of the detailed findings
(2018), para. 1036; International Crisis Group, “Myanmar: A new Muslim insurgency in Rakhine
State”, Asia Report No. 283 (15 December 2016), p. 6). While subsequent clashes allegedly occurred
between October and November 2016, including several attacks on security force convoys, the record
does not establish whether ARSA was involved (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),
para. 1037; see also International Crisis Group, “Myanmar: A new Muslim insurgency in Rakhine
State”, Asia Report No. 283 (15 December 2016), pp. 6-7). Myanmar’s first clearance operation was
in response to the October 2016 attacks. The resulting violence and conflict led to the displacement
and flight of approximately 87,000 Rohingya to Bangladesh (see paragraph 75 above; FFM, Report

- 60 of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 1070 and 1404). Following ARSA’s 2016 attacks, at least
416 individuals suspected of involvement in the ARSA attacks were arrested, investigated, charged
and prosecuted (IIMM, “Evidence related to detention of Rohingya in Buthidaung Prison and other
locations” (15 May 2024), paras. 28-30).
176. ARSA killed or abducted Rohingya informants both before and after 9 October 2016, and
such practices increased in 2017 (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 1052-1055;
International Crisis Group, “Myanmar: A new Muslim insurgency in Rakhine State”, Asia Report
