No. 283 (15 December 2016), p. 16). Myanmar referred to five ARSA attacks against other ethnic
groups in the months preceding 25 August 2017. The evidence it relied upon, however, indicates that
only the attack in the Kyun Pauk Pyu Su village tract on 24 June 2017, in which two villagers were
killed, could be conclusively attributed to ARSA (International Crisis Group, “Myanmar’s Rohingya
crisis enters a dangerous new phase”, Asia Report No. 292 (7 December 2017), p. 3; Amnesty
International, “We will destroy everything”, p. 44; see also FFM, Report of the detailed findings
(2018), para. 1137). The Court notes that Myanmar did not declare ARSA a terrorist organization
prior to 25 August 2017.
177. On 25 August 2017, ARSA carried out co-ordinated attacks on at least 30 security force
outposts across northern Rakhine State using the weaponry indicated above (see paragraph 173), with
further attacks occurring in the days that followed. The Court will review the evidence regarding
ARSA attacks in relation to specific locations below. The Court observes that, while these attacks
primarily targeted police and military outposts, there is credible evidence that ARSA also carried out
several attacks against civilians. In two villages in the Kha Maung Seik village tract, where a police
station was also attacked on 25 August, up to 99 Hindus were killed, while another attack the
following day near Myo Thu Gyi killed 6 other Hindus (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),
paras. 1039, 1042, 1050, 1060 and 1163; Amnesty International, “We will destroy everything”, p. 45;
Amnesty International, “Myanmar: New evidence reveals Rohingya armed group massacred scores
in Rakhine State” (22 May 2018), pp. 2-6; International Crisis Group, “Myanmar’s Rohingya crisis
enters a dangerous new phase”, Asia Report No. 292 (7 December 2017), pp. 4 and 7).
178. The record supports Myanmar’s contention that in the days following the co-ordinated
attacks of 25 August 2017, the ARSA commander-in-chief issued a message ordering the burning of
non-Rohingya villages (International Crisis Group, “Myanmar’s Rohingya crisis enters a dangerous
new phase”, Asia Report No. 292 (7 December 2017), p. 6; Amnesty International, “We will destroy
everything”, p. 47). The Court is satisfied, on the basis of satellite imagery and other evidence in the
record, that ARSA burned the villages of Ah Htet Pyu Ma and Khu Daing (FFM, Report of the
detailed findings (2018), paras. 1061-1064; International Crisis Group, “Myanmar’s Rohingya crisis
enters a dangerous new phase”, Asia Report No. 292 (7 December 2017), p. 7). With respect to
Ah Htet Pyu Ma, satellite imagery indicated that both the Rakhine and Rohingya parts of the village
were burned, in contrast to other villages burned during the 2017 clearance operations, in which
Rakhine areas were left intact (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 1063; see also
paragraph 271 below). Although Myanmar suggests the possibility of other burnings, no additional
specific instances of village burning attributable to ARSA are substantiated by the record (FFM,
Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 757 and 1062-1065; International Crisis Group,
“Myanmar’s Rohingya crisis enters a dangerous new phase”, Asia Report No. 292 (7 December
2017), p. 7).

- 61 C. Myanmar’s clearance operations
179. The Court now turns to the clearance operations launched by the Myanmar security forces
in Rohingya villages in northern Rakhine State in August 2017. The Parties agree that in the months
leading up to the co-ordinated ARSA attacks of 25 August 2017, the Tatmadaw built up its military
capacity in northern Rakhine State, which Myanmar contends was in response to ARSA’s increasing
activities. During this period, Myanmar also took measures such as confiscating any objects
considered capable of being used as weapons from Rohingya across the region and ordering the
removal of barriers and fences around Rohingya homes and land (see, for instance, Affidavit of
Witness NJ, para. 29; Affidavit of Witness MS, para. 25; IIMM, Witness Statement
No. IIMM0019629206, para. 58; IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027971404, para. 32; IIMM,
Witness Statement No. IIMM0027992947, para. 63; FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),
paras. 1123-1126). The Gambia contends that these actions rendered the Rohingya defenceless;
Myanmar maintains that they were legitimate counter-terrorism measures. Even if adopted for the
security reasons advanced by Myanmar, these measures foreseeably reduced the ability of Rohingya
civilians to protect themselves.
180. On 10 August 2017, over 1,600 members of the Tatmadaw’s 33rd and 99th Light Infantry
Divisions were airlifted into Rakhine State (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 1151).
Additional military units were attached to Military Operational Command (MOC)-15, covering
Buthidaung Township (ibid., para. 1152). Moreover, in the weeks prior to 25 August 2017, the
Tatmadaw moved closer to the border areas and increased its presence in areas normally guarded by
BGP units (ibid., para. 1153). According to Myanmar, 3,000 to 3,500 frontline and 1,000 to
1,500 support security forces were ultimately deployed.
181. The clearance operations began on 25 August 2017, the day the ARSA attacks
commenced. The evidence demonstrates that in the hours following ARSA’s initial attacks,
Myanmar’s security forces were deployed to several Rohingya villages which housed security force
outposts targeted by ARSA’s attacks, including Kha Maung Seik, Ta Man Thar, Myin Hlut (see
paragraph 245 below) and Ah Lel Than Kyaw (see paragraph 242 below), all located in Maungdaw
Township. Myanmar’s security forces conducted wider and more systematic operations over the
following days, with multiple units operating simultaneously across all three townships, including in
the villages of Chut Pyin (Rathedaung) and Maung Nu (Buthidaung) on 27 August 2017 (see
paragraphs 200 and 213 below). The following day, separate units undertook operations in villages
including Gu Dar Pyin (Buthidaung) (see paragraph 226 below), and Koe Tan Kauk and
Chein Khar Li (Rathedaung) (see paragraph 234 below). Min Gyi (Maungdaw) was among the
villages subject to clearance operations on 30 August 2017 (see paragraph 186 below), while
locations including Inn Din (Maungdaw) were targeted on 1 September 2017 (see paragraph 230
below) (see sketch-map No. 1 for locations).

- 62 SKETCH-MAP NO. 1 — THE TOWNSHIPS OF NORTHERN RAKHINE STATE AND THE LOCATION OF
VILLAGES REFERRED TO IN THE GAMBIA’S MEMORIAL

(Source: Memorial of The Gambia, Vol. XII, fig. 1.2.)

- 63 182. Both ARSA’s offensive and Myanmar’s response caused residents to flee into nearby
villages, some of which were in turn targeted by clearance operations. For instance, in
Buthidaung Township, ARSA’s attack on Hpaung Taw Pyin’s BGP outpost and Myanmar’s
subsequent operation in that village on 25 August 2017 caused residents to flee into Maung Nu,
which was in turn targeted on 27 August 2017 (see paragraph 217 below). The security forces
involved in the Maung Nu clearance operation, specifically the 552nd and 564th Light Infantry
Regiments, had conducted operations in the preceding days in Taung Bazar and Nga Yant Chaung.
183. In Maungdaw Township, clearance operations in Wet Kyein (also spelled “Wat Kyein”)
and Pa Da Kar Ywar Thit on 26 and 29 August 2017 followed ARSA’s attacks at Net Chaung and
Wet Kyein on 25 and 26 August 2017. These clearance operations caused numerous residents of
those two villages to flee to Min Gyi, which was in turn targeted by the security forces on 30 August
2017 (see paragraph 187 below). Witness NJ recalled that several days after she fled Chut Pyin, the
village in which she was sheltering was in turn attacked by Myanmar’s security forces (Affidavit of
Witness NJ, para. 70).
184. Myanmar contends that the clearance operations officially ended on 5 September 2017
but acknowledges sporadic incidents thereafter (see paragraph 75 above). The evidence establishes
that, after that date, Myanmar continued to engage in the burning and destruction of villages targeted
by clearance operations (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 969; see
paragraphs 272-282 below).
185. As noted, the Court will not examine every alleged incident, finding it sufficient to
examine those facts that may elucidate whether acts prohibited under the Genocide Convention were
committed (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 143, para. 242; see paragraph 132
above). The Court accordingly will examine (1) the events in the three locations primarily addressed
by the Parties: (a) Min Gyi, (b) Chut Pyin and (c) Maung Nu. It will then examine (2) other locations
identified as sites of grave incidents during the clearance operations, before finally assessing (3) the
broader evidence regarding the common aspects of Myanmar’s clearance operations. The Court will
use the Burmese name for locations and indicate the Rohingya name in parentheses, in line with the
practice followed by both Parties.
1. Events in Min Gyi, Chut Pyin and Maung Nu
(a) Min Gyi (Tula Toli)
186. Min Gyi was a majority Rohingya village situated in Maungdaw Township, which was
surrounded on three sides by the Purma River (see sketch-map No. 2 below). As set forth below, the
Court finds that on the morning of 30 August 2017, the Tatmadaw’s 99th Light Infantry Division
conducted a clearance operation during which it engaged in the indiscriminate and targeted killing
of civilians, including children and persons fleeing; separated and executed men; committed
widespread sexual violence; and burned people, bodies and homes, while leaving the settlement
inhabited by the Rakhine ethnic group largely untouched.

- 64 SKETCH-MAP NO. 2 — MIN GYI AND SURROUNDING VILLAGES

(Source: Material submitted by Myanmar at the hearing pursuant to Practice Direction IXquater.)

187. Myanmar alleges that the operation was conducted in response to ARSA violence on
25 and 26 August 2017 in the vicinity of Min Gyi. Myanmar does not contend that any ARSA
operation took place within the village of Min Gyi itself prior to the clearance operation. The record,
however, indicates that some ARSA activity occurred in nearby areas across the river from Min Gyi
beginning on 25 August 2017, including at the police checkpoint at Net Chaung, a few kilometres
north of Min Gyi, and at the deserted police checkpoint at Wet Kyein (FFM, Report of the detailed
findings (2018), para. 757; see also Amnesty International, “We will destroy everything”, p. 73; see
paragraph 183 above). Witnesses confirmed signs of skirmishes between ARSA and the military in
the period between 26 and 29 August 2017 (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027992990,
para. 153; see also confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 157 to Myanmar’s
Counter-Memorial, pp. 1-3; confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 83 to Myanmar’s
Rejoinder, pp. 1-3). As noted above (see paragraph 183), the security forces conducted two clearance
operations between 26 and 29 August 2017 in the Rohingya villages in the Wet Kyein and
Pa Da Kar Ywar Thit village tracts across the river from Min Gyi, shooting villagers and using
“launchers” — weapons firing explosive projectiles — to set houses on fire (FFM, Report of the
detailed findings (2018), paras. 758-759, and satellite image, p. 181). A number of villagers who
escaped those operations fled to Min Gyi.
188. On the morning of 30 August 2017, Myanmar’s security forces entered Min Gyi from the
north, accompanied by ethnic Rakhine and members of other ethnic groups from nearby villages
equipped with long knives, machetes and other types of local weapons (FFM, Report of the detailed
findings (2018), paras. 761-762 and 777). Upon approaching the village, the security forces opened
fire and began burning houses from the outskirts, using launchers (ibid., para. 761). An IIMM witness
and other written testimony establish the presence of a helicopter flying over the village that morning,
which was observed landing in a nearby ethnic Rakhine village and unloading containers of petrol

- 65 (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027993001, paras. 154-155; LAW, Witness Statement
No. 009, paras. 37-38; confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 355 to The Gambia’s
Memorial, para. 16).
189. As the security forces advanced into the village, inhabitants fled, some towards the
surrounding hills and others towards a large sandbank area by the river, commonly referred to as the
shore. Witness MS testified before the Court that he observed houses burning and heard continuous
gunfire as he and his family fled towards the shore (CR 2026/15, pp. 13-14 (Witness MS)). IIMM
Witness P1301 similarly recounted that he was at home that morning and heard shooting, so he and
his family left their house and fled towards the riverbank, with the hope of crossing to Wet Kyein
(IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027993001, paras. 122-123). Another IIMM witness,
identified as P1477, watched from the opposite side of the river as soldiers shot at villagers fleeing
towards the riverbank (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019629206, paras. 138-145 and 156).
190. The evidence before the Court indicates that those who managed to reach the shore were
effectively cornered by security forces on the riverbank, the only available route to safety being to
swim across the river (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 761-764 and 766-770).
Security forces opened fire on the population fleeing to, and assembled along, the riverbank (IIMM,
Witness Statement No. IIMM0027993001, paras. 122-123 and 127; IIMM, Witness Statement
No. IIMM0019629206, para. 138; LAW, Witness Statement No. 011, para. 23; confidential witness
statement submitted as Annex 349 to The Gambia’s Memorial, paras. 18 and 21). Witness MS
testified orally that the security forces trapped the villagers against the shore, “then they targeted and
intentionally started shooting towards the people who gathered in that area” (CR 2026/15, p. 14
(Witness MS)). IIMM Witness P1301 also described standing on the shore with his family and other
villagers when security forces surrounded them on the bank of the river, then started shooting at the
crowd indiscriminately (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027993001, para. 127). Those on the
shore “numbered in the hundreds” (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 766). These
accounts are corroborated by extensive non-governmental organization reporting (see Human Rights
Watch, Massacre by the River: Burmese Army Crimes against Humanity in Tula Toli (19 December
2017), pp. 13-15; Fortify Rights and United States Holocaust Memorial Museum, “‘They tried to kill
us all’: Atrocity crimes against Rohingya Muslims in Rakhine State, Myanmar” (November 2017),
pp. 1 and 9; Amnesty International, “We will destroy everything”, p. 73).
191. A number of individuals, including Witness MS and IIMM Witness P1301, attempted to
cross the river (CR 2026/15, p. 14 (Witness MS); IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027993001,
para. 128; see FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 764). Witness MS recounted that
many, particularly the elderly and children, drowned in the process, while others were shot by
security forces as they tried to swim to safety (CR 2026/15, p. 16 (Witness MS)). There were
numerous reports of bodies floating in the river (LAW, Witness Statement No. 009, para. 31; FFM,
Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 763-765).
192. The security forces subsequently rounded up the several hundred people who remained
on the shore. According to the testimony of Witness MS and other evidence, women and children

- 66 were separated from the men and compelled to sit in a depressed area of the shoreline (CR 2026/15,
p. 15 (Witness MS); FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 767; Amnesty International,
“We will destroy everything”, p. 73; Human Rights Watch, Massacre by the River: Burmese Army
Crimes against Humanity in Tula Toli (19 December 2017), p. 13). The men who remained on the
shore were instructed to sit in a line and subsequently ordered to walk (CR 2026/15, p. 15
(Witness MS)). Witness MS testified orally that as the men began to move, the military “started
shooting, gun firing towards [them]” (ibid.; Affidavit of Witness MS, paras. 23 and 26). Soldiers
then turned to the women and children, some of whom were shot, some thrown into the river and
others onto fires (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 770). Individuals who survived
initial gunshot wounds, including children, were killed with long knives (ibid., para. 767;
confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 347 to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 23; LAW,
Witness Statement No. 024, para. 21; see also Affidavit of Witness MS, para. 26).
193. The security forces placed the bodies of those killed in multiple pits and set them alight
using petrol. One IIMM witness described seeing the security forces “taking some of the men they
found alive and making them dig . . . As soon as the hole was ready, the soldiers shot the people who
had been digging and everyone fell in the holes.” (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027993001,
paras. 147 and 149.) After the holes were dug, the witness saw security forces drag dead bodies to
the holes and “take the gallons delivered by the helicopter . . . and pour liquid out of the gallons over
the bodies in the holes” (ibid., para. 157). Witness MS similarly testified: “the military and
Rakhine . . . put [the bodies] all together in 4 big holes” (Affidavit of Witness MS, para. 28). He “saw
them pour something from the bottle and set light to these holes full of bodies” (ibid.; see also IIMM,
Witness Statement No. IIMM0027993001, paras. 147-157; LAW, Witness Statement No. 030,
para. 27; LAW, Witness Statement No. 009, para. 39; FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),
paras. 761-764 and 766-770).
194. The evidence establishes that children were deliberately shot, killed with knives or thrown
into the river or onto fires. Witness MS testified:
“The children were running in the shore and they were crying, the children who
were close to the military, the military took them and threw them in the water or on the
fire.
Some of the small children died from the initial shooting or being attacked by
knives but most were later thrown on the fire in the graves that were dug. Some of the
children were walking and crawling in the sand, and the military hit them and put them
in the fire. Some were thrown into the water.” (Affidavit of Witness MS, paras. 29-30;
see also CR 2026/15, pp. 15-16 (Witness MS).)
IIMM Witness P1301 saw children “who were scattered everywhere . . . being killed with knives and
machetes” and testified that “the soldiers snatched the babies from their mothers and threw the babies
into the holes after they had been set on fire” (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027993001,
paras. 137 and 161). Six of Witness MS’s family members were killed that day, including five
children (Affidavit of Witness MS, paras. 1-2; CR 2026/15, p. 17 (Witness MS)). IIMM
Witness P1477 lost ten family members, including a two-and-a-half-year-old daughter, and testified
to the killings of other children and babies and seeing children’s bodies recovered from the river
(IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019629206, paras. 10-15, 125 and 146). One FFM witness

- 67 recounted that soldiers took infants from their mothers’ laps and threw them into the river, and
recalled seeing children’s bodies floating away. Another described seeing soldiers stabbing a
ten-year-old boy who was trying to run away (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),
para. 770). Other witnesses testified to the killing of children (see confidential witness statement
submitted as Annex 345 to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 33; LAW, Witness Statement No. 009,
para. 40; confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 347 to The Gambia’s Memorial,
para. 23; confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 360 to The Gambia’s Memorial,
paras. 25-29; LAW, Witness Statement No. 024, para. 22; confidential witness statement submitted
as Annex 364 to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 17; confidential witness statement submitted as
Annex 365 to The Gambia’s Memorial, paras. 28-29).
195. Thereafter, security forces took groups of women and girls to larger houses within the
village, together with any surviving young children and infants accompanying them. IIMM
Witness P1477 saw “70 to 80 women all together” taken in groups by soldiers into houses (IIMM,
Witness Statement No. IIMM0019629206, paras. 149-150). Another IIMM witness saw
approximately 200 women taken into houses, and testified that the soldiers “took not only the women
but also the babies they were carrying and the toddlers they had with them”. His own wife escaped
from one of the houses (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027993001, paras. 158 and 167).
Other witnesses consistently reported seeing, or being part of, groups of five or more women who
were taken by the military into houses (LAW, Witness Statement No. 009, para. 42; confidential
witness statement submitted as Annex 349 to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 19; see also Human
Rights Watch, Massacre by the River: Burmese Army Crimes against Humanity in Tula Toli
(19 December 2017), p. 21; Fortify Rights and United States Holocaust Memorial Museum, “‘They
tried to kill us all’: Atrocity crimes against Rohingya Muslims in Rakhine State, Myanmar”
(November 2017), p. 10). Additional evidence indicates that once the groups were inside the houses,
the women were subjected to beatings, rape and, in numerous instances, stabbing, while the children
and infants were killed or seriously injured, frequently by stabbing (confidential witness statement
submitted as Annex 355 to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 17; confidential witness statement
submitted as Annex 357 to The Gambia’s Memorial, paras. 26-33; confidential witness statement
submitted as Annex 360 to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 29; FFM, Report of the detailed findings
(2018), paras. 771-772; Human Rights Watch, Massacre by the River: Burmese Army Crimes against
Humanity in Tula Toli (19 December 2017), pp. 21-24, 26 and 28; Amnesty International, “We will
destroy everything”, p. 91).
196. Some witnesses stated that their groups were taken into a house where women had been
killed and reported seeing numerous dead bodies (confidential witness statement submitted as
Annex 357 to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 28; see also confidential witness statement submitted
as Annex 360 to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 29; “Myanmar’s Killing Fields”, transcript,
Frontline (8 May 2018)). One witness testified that she was taken into a house with her
three-month-old daughter, where she witnessed dead women on the ground and saw the military rip
off the clothing of other women, rape them and beat them to death. She reported that she and her
infant were slashed and that she was beaten with a large stick by the military and subsequently raped
by five of them, after which more women were brought into the house (confidential witness statement
submitted as Annex 360 to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 29). Women survivors from Min Gyi who
spoke with the FFM, “displayed both serious burn marks and stab wounds, which were consistent
with their accounts, and were deeply traumatised” (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),
para. 772).

- 68 197. Oral and written testimony, including that of Witness MS, indicates that the houses in
which these acts occurred were subsequently locked and set on fire, killing the women and children
who remained inside, save the few who were able to escape (CR 2026/15, p. 16 (Witness MS);
confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 349 to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 19;
confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 355 to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 17;
confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 357 to The Gambia’s Memorial, paras. 31-33;
confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 360 to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 30; FFM,
Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 771-772, 909 and 924; Human Rights Watch, Massacre
by the River: Burmese Army Crimes against Humanity in Tula Toli (19 December 2017), pp. 21-22
and 24).
198. Satellite imagery was collected on 16 September 2017 of the locations examined by the
Court. That imagery, together with witness accounts, establishes that the approximately 440 houses
and other structures in the various Rohingya hamlets of Min Gyi were burned and completely
destroyed (CR 2026/15, pp. 14 and 16 (Witness MS); LAW, Witness Statement No. 009, para. 42;
confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 349 to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 19;
confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 355 to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 17;
confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 364 to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 18;
confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 365 to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 29; FFM,
Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 773). The satellite imagery shows Min Gyi to have been
completely destroyed, in contrast to the neighbouring Rakhine settlement which remained intact
(FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 773, and satellite image, p. 185).
199. While the Court is unable to determine the precise number of deaths at Min Gyi on
30 August 2017, the FFM found to be credible information compiled by the Rohingya refugee
community that 750 people were killed, including at least 400 Min Gyi residents, as well as
Rohingya who had fled from other villages (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),
paras. 774-775). The Court finds these figures supported by the evidence.
(b) Chut Pyin (Shuap Praung)
200. Chut Pyin was a Rohingya village situated within a village tract of the same name in
Rathedaung Township, close to the junction of the three townships in northern Rakhine State. It
included a Rohingya village and an ethnically Rakhine village (see sketch-map No. 3 below). On
27 August 2017, the Tatmadaw’s 33rd Light Infantry Division conducted a clearance operation in
Chut Pyin. As set forth below, the operation was characterized by the encirclement of the Rohingya
civilian population, indiscriminate and targeted killings, including of children, the separation and
killing of men, sexual violence, shooting of villagers who were fleeing, and the deliberate burning of
Rohingya homes, while the nearby Rakhine village was left intact.

- 69 SKETCH-MAP NO. 3 — CHUT PYIN AND SURROUNDING VILLAGES

(Source: Material submitted by Myanmar at the hearing pursuant to Practice Direction IXquater.)

201. The Respondent contends that Chut Pyin was “a particularly important foothold” for
ARSA fighters. The material in the record indicates limited ARSA activity in that village in the
months preceding 25 August 2017, but establishes heightened tensions in July and August before the
clearance operation, including intercommunal conflict, increased security presence and restrictions
on movement (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 780).
202. ARSA attacked several outposts in the general vicinity of Chut Pyin on 25 August 2017
(Kyauk Pan Du, Ah Lel Than Kyaw, Thin Baw Kway, and Gwa Sone). Myanmar contends that the
clearance operation in Chut Pyin responded to an attack on the Chut Pyin police outpost on
27 August 2017. According to Myanmar’s records, that attack was carried out by one individual who
threw an IED and ran away towards Chut Pyin, where pursuing security forces were confronted with
several hundred attackers bearing “IEDs and homemade guns” (confidential document submitted as
Annex 120 to Myanmar’s Counter-Memorial, pp. 5-6, para. (B)). The written testimony to support
this claim, however, is inconsistent regarding the number of attackers and was given by persons in
Myanmar’s custody (see paragraph 125 above; confidential witness statement submitted as
Annex 105 to Myanmar’s Rejoinder; confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 106 to
Myanmar’s Rejoinder). IIMM witness testimonies regarding Chut Pyin do not mention such a
confrontation. While the Court cannot rule out that ARSA members may have been present in
Chut Pyin at the time of the clearance operation, the evidence indicates that the operation was
primarily directed against civilians.
203. The record establishes that Myanmar’s security forces encircled the village of Chut Pyin
at around 2 p.m. on 27 August 2017; this was followed by an immediate escalation of violence,
including heavy gunfire across the village and direct attacks on civilians at or near their homes

- 70 (Affidavit of Witness NJ, paras. 41, 43-46, 48 and 51-52; CR 2026/14, pp. 13-14 and 31
(Witness NJ); IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027971404, paras. 98-103; IIMM, Witness
Statement No. IIMM0027992301, paras. 31-37; IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027999848,
paras. 63-74; IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019925304, paras. 103-110; FFM, Report of the
detailed findings (2018), paras. 782-783). Witness NJ testified that she “could see the military
personnel moving door to door” and “heard them yelling words such as ‘minglo’, ‘kola’, ‘bengali’”
(Affidavit of Witness NJ, para. 44). Witness NJ testified that the members of the military were
accompanied by “non-military Rakhine men assisting with the mission” (ibid., paras. 44 and 46).
Other witnesses reported to the FFM that the security forces were accompanied by armed ethnic
Rakhine, including from neighbouring villages. Some of the ethnic Rakhine were wearing
military-style uniforms and equipped with guns, while others were in non-military clothing and
equipped with weapons such as knives and machetes (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),
paras. 782 and 797-798).
204. IIMM Witness P2334 stated that the military used “[self-loading rifles] and Bren guns”,
with which “you can shoot non-stop and you wear the bullets in a big chain across the shoulder”, and
that the military were shooting from all directions. The sight of so many bullets looked like “drops
of rain” (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027971404, paras. 99-102). Another IIMM witness
“saw two military soldiers positioning two of those big weapons . . . with a round wheel and a tube
in front . . . The militar[y] fired, the bullets from those weapons were non-stop” (IIMM,
Witness Statement No. IIMM0027999848, para. 73).
205. Men and boys were separated from women and younger children (IIMM, Witness
Statement No. IIMM0019925304, paras. 107-109 and 117). Witness NJ testified before the Court
that the military severely beat and took away her husband and father-in-law, after which she never
saw them again (CR 2026/14, pp. 13 and 30-31 (Witness NJ)). The evidence describes individuals
being captured and held in a paddy field, followed by separation and killing. IIMM witnesses testified
that the women were made to kneel in one row and the men in another. The men were then taken
away from the field and gunfire was heard; women later saw bodies or learned that the men had been
killed (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027971404, para. 98, see also paras. 115-116; IIMM,
Witness Statement No. IIMM0027992301, paras. 39-45). IIMM witnesses described point-blank
executions (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027992301, paras. 43-44; IIMM, Witness
Statement No. IIMM0027999848, para. 63). IIMM Witness P2252 testified that, once her family
reached the paddy field, “[f]our soldiers surrounded” her son, then “a soldier put the bullets into his
long gun and shot him in the temple”. Her son “died immediately” before her eyes. She saw her
husband and two other sons handcuffed with a group of men outside a school near the field but never
saw them again (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027992301, paras. 41-42 and 45).
206. There are numerous reports of sexual violence at Chut Pyin. Witness NJ told the Court
that, following the killing of her child, she was beaten, stabbed and gang-raped by two soldiers “one
after another” in her home until she lost consciousness (Affidavit of Witness NJ, paras. 54-59;
CR 2026/14, p. 15 (Witness NJ)). IIMM Witness P2499 testified to being gang-raped at a nearby
school that was being used as a military base:

- 71 “I feel ashamed to talk about what happened to me at the school . . . The soldiers
took me inside the school before Maghrib prayer time . . . [I]nside the school, they
blindfolded me with my hijab . . . I tried to fight back as much as I could, I remember I
even bit one of them . . . I felt in my heart they would kill me . . . Then the soldiers tore
my clothes apart. When I was resisting so much, a soldier pointed the gun at my back.
At that point, I did not fight back anymore; I gave up the hope for life . . . More than
one raped me but I don’t know how many, maybe two or three” (IIMM, Witness
Statement No. IIMM0027999848, paras. 81-84).
Other accounts confirm gang rapes perpetrated at the school (FFM, Report of the detailed findings
(2018), para. 790; Amnesty International, “We will destroy everything”, p. 91; Physicians for Human
Rights, “‘Please tell the world what they have done to us’: The Chut Pyin massacre — Forensic
evidence of violence against the Rohingya in Myanmar” (July 2018), p. 19; see paragraph 124
above).
207. The testimonies of multiple witnesses, including that of Witness NJ above, establish that
women were subjected to serious physical injuries before or after being raped, and that some were
then killed. IIMM Witness P1461 testified that women were taken into houses, sometimes at
knifepoint, and raped. She stated: “I have seen with my own eyes a woman nine month pregnant
being raped and killed with a knife. They attacked her with a knife and cut her belly.” (IIMM, Witness
Statement No. IIMM0019922097, para. 50; see also confidential document submitted as Annex 336
to The Gambia’s Memorial, pp. 2, 6, 8, 11-14, 16-18, 20, 49, 61, 65 and 69.) The evidence indicates
at least one instance of serious sexual mutilation. IIMM Witness P1235 testified:
“I saw a lady whose breast was cut and who died instantly . . . I saw that she was
taken by [the] militar[y] . . . When she tried to resist, I saw that a mogh [Rakhine] man
came, held one of her breasts and chopped it off. She started bleeding a lot and
screaming, and the militar[y] shot her from a distance.” (IIMM, Witness Statement
No. IIMM0019925304, para. 161.)
Similar evidence was reported by the FFM (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 790;
see also confidential document submitted as Annex 336 to The Gambia’s Memorial, p. 49; Fortify
Rights, “‘They gave them long swords’: Preparations for genocide and crimes against humanity
against Rohingya Muslims in Rakhine State, Myanmar” (July 2018), p. 69).
208. The evidence in the record shows that disabled persons, the elderly and children were
among the victims (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019922097, para. 62; IIMM, Witness
Statement No. IIMM0027971404, para. 116; IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027992301,
paras. 42 and 52; IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019925304, para. 161; IIMM, Witness
Statement No. IIMM0028059653, para. 157; FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),
paras. 785-789). Witness NJ testified that her child was taken from her arms by the military, stabbed
with a knife and thrown into a burning house. IIMM Witness P2252 “saw that the militar[y] gathered
hay to start a fire and threw some little children in the fire. I saw four or five of them being thrown . . .
They were throwing them like a ball” (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027992301, para. 52;

- 72 see also FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 789; “Myanmar’s Killing Fields”,
transcript, Frontline (8 May 2018)).
209. Many people attempting to flee were killed, as a result of both deliberate targeting and
indiscriminate shooting. IIMM Witness P2334 reported that, “[a]mongst all the shooting and
burning, people started fleeing from Shuap Praung towards the west, crossing the rice fields, because
they couldn’t flee to other directions because of the military” (IIMM, Witness Statement
No. IIMM0027971404, para. 103). He later “saw the bodies” of the villagers who “got shot while
they were running away from the village” (ibid.). IIMM witness statements indicate that villagers
were shot as they ran away (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027992301, para. 36; IIMM,
Witness Statement No. IIMM0027971404, para. 103; Amnesty International, “We will destroy
everything”, pp. 65-66). Women were sometimes slower to flee and were therefore easier targets,
particularly if they were carrying infants and children (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),
para. 789).
210. The evidence establishes that the destruction of the Rohingya part of Chut Pyin was
extensive and deliberate. Witness NJ and numerous IIMM witnesses confirmed that the security
forces burned down houses, including buildings that were occupied, using launchers (Affidavit of
Witness NJ, para. 43; IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027999848, para. 73; FFM, Report of
the detailed findings (2018), para. 784; PILPG, Factual findings & legal analysis report, pp. 44-46).
IIMM Witness P1235 recounted that the soldiers burned houses when the people inside did not come
out (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019925304, paras. 107-109), and that men were then
attacked as they came out of burning buildings (ibid., para. 129). IIMM Witness P2334 provided this
account:
“I heard the military saying ‘Mee Shoet Lite’ (set fire) and they started to set the
houses in Shuap Praung on fire . . . I could hear the loud sound, like ‘GOONE!!’ and I
knew they were using launchers to set fire to the houses . . . A launcher has something
like mortar and it was like a fire-cracker. It could be aimed from a distance at a house,
and as soon as it lands on the house, it would immediately set fire.” (IIMM, Witness
Statement No. IIMM0027971404, paras. 99-100.)
211. Satellite images captured on 16 September 2017 show that Chut Pyin was entirely
destroyed while the neighbouring ethnic Rakhine village was left intact (FFM, Report of the detailed
findings (2018), satellite image, p. 189).
212. The Tatmadaw reported using 1,700 rounds of ammunition and killing
“32 ARSA extremist Bengali terrorists” at Chut Pyin (confidential document submitted as
Annex 120 to Myanmar’s Counter-Memorial, p. 7, para. (F)). Rohingya refugees in Bangladesh
prepared a list indicating that 358 persons were killed in Chut Pyin, which the FFM considered
consistent with the information it had received (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),
para. 796). The list also indicated that 127 children aged five or under were killed, 19 women were
raped, 94 people lost limbs and 57 people remained unaccounted for (ibid.). While the Court cannot
confirm these estimates, they are credible in light of the evidence at the Court’s disposal.

- 73 (c) Maung Nu (Monu Para)
213. Maung Nu is a village in Buthidaung Township, situated near two military bases under
the Western Command of the Myanmar military (see sketch-map No. 4 below). As set forth below,
a company of the 564th Light Infantry Regiment conducted a clearance operation in Maung Nu on
27 August 2017. The operation was characterized by indiscriminate shooting, the separation and
execution of men and boys, sexual violence and the burning of the village.
SKETCH-MAP NO. 4 — MAUNG NU AND SURROUNDING VILLAGES

(Source: Material submitted by Myanmar at the hearing pursuant to Practice Direction IXquater.)

214. The evidence before the Court shows that there was some ARSA presence in Maung Nu
prior to the military operation. One IIMM witness relied upon by both Parties reported that, prior to
the clearance operation, ARSA was active in Maung Nu and had patrolled the streets, killed
suspected informants and attempted to make crude IEDs. The witness also testified that ARSA’s
numbers were relatively limited (estimated at between 20 and 60 persons), that its weapons were
rudimentary and that it was not able to assemble working explosive devices (IIMM, Witness
Statement No. IIMM0019628625, paras. 53, 80 and 82). At least one ARSA leader, identified
alternatively as “Mojia” or “Mojir”, lived in Maung Nu (ibid., para. 80).
215. Three ARSA attacks were reported in the immediate vicinity of Maung Nu on 25 August
2017, including an attack on the 552nd Light Infantry Regiment near Taung Bazar and on BGP posts
in Hpaung Taw Pyin and Nga Yant Chaung (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),
paras. 801-802). Thereafter, Tatmadaw soldiers entered Hpaung Taw Pyin and opened fire, causing
many people to flee to Maung Nu (ibid., paras. 803-804). Evidence also points to a confrontation
between the 562nd Light Infantry Regiment in Sin Oe Pyin and around 300 to 500 men armed with
farming tools on 27 August 2017 (confidential screening note submitted by The Gambia under
Article 56, paragraph 2, of the Rules of Court, para. 23).

- 74 216. Myanmar offered two accounts of the purposes of the clearance operation which are
difficult to reconcile. On the one hand, Myanmar characterized the operation as a targeted action
against an alleged ARSA cell leader, referred to as “Zahid Hussein”, and his home address, although
Myanmar, and the IIMM witness on whom it relies, acknowledged that the house that was actually
attacked belonged to a different individual of the same name (see IIMM, Witness Statement
No. IIMM0019628625, para. 80). On the other hand, Myanmar also asserted that its actions were a
response to the alleged attack by “about 100 militants” in Maung Nu, followed by a further attack by
about 300 others several hours later (confidential document submitted as Annex 118 to Myanmar’s
Counter-Memorial, p. 4, para. 14). Myanmar provides no evidence corroborating its assertions in the
internal reporting offered in support of this second contention, however, and the IIMM witness upon
whom it relies does not mention such an attack (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019628625,
paras. 101-103; see also Affidavit of Witness MN, paras. 14 and 16; CR 2026/13, pp. 17-19
(Witness MN)).
217. When the security forces entered Maung Nu on the morning of 27 August 2017, the
village was temporarily housing people who had recently fled from Hpaung Taw Pyin and
surrounding areas (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019915116, para. 78; FFM, Report of the
detailed findings (2018), para. 804; Amnesty International, “We will destroy everything”, pp. 69-70).
Witness MN testified before the Court that at around 9 a.m., the military “came and started firing”
(Affidavit of Witness MN, paras. 14 and 16; CR 2026/13, p. 19 (Witness MN)). His account is
corroborated by other witness statements.
218. The record shows that a large number of villagers attempted to seek refuge in the
compound of Zahid Hussein (the non-ARSA member), which many believed would be safe because
Zahid Hussein was thought to have good relations with the security forces (IIMM, Witness Statement
No. IIMM0019628625, para. 103; Affidavit of Witness MN, paras. 14 and 16; CR 2026/13, pp. 19
and 33 (Witness MN); Amnesty International, “We will destroy everything”, pp. 69-70), and because
it was less visible from the road (CR 2026/13, pp. 18-19 (Witness MN)).
219. The security forces entered the compound in large numbers and threatened to fire
launchers at the houses in the compound if people did not come out. Zahid Hussein was ordered out
of the house and was injured (LAW, Witness Statement No. 003, para. 21; FFM, Report of the
detailed findings (2018), para. 808). Witness MN testified orally that soldiers then broke into the
house and forced people out (CR 2026/13, pp. 19-22 (Witness MN)). Men and older boys were
separated from women and younger children. The military tied the hands of the men and boys behind
their backs and made them lie face down while beating and kicking them on the neck and back
(CR 2026/13, pp. 20-22 (Witness MN)). Some of the men and boys were bound with women’s
headscarves (LAW, Witness Statement No. 003, para. 22). This testimony is corroborated by IIMM
and other witnesses (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0028048879, paras. 34-35, 44 and 52;
IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019915116, paras. 96-97 and 116-117; IIMM, Witness
Statement No. IIMM0027992867, paras. 67-69; Affidavit of Witness MN, para. 17; FFM, Report of
the detailed findings (2018), paras. 805-809; Amnesty International, “We will destroy everything”,
p. 70).

- 75 220. Witness MN recounted that he heard one of the officers make a phone call asking what
to do with the 87 people who had been caught. The soldiers then began to take groups of five to six
people to a place he could not see, with their hands tied (CR 2026/13, pp. 22-23 (Witness MN);
Affidavit of Witness MN, paras. 17 and 23). Numerous witnesses testified that after being beaten
and tied, the detained men and boys were moved to another location and then killed. Witnesses
reported passing sites containing large numbers of dead bodies, including members of their families
(IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019915116, para. 103; IIMM, Witness Statement
No. IIMM0028048879, para. 51; confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 339 to
The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 11; LAW, Witness Statement No. 004, paras. 23-34; confidential
witness statement submitted as Annex 342 to The Gambia’s Memorial, paras. 21-24; confidential
witness statement submitted as Annex 343 to The Gambia’s Memorial, paras. 29-36; confidential
witness statement submitted as Annex 361 to The Gambia’s Memorial, paras. 19-21; FFM, Report
of the detailed findings (2018), para. 808; Amnesty International, “We will destroy everything”,
p. 71). Witness MN stated that he was made to walk with his hands tied behind his back to a place
where he saw piles of dead bodies, including that of his father (CR 2026/13, pp. 22-24 and 43
(Witness MN); Affidavit of Witness MN, paras. 24-26). His eight-year-old nephew was also killed
that day (ibid., para. 25). IIMM Witness P1244 “saw that soldiers started shooting men in the front
yard as they were still lying face down on the ground” and “[a]fter shooting them, the soldiers cut
the men with . . . a long sword, until they died” (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019915116,
para. 103; LAW, Witness Statement No. 004, para. 22; FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),
para. 808). An IIMM witness recounted that soldiers took away her two sons, who were 9 and
12 years old, and made them lie face down with adults. She was taken away and “never saw [her]
two sons again” (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0028048879, para. 50; CR 2026/13, pp. 22-24
and 43 (Witness MN); IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027992867, paras. 69 and 78-80). In
the aftermath of the attack, witnesses reported “seeing bodies, including of children, and body parts
scattered on the ground” and some bodies with their throats slit (FFM, Report of the detailed findings
(2018), para. 813).
221. The Court observes that women and girls do not appear to have been targeted for
execution. Witnesses testified that after the men were separated, women hiding at Zahid Hussein’s
compound were taken to another location where they suffered no further harm (IIMM, Witness
Statement No. IIMM0019915116, paras. 99-102; LAW, Witness Statement No. 004, para. 29;
confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 342 to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 31).
However, several FFM witnesses recounted that some women in Maung Nu were subjected to sexual
violence, including gang rape and sexual mutilation (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),
paras. 810-811). One survivor described being caught while fleeing Maung Nu with her husband,
being stripped and raped by two men in front of her husband (ibid., para. 811). The Court finds all
these accounts to be credible.
222. Witnesses described the presence of piles of corpses and of soldiers removing bodies with
carts and loading bodies onto military trucks, as well as evidence of shallow mass graves (FFM,
Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 812-814). IIMM Witness P1493 returned to
Zahid Hussein’s house around 10 p.m. on the night of the operation to search for his missing brother

- 76 (confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 152 to Myanmar’s Rejoinder, paras. 128-130)
and found the ground covered with sticky blood, women’s scarves and slippers (ibid., para. 130; see
also FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 813). Outside Zahid Hussein’s house, he saw
the “dead body of a child, aged about two years old” with part of the child’s head missing (IIMM,
Witness Statement No. IIMM0027992947, para. 130).
223. Witness MN testified orally that the village was “burned down” and that his own house
had been “burned down to ash” (CR 2026/13, pp. 26 and 39 (Witness MN)). Satellite imagery
confirms that more than 320 structures were destroyed by fire in the area between Maung Nu and the
neighbouring Rohingya village of Hpaung Taw Pyin (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),
paras. 816-817, and satellite image, p. 194).
224. The number of deaths in Maung Nu is difficult to estimate. Witness MN overheard a
military officer state that he had 87 detained men and boys, and testified that those men and boys
were later executed (CR 2026/13, p. 22 (Witness MN)) (see paragraph 220 above). A non-exhaustive
list of the dead compiled by the Rohingya refugee community identified at least 82 people killed,
and the FFM estimated that up to 100 people were executed in Maung Nu (FFM, Report of the
detailed findings (2018), para. 815). The Court finds these figures supported by the evidence.
2. Other locations
225. Having examined the events in Min Gyi, Chut Pyin and Maung Nu, the Court will now
address six additional locations which the FFM identified as sites of grave incidents during the
clearance operations and which it subjected to specific and detailed examination. The six locations
are: (a) Gu Dar Pyin, (b) Inn Din, (c) the neighbouring villages of Koe Tan Kauk and Chein Khar Li
(addressed together), (d) Kyauk Pan Du, (e) Ah Lel Than Kyaw and (f) Myin Hlut.
(a) Gu Dar Pyin (Gudam Para)
226. Gu Dar Pyin was a Rohingya village situated within a village tract of the same name in
Buthidaung Township that was inhabited by persons of different ethnicities. While Myanmar
presented contemporaneous official reports asserting that the actions of its military forces in
Gu Dar Pyin were undertaken in response to an attack by “500 ARSA terrorists” on 28 August 2017,
no other evidence before the Court corroborates that assertion.
227. The clearance operation was conducted in two distinct phases: in the early morning of
28 August 2017 and then on 29 August 2017. In the course of these operations, security forces
encircled the village and fired upon those attempting to flee, including children (IIMM, Witness
Statement No. IIMM0019629265, para. 84; confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 44 to
The Gambia’s Reply, paras. 8-14, 18-20 and 23-29; confidential witness statement submitted as
Annex 45 to The Gambia’s Reply, paras. 16-28; confidential witness statement submitted as
Annex 64 to The Gambia’s Reply, paras. 27-31; FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),
para. 824; Amnesty International, “We will destroy everything”, pp. 11 and 80). Launchers were
used to burn houses (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019629265, paras. 91-92; IIMM, Witness
Statement No. IIMM0027997604, paras. 104 and 106; FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),

- 77 para. 822), and acts of rape and gang rape occurred, including in the surrounding forested areas as
women and girls fled (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 826). The security forces
were accompanied by non-uniformed ethnic Rakhine individuals, including people recognized as
being from a nearby village, who captured Rohingya persons, killed them, or injured them by slitting
their throats and helped burn houses with petrol (ibid., paras. 821-822 and 827).
228. Bodies were placed in mass graves. Citing numerous witnesses, the FFM found that
“[a] series of pits were dug in different locations and the bodies put in them. A number
of villagers who returned to Gu Dar Pyin some days after the operation described seeing
bodies, uncovered in shallow graves, that were blackened and swollen. Video footage
reviewed by the Mission shows blackened and swollen human heads demonstrating
putrefactive change (decomposition).” (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),
para. 830; see also Foster Klug, “AP finds evidence for graves, Rohingya massacre in
Myanmar”, AP News (1 February 2018).)
229. One woman described how she had to choose to try to save her two younger children
when her older girl and boy were attacked by the security forces. The next day her husband dug
through pits of bodies until he found the corpse of their son. They never found the body of their
daughter (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 825). Satellite imagery confirms that
the entire Rohingya village was burned (ibid., para. 828, and satellite image, p. 197). Rohingya
community volunteers estimate that 243 people were killed in Gu Dar Pyin (ibid., para. 833).
(b) Inn Din (Aung Dung)
230. Inn Din is a village tract in Maungdaw Township that comprised six Rohingya
settlements and two small ethnic Rakhine hamlets. Myanmar carried out clearance operations in
Inn Din following ARSA attacks on 25 August 2017 against police posts in neighbouring villages,
including Thin Baw Kwe and Tha Win Chaung (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),
para. 871). Myanmar also refers to an alleged ARSA attack on Inn Din on 29 August, but the
assertions in its internal reporting are not corroborated by any other evidence before the Court
(confidential document submitted as Annex 148 to Myanmar’s Counter-Memorial, para. 12).
231. The Court finds that Myanmar’s security forces, accompanied by ethnic Rakhine,
conducted clearance operations across the Rohingya settlements of Inn Din in the days following
25 August 2017 for approximately one week (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019922078,
paras. 41-66; FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 873 and 878). They arrived, often
in the early morning, shooting, and burning houses. Men, women and children were shot; they were
also slashed with large knives, including by persons in civilian clothes, believed to be ethnic Rakhine
(IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019922078, paras. 42-60; FFM, Report of the detailed
findings (2018), para. 873). Homes were set on fire with launchers — at times with the occupants
still inside — and security forces opened fire on those attempting to resist or flee (IIMM, Witness

- 78 Statement No. IIMM0019922078, para. 64; FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),
paras. 873-876; Amnesty International, “We will destroy everything”, p. 83; Wa Lone, Kyaw Soe
Oo, Simon Lewis and Antoni Slodkowski, “Massacre in Myanmar: How Myanmar forces burned,
looted and killed in a remote village”, Reuters (8 February 2018); BBC, “Rohingya crisis: Reuters
journalists held ‘for investigating Myanmar killings’” (8 February 2018)).
232. The Court finds that during a clearance operation on 1 September 2017, as hundreds of
Rohingya were assembled on the beach close to Inn Din, soldiers selected ten adult males and
separated them from the group. They were then marched towards the ethnic Rakhine part of the
village tract, where they were made to sit in a row with their hands tied behind their backs. These
individuals were executed the following day: one was beheaded, a second had his throat slit, the other
eight were killed by firing squad (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 877, 957 and
1331; Amnesty International, “We will destroy everything”, pp. 83-84). The killings were later
disclosed by Reuters journalists (Wa Lone, Kyaw Soe Oo, Simon Lewis and Antoni Slodkowski,
“Massacre in Myanmar: How Myanmar forces burned, looted and killed in a remote village”, Reuters
(8 February 2018); BBC, “Rohingya crisis: Reuters journalists held ‘for investigating Myanmar
killings’” (8 February 2018)) (see also paragraph 544 below). Myanmar later acknowledged that the
Tatmadaw had been involved in killing the ten men in breach of the applicable rules of engagement.
233. Witness testimony, satellite imagery and other evidence indicated that the majority of, if
not all, Rohingya structures were destroyed by 16 September 2017, while the neighbouring ethnic
Rakhine village of Pae Youne remained unaffected (IIMM, Witness Statement
No. IIMM0019922078, para. 67; FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 879; Amnesty
International, “We will destroy everything”, pp. 82-83).
(c) Koe Tan Kauk (Ko Tan Kaung) and Chein Khar Li (Ku Lar)
234. Koe Tan Kauk and Chein Khar Li were neighbouring Rohingya villages in the Koe Tan
Kauk village tract in Rathedaung Township, with a shoreline along the Bay of Bengal. Myanmar
contends that clearance operations were undertaken here in response to “repeated ARSA attacks
between 25 and 28 August 2017, involving hundreds of armed terrorists confronting the military”.
The assertions in Myanmar’s internal government reporting, however, are not corroborated by any
other evidence before the Court (confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 92 to Myanmar’s
Rejoinder, pp. 1-2; confidential document submitted as Annex 117 to Myanmar’s
Counter-Memorial, pp. 83-86).
235. On 25 August 2017, security forces and ethnic Rakhine individuals entered
Chein Khar Li. The security forces opened fire on villagers as they attempted to flee, killed others at
close range and then systematically burned the village from south to north, using launchers,
accelerants such as petrol, and matches (LAW, Witness Statement No. 043, paras. 23-27 and 31;
FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 837-838). On 28 August 2017, security forces
conducted a similar operation in Koe Tan Kauk. Security forces and ethnic Rakhine individuals
entered the village from two sides, firing on those attempting to escape and using launchers against
residential structures. Weapons were also fired from two naval vessels that had been stationed
offshore since mid-August (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 839-840).

- 79 236. With respect to both locations, evidence indicates that security forces shot individuals
attempting to flee (Amnesty International, “We will destroy everything”, pp. 76-77). A number of
people who were unable to flee, particularly the elderly, children and persons with disabilities, were
burned inside their houses (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 838 and 840).
237. The Court takes note of the FFM’s finding of sexual violence, including rape, in both
locations (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 841) but considers the available
evidence insufficient as it is uncorroborated.
238. Information compiled by the Rohingya refugee community suggests that more than
180 persons were killed in the two villages, and other credible sources noted significant casualties
(FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 843).
239. Satellite imagery and other evidence indicate that the Rohingya villages were completely
destroyed by fire, while neighbouring Rakhine villages remained intact (FFM, Report of the detailed
findings (2018), para. 842; Amnesty International, “‘My world is finished’: Rohingya targeted in
crimes against humanity in Myanmar” (October 2017), p. 27; confidential document submitted as
Annex 336 to The Gambia’s Memorial, pp. 70-71).
(d) Kyauk Pan Du (Shitar Fawrikka)
240. Kyauk Pan Du, situated in southern Maungdaw Township, comprised two Rohingya
hamlets and one Rakhine hamlet. According to the FFM, ARSA attacked a BGP camp there on
25 August 2017, and reportedly destroyed a small bridge (FFM, Report of the detailed findings
(2018), para. 846). Myanmar claims that military reinforcements sent on 28 August were confronted
by “30-40 terrorists”, but the assertions in the internal reporting on which it relies are not
corroborated by any other evidence before the Court (confidential document submitted as Annex 149
to Myanmar’s Counter-Memorial, p. 16, Item No. 5; confidential document submitted as Annex 121
to Myanmar’s Counter-Memorial, p. 5, Sec. B, para. (10)).
241. The clearance operation was conducted by the Tatmadaw accompanied by the BGP and
non-uniformed persons believed to be ethnic Rakhine villagers, and lasted for at least two days.
According to the FFM, additional Tatmadaw soldiers reportedly arrived on the second day (FFM,
Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 847). The security forces used automatic weapons to
open fire on people as they fled, and used knives to kill those who were caught or injured (ibid.,
paras. 848-849). The security forces used launchers to destroy the entire village (ibid., para. 850),
and some who tried to escape burning houses were pushed back inside (ibid., para. 946). There is
credible evidence that women and girls were subjected to rape, including gang rape (ibid., para. 854).
Satellite imagery shows that nearly all Rohingya property in the village was burned (ibid., para. 847
and fn. 1896).

- 80 (e) Ah Lel Than Kyaw (Hassu Rata)
242. Ah Lel Than Kyaw consisted of seven hamlets in southern Maungdaw Township, with a
large Rohingya majority and a small ethnic Rakhine population. It contained a BGP checkpoint and
camp, where Tatmadaw soldiers were also based. On 25 August 2017, ARSA attacked the two BGP
locations (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 866; confidential document submitted
as Annex 149 to Myanmar’s Counter-Memorial, Item No. 19). Myanmar contends that the attack
involved “hundreds of terrorists” armed with small weapons, and that it lasted between one and three
hours, and resulted in the death of one government official and 17 attackers. A police official
indicated that advance warning of the attack had enabled him to evacuate ethnic Rakhine civilians to
safety (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 866; confidential document submitted as
Annex 148 to Myanmar’s Counter-Memorial, para. 8 (xxiv)). The Gambia does not dispute the
presence and activity of ARSA in this location but contends that any threat from ARSA had been
neutralized before the commencement of the clearance operation.
243. Shortly after the ARSA attack, a clearance operation lasting three days was launched by
the Tatmadaw and police from the BGP camp. The operation involved continuous, indiscriminate
shooting directed at Rohingya civilians, including young children and the elderly, and at people
fleeing (confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 353 to The Gambia’s Memorial,
paras. 8-12, 15 and 24; FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 867-868). Security forces
used launchers to systematically destroy most of the Rohingya property. Witnesses later saw bodies
in the village, including up to 15 bodies discovered in a well in the market area (confidential witness
statement submitted as Annex 353 to The Gambia’s Memorial, paras. 15-16, 22 and 31-32; FFM,
Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 867-868).
244. The number of casualties is difficult to quantify with precision. The Court notes that a
casualty list prepared by Rohingya villagers recorded up to 77 deaths. The FFM could not verify this
figure, but found it consistent with the interviews it conducted (FFM, Report of the detailed findings
(2018), para. 869). One witness statement collected by LAW, while corroborating the FFM’s account
of the series of events, reports a higher number of bodies found in the well in the market area and
provides a substantially higher estimate of deaths at a nearby reservoir, but acknowledges that this
latter observation was made from a distance (confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 353
to The Gambia’s Memorial, paras. 31 and 33).
(f) Myin Hlut (May Rulla)
245. Myin Hlut was a majority Rohingya village tract in southern Maungdaw Township. On
25 August 2017, ARSA attacked a police outpost there (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),
para. 857).
246. The evidence in the record shows that a clearance operation commenced that same
morning, lasting several days (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 857; confidential
witness statement submitted as Annex 69 to The Gambia’s Reply, paras. 26-29). A witness described
approximately 300 military and police personnel surrounding the village from all parts, arriving on

- 81 motorcycles and in military vehicles, and shooting randomly as civilians fled. The witness himself
was shot in the right leg while escaping, an injury confirmed by contemporaneous Médecins Sans
Frontières referral documents (confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 69 to
The Gambia’s Reply, paras. 28-29, 33 and 43-44). The FFM likewise reported that Tatmadaw
soldiers, assisted by members of the BGP, entered the village, firing weapons at people fleeing,
slitting peoples’ throats with knives and burning buildings with launchers as they proceeded through
the tract (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 858-860). Some women and girls were
subjected to sexual violence, including rape, by members of the security forces both in their homes
and in forested areas as they attempted to escape (ibid., para. 862).
247. Satellite imagery confirmed that the Rohingya hamlets were burned and destroyed (FFM,
Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 862-863 and 1223). A list of casualties prepared by
Rohingya in the refugee community indicates up to 70 deaths, a figure that the FFM could not verify
but that it found to be consistent with testimony it had received (ibid., para. 864).
3. Assessment of common aspects of the clearance operations
248. Having examined the events in specific locations, the Court now turns to assessing the
broader evidence concerning Myanmar’s conduct during the clearance operations. The FFM
recorded that it had “verified, through multiple interviews and other information, ‘clearance
operations’ . . . in a total of 54 separate locations”, and that it had “received first-hand accounts of
additional ‘clearance operations’ in a further 22 locations” (FFM, Report of the detailed findings
(2018), paras. 880-881). The Court observes that the manner in which the operations were carried
out reveals a degree of consistency across a wide geographic area. Witnesses described substantial
similarities in the timing and sequence of events, the types of weapons employed, the co-ordination
among different perpetrators and the nature of the violations committed. Evidence regarding such
common aspects of the clearance operations was also gathered by the FFM and the IIMM and can be
found in numerous reports of the United Nations and non-governmental organizations. The Court
will consider, in turn, common aspects of the evidence with respect to (a) deaths, (b) sexual violence,
(c) attacks on children, (d) attacks on fleeing Rohingya and (e) the destruction of villages and their
subsequent replacement with new infrastructure.
(a) Deaths
249. The precise number of deaths resulting from the clearance operations is not definitively
established. Myanmar maintains that during the 2017 confrontations 13 members of the security
forces, 376 ARSA fighters, and 131 “ethnic/Hindu/Bengali people” were killed. Although the
reference to “ethnic/Hindu/Bengali” people appears to refer to civilian deaths, Myanmar
acknowledges that in establishing the number of casualties from the clearance operations, it did not
estimate casualties among civilians not taking part in hostilities.
250. The Court observes that the numbers reported by Myanmar are significantly lower than
the number that the evidence establishes were killed during the 2017 clearance operations. The FFM

- 82 estimated that these operations caused 10,000 violent deaths and considered this number to be
conservative (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 1006-1008 and 1275). The FFM
also pointed to substantially higher estimates calculated by other entities (ibid., para. 1007). In
reaching its conclusion, the FFM relied, inter alia, on evidence of large-scale massacres in several
locations, as well as on detailed accounts of numerous other incidents in which dozens of individuals
were killed. Rohingya refugee camp volunteers, with the help of affected family members, prepared
casualty lists identifying the names, ages and villages of persons killed or presumed killed, which
identified a total of 9,208 persons killed and 1,358 presumed disappeared or killed. The FFM
indicated that the list had not been completed at the time of reporting (ibid., para. 1006).
251. The Court observes that a finding of large-scale deaths is corroborated by the evidence
from numerous locations, including from those examined above. The evidence establishes deliberate
executions, indiscriminate shootings, killings at riverbanks and of persons injured or captured,
shooting of those attempting to flee, burnings, and killings of children and infants (see
paragraphs 188-197, 203-205, 207, 208-210, 217, 220, 227, 229, 231-232, 235-236, 241, 243 and
246 above).
252. The Court further notes the results of six pooled surveys conducted by Médecins Sans
Frontières among Rohingya families who had fled to Bangladesh, which estimated that at least 6,700
to 9,867 people died as a result of violence between 25 August and 24 September 2017 (Médecins
Sans Frontières, “‘No one was left’: Death and violence against the Rohingya in Rakhine State,
Myanmar” (2018), p. 5, fns. 4 and 6). These surveys were limited to deaths witnessed by family
members and thus may have undercounted the total number of deaths. The surveys, which are
publicly available, involved over 11,000 participants and sought to assess mortality rates and causes
of death based on a detailed methodology (see paragraph 124 above). They were not meaningfully
challenged by Myanmar. Médecins Sans Frontières estimated that 69.4 per cent of violent deaths
resulted from gunshots, 8.8 per cent from being burned inside homes and 5 per cent from being
beaten to death, and also noted violent deaths resulting from sexual violence and landmine explosions
(Médecins Sans Frontières, “‘No one was left’: Death and violence against the Rohingya in Rakhine
State, Myanmar” (2018), p. 9). Médecins Sans Frontières further estimated that, in total, between
9,425 and 13,759 Rohingya deaths occurred between 25 August and 24 September 2017 (ibid., p. 5).
Some of the non-violent deaths may also be attributable to the clearance operations, for example, as
a result of risks associated with the flight to Bangladesh (see paragraphs 262-263 below; FFM,
Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 1006).
PILPG also interviewed 1,024 Rohingya individuals in Bangladesh following a methodology
similar to that of the FFM. Its investigators gathered extensive evidence of killings in a variety of
forms and documented “an exceptionally high incidence of severe brutality in the attacks against the
Rohingya” (PILPG, Factual findings & legal analysis report, pp. vi, 40-43 and 49; see paragraph 124
above).
253. The Court considers that the FFM’s estimate of at least 10,000 deaths is supported by the
record. The evidence before the Court demonstrates the pervasive and lethal nature of the violence
inflicted upon the Rohingya population, and establishes that thousands of Rohingya civilians were
killed by the Myanmar security forces during the clearance operations.

- 83 (b) Sexual violence
254. The evidence establishes that the Myanmar security forces committed extensive acts of
rape and other forms of sexual violence during the clearance operations. The Court observes in
particular that gang and mass rape was a notable and recurrent feature of the violence (see
paragraphs 195-196, 206-207, 212, 221, 227 and 241 above; see also confidential witness statement
submitted as Annex 64 to The Gambia’s Reply, paras. 84-119; FFM, Report of the detailed findings
(2018), paras. 921, 932 and 1006; FFM, “Sexual and gender-based violence in Myanmar and the
gendered impact of its ethnic conflicts”, UN doc. A/HRC/42/CRP.4 (22 August 2019), para. 83;
PILPG, Factual findings & legal analysis report, pp. 37-38). The FFM reported that it had verified
that mass gang rapes, involving multiple perpetrators and multiple victims in the same incident,
occurred in at least ten village tracts in northern Rakhine State (FFM, Report of the detailed findings
(2018), para. 921). The IIMM also concluded, on the basis of extensive testimonial and corroborative
evidence, that Myanmar security forces committed a large number of sexual and gender-based crimes
during the clearance operations (IIMM, “Efforts to investigate and punish sexual and gender-based
crimes committed against Rohingya: Evidence analysis” (27 March 2024), para. 100; see also
paras. 7-22 (collecting reports on sexual violence)). A report by Physicians for Human Rights on
sexual and other violence experienced by the Rohingya community as a result of the clearance
operations also found that sexual violence against the Rohingya was widespread and followed
common patterns (Physicians for Human Rights, “Sexual violence, trauma, and neglect:
Observations of health care providers treating Rohingya survivors in refugee camps in Bangladesh”
(October 2020), p. 2). The assessment, which was conducted by experts in documenting or
responding to violence, was based on in-depth interviews with 26 healthcare workers who provided
direct services to Rohingya refugees in Bangladesh between August 2017 and 2020 and was designed
to identify experiences of physical and sexual violence based on medical evaluations and clinical
encounters (see paragraph 124 above). The record also contains some evidence regarding incidents
of sexual violence against men and boys during the 2017 clearance operations (FFM, “Sexual and
gender-based violence in Myanmar and the gendered impact of its ethnic conflicts”,
UN doc. A/HRC/42/CRP.4 (22 August 2019), para. 167; FFM, Report of the detailed findings
(2018), para. 939; Physicians for Human Rights, “Sexual violence, trauma, and neglect:
Observations of health care providers treating Rohingya survivors in refugee camps in Bangladesh”
(October 2020), p. 12).
255. Most rapes were perpetrated by members of the Tatmadaw (FFM, Report of the detailed
findings (2018), para. 932). Acts of sexual violence were frequently accompanied by extreme
physical brutality. Victims were subjected to severe beatings prior to rape, including with weapons
and other implements, and perpetrators killed victims following acts of rape or inflicted fatal injuries
during the commission of such acts (ibid., para. 934). PILPG’s survey recorded such killings, as well
as “the use of extreme violence targeting young and unmarried women” (PILPG, Factual findings &
legal analysis report, pp. 35 and 38). Pregnant women were also raped (IIMM, Witness Statement
No. IIMM0019922097, para. 50; FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 921). A witness
self-identifying as a former Tatmadaw soldier stated that “many” rapes occurred during the clearance
operations and that the women were often then killed (Interview of Zaw Naing Tun (15 August
2020), pp. 8-10; see also Interview of Myo Win Tun (15 August 2020), pp. 6-7).

- 84 256. Sexual violence was also accompanied by incidents of mutilation, scarring or branding,
including of breasts or genitalia (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019922097, para. 50; IIMM,
Witness Statement No. IIMM0019925304, para. 161; FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),
paras. 790 and 810). PILPG found “a high incidence of mutilation related to sexual violence”
(PILPG, Factual findings & legal analysis report, p. 38; see also LAW, “‘Every day, I remember they
destroyed my life’: Long-term physical and psychosocial consequences of genocidal sexual and
gender-based violence the Myanmar military committed against the Rohingya in its 2017 ‘clearance
operations’” (November 2023), pp. 32-33; Fortify Rights, “‘They gave them long swords’:
Preparations for genocide and crimes against humanity against Rohingya Muslims in Rakhine State,
Myanmar” (July 2018), p. 61). Such scarring contributed to the visibility, stigma and long-term
impact of sexual violence on the survivors (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 926
and 1397; Physicians for Human Rights, “Sexual violence, trauma, and neglect: Observations of
health care providers treating Rohingya survivors in refugee camps in Bangladesh” (October 2020),
p. 2; Médecins Sans Frontières, “‘No one was left’: Death and violence against the Rohingya in
Rakhine State, Myanmar” (2018), pp. 18-19; LAW, “‘Every day, I remember they destroyed my
life’: Long-term physical and psychosocial consequences of genocidal sexual and gender-based
violence the Myanmar military committed against the Rohingya in its 2017 ‘clearance operations’”
(November 2023), p. 30).
257. The evidence establishes that members of the Rohingya community have been
traumatized by the brutal sexual violence that they suffered or witnessed (FFM, Report of the detailed
findings (2018), para. 941). The FFM obtained credible information and an expert opinion
“indicating that many survivors of rape and other forms of sexual violence currently residing in the
refugee camps in southern Bangladesh have become socially withdrawn, reluctant to eat and passive
and fearful in their behaviour” (ibid., para. 1398). PILPG reported that women described physical
and mental health symptoms, “including feelings of shame and despair”, and its psychosocial expert
documented that many Rohingya women exhibited symptoms of post-traumatic stress disorder
(PILPG, Factual findings & legal analysis report, pp. 35 and 39). A focused expert medical
assessment of the physical and psychological impact of sexual violence from the 2017 clearance
operations on the Rohingya refugee population in Bangladesh undertaken by LAW also documented
significant long-term physical impacts affecting survivors, as well as complex psychological, social
and community effects (LAW, “‘Every day, I remember they destroyed my life’: Long-term physical
and psychosocial consequences of genocidal sexual and gender-based violence the Myanmar military
committed against the Rohingya in its 2017 ‘clearance operations’” (November 2023), pp. 31-54).
The assessment was conducted by four expert psychologists and a medical doctor, and was based on
30 in-depth interviews with Rohingya survivors of sexual violence, focus groups with 34 Rohingya
survivors or witnesses of sexual violence, reviews of medical records, and key informant interviews
with service providers from United Nations agencies, humanitarian organizations and healthcare
clinics implementing sexual and reproductive health programmes for the Rohingya community in
Cox’s Bazar (ibid., p. 24; see paragraph 124 above).
258. The precise number of victims of sexual violence is difficult to establish due, inter alia,
to the significant stigma associated with reporting such acts (United Nations Human Rights Council,
“Report of the independent international fact-finding mission on Myanmar”, UN doc. A/HRC/42/50
(8 August 2019), paras. 113 and 934; Amnesty International, “We will destroy everything”;

- 85 Physicians for Human Rights, “Sexual violence, trauma, and neglect: Observations of health care
providers treating Rohingya survivors in refugee camps in Bangladesh” (October 2020), p. 5). The
Court notes the detailed lists provided by Rohingya community volunteers to the FFM estimating
