that at least 1,834 individuals were raped during the clearance operations (FFM, Report of the
detailed findings (2018), para. 1006; see paragraph 250 above). The Court considers this figure to be
supported by the evidence. Based on the record before it, the Court concludes that widespread acts
of gang and mass rape and other forms of sexual violence against the Rohingya were perpetrated by
the Myanmar security forces in northern Rakhine State.
(c) Attacks on children
259. The evidence before the Court demonstrates the widespread intentional killing of
children, including very young children, during the clearance operations (see, for example,
paragraphs 191-192, 194-195, 197, 205, 208, 212, 220, 222, 227, 229, 231, 236 and 243 above). As
set out above, witness testimony from a number of locations describes incidents of children killed in
burning houses or by being thrown onto fires, of boys being separated out for execution, and of
children being stabbed, shot or thrown into rivers — evidence that is corroborated by the findings of
the FFM (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 770, 789, 909-911, 924 and 942-947).
PILPG identified, inter alia, incidents involving the execution of large numbers of children, as well
as acts of extreme cruelty, including “children being stabbed, slaughtered, dismembered and
beheaded” (PILPG, Factual findings & legal analysis report, pp. 33-34). Pooled surveys conducted
in November 2017 by Médecins Sans Frontières with over 11,000 Rohingya who had fled northern
Rakhine State indicated that between 25 August and 24 September 2017, at least 730 and up to
2,109 Rohingya children under the age of five died as a result of violence (Médecins Sans Frontières,
“‘No one was left’: Death and violence against the Rohingya in Rakhine State, Myanmar” (2018),
p. 5, fn. 6; see paragraph 252 above). Médecins Sans Frontières estimated that the most common
causes of violent death for children under the age of five were by gunshot (59.1 per cent), by being
burned to death in their home (14.8 per cent), by being beaten (6.9 per cent) and as a result of
stepping on a landmine (2.3 per cent; see ibid., p. 9).
260. Children were also victims of sexual violence. Like women, girls were the victims of
rape, gang rape, sexual humiliation, and physical and mental torture (FFM, Report of the detailed
findings (2018), para. 1372). There is some evidence that boys were also victims of sexual violence
(see paragraph 254 above). Witnesses testified to the rape of children; IIMM Witness P1235 listed
victims including girls estimated to be 13 years old (IIMM, Witness Statement
No. IIMM0019925304, para. 159) and IIMM Witness P2894 was herself a rape victim at under
14 years of age (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0028123039, paras. 52 and 59; see also
confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 64 to The Gambia’s Reply, para. 116). Médecins
Sans Frontières treated survivors of sexual violence as young as age nine and reported, based on its
health surveys, that “[s]exual violence was experienced or witnessed by 3.3% of the recently
displaced female population between 25 August and 24 September, and did not appear to be limited
to any age group” (Médecins Sans Frontières, “‘No one was left’: Death and violence against the
Rohingya in Rakhine State, Myanmar” (2018), p. 18). PILPG also recorded victims of rape as young
as age seven (PILPG, Factual findings & legal analysis report, p. 37).

- 86 261. The Court finds that widespread acts of extreme violence were perpetrated by the
Myanmar security forces against children, including very young children, in northern Rakhine State
in the course of the clearance operations.
(d) Attacks on fleeing Rohingya and conditions on the flight to Bangladesh
262. Over 700,000 Rohingya, representing over 85 per cent of the Rohingya population who
remained in northern Rakhine State after the events of 2016, fled to Bangladesh as a result of the
2017 clearance operations (see paragraph 66 above). Many travelled significant distances under
extreme hardship, some walking for two weeks through jungles and mountainous terrain and crossing
rivers swollen by the monsoon, with no shelter and limited access to food or water (IIMM, Witness
Statement No. IIMM0019915116, para. 137; confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 345
to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 43; LAW, Witness Statement No. 009, para. 45; confidential
witness statement submitted as Annex 347 to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 29; confidential witness
statement submitted as Annex 355 to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 18; confidential witness
statement submitted as Annex 357 to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 36; confidential witness
statement submitted as Annex 360 to The Gambia’s Memorial, paras. 33-34; LAW, Witness
Statement No. 032, para. 34; see also FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 984-998).
As IIMM Witness P1461 stated:
“[A]t one of [the] rivers . . . because of the injury in my arm I couldn’t swim
properly and I almost drowned . . . People had water up to their throats and carried small
children with the arms over their heads through these waters. I have heard many stories
about people drowning, but only saw one with my eyes . . . He was very old, he was on
that gallon makeshift boat to cross the big river, when a wave washed it away and he
drowned.” (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019922097, para. 70.)
Another witness recounted that their family did not eat until their arrival in Bangladesh. Her
children were crying and screaming from hunger, and the family was compelled to drink
contaminated river water that made them nauseous. She further stated that they were too afraid to
sleep (confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 347 to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 29).
Rohingya fled with few, if any, possessions. Many had sustained injuries and needed urgent
medical care; others had to assist children, the wounded or the elderly during the journey (IIMM,
Witness Statement No. IIMM0019915116, para. 137; confidential witness statement submitted as
Annex 355 to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 18; confidential witness statement submitted as
Annex 360 to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 33; confidential witness statement submitted as
Annex 69 to The Gambia’s Reply, para. 44; FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 989).
Pregnant women gave birth on the route (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019629265,
para. 120; FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 992).

- 87 263. Numerous Rohingya died on the journey as a result, particularly the elderly and wounded.
One IIMM witness recounted walking with a group of between 2,000 and 3,000 Rohingya villagers
to Bangladesh and having to walk past dead and dying people left by those ahead (IIMM, Witness
Statement No. IIMM0019628625, paras. 115, 119 and 121). Another described walking with the
inhabitants of eight villages, saying:
“A lot of people disappeared. I lost my mother like that. I was carrying my son
[redacted] who had a bullet injury, we couldn’t find any better way to carry him, so I
was carrying him on my shoulder . . . [My son’s] mother was very upset, she was
screaming, crying, sometimes she would fall down on the ground and have difficulties
in walking. By that time my mother was still walking, but at some point we lost her and
we couldn’t find her later, not ever after coming here to Bangladesh. We searched for
her and asked everyone, both on the spot and once arrived.” (IIMM, Witness Statement
No. IIMM0019925304, para. 173.)
An additional IIMM witness recalled:
“It took a long time to cross the mountain . . . It was so hard for us to walk. I
cannot tell you the suffering of this journey. I saw people become ill. I saw people dying.
Some people died on the way in the mountains . . . People who died were older people
who could not walk themselves.” (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019915116,
para. 137.)
Rohingya who were fleeing also drowned trying to cross rivers and from boats capsizing in
the Bay of Bengal (see, for example, IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027997604, paras. 127
and 129; FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 984-998). Numerous witnesses related
similar descriptions of deaths during the journey (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019629265,
para. 120; IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027971404, para. 133; IIMM, Witness Statement
No. IIMM0027992301, para. 57; IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019629302, para. 72; IIMM,
Witness Statement No. IIMM0028048879, para. 82).
264. Fleeing Rohingya were also attacked. Written and oral witness testimonies establish that
in numerous locations Myanmar security forces targeted fleeing Rohingya. As noted above, this
included Rohingya who were first attacked in their villages as they sought to flee from the clearance
operations (see paragraphs 189, 191, 194, 209, 227, 231, 235-236, 241, 243 and 246 above; see also
confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 342 to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 12;
confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 344 to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 27;
confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 345 to The Gambia’s Memorial, paras. 18-19;
LAW, Witness Statement No. 009, para. 31; confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 360
to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 19). It also included Rohingya who were attacked again during
subsequent clearance operations in villages where they had sought refuge (see, for example,
paragraphs 217 and 182 above; IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019629265, paras. 109 and
111; LAW, Witness Statement No. 009, paras. 21, 22-25 and 47). These testimonies are corroborated
by other evidence (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 758, 837, 848, 858 and 867;
Amnesty International, “We will destroy everything”, pp. 61-62).

- 88 265. Many Rohingya avoided main roads, travelling longer and arduous routes and hiding
while fleeing out of fear that they would be targeted by security forces (see IIMM, Witness Statement
No. IIMM0019922097, para. 66; IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027992990, para. 198;
IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027993001, paras. 181 and 193; IIMM, Witness Statement
No. IIMM0028048879, paras. 80-81 and 89; IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019915259,
paras. 148 and 161; confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 344 to The Gambia’s
Memorial, paras. 34-35; confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 347 to The Gambia’s
Memorial, para. 29; confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 352 to The Gambia’s
Memorial, para. 14; confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 363 to The Gambia’s
Memorial, para. 35; confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 364 to The Gambia’s
Memorial, para. 20; LAW, Witness Statement No. 030, para. 31; LAW, Witness Statement No. 031,
para. 35; LAW, Witness Statement No. 032, para. 35). Many Rohingya thus did not encounter
security forces once on the journey but endured extreme hardship (see paragraphs 262-263 above).
266. However, numerous witness statements also establish that fleeing Rohingya were killed
or attacked by Myanmar’s security forces on their way to Bangladesh (IIMM, Witness Statement
No. IIMM0019629265, paras. 123 and 128-130; IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027992301,
para. 51; IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019925094, para. 64; IIMM, Witness Statement
No. IIMM0019915259, para. 173; LAW, Witness Statement No. 011, para. 28; confidential witness
statement submitted as Annex 358 to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 24; confidential witness
statement submitted as Annex 61 to The Gambia’s Reply, para. 101; confidential witness statement
submitted as Annex 62 to The Gambia’s Reply, paras. 79-82; confidential witness statement
submitted as Annex 64 to The Gambia’s Reply, para. 125; see also FFM, Report of the detailed
findings (2018), paras. 985-987).
One witness recounted:
“We walked approximately 15 miles through the forest. While in Balukhali, a
group of approximately 60 to 70 militaries arrived and started shooting at us. There were
maybe 10,000 people in the group I was travelling with . . . I saw people running in
different directions; some ran into the forest, some into the fields, into the village or
toward the river, all trying to hide. I ran toward the river.” (LAW, Witness Statement
No. 009, para. 47.)
Another witness explained that the journey was very difficult. Having lost significant amounts
of blood from a prior attack during the clearance operation (see paragraph 197 above), she and her
child were extremely weak and could walk only brief periods before needing to rest. They lived for
nine days in the mountains without food, subsisting on leaves; on the last day they were fired upon
by security forces, but managed to hide (confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 355 to
The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 18).

- 89 According to an IIMM witness, security forces opened fire on groups of Rohingya at or close
to border crossing points, including on large numbers who gathered on the shores of the Bay of
Bengal or Naf River while they were seeking to cross to Bangladesh (LAW, Witness Statement
No. 009, para. 48; see also FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 900-903). Another
stated that security forces shot at boats carrying Rohingya to Bangladesh (IIMM, Witness Statement
No. IIMM0028048879, para. 89; see also FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 901).
Security forces tried to take young women from groups, sometimes successfully. For example, an
IIMM witness recalled that at one point the military separated the men from the women in their
group, picked out seven girls
“and said they had to go with them. . .
................................................................
The next morning, four of the girls returned . . . They had escaped from the camp
while the troops slept . . . They told us the soldiers raped them and tortured them at the
camp . . .
The three other girls . . . never came back” (IIMM, Witness Statement
No. IIMM0019629265, paras. 128 and 130-131; see also confidential witness statement
submitted as Annex 64 to The Gambia’s Reply, para. 125).
267. The record contains evidence that Myanmar had planted landmines along certain roads
and at some crossing points on the border with Bangladesh, resulting in several casualties and forcing
Rohingya off the main roads and into mountainous terrain (IIMM, Witness Statement
No. IIMM0027992867, para. 100; see also FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),
paras. 412-414 and 914; Amnesty International, “We will destroy everything”, pp. 97-100; Fortify
Rights, “‘They gave them long swords’: Preparations for genocide and crimes against humanity
against Rohingya Muslims in Rakhine State, Myanmar” (July 2018), pp. 69-70). Multiple witnesses
stated that they were prevented by groups of security forces from crossing bridges and instead were
forced to swim or use makeshift rafts to cross rivers within Myanmar (IIMM, Witness Statement
No. IIMM0019628625, paras. 120-121; IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019922078, para. 69;
IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027995869, paras. 130-131; IIMM, Witness Statement
No. IIMM0028048879, paras. 81-82).
268. The FFM documented numerous witnesses reporting attacks on Rohingya en route to
Bangladesh or at the border, while other witnesses described encountering bodies of persons who
had been violently attacked along the route (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),
paras. 900-903 and 985-987). PILPG also reported multiple accounts of attacks on the Rohingya as
they fled their villages for Bangladesh, including dozens of documented attacks at the border:

- 90 “The investigation documented attacks on refugees travelling on foot, including
an attack on an ‘enormous crowd,’ which the military fired upon and a respondent
estimated to have killed approximately 1,000 people; the military shooting randomly at
large groups of fleeing Rohingya, creating a ‘killing field;’ and the military firing from
a clifftop on a line of refugees walking towards the border with Bangladesh.
................................................................
The Myanmar armed forces also used helicopters to attack columns of fleeing
Rohingya. As one respondent described, ‘Helicopters would search for us and then
throw light and shoot at us.’
The Rohingya were often attacked while waiting for boats to cross into
Bangladesh, including attacks from military ‘speedboats.’ Myanmar Navy boats
rammed boats or rafts full of fleeing Rohingya refugees to flip or sink them, resulting
in numerous drownings at the border with Bangladesh.
The military also raped women on the way to Bangladesh, usually on the side of
the road, and they sometimes then killed the women. Multiple interviewees reported
seeing naked dead women near the border.” (PILPG, Factual findings & legal analysis
report, pp. 47-48.)
On the basis of two of the six pooled surveys covering 6,799 Rohingya refugees in
Bangladeshi camps (see paragraph 252 above), who reported 209 violent deaths, Médecins Sans
Frontières estimated that “13.4 per cent of violent deaths [among the Rohingya] occurred during the
period between displacement from their village and arrival in Bangladesh” (FFM, Report of the
detailed findings (2018), para. 988, citing Médecins Sans Frontières, “Retrospective mortality,
nutrition and measles vaccination coverage: Survey in Balukhali 2 & Tasnimarkhola camps, Cox’s
Bazar, Bangladesh” (November 2017), p. 20; see also Médecins Sans Frontières, “‘No one was left’:
Death and violence against the Rohingya in Rakhine State, Myanmar” (2018), p. 10, fn. 23).
269. The Court observes that the record also contains credible statements from numerous
witnesses who encountered security forces on their journey to Bangladesh but were permitted to
proceed unimpeded (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027999848, para. 101; IIMM, Witness
Statement No. IIMM0027971404, para. 132; IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019925304,
para. 172; IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0028059653, para. 184; IIMM, Witness Statement
No. IIMM0028050361, para. 144; LAW, Witness Statement No. 024, para. 35; confidential witness
statement submitted as Annex 365 to The Gambia’s Memorial, para. 46; LAW, Witness Statement
No. 029, para. 29; confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 47 to The Gambia’s Reply,
para. 48). Multiple statements also report security forces ordering Rohingya to remain in Myanmar
or to return to their point of origin, despite their villages having been burned. As one IIMM Witness
explained, “[w]e thought they told us to stay, so that they could kill us later” (IIMM, Witness

- 91 Statement No. IIMM0027997604, para. 123; see also IIMM, Witness Statement
No. IIMM0019629265, para. 125; IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0019627993, para. 76;
confidential witness statement submitted as Annex 45 to The Gambia’s Reply, para. 43; IIMM,
Witness Statement No. IIMM0019922078, paras. 70-71; IIMM, Witness Statement
No. IIMM0019629302, para. 80). Some Rohingya interviewed by the FFM who were not attacked
reported being ordered by the security forces they encountered during the journey to leave or they
would be killed (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 903).
270. Based on the evidence before it, the Court is convinced that, in addition to the extreme
hardship of the journey, the Myanmar security forces systematically shot at Rohingya as they were
fleeing the clearance operations in villages, and that a significant number of Rohingya were subjected
to killing, rape and other attacks or threats of attack during the flight to Bangladesh.
(e) Destruction of villages and their subsequent replacement with new infrastructure
271. Satellite imagery and satellite-based fire detection data analysed by UNITAR-UNOSAT
(see paragraph 121 above), together with witness accounts, document the destruction of Rohingya
villages targeted by the clearance operations. The Court notes that while satellite imagery, which was
taken periodically, cannot confirm the particular date on which events may have occurred, it
demonstrates changes in the situation as of the date that an image is collected and also provides data
regarding the scale and precise location of the destruction. Satellite-based fire detection data, on the
other hand, which was collected at least once every two days (UNITAR-UNOSAT, “UNOSAT
analysis of destruction and other developments in Rakhine State, Myanmar” (7 September 2018),
p. 7) uses heat anomaly detection to identify the number and locations of active fires and to more
accurately identify the date on which a burning event occurred, provided that the fires were active at
the moment of the satellite overpass (ibid.). Both systems may nevertheless fail to detect fires due to
considerations such as unfavourable timing or cloud cover.
272. The Court has observed that satellite imagery collected on 16 September 2017, together
with witness testimonies, documents the partial or total destruction of each of the Rohingya villages
examined above (see paragraphs 187-189, 197-198, 208, 210-211, 223, 227, 229, 231, 233, 235, 239,
241, 243 and 246-247). Satellite imagery covering the period up to March 2018 also shows that, after
25 August 2017, approximately 392 villages were either partially destroyed (214 villages) or totally
destroyed (178 villages), representing approximately 40 per cent of all 993 villages identified in the
3 townships of northern Rakhine State (UNITAR-UNOSAT, “UNOSAT analysis of destruction and
other developments in Rakhine State, Myanmar” (7 September 2018), p. 7; FFM, Report of the
detailed findings (2018), para. 959; see sketch-map No. 5 below). Seventy per cent, or 277 of these
villages, were located in Maungdaw Township, where the majority of the Rohingya population of
northern Rakhine State resided (UNITAR-UNOSAT, “UNOSAT analysis of destruction and other
developments in Rakhine State, Myanmar” (7 September 2018), p. 7). It is estimated that the
combined population of the partially or totally destroyed villages amounted to approximately
326,000 people.

- 92 273. UNOSAT analysis further concluded that approximately 80 per cent of the destruction of
the 392 villages was visible by 16 September 2017, and that this figure is “very likely
underestimated” owing to the presence of extensive cloud cover in images collected on that date,
making it difficult to determine whether subsequently identified destruction occurred before or after
16 September 2017 (UNITAR-UNOSAT, “UNOSAT analysis of destruction and other
developments in Rakhine State, Myanmar” (7 September 2018), p. 8). Satellite-based fire detection
data also indicates that, between 25 August and 15 September 2017, fires were detected in
140 locations over the 3 townships, with nearly half of the fires occurring prior to 5 September 2017,
and 130 of them occurring in Maungdaw Township (ibid., pp. 21-22). UNOSAT noted that historical
fire detections over the same period in those 3 townships were “exceedingly rare” and that no
seasonal pattern explained the number of fires detected during this period (ibid., p. 22). Evidence of
burn injuries among those who fled (see paragraphs 252 and 259 above), and of bodies found in
burned homes, also corroborate reports that Rohingya were present in many cases when their homes
were burned, at times locked, forced or trapped inside (see paragraphs 196-197, 208, 210, 231, 236
and 241 above; FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 905-911). PILPG found that of
the 1,024 Rohingya it interviewed, “nearly 70 percent witnessed their homes or villages being
destroyed” (PILPG, Factual findings & legal analysis report, p. vi).
274. The Court considers that, together with the numerous first-hand witness accounts, the
satellite imagery and fire detection data indicate that the vast majority of the village destruction was
caused by fire and mostly occurred while clearance operations were ongoing. However, the Court is
not in a position to determine precisely how many structures were burned while Rohingya were
present at the locations concerned.

- 93 SKETCH-MAP NO. 5 — AREAS WITH VISIBLE DESTRUCTION BETWEEN
25 AUGUST 2017 AND 18 MARCH 2018

(Source: FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), p. 225.)

- 94 275. Myanmar does not dispute that the burning took place, but it argues that its security forces
were not solely responsible or suggests that either ARSA or unknown persons might have burned
villages. Witness accounts regarding numerous villages, however, including the specific locations
examined by the Court, establish that Myanmar security forces entered Rohingya villages armed with
launchers, and at times petrol; they then systematically burned the villages as part of the clearance
operations, and the burning in some locations continued for some period of time. Two witnesses who
self-identified as former Tatmadaw soldiers also stated that soldiers participating in the clearance
operations were instructed to burn the villages (Interview of Myo Win Tun (15 August 2020), p. 5;
Interview of Zaw Naing Tun (15 August 2020), p. 7).
276. The evidence in the record does not support a conclusion that systematic burning of
villages was perpetrated by ARSA or other actors (see paragraph 178 above; see also Witness
Statement of Jonathan Head (14 October 2020), paras. 18-19). The Court considers that the totality
of the evidence establishes that it was primarily Myanmar’s security forces that burned the Rohingya
villages during and after the clearance operations.
277. The Court further observes that the destruction of Rohingya villages was followed by
bulldozing and large-scale terrain clearance. The FFM found that as of December 2017,
“the Myanmar Government embarked on a major operation to remove and clear burned
structures, with the use of heavy machinery, and build new infrastructure in their
place . . . Through this process, many Rohingya villages have been rendered
unrecognisable, devoid of all structures, trees and vegetation” (FFM, Report of the
detailed findings (2018), para. 1216).
In 2018, UNOSAT estimated that 78 of the villages previously destroyed during the clearance
operations evidenced signs of bulldozing or terrain clearance. In most cases, this occurred on a large
scale, affecting the totality of the village (UNITAR-UNOSAT, “UNOSAT analysis of destruction
and other developments in Rakhine State, Myanmar” (7 September 2018), pp. 9-10). The destruction
included structures that had survived the burnings, in particular in Maungdaw Township, including
in Chein Khar Li, War Char, Gaw Du Thar Ra (Ywar Thit Kay), Du Chee Yar Tan and Myin Hlut
(ibid., p. 11). The IIMM and FFM also found evidence of further destruction after 2018 (IIMM, “The
destruction and dispossession of Rohingya land and property during the 2017 clearance operations —
public summary” (29 September 2025), para. 43; FFM, Detailed findings (2019), para. 128).
UNOSAT estimates that, in total, approximately 40,600 structures across Maungdaw, Buthidaung
and Rathedaung Townships were destroyed (IIMM, “The destruction and dispossession of Rohingya
land and property during the 2017 clearance operations — public summary” (29 September 2025),
para. 43).
278. The evidence also establishes that the clearing of terrain was followed by rapid
construction and development of new infrastructure, including BGP battalion bases, other security
installations, and other government or civilian infrastructure (FFM, Report of the detailed findings
(2018), para. 1224).
279. In September 2025, the IIMM published a public summary of its report on Myanmar’s
destruction and dispossession of Rohingya land and property. The report focused on seven village

- 95 tracts in northern Rakhine State where Rohingya villages were destroyed in the 2017 clearance
operations (IIMM, “The destruction and dispossession of Rohingya land and property during the
2017 clearance operations — public summary” (29 September 2025), para. 5). The IIMM’s
conclusions were based on, among other things, UNOSAT satellite imagery, aerial photographs with
available metadata indicating GPS co-ordinates, eyewitness accounts, sale contracts, property
records, other Myanmar government documents, non-governmental organizations’ reports and news
media (ibid., para. 5). In each of the village tracts, the IIMM found that villages had been replaced
by BGP battalion bases. These included Rohingya villages in U Daung, Myo Thu Gyi (Hainda Fara),
Inn Din (Aung Dung), Aung Seik Pyin (Done Paik), Myin Hlut (May Rulla) and Zin Paing Nyar
(Doinor Zum Boyim Na) village tracts (ibid., paras. 5, 6 and 61).
280. The IIMM found that in addition to building new infrastructure, Myanmar actively
promoted resettlement by Buddhists from Bangladesh and other ethnic groups on lands on which
Rohingya had formerly lived, particularly in Maungdaw Township (IIMM, “The destruction and
dispossession of Rohingya land and property during the 2017 clearance operations — public
summary” (29 September 2025), paras. 55-56; see also IIMM, “Analysis of the Myanmar
Government’s encouragement of resettlement by Buddhists from Bangladesh to Rakhine State”
(22 March 2024), paras. 2, 10, 22-24 and 31). Such measures included constructing housing and
facilitating the use of confiscated Rohingya farmland. For example, “[b]etween April and May 2018,
hundreds of new houses funded by regional governments and foundations were handed over to ethnic
Mro and Daing Net families and recent [Buddhist] immigrants from Bangladesh” (IIMM, “The
destruction and dispossession of Rohingya land and property during the 2017 clearance operations —
public summary” (29 September 2025), para. 55).
281. The IIMM concluded that, “[b]y destroying and repurposing Rohingya farms, homes,
businesses and land”, Myanmar “authorities reinforced the effects of the clearance operations and
forced deportations, and created very serious impediments to the ability of Rohingya to ever
reconstitute the communities where they had lived for generations” (IIMM, “The destruction and
dispossession of Rohingya land and property during the 2017 clearance operations — public
summary” (29 September 2025), para. 61). It further observed that, in so doing,
“[b]y fundamentally altering or destroying crime scenes, Myanmar authorities have
obstructed the possibility of onsite investigations and forensic analysis, making other
forms of evidence, such as official records, geospatial and aerial imagery, crime scene
photographs, environmental spatial evidence, and open-source material, indispensable
in the investigation of destruction and dispossession of Rohingya homes, businesses,
farms, schools and places of worship” (ibid., para. 59).
282. The Court concludes that the evidence demonstrates a deliberate, organized process of
destroying Rohingya villages and farmland, replacing them with other permanent structures,
including security force bases, and promoting resettlement by non-Rohingya ethnic groups on land
on which Rohingya had formerly lived.

- 96 IV. APPLICABLE LAW
283. The Court will now consider the definition of genocide under the Convention (Section A),
followed by the relationship between international humanitarian law and the Convention (Section B).
A. Definition of genocide
284. The Court recalls that the object and purpose of the Convention is to prevent and punish
the crime of genocide which, at its core, involves “a denial of the right of existence of entire human
groups, a denial which shocks the conscience of mankind” (Reservations to the Convention on the
Prevention and Punishment of the Crime of Genocide, Advisory Opinion, I.C.J. Reports 1951, p. 23,
quoting United Nations General Assembly resolution 96 (I) of 11 December 1946). The preamble to
the Convention underscores that “genocide has inflicted great losses on humanity”, and that the
contracting parties seek to “liberate mankind from such an odious scourge”.
285. Article II of the Convention defines genocide as
“any of the following acts committed with intent to destroy, in whole or in part, a
national, ethnical, racial or religious group, as such:
(a) Killing members of the group;
(b) Causing serious bodily or mental harm to members of the group;
(c) Deliberately inflicting on the group conditions of life calculated to bring about its
physical destruction in whole or in part;
(d) Imposing measures intended to prevent births within the group;
(e) Forcibly transferring children of the group to another group”.
286. According to this provision, the crime of genocide has two constituent elements: the
material element or actus reus set forth in Article II (a) to (e); and the specific mental element or
mens rea of genocide (dolus specialis) set forth in the chapeau of Article II — namely the “intent to
destroy, in whole or in part, a national, ethnical, racial or religious group, as such” (2015 Croatia
Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 62, para. 130).
287. The Court recalls that each of the material acts under Article II also carries its own general
mental element (dolus generalis), which is distinct from genocidal intent. Thus,
“‘[k]illing’ [under subparagraph (a)] must be intentional, as must ‘causing serious
bodily or mental harm’ [under subparagraph (b)]. Mental elements are made explicit in
[sub]paragraphs (c) and (d) of Article II by the words ‘deliberately’ and ‘intended’,
quite apart from the implications of the words ‘inflicting’ and ‘imposing’; and forcible
transfer [under subparagraph (e)] too requires deliberate intentional acts.” (2007 Bosnia
Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 121, para. 186.)
Moreover, “[t]he prohibited acts enumerated in subparagraphs (a) to (e) are by their very nature
conscious, intentional or volitional acts which an individual could not usually commit without

- 97 knowing that certain consequences were likely to result” (International Law Commission (hereinafter
the “ILC”), Draft Code of Crimes against the Peace and Security of Mankind, Yearbook of the
International Law Commission, 1996, Vol. II, Part Two, p. 44, paragraph 5 of the commentary on
Article 17; 2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 121, para. 186).
288. In order for genocide to be established, the specific intent to destroy the group in whole
or in part must be present, in addition to the general intent required for each of the individual material
acts involved (2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 62, para. 132, citing the 2007
Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 121, para. 187). The requirement of genocidal intent is
the essential characteristic of genocide, which distinguishes it from other serious crimes such as
crimes against humanity (2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 62, para. 132; 2007
Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), pp. 121-122, paras. 187-188). Acts that fall within the
actus reus, when committed with genocidal intent, constitute acts of genocide under the Convention.
289. In particular, it is the specific intent to destroy the “group, as such”, in whole or in part,
that is the defining feature of genocide (2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 62,
para. 132; 2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), pp. 121-122, paras. 187-188). The Court
has indicated that the term “destroy”, for the purposes of genocidal intent, refers to the physical or
biological destruction of the group rather than destruction of the group’s culture in its various aspects
(2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 63, para. 136). Such destruction of the group,
however, is not confined to the death or biological destruction of its individual members, or even
their physical harm. This is demonstrated by the exhaustive list of material acts set forth in
Article II (a) to (e) of the Convention, which are not cumulative. Acts causing serious bodily or
mental harm to members of the group under Article II (b) do not, per se, involve their death or
biological destruction, and serious mental harm may not involve physical harm at all. The injuries
set forth in subparagraph (b) can, however, impact the ability of the group, as such, to maintain its
existence as a group.
290. Similarly, forcibly transferring children of the group to another group under Article II (e)
does not in and of itself involve the death or biological destruction of any individual member of the
group. It does, however, lead to the dispersal of at least a part of the group, making it difficult for
that part to continue its existence as a group. The forcible transfer of children, as the biological future
of the group, can also “have consequences for the group’s capacity to renew itself, and hence to
ensure its long-term survival” (2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 63, para. 136).
291. The preparatory work regarding Article II (e) confirms that the concern of the drafters of
the Convention was the destruction of the group, as distinguished from the physical destruction of
its individual members. Some United Nations Member States in the Sixth Committee of the General
Assembly opposed including the forcible transfer of children among the actus reus on the grounds
that genocide was limited to physical extermination; others supported including the forcible transfer
of children due to the gravity of its consequences, despite the fact that it does not require the physical
destruction of any individual member of the group (United Nations, Official Records of the General
Assembly, Third Session, Part I, Sixth Committee, Summary Records of Meetings,
UN doc. A/C.6/SR.61-140, 82nd meeting (23 October 1948), pp. 186-191). As with serious mental
harm under Article II (b), the ultimate inclusion of the forcible transfer of children within the
actus reus of genocide thus recognized that, for the purposes of the Convention, a group may be
destroyed in the absence of physical harm to its individual members.

- 98 292. The Court notes that the only explicit reference to “physical destruction” in Article II is
found in subparagraph (c), which prohibits “[d]eliberately inflicting on the group conditions of life
calculated to bring about its physical destruction in whole or in part”. This provision, which concerns
the actus reus of genocide, has been understood as referring to the death of the group’s members (see
paragraph 345 below). The absence of the term “physical” in the chapeau of Article II, however,
further suggests that, for the purposes of genocidal intent, the intent “to destroy” the group conveys
a broader meaning than the death of its members.
293. Article II of the Convention recognizes that destruction of the group “as such” may result
from any of the five material acts set forth therein, whether occurring individually or in combination,
when directed against at least a substantial part of the group. Thus, while the final text of the
Convention excludes specific acts relating to “the destruction of historical, cultural and religious
heritage” from the list of acts that must be established in order for genocide to occur (2007 Bosnia
Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), pp. 185-186, para. 344), measures such as serious bodily or mental
harm or forcible transfer of children, which can severely impact the existence of the protected group
and its ability to function as a group, were considered sufficiently grave to constitute material acts
under Article II of the Convention, without involving the death or biological destruction of individual
members. The Court accordingly considers that the destruction of the “group, as such”, in whole or
in part, as set out in the chapeau of Article II, refers to forms of destruction of the group in whole or
in part that may result from the acts enumerated in Article II (a) to (e).
294. Article I of the Convention obliges States parties to prevent and punish genocide,
obligations which are further elaborated in other provisions of the Convention. Article III renders
genocide as defined in Article II, as well as related acts, punishable.
295. The Court has recognized that a State may itself be held responsible for genocide or other
acts set forth in Article III if such acts are committed by its organs or by persons or groups whose
acts are attributable to it (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 119, para. 179). The
Court has further noted that, although the Convention addresses criminal responsibility of
individuals, Article I of the Convention, read together with Article III, imposes obligations on States
separate from the obligations that the Convention requires States to place on individuals (ibid.,
p. 117, para. 174). The question of individual criminal responsibility is in principle distinct from the
question of State responsibility, and such duality of responsibility is “a constant feature of
international law” (ibid., p. 116, para. 173). The Court recalls that “State responsibility and
individual criminal responsibility are governed by different legal régimes and pursue different aims”
(2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 61, para. 129). The Court therefore reiterates that
the obligations “arising from the terms of the Convention, and the responsibilities of States that would
arise from breach of such obligations, are obligations and responsibilities under international law.
They are not of a criminal nature” (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 115, para. 170).
B. Relationship between international humanitarian law
and the Genocide Convention
296. The claims before the Court raise questions regarding the relationship between
international humanitarian law (jus in bello) and the Genocide Convention. Article I of the

- 99 Convention confirms that genocide is a crime under international law “whether committed in time of
peace or in time of war”. The Convention thus applies, by its express terms, in situations of armed
conflict to which international humanitarian law also applies.
297. The Gambia emphasizes that genocide may be a means for achieving military objectives
just as readily as military conflict may be a means for instigating a genocidal plan. Myanmar, in turn,
recognizes that “genocide can be committed during counter-terrorism operations”.
298. The Parties disagree, however, whether killings and other acts that are lawful under
international humanitarian law may nevertheless fall within Article II of the Genocide Convention.
With respect to the actus reus of genocide, The Gambia accepts that it is “in theory possible” that
killing members of a protected group in a manner not prohibited by international humanitarian law
during an armed conflict “may not” fall within Article II (a) of the Genocide Convention. It
maintains, however, that killings of members who are not engaged in any hostile activity, and who
are targeted because of their membership in the protected group, would fall within Article II (a). In
The Gambia’s view, deliberately inflicting unnecessary and serious harm on civilians during alleged
counter-terrorism operations would likewise be covered by Article II (b).
299. Myanmar contends that deaths and injuries caused in an armed conflict or law
enforcement action that are “legitimate” under international humanitarian law do not fall within
Article II (a) or (b) of the Genocide Convention. In particular, it argues that these provisions do not
apply to “legitimate killings or bodily harm caused to insurgents or terrorists” in an armed conflict
or to collateral civilian harms, if the operations are exclusively directed at legitimate targets and the
civilian casualties are not caused deliberately. With respect to genocidal intent, Myanmar submits
that “[t]he existence of an armed conflict or counter-terrorism operation may provide a basis for
allowing reasonable inferences that do not involve genocide”.
300. Among the interveners, the DRC maintains that the decisive question with respect to
whether acts occurring in an armed conflict may be genocidal is the presence or absence of genocidal
intent:
“If intent to destroy a group protected by the Convention as such is established,
and this intent is manifested through acts set out in the subparagraphs of Article II, then
that provision has indeed been violated . . . Theoretically, even the possibility of
genocide being perpetrated through acts directed exclusively at combatants, in a manner
that appears to be consistent with the jus in bello, cannot be excluded.”
Belgium likewise submits that the possibility that acts prohibited by the Convention may be
in conformity with international humanitarian law is irrelevant for the purpose of identifying
genocidal intent. It contends that the Genocide Convention and international humanitarian law
involve two separate legal régimes with different aims, follow two specific logics and are developed
through distinct bodies of rules.

*

*

- 100 301. The Court observes that in an armed conflict, as elsewhere, only acts committed with the
specific intent to destroy the group in whole or in part can constitute genocide within the meaning of
Article II of the Convention. The Court recalls that the Genocide Convention and international
humanitarian law are two distinct bodies of rules, pursuing different aims, both of which are
applicable in the context of armed conflict (2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 68,
para. 153). The Court has jurisdiction in this case to make a determination regarding breaches only
of the Genocide Convention and not of international humanitarian law or any other body of
international law. The Court is not prevented, however, from considering, in its reasoning, whether
a violation of international humanitarian law has occurred, to the extent that this is relevant for the
Court’s determination of whether there has been a breach of an obligation under the Genocide
Convention (ibid., pp. 45-46, para. 85).
302. In its 2015 Croatia Judgment, the Court considered whether acts committed during the
course of an armed conflict must be unlawful under international humanitarian law in order to
constitute the actus reus of genocide (I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 67, para. 150). The Court reasoned
that
“[t]here can be no doubt that, as a general rule, a particular act may be perfectly lawful
under one body of legal rules and unlawful under another. Thus it cannot be excluded
in principle that an act carried out during an armed conflict and lawful under
international humanitarian law can at the same time constitute a violation by the State
in question of some other international obligation incumbent upon it.” (Ibid., p. 138,
para. 474.)
303. The text of Article II of the Convention does not require that the acts enumerated therein
be unlawful pursuant to a distinct body of international law. The Court has observed, however, that
“the rules of international humanitarian law might be relevant in order to decide whether the acts
alleged by the Parties constitute genocide within the meaning of Article II of the Convention”
(2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 68, para. 153). With respect to the actus reus of
genocide, the Court has recognized, for example, that the question whether attacks are “exclusively
directed at military targets” is relevant to the question whether any resulting civilian deaths may be
intentional within the meaning of Article II (a) (ibid., p. 138, para. 474; see paragraphs 287 above
and 315 and 318 below).
304. With respect to genocidal intent, the Court likewise has considered that compliance or
non-compliance with international humanitarian law, including whether attacks “caused casualties
and damage far in excess of what was justified by military necessity”, may be relevant in inferring
the presence or absence of the specific intent to commit genocide (2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J.
Reports 2015 (I), p. 121, para. 413). On the other hand, the fact that members of a protected group
could be lawfully targeted or incidentally harmed under international humanitarian law does not
exclude the possibility that they may be killed or harmed with genocidal intent (Akayesu,
ICTR-96-4-T, Trial Chamber I, Judgement of 2 September 1998, para. 125). In the 2007 Bosnia
Judgment, for example, the Court considered irrelevant the contention that acts of deportation and
expulsion could be justified under international humanitarian law, observing that “no such
justification could be accepted in the face of proof of specific intent” to commit genocide (I.C.J.
Reports 2007 (I), pp. 181-182, para. 334).
305. Accordingly, the Court concludes that although compliance with another body of
international law, including international humanitarian law, may inform the identification of intent

- 101 with respect to the actus reus of genocide, as well as genocidal intent, such compliance does not
exclude conduct from the scope of the Genocide Convention.
V. OBLIGATION NOT TO COMMIT GENOCIDE
306. Having examined the evidentiary record and the applicable law, the Court will now
consider The Gambia’s claim that Myanmar breached its obligation not to commit genocide pursuant
to Articles I and III (a) of the Convention.
307. The Court notes that, for the purpose of establishing State responsibility under the
Genocide Convention, both Parties accept that the acts at issue were carried out primarily by
Myanmar’s security forces. It is undisputed that the conduct of Myanmar’s security forces and other
organs of the State is attributable to Myanmar under the law of State responsibility. The question of
the duty to prevent acts of genocide by persons whose conduct is not attributable to Myanmar will
be considered below (see paragraph 472).
A. The Rohingya as a protected group
308. Under Article II of the Genocide Convention, the prohibited acts are limited in scope to
those directed against a “national, ethnical, racial or religious group, as such”. The jurisprudence of
the Court and of international criminal tribunals has established that qualification as a protected
group is assessed by reference to a combination of objective and subjective criteria. The Court has
considered positive characteristics of nationality, ethnic origin, race or religion as objective criteria
(2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 124, para. 193). As regards subjective criteria,
self-identification as a national, ethnic, racial or religious group and the alleged perpetrators’
stigmatization of the group are generally considered as relevant factors (Rutaganda, ICTR-96-3-T,
Trial Chamber I, Judgement of 6 December 1999, para. 56; see also Krstić, IT-98-33-T,
Trial Chamber, Judgement of 2 August 2001, paras. 557-559).
309. The Parties initially disagreed as to whether the Rohingya are a protected group for the
purposes of Article II. During the oral proceedings, Myanmar, while maintaining that the Rohingya
living in Myanmar are “culturally, ethnically and religiously part of the same group as the population
living immediately across the border in Bangladesh”, conceded that they constitute at least a “part”
of a protected group for the purposes of the Genocide Convention.
310. In the Court’s view, the Rohingya of Myanmar constitute a distinct ethnic group.
Linguistic studies and historical evidence make clear that the Rohingya language is distinguishable
from other languages spoken in Myanmar. The Rohingya are predominantly Muslim (see
paragraph 68 above) and are identified as such by Myanmar. Moreover, the Rohingya have a distinct
culture, with unique art forms, and social and religious institutions that are used for the settlement of
disputes. The Rohingya population’s common language and culture enable its members to
“distinguish[] [themselves], as such” (Kayishema and Ruzindana, ICTR 95-1-T, Trial Chamber II,
Judgement of 21 May 1999, para. 98). The Court also considers it significant that the Rohingya
self-identify as an ethnic group (United Nations Human Rights Council, “Situation of human rights
of Rohingya Muslims and other minorities in Myanmar: Report of the United Nations High

- 102 Commissioner for Human Rights”, UN doc. A/HRC/32/18 (29 June 2016), para. 3). The evidentiary
record demonstrates that the Rohingya have been stigmatized by Myanmar for decades (see
paragraphs 134-168 above) and that Myanmar uses the term “Bengalis” to refer to the Rohingya
population (see paragraph 67 above), supporting the proposition that Myanmar also considers them
to be a distinct group. The Court thus finds that the Rohingya of Myanmar have a “particular group
identity” (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 125, para. 193) and, as an ethnic group,
qualify as a protected group for the purposes of Article II.
B. Actus reus of genocide
311. As noted, the acts listed in Article II (a) to (e) of the Convention constitute the actus reus
of genocide (see paragraph 286 above). The Gambia claims that acts within the meaning of
Article II (a) to (d) of the Convention were committed by Myanmar’s security forces against
members of the Rohingya group, but does not assert that Myanmar has forcibly transferred children
of the group to another group (Article II (e) of the Convention). The Court will thus consider in turn
whether the acts that are established by the evidence fall within the actus reus under Article II (a)
to (d).
1. Article II (a): killing of members of the group
312. The Parties do not dispute that for killings of members of the protected group to fall
within Article II (a) of the Convention, they must be intentional (see 2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J.
Reports 2007 (I), p. 121, para. 186; 2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), pp. 68-69,
para. 156). They disagree, however, on whether a certain minimum number of victims is required.
313. The Gambia contends that there is no numerical threshold inherent in the actus reus set
forth in Article II (a) or in any other subparagraph of Article II. It argues that questions of scale or
numbers are relevant to the determination of genocidal intent, but not to the actus reus. Myanmar,
for its part, maintains that each actus reus under Article II must be read in light of the specific intent
set forth in the chapeau, and the acts thus “must be of a certain degree of gravity and assume a certain
scale”. Therefore, in Myanmar’s view, killings can fall within Article II (a) only if they are part of a
campaign committed on a scale that is capable of bringing about the physical or biological destruction
of all or part of the group.
314. The Parties further dispute whether Myanmar committed the act of killing members of
the protected group within the meaning of Article II (a) of the Convention. The Gambia asserts that
at least 10,000 Rohingya were killed by Myanmar in the course of the 2017 clearance operations,
which, it maintains, directly targeted Rohingya civilians rather than members of ARSA. Myanmar,
in turn, claims that its security forces conducted counter-terrorism operations in compliance with its
rules of engagement and that The Gambia has failed to prove that any individuals who were killed
were not participants in ARSA violence or collateral casualties. It also contends that even if the
Applicant’s claim of 10,000 intentional killings of members of the protected group were established,
this would be insufficient to physically destroy the population of Rohingya living in northern
Rakhine State. Thus, the Respondent submits that the evidence does not establish that any killings
fall within the meaning of Article II (a) of the Convention.

*

*

- 103 315. The Court recalls that the words “killing” and “meurtre”, appearing in the English and
French versions of Article II (a) of the Convention respectively, have the same meaning, referring to
the act of intentionally killing members of the group (2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports
2015 (I), pp. 68-69, para. 156, citing the 2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 121,
para. 186, and Blagojević and Jokić, IT‑02‑60‑T, Trial Chamber I, Section A, Judgement of
17 January 2005, para. 642). In this regard, it observes that the expression “intentional killing” has
been given the same meaning as “murder” within the definition of crimes against humanity in the
jurisprudence of international criminal tribunals. In the Court’s view, a killing is intentional within
the meaning of Article II (a) of the Convention where the perpetrator meant to cause the death of the
victim or acted with knowledge that death was a likely consequence of the act or omission. This does
not include negligence or gross negligence (Karadžić, IT-95-5/18-T, Trial Chamber, Judgement of
24 March 2016, paras. 448 and 542; Blagojević and Jokić, IT‑02‑60‑T, Trial Chamber I, Section A,
Judgement of 17 January 2005, paras. 572 and 642; Krstić, IT-98-33-T, Trial Chamber, Judgement
of 2 August 2001, para. 543; Extraordinary Chambers in the Courts of Cambodia, Case 002/02,
Trial Chamber, Judgement of 16 November 2018, paras. 635-651 and 796; see paragraph 287
above).
316. With respect to whether Article II (a) requires harm to be committed at a certain scale in
order for the actus reus to be satisfied, the Court notes that the crime of genocide under the
Convention requires that a perpetrator intend to destroy the protected group in whole or in part, not
merely one or more individual members. Matters of scale with respect to victims, however, form part
of the inquiry into the specific intent to destroy the group in whole or in part set forth in the chapeau
of Article II, specifically when genocidal intent is inferred from circumstantial evidence (see
paragraphs 374 and 427 below; see also Karadžić, IT-95-5/18-T, Trial Chamber, Judgement of
24 March 2016, para. 542). The Court therefore finds that these are considerations for the mens rea
of genocide and not for the actus reus.
317. Regarding the other subparagraphs of Article II, the Court considers that, other than the
distinctive language of Article II (c), which the Court will address below (see paragraphs 344-349),
the material elements under Article II do not, in and of themselves, require the demonstration of any
quantitative impact on the group. There is no requirement of a minimum number of victims, so long
as the acts are those set forth in one of the provisions of Article II and one or more victims are
members of the protected group.
318. Turning to the circumstances of this case, the facts as previously determined by the Court
establish that Myanmar’s security forces committed large-scale killings of members of the protected
group during the course of the 2017 clearance operations (see paragraphs 251-253). The Court
concludes that the killings included, inter alia, the direct targeting of civilians, including women and
very young children, and killings with knives and burning of people in houses. They also included
killing by widespread indiscriminate shooting. Such killings are intentional within the meaning of
Article II (a). Myanmar also concedes that its troops were involved in the killing of the ten captive
Rohingya men at Inn Din, in violation of its rules of engagement (see paragraph 232 above).
319. The Court has already determined that the estimate of 10,000 Rohingya violently killed
during the 2017 clearance operations is supported by the evidence (see paragraph 253). The Court is
fully convinced that Myanmar’s security forces intentionally killed members of the protected group
on a large scale in the course of the clearance operations, and that the actus reus set forth

- 104 in Article II (a) of the Convention is established. The Court is not called upon, for the purpose of
establishing the actus reus of killing, to list the specific killings or to make a conclusive finding on
the total number of victims (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 154, para. 276).
2. Article II (b): causing serious bodily or mental harm to members of the group
320. Acts causing either serious bodily harm or serious mental harm to members of the
protected group constitute the actus reus of genocide under Article II (b). As with killings under
Article II (a), such acts must be intentional (see paragraph 287 above).
321. Invoking the jurisprudence of international criminal tribunals, The Gambia contends that
“serious” bodily or mental harm must involve a “grave and long-term disadvantage to a person’s
ability to lead a normal and constructive life”, though the harm caused need not be permanent or
irremediable. Myanmar agrees that the word “serious” in Article II (b) creates a threshold of severity.
The Parties also agree that rape and other acts of sexual violence, and the forcible transfer of members
of a group are capable of falling within this provision, although their views diverge with respect to
the circumstances under which such acts may do so.
322. The Gambia alleges that Myanmar’s security forces caused serious bodily and mental
harm to members of the Rohingya group. This includes the physical injuries suffered by those who
survived acts such as shootings, burnings and sexual violence. It also includes the serious mental
harm experienced by those who witnessed or escaped death and suffered sexual and other forms of
violence. In the Applicant’s view, the circumstances of the clearance operations, including the
ransacking and burning of homes, the forced exodus to Bangladesh and the inability of Rohingya to
return, has resulted in lasting mental harm for those now forced to live in refugee camps.
323. Myanmar, for its part, submits that The Gambia’s evidence is not capable of proving that
acts satisfying the actus reus of genocide under Article II (b) were committed or resulted from any
use of excessive or unlawful force by Myanmar’s security forces. The Respondent suggests that some
injuries may have resulted from the activities of ARSA or involved “collateral damage” in the context
of an armed conflict, and contests the precise number of deaths and injuries alleged by The Gambia.
324. The joint intervening States contend that the harm resulting from sexual and gender-based
violence is “almost universally ‘serious’ by its nature”, while the DRC emphasizes that rape and
sexual violence “by definition” cause serious bodily or mental harm to victims.

*

*

- 105 325. The Court observes that acts committed against members of the protected group fall
within the scope of Article II (b) only if they result in physical or mental harm that is “serious”. Such
harm “need not be permanent and irremediable” (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I),
p. 167, para. 300, citing Stakić, IT-97-24-T, Trial Chamber II, Judgement of 31 July 2003,
para. 516). Long-standing jurisprudence of international criminal tribunals establishes that serious
bodily or mental harm must, however, involve “a grave and long-term disadvantage to a person’s
ability to lead a normal and constructive life”, and requires proof of a result (Karadžić, IT-95-5/18-T,
Trial Chamber, Judgement of 24 March 2016, para. 543; see also Tolimir, IT-05-88/2-T, Trial
Chamber II, Judgement of 12 December 2012, para. 738; Krajišnik, IT-00-39-T, Trial Chamber I,
Judgement of 27 September 2006, para. 862; Krstić, IT-98-33-T, Trial Chamber, Judgement of
2 August 2001, para. 513). The seriousness of the harm in question is to be assessed on a case-by-case
basis, through a holistic evaluation of the evidence pertaining to the case (Tolimir, IT-05-88/2-A,
Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 8 April 2015, paras. 201 and 210-211).
326. The Court recalls that rape and other forms of sexual and gender-based violence are
“capable of constituting the actus reus of genocide within the meaning of Article II (b)” (2015
Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 70, para. 158; see also 2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J.
Reports 2007 (I), p. 167, para. 300). Indeed, such acts constitute “one of the worst ways of inflicting
harm on the victim as he or she suffers both bodily and mental harm” (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J.
Reports 2007 (I), p. 167, para. 300, citing Akayesu, ICTR-96-4-T, Trial Chamber I, Judgement of
2 September 1998, para. 731).
327. In the context of the situation in Srebrenica, serious bodily or mental harm was found to
have been experienced by persons who were about to be executed, persons who were separated by
forcible displacement from those who were executed, and survivors among them suffering the
resulting loss (see 2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 162, para. 290, citing Krstić,
IT-98-33-T, Trial Chamber, Judgement of 2 August 2001, para. 543, and Blagojević and Jokić,
IT-02-60-T, Trial Chamber I, Section A, Judgement of 17 January 2005, paras. 644-654). The ICTY
accepted that “‘threats of death’ and knowledge of impending death” may result in serious mental
harm and that “survivors of killing operations may suffer serious mental harm” (Tolimir,
IT-05-88/2-A, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 8 April 2015, paras. 206-207). The 2007 Bosnia
Judgment also found that massive mistreatment, beatings, rape and torture committed during the
conflict caused serious bodily and mental harm as defined by Article II (b) of the Convention (I.C.J.
Reports 2007 (I), p. 175, para. 319). The Court has recognized that the psychological pain suffered
by the relatives of persons who have disappeared in the context of an alleged genocide, as a result of
the persistent refusal of the competent authorities to provide the information in their possession
regarding the fate of the disappeared, is capable of causing serious mental harm within the meaning
of Article II (b), so long as the requirements of that provision are satisfied (2015 Croatia Judgment,
I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 70, para. 160, and pp. 108-109, para. 356; see also Tolimir, IT-05-88/2-T,
Trial Chamber II, Judgement of 12 December 2012, para. 756; Popović et al., IT-05-88-T, Trial
Chamber II, Judgement of 10 June 2010, paras. 844 and 846).
328. With respect to forcible displacement, the Court recalls that a proposal to include
“measures intended to oblige members of a group to abandon their homes in order to escape the
threat of subsequent ill-treatment” was not accepted as an element of the actus reus by the drafters

- 106 of the Convention (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 123, para. 190, citing
UN doc. A/C.6/234; emphasis added). However, it is well established that while forcible transfer
“does not of itself” constitute a material act of genocide, “depending on the circumstances of a given
case, it may cause such serious bodily or mental harm as to constitute an act of genocide” (see
Karadžić, IT-95-5/18-T, Trial Chamber, Judgement of 24 March 2016, para. 545; Tolimir,
IT-05-88/2-T, Trial Chamber II, Judgement of 12 December 2012, para. 739, and IT-05-88/2-A,
Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 8 April 2015, para. 209). Specifically, the particular circumstances
of a forcible transfer, such as separation from and loss of family members, appalling transfer
conditions, the destruction of homes and the inability to return home, can cause “a grave and
long-term disadvantage to a person’s ability to lead a normal and constructive life” (see
paragraph 325 above), and thus constitute “serious mental harm” for the purposes of the actus reus
of genocide (Tolimir, IT-05-88/2-A, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 8 April 2015, paras. 209-211;
Popović et al., IT-05-88-T, Trial Chamber II, Judgement of 10 June 2010, paras. 846-847; Blagojević
and Jokić, IT-02-60-T, Trial Chamber I, Section A, Judgement of 17 January 2005, paras. 646 and
650-653; Krstić, IT-98-33-T, Trial Chamber, Judgement of 2 August 2001, para. 513).
329. The record before the Court establishes that acts of the Myanmar security forces inflicted
serious physical injuries on members of the protected group, including injuries resulting from
shooting, knife wounds and burning. Widespread acts of rape, including gang and mass rape, and
other forms of sexual violence, often accompanied by beatings or mutilation, also resulted in serious
physical harm (see paragraphs 254-256 and 260 above). In the view of the Court, the record
establishes by fully conclusive evidence that the Myanmar security forces intentionally caused
serious bodily injuries resulting in a grave and long-term disadvantage to the ability of members of
the protected group to lead a normal and constructive life within the meaning of Article II (b) of the
Convention.
330. A holistic assessment of the evidence also establishes that acts of the Myanmar security
forces resulted in serious mental harm to members of the protected group. This includes the mental
harm inflicted on persons prior to their deaths, the trauma of escaping death and of witnessing or
knowing of the deaths of family members, the corresponding anguish of separation and loss, and the
psychological suffering of relatives regarding family members whose circumstances were and remain
unknown. For example, witnesses MN, NJ and NS all exhibited the psychological harm they continue
to suffer from such losses. As PILPG found, “[o]f the 1,024 Rohingya interviewed, 20 percent were
themselves physically injured in the attacks . . . and 80 percent witnessed the killing of a family
member, friend, or personal acquaintance” (PILPG, Factual findings & legal analysis report, p. vi).
PILPG further found that the killings and disappearances left survivors “with uncertainty about their
future and fear about the fate of those they loved and [that they] suffer profound physical and
psychological harm as a result” (ibid., p. 74). These findings are supported by other evidence in the
record.
331. Acts of rape and other forms of sexual violence, including gang rapes and sexual violence
accompanied by beatings, mutilation, scarring or branding (see paragraphs 254-256 and 260 above),
contributed to lasting mental harm to survivors, as well as to those who witnessed the acts (see
paragraph 257 above). For example, in her affidavit, Witness NJ described the “mental pain,

- 107 including the loss of . . . dignity” inflicted on her by the soldiers who raped her (Affidavit of
Witness NJ, para. 78). A report by Physicians for Human Rights on sexual and other violence
experienced by the Rohingya community as a result of the clearance operations confirms that
“[s]exual violence had a deep and long-lasting impact on the mental health of Rohingya survivors”
(Physicians for Human Rights, “Sexual violence, trauma, and neglect: Observations of health care
providers treating Rohingya survivors in refugee camps in Bangladesh” (October 2020), p. 23).
332. Numerous persons who survived the clearance operations witnessed the burning of homes
and villages — sometimes with family members and other persons locked inside (see paragraph 273
above) — and over 700,000 members of the Rohingya group were forced to flee from northern
Rakhine State to Bangladesh (see paragraph 262 above). Those making the journey experienced loss,
separation and uncertainty regarding their own survival and that of their community. Although some
witnesses reported being able to travel to Bangladesh without incident, for others the journey itself
was traumatic and violent, including risks of rape and other attacks by security forces, drownings
and other risks of death (see paragraphs 262-270 above). The record also establishes that the
extensive destruction and bulldozing of villages and their replacement with other infrastructure (see
paragraphs 271-282 above) has meant that, for more than nine years, most of those who survived the
journey have been consigned to live in squalid and at times dangerous refugee camps, with no
opportunity to return and no homes or livelihoods to return to. The above harms were widespread.
333. The Court recalls that, while forcible displacement does not in and of itself inflict serious
mental harm for the purposes of the actus reus of genocide, the circumstances of such forcible
displacement, taken together, can cause serious mental harm within the meaning of Article II (b) (see
paragraph 328 above). Relevant circumstances have included violent separation from and loss of
family members, the destruction of homes and the inability to return home, ongoing uncertainty
regarding the fate of relatives and their whereabouts, “appalling conditions of the journey . . . by bus
and on foot”, and “post-transfer suffering” (Tolimir, IT-05-88/2-A, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of
8 April 2015, paras. 209-211, confirming the approach taken by the Trial Chamber II, IT-05-88/2-T,
Judgement of 12 December 2012, paras. 756-757; see also Karadžić, IT-95-5/18-T, Trial Chamber,
Judgement of 24 March 2016, para. 5664). The Court considers that members of the protected group
experienced such circumstances in this case.
334. With respect to the general intent required for Article II (b), the Court considers, at a
minimum, that the above violent acts were committed by the Myanmar security forces with
knowledge that they were likely to result in serious mental harm to the Rohingya population. They
thus constitute intentional acts within the meaning of Article II (b) (see paragraphs 287, 315 and 320
above). In the Court’s view, taken together, the acts of the Myanmar security forces described above
have resulted in a grave and long-term disadvantage to the ability of members of the Rohingya group
to lead a normal and constructive life, amounting to serious mental harm within the meaning of
Article II (b).
335. With respect to the number of persons who suffered serious mental harm, the Court
observes that the killing of approximately 10,000 Rohingya (see paragraph 253 above) inflicted

- 108 acute suffering on numerous surviving family members, including pain from loss and separation,
from witnessing deaths and from uncertainty regarding the fate of family members whose
whereabouts remain unknown. At least 1,834 acts of rape (see paragraph 258 above) caused severe
suffering for the victims, as well as suffering for their family members and persons who witnessed
the acts. Approximately 326,000 Rohingya had their homes burned (see paragraph 272 above),
leaving them with no place to return to, and many personally experienced the burning of their homes
and villages (see paragraph 273 above). Finally, while not all of the approximately 700,000 Rohingya
who were forced to flee experienced extreme hardship during the journey to Bangladesh, the
evidence establishes that many of them did (see paragraphs 262-270 above), and those who fled have
not been allowed to return.
336. The Court does not consider that each of the above types of acts alone caused serious
mental harm to every member of the Rohingya group who experienced them. Grave and long-term
disadvantage to the ability to lead a normal and constructive life within the meaning of Article II (b)
is determined pursuant to a holistic assessment of the evidence (see paragraph 325 above).
Nevertheless, some of the acts inflicted on individual Rohingya were extreme enough to cause
serious mental harm in and of themselves; for other members, serious mental harm resulted from a
combination of acts. The Court therefore concludes that the 2017 clearance operations caused serious
mental harm to a very significant number of members of the Rohingya group in northern Rakhine
State.
337. The Court recalls that for the purpose of establishing the actus reus under Article II (b),
it need not set out the precise extent of the injuries suffered or make a conclusive finding on the total
number of victims. The Court is fully convinced that the 2017 clearance operations caused serious
bodily and mental harm to a very significant number of members of the protected group and that the
actus reus set forth in Article II (b) is established.
3. Article II (c): deliberately inflicting on the group conditions of life calculated to bring about
its physical destruction in whole or in part
338. Article II (c) of the Genocide Convention concerns the deliberate infliction on the group
of conditions of life calculated to bring about its physical destruction in whole or in part (2007 Bosnia
Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 176, para. 320). The Court has explained that this provision
covers “methods of physical destruction, other than killing, whereby the perpetrator ultimately seeks
the death of the members of the group” (2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 70,
para. 161).
339. The Parties agree that the actus reus under Article II (c) does not require that physical
destruction actually occur. Rather, in order to fall within Article II (c), conditions of life need only
be “capable of bringing about” the physical destruction of the group in whole or in part.
340. For the Applicant, such conditions of life include measures “imposed to make meaningful
life impossible for the Rohingya — and eliminate the group”. According to The Gambia,
long-standing discrimination and deprivation of the Rohingya’s basic needs, together with

- 109 widespread rape and other forms of sexual violence, the destruction of houses and livelihoods, and
the forced mass exodus to Bangladesh resulting from the clearance operations have inflicted on the
group conditions of life calculated to bring about its physical destruction in whole or in part. With
respect to basic needs, The Gambia alleges that Myanmar for decades imposed on the Rohingya
widespread and severe restrictions on movement, employment, access to food and healthcare,
education, citizenship, and marriage and births, which resulted, inter alia, in severe acute
malnutrition among the Rohingya. This includes, in the Applicant’s view, the conditions of the
internally displaced Rohingya, who since 2012 have been confined in internment camps and ghettos.
341. The Gambia maintains that the clearance operations compounded these conditions,
including through rape and other sexual violence; systemic burning and bulldozing, which eliminated
essential means of subsistence even for those who remained; and hardship during the journey to
Bangladesh, when many died or were injured. The Applicant argues that sexual violence was
perpetrated on a massive scale with the intended effect, at least in part, of weakening the social
cohesion of the Rohingya community and contributing to the destruction of the Rohingya as a group.
342. For Myanmar, the words “[d]eliberately inflicting” together with “calculated” in
Article II (c) require that the perpetrator has deliberately and intentionally imposed the conditions of
life for the purpose of bringing about the destruction of the group, in whole or in part. The Respondent
contends that this provision requires conduct at a scale or level of systematicity that the Court did
not previously find satisfied in the 2015 Croatia Judgment with respect to sexual violence and denial
of food supplies. Thus, according to Myanmar, conditions of life “could fall within Article II (c) only
if deliberately imposed on the group for the purpose of bringing about the physical destruction of
the group in whole or in part, and only if capable of bringing about this result” (emphasis in original).
343. In addition to contesting the basic facts alleged by the Applicant, Myanmar contends that
The Gambia has failed to establish that any deliberate restrictions on food or deprivation of medical
care were sufficiently systematic or general to satisfy Article II (c). The Respondent further
maintains that there is no evidence that the measures invoked by the Applicant sought, as their
ultimate goal, the total or partial physical destruction of the group. Myanmar submits that, to the
contrary, members of the protected group have readily been able to marry, procreate and earn a living
in northern Rakhine State.

*

*

344. The Court observes that subparagraph (c) is distinctive among the material elements set
