forth in Article II in several respects. First, the provision requires that measures be imposed upon at
least a substantial part of “the group”, rather than a single individual, and that the imposition of
measures occur sufficiently consistently to constitute a “condition[] of life”. Article II (c) thus
incorporates a consideration of scale or systematicity with respect to the acts concerned.

- 110 345. Second, as noted, subparagraph (c) is the only provision in Article II that explicitly refers
to the “physical destruction” of the group, in whole or in part (see paragraph 292 above), which has
been understood as referring to the death of at least a substantial part of the group (2015 Croatia
Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 71, para. 161; Tolimir, IT-05-88/2-A, Appeals Chamber,
Judgement of 8 April 2015, para. 233). The actus reus set forth in Article II (c) is therefore analogous
to that in Article II (a), in that it is limited to measures capable of causing the death of members of
the protected population, albeit over a period of time.
346. Third, subparagraph (c) provides that measures be “[d]eliberately” inflicted, as well as
“calculated” to bring about the group’s physical destruction, in whole or in part. The Court has
recognized that the term “deliberately” forms the general mental element of the actus reus of
Article II (c) (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 121, para. 186; see paragraph 287
above). The term “calculated” could be understood as incorporating into Article II (c) an additional
specific mental element of intent or purpose. Under this interpretation, conditions of life would have
to be both deliberately inflicted and also intended to bring about the group’s physical destruction in
whole or in part. Such an interpretation, however, would establish a specific intent requirement for
Article II (c) that largely replicates the specific genocidal intent already required by the chapeau
(“intent to destroy, in whole or in part”), and render the word “calculated” in Article II (c)
superfluous.
347. The Court considers that the term “calculated” in Article II (c) requires that the conditions
of life imposed be objectively capable of bringing about destruction of the group, in whole or in part,
through the slow death of members of the group. As the ICTY Trial Chamber explained in Karadžić,
determining whether measures were “calculated to bring about” the physical destruction of the group
requires focusing on the “objective probability of these conditions leading to the physical
destruction” (Karadžić, IT-95-5/18-T, Trial Chamber, Judgement of 24 March 2016, paras. 546-548;
see also Karadžić, IT-95-5/18-AR98bis.1, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 11 July 2013, para. 40).
Like the other material acts set forth in Article II, such measures nevertheless would not constitute
genocide unless they were also committed with the genocidal intent required by the chapeau.
348. The Court further observes that while the actus reus under Article II (c) requires that the
conditions inflicted be objectively capable over time of resulting in the physical destruction of the
group in whole or in part, this physical destruction need not actually occur in order for the actus reus
to be fulfilled (2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 110, para. 364).
349. In sum, the material act set forth in Article II (c) prohibits a State from engaging in
conduct that deliberately inflicts conditions of life that are objectively capable of causing the death,
over time, of at least a substantial part of the protected group.
350. The Court has recognized that relevant conditions of life for the purposes of Article II (c)
can include “deprivation of food, medical care, shelter or clothing, as well as lack of hygiene,
systematic expulsion from homes, or exhaustion as a result of excessive work or physical exertion”
(2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 70, para. 161, citing Brđanin, IT-99-36-T, Trial
Chamber II, Judgement of 1 September 2004, para. 691). Rape and other acts of sexual violence, and
forcible displacement, may fall within the scope of Article II (c) (ibid., p. 71, para. 162, and p. 110,

- 111 para. 364; 2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 123, para. 190; see also Tolimir,
IT-05-88/2-A, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 8 April 2015, para. 209; Kayishema and Ruzindana,
ICTR-95-1-T, Trial Chamber II, Judgement of 21 May 1999, para. 116).
351. In assessing the conditions of life relevant for this actus reus in the absence of direct
evidence, the relevant inquiry is an “overall factual assessment”, taking into account all the relevant
conditions of life inflicted on all or a substantial part of the group (Tolimir, IT-05-88/2-A, Appeals
Chamber, Judgement of 8 April 2015, para. 209; Karadžić, MICT-13-55-A, Appeals Chamber,
Judgement of 20 March 2019, para. 704). The International Criminal Tribunal for Rwanda (ICTR)
has similarly held that the “conditions of life envisaged” can include a range of measures, provided
they “would lead to the destruction of the group in whole or in part” (Kayishema and Ruzindana,
ICTR-95-1-T, Trial Chamber II, Judgement of 21 May 1999, para. 116).
352. The threshold for establishing within the meaning of Article II (c) that conditions of life
have been deliberately inflicted that are objectively capable of causing the death of all or a substantial
part of the members of the protected group is demanding. In considering acts of rape and sexual
violence, denial of food supplies and food shortages, alleged denial of medical supplies, systematic
expulsion from homes and forced transfer, restrictions on movement and destruction of property,
among other measures, the Court in 2015 did not find the requirements of Article II (c) satisfied
(2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), pp. 110-115, paras. 362-385; see also Karadžić,
IT-95-5/18-T, Trial Chamber, Judgement of 24 March 2016, paras. 2586-2587; Tolimir,
IT-05-88/2-A, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 8 April 2015, para. 233). On the other hand, extreme
deprivation has, in some circumstances, been found to fall within Article II (c) (see Brđanin,
IT-99-36-T, Trial Chamber II, Judgement of 1 September 2004, para. 909).
353. In the present case, the evidence indicates that, for many years, numerous restrictions on
the Rohingya population in northern Rakhine State, including with respect to access to citizenship
and freedom of movement, prevented the Rohingya from being able to secure adequate food and
medical care and to pursue livelihoods (see paragraphs 142 and 147-149 above). Restrictions on
movement increased following the 2012 sectarian violence (FFM, Report of the detailed findings
(2018), para. 509) and tightened further at the time of the 2016 and 2017 clearance operations,
including with respect to access to humanitarian aid (see paragraph 150 above). The over
100,000 Rohingya who remained confined in IDP camps following the 2012 violence also were
subject to severe limitations on their freedom of movement, overcrowded shelters and inadequate
services (see paragraphs 151-152 above). As a result of these and other measures, the Rohingya
population of northern Rakhine State, and particularly its children, faced serious malnutrition in 2017
(World Food Programme, “Food security assessment in the northern part of Rakhine State: Final
report” (July 2017), p. 1).
354. Such measures, together with the events of the 2017 clearance operations, compounded
the acute hardship and vulnerability of the protected group. The Court has recognized that many of
the acts committed during the clearance operations were intentional and inflicted serious bodily or
mental harm within the meaning of Article II (b), and therefore establish the actus reus of genocide
(see paragraph 337 above).

- 112 355. The requirement of Article II (c), however, is not that the conditions of life must be
capable of causing the death of one or more members of the group over time, but that they be capable
of causing the death of at least a substantial part of the group over time. The Court is not convinced
that the evidence sufficiently establishes that the restrictions on movement and deprivation of access
to food and medical care described by the Applicant were objectively capable of bringing about the
ultimate death of a substantial part of the members of the protected group in this manner. The Court
further considers that the circumstances of the 2017 clearance operations themselves, which involved
extreme violence over a concentrated period of time, did not impose conditions of life of the character
contemplated by Article II (c).
356. The Court accordingly concludes that the evidence before it does not conclusively
establish the commission of the actus reus under Article II (c) of the Convention.
4. Article II (d): imposing measures intended to prevent births within the group
357. The actus reus set forth in Article II (d) of the Convention includes a mental element that
measures must be “intended” to prevent births within the group (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J.
Reports 2007 (I), p. 121, para. 186).
358. The Parties disagree regarding the scale and effects of the acts necessary to fall within
subparagraph (d). In The Gambia’s view, measures intended to prevent births need not be inflicted
on all members of the group, nor must births be reduced by a particular rate or demographic decline
be empirically demonstrated. The Applicant maintains that Myanmar “repeatedly characterized the
growth rate of the Rohingya as an existential threat to the survival of the State’s favoured national
races” and imposed a number of measures intended to prevent births within the group within the
meaning of Article II (d). In its view, these include rape and other acts of sexual violence during the
clearance operations, the imposition of obstacles to Islamic marriages and limitations on the number
of children in Maungdaw Township, and denial of birth certificates to Rohingya children.
359. For Myanmar, Article II (d) requires both that the measures be systematically inflicted
on the group, not merely on certain members, and that they result in a negative birth rate that threatens
the existence of the group. It submits that there is no evidence that any measures were intended to
prevent births within the meaning of Article II (d). The Respondent maintains that the restrictions on
childbirth under Regional Order 1/2005 were in force only in Maungdaw Township and did not apply
exclusively to Muslims, and that there is no evidence that the order was “strictly enforced”. Myanmar
further argues that The Gambia has failed to establish an evidential nexus between any alleged
incident of sexual violence or denial of birth certificates and the prevention of births.

*

*

360. The Court observes that Article II (d) encompasses measures intended to bring about the
failure of a group “to live and reproduce normally” (Popović et al., IT-05-88-T, Trial Chamber II,

- 113 Judgement of 10 June 2010, para. 855). As with subparagraphs (b) and (c) of Article II, the Court
has recognized that rape and other acts of sexual violence may fall within subparagraph (d), provided
that the requirements of that provision are fulfilled (2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I),
p. 72, para. 166). In addition, measures such as sexual mutilation, forced sterilization, forced birth
control, separation of the sexes, prohibitions on marriage, and forced impregnation by persons who
are not members of the protected group are capable of falling within the scope of Article II (d), if
they are imposed with the intent of preventing births within the group (Akayesu, ICTR-96-4-T, Trial
Chamber I, Judgement of 2 September 1998, para. 507; Tolimir, IT-05-88/2-T, Trial Chamber II,
Judgement of 12 December 2012, para. 743).
361. International criminal tribunals have recognized that births may be prevented by both the
physical and psychological impacts of measures such as those described above (Akayesu,
ICTR-96-4-T, Trial Chamber I, Judgement of 2 September 1998, para. 508; Tolimir, IT-05-88/2-T,
Trial Chamber II, Judgement of 12 December 2012, para. 743). The impact on victims must also be
considered in light of the social and cultural context (Akayesu, ICTR-96-4-T, Trial Chamber I,
Judgement of 2 September 1998, para. 507). Sexual violence, for example, can hinder future births
because of its physical consequences, as well as its impact on how the victims are perceived within
their group.
362. Article II (d) requires an applicant to demonstrate that the measures imposed are intended
to prevent births within the group (see paragraph 287 above) and are objectively capable of doing so.
The language of subparagraph (d), however, does not require that an applicant additionally establish
that the measures were actually successful in reducing the birth rate of the group. While
considerations of scale or an actual impact on the birth rate may be relevant to the determination of
the mental element included in Article II (d), namely whether a measure was intended to prevent
births, they do not form distinct requirements under Article II (d) (see 2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J.
Reports 2015 (I), p. 117, para. 400; see also 2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 192,
para. 361).
363. The evidence before the Court establishes that through official government speeches and
the Facebook military network, Myanmar authorities maintained over a lengthy period that “Bengali”
population growth threatened to “swallow up” Buddhism and the nation (see paragraphs 157-160
and 164 above). From 2005 to 2018, Maungdaw Township, which contained the highest percentage
of Rohingya of any township in the country — namely 93 per cent or nearly 500,000 Rohingya (see
paragraph 69 above) — imposed Regional Order 1/2005, which required, inter alia, special marriage
permission requirements for “Bengalis” (see paragraph 143 above). The order further provided that
all persons with permission to marry “must limit the number of children” they had (see paragraph 144
above). Myanmar acknowledges that the order was intended to “slow the rate of growth of the
population in Maungdaw Township”, which was overwhelmingly Rohingya (emphasis in original).
In this regard, the Court notes that the evidence does not indicate that the birth restrictions were
actually applied to members of the non-Rohingya population. Witnesses also attested to the
enforcement of the measures against the Rohingya population, including Witness NJ, who recounted
that, in order to marry, she had to sign a document stating that she and her husband would not have
more than two children (see paragraph 145 above). While evidence indicates that the policies did not
succeed in restricting the Rohingya birth rate to two children in practice, such success is not required
by Article II (d).

- 114 364. Regional Order 1/2005 was adopted pursuant to a 1993 temporary regional order that
explicitly focused on the “extremely high” population increase in Maungdaw Township and the fact
that the “majority of residents” were Muslims, and imposed permission restrictions for “Islamic”
marriages (see paragraph 143 above). The marriage and birth restrictions in Regional Order 1/2005
itself were officially described by the Government as “Processes for Controlling Bengali Population”
(see paragraph 144 above). Myanmar accepts that the purpose of the measure was to slow the
birthrate, and the evidence demonstrates its enforcement against members of the Rohingya group.
Under these circumstances, the Court is convinced that the order was a coercive measure directed at
the Rohingya group, which was intended, at the very least, to reduce births within the group,
including at the time of the clearance operations.
365. The restrictions on births in Maungdaw Township were coupled with the long-standing
denial of birth registration and issuance of birth certificates for Rohingya children in northern
Rakhine State, as well as the difficulty of having Rohingya children included on household lists (see
paragraph 147 above). Birth certificates establish the legal identity of children, and for Rohingya in
Rakhine State, inclusion of children in the household list was the prerequisite for, among other things,
the issuance of identity documents, access to education and permission to travel, and thus access to
basic services. In the view of the Court, the denial of legal identity for Rohingya children, and the
onerous and intrusive annual verification of the household lists in northern Rakhine State (FFM,
Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 583-588), like the verifications for the restrictions on
births, are indicative of an intent to deter the Rohingya from reproducing normally.
366. The Court further observes that the magnitude and character of the acts of sexual violence
during the clearance operations support the conclusion that the acts were intended to physically and
mentally impair the willingness and ability of the population to procreate. Gang rapes were
widespread, including mass rapes involving multiple victims and perpetrators (see paragraph 254
above), with rape frequently accompanied by extreme brutality or murder (see paragraph 255 above).
The Court observes that rape and other forms of sexual violence were primarily directed at women
and girls of reproductive age, including, in some instances, pregnant women (ibid.). Young and
unmarried women were targeted with extreme violence (ibid.), and girls as young as seven were
raped (see paragraph 260 above). Sexual violence was also accompanied at times by biting, scarring
and other mutilation, including of breasts and genitalia (see paragraph 256 above), contributing to
the visibility, stigma and long-term impact of sexual violence on the survivors, and committed in
front of husbands, other relatives or community members.
367. Acts of rape and other sexual violence caused lasting physical harm to reproductive
functioning (see IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027999848, paras. 83-85 and 106-109; FFM,
Report of the Detailed Findings (2018), para. 934). The focused expert medical assessment of the
impact of sexual violence on the Rohingya refugee population undertaken by LAW found that
survivors of sexual violence suffered long-term physical consequences, including permanent damage
to genitalia, pain during intercourse and other forms of sexual dysfunction that negatively impacted

- 115 their ability to procreate (LAW, “‘Every day, I remember they destroyed my life’: Long-term
physical and psychosocial consequences of genocidal sexual and gender-based violence the
Myanmar military committed against the Rohingya in its 2017 ‘clearance operations’” (November
2023), p. 8 and Annex 1; see paragraph 257 above). Months after being raped, witnesses interviewed
by the FFM continued to suffer from severe injuries that prevented them from having sexual
intercourse with their husbands. The Court notes that United Nations and other healthcare providers
in the refugee camps also detected evidence of forcible impregnation resulting from rapes (FFM,
Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 934-936).
368. Survivors of sexual violence and their partners also experienced lasting psychological
trauma, social ostracism and cultural stigma, which affected the possibility of marriage and
procreation, particularly in light of the social structure and gender roles in the Rohingya community.
IIMM Witness P2499 stated that
“[b]eing raped was worse than being killed to me. To this day, I am still ashamed for
being a rape victim. I get told things like, ‘you were touched by moghs’, ‘you had a
mogh penis inside you’, or . . . ‘this is why moghs raped you’. Those who shame me are
people from my community” (IIMM, Witness Statement No. IIMM0027999848,
para. 111).
Female survivors voiced concerns to the FFM that their husbands would leave them and that they
would no longer be able to have children (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 934).
Physicians for Human Rights also found that some survivors were reluctant to discuss or disclose
sexual violence out of fear that they would not be able to get married or would be rejected by their
husband (Physicians for Human Rights, “Sexual violence, trauma, and neglect: Observations of
health care providers treating Rohingya survivors in refugee camps in Bangladesh” (October 2020),
p. 14; see paragraph 331 above). The expert assessment conducted by LAW found that survivors
suffered severe psychological injuries which affected their spousal relationships and psychological
capacity to procreate, including severe ostracization and at times spousal abandonment for women
and feelings of powerlessness and emasculation for men. Some survivors indicated that they had
been reduced to a position of being perceived as racially different from the rest of the community
and trapped in identities as rape survivors, while offspring of rape by the security forces were treated
as “polluted” (LAW, “‘Every day, I remember they destroyed my life’: Long-term physical and
psychosocial consequences of genocidal sexual and gender-based violence the Myanmar military
committed against the Rohingya in its 2017 ‘clearance operations’” (November 2023), p. 8).
369. The Court observes that widespread sexual violence including the rape of underage girls
and pregnant women, gang and mass rapes, and rapes in front of family members have been found
to impact both the physical capacity of women to reproduce and the psychological procreative
capacity of both sexes, and to constitute measures to prevent the reproduction of members of the
group for purposes of the actus reus of genocide. With respect to intent, the Court considers that the
likely effects of the widespread sexual violence against the Rohingya population would have been
understood by the Myanmar security forces who perpetrated them, particularly in light of the cultural
context, and that the acts taken must have been taken with the knowledge that these consequences
were likely to occur (see paragraphs 287 and 315 above). The Court further notes that the separation
of the sexes and killing of men in multiple villages during the clearance operations (see, for example,

- 116 paragraphs 192, 205, 219 and 232 above), as well as the brutal murder of infants (see, for example,
paragraphs 194-195, 197, 208, 212, 222, 259 and 261 above), also support the inference that the
Myanmar security forces acted with the intent to prevent births among the group.
370. The Court finds that, taken together, the restrictions on marriages and births on Rohingya
in Maungdaw Township, where nearly 500,000 Rohingya resided, the denial of legal registration for
Rohingya children and the commission of at least 1,834 acts of rape targeting the Rohingya
population during the 2017 clearance operations, when viewed in the context of the Government’s
messages regarding uncontrolled Rohingya population growth, establish an intent to impact the
capacity of members of the Rohingya community to reproduce normally and thus an intent to prevent
births within the group.
371. The Court is therefore fully convinced that Myanmar imposed measures intended to
prevent births within the group and that the actus reus of genocide within the meaning of
Article II (d) of the Convention is established.

*

372. In sum, the Court considers that the evidence conclusively establishes that the Myanmar
security forces and other State organs committed acts falling within the actus reus set forth in
Article II (a), (b) and (d) of the Genocide Convention. The evidence does not, however, conclusively
establish the commission of the actus reus set forth in Article II (c).
C. Mens rea of genocide (genocidal intent)
373. Article II of the Genocide Convention provides that, in order to constitute genocide, the
actus reus of genocide, discussed above, must be “committed with intent to destroy, in whole or in
part, a [protected] group, as such”. The Court will now examine the meaning of the phrase “in part”
and the requirement that the acts be committed with intent to destroy the protected group.
1. Meaning of the term “in part” in Article II
374. Where allegations of genocide are based on the targeting of a part of the protected group,
the intention to destroy must relate to at least a substantial part of that group (2007 Bosnia Judgment,
I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 126, para. 198; 2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 65,
para. 142; Krstić, IT-98-33-A, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 19 April 2004, para. 8; Kayishema
and Ruzindana, ICTR 95-1-T, Trial Chamber II, Judgement of 21 May 1999, para. 97). As the Court
observed in the 2007 Bosnia Judgment, “since the object and purpose of the Convention as a whole
is to prevent the intentional destruction of groups, the part targeted must be significant enough to
have an impact on the group as a whole” (I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 126, para. 198). The
jurisprudence of the Court and of international criminal tribunals identifies three elements relevant
to the assessment of whether the targeted part of the group is substantial and thus comes within the
meaning of the Convention.

- 117 375. The first, quantitative, element concerns the numerical size of the targeted part of the
group (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 126, para. 198; 2015 Croatia Judgment,
I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 65, para. 142), which “should be evaluated not only in absolute terms, but
also in relation to the overall size of the entire group” (Krstić, IT-98-33-A, Appeals Chamber,
Judgement of 19 April 2004, para. 12). Second, a geographic element, that is “the area of the
perpetrators’ activity and control, as well as the possible extent of their reach” (ibid., para. 13; see
also 2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 126, para. 199; 2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J.
Reports 2015 (I), p. 65, para. 142), should be considered. A third, qualitative, element, the
“prominence” or “emblematic” nature of the targeted portion within the group, may also be relevant
(2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 127, para. 200; Krstić, IT-98-33-A, Appeals
Chamber, Judgement of 19 April 2004, para. 12). These conditions are not exhaustive (2007 Bosnia
Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 127, para. 201), nor need all three conditions be met; however,
the Court has emphasized that the quantitative criterion is “an essential starting point” (ibid.,
para. 200) and must be given priority (ibid., para. 201).
376. According to The Gambia, in assessing whether a substantial “part” of the Rohingya was
targeted, the Court can rely on the number of people and villages that were attacked. The Gambia
argues that while the “headline victims” are those Rohingya who were killed, consideration of the
number of targeted people must also include those seriously harmed, including through forcible
displacement. The Gambia also maintains, on the basis of the FFM’s reports, that 18 per cent of
villages in northern Rakhine State were totally destroyed, and a further 22 per cent of such villages
were partially destroyed. It estimates that the combined population of these villages amounted to
some 326,000 people. While in its arguments The Gambia focuses mainly on the numerical size of
the targeted part of the group, it claims that this part is substantial also in light of the geographical
and qualitative elements.
377. Myanmar criticizes The Gambia for failing to adequately identify the relevant part of the
protected group according to the quantitative, geographic and qualitative elements. In particular,
Myanmar argues that it is unclear on what basis The Gambia seeks to exclude Rohingya living in
IDP camps in Sittwe Township from the targeted part of the group. Myanmar also takes issue with
The Gambia for “present[ing] . . . a blizzard of statistics” concerning the numerical size of the
targeted part of the group, characterizing this as an attempt by the Applicant to “artificially shrink
the ‘size of the targeted part of the protected group’” in order to strengthen its position when
comparing the numbers of targeted persons to the number of victims.
378. During the oral proceedings, The Gambia clarified that it considered the part of the
Rohingya population that was targeted for destruction to be the approximately 815,000 Rohingya
living in northern Rakhine State prior to the clearance operations. Myanmar did not disagree that, if
the targeted part of the Rohingya population was the population in northern Rakhine State, it would
qualify as a substantial part of the protected group. On this basis, Myanmar maintained that the
substantiality criterion had no further role to play in the mens rea analysis, with the relevant question
then being whether there was an intent to destroy that part of the group “as such”.

*

*

- 118 379. The Court has found above that the actus reus of genocide is established under
Article II (a), (b) and (d) of the Genocide Convention (see paragraph 372). In light of this, for the
purpose of ascertaining the numerical size of the targeted part of the protected group in order to
assess whether it is significant enough in a quantitative sense, account must be taken of all members
of that group who were targeted by any of the relevant material acts. The Court observes that at least
some of these acts can be understood as targeting the entire Rohingya population in northern Rakhine
State (see paragraphs 332-335 above). Thus, the Court finds that the targeted part of the protected
group is the approximately 815,000 Rohingya who lived in northern Rakhine State prior to the 2017
clearance operations (see paragraph 69 above). The Court considers that this number is clearly
significant both in absolute terms and in relation to the overall Rohingya population in Myanmar (see
ibid.).
380. The Court concludes that, based on the numerical size of the group alone, the part of the
Rohingya population targeted by Myanmar is substantial for the purposes of the Genocide
Convention. Consequently, the Court does not consider it necessary to examine the geographic or
qualitative elements in this case.
2. Establishing genocidal intent
381. As noted above, Article II of the Genocide Convention contains a specific mental
element, namely the intent to destroy the protected group, as such, in whole or in part (see
paragraph 286). The Gambia must therefore show that the material acts established above (see
paragraphs 311-372) committed by Myanmar were accompanied by genocidal intent.
382. Invoking the Court’s jurisprudence, The Gambia identifies three possible pathways to
prove genocidal intent: (1) direct evidence of a general plan; (2) “particular circumstances”; and (3) a
“pattern of conduct”. Given that there is no direct evidence that Myanmar had a general genocidal
plan, The Gambia focuses on the second and third pathways. In defining the second pathway,
The Gambia suggests that the phrase “particular circumstances” implies the possibility of
establishing genocidal intent “in respect of a single incident or place” ⎯ similar to the Court’s
findings in its 2007 Bosnia Judgment with respect to Srebrenica ⎯ or in respect of a smaller subset
of locations. On this basis, The Gambia invites the Court to focus its analysis on three individual
locations: the villages of Min Gyi, Chut Pyin and Maung Nu. Alternatively, The Gambia contends
that genocidal intent can be established on the basis of a “wider ‘pattern of conduct’” wherein
widespread attacks were carried out in northern Rakhine State “according to a generally similar
modus operandi”. In this context, The Gambia relies on a “pattern of conduct” as evidenced by the
indicators of genocidal intent identified by the FFM (see paragraph 391 below). The Gambia argues
that, in this case, the evidence before the Court demonstrates the existence of both “particular
circumstances” and a “pattern of conduct”, thus establishing that genocidal intent is the only
reasonable inference to be drawn from Myanmar’s conduct.
383. Myanmar rejects The Gambia’s interpretation of the Court’s jurisprudence that genocidal
intent may be inferred from “particular circumstances”. It contends that, in the absence of direct
evidence, a pattern of conduct is the sole basis from which genocidal intent may be inferred. With

- 119 respect to The Gambia’s reliance on indicators of genocidal intent, Myanmar argues that the logic of
proof by inference means that the existence of genocidal intent cannot be determined by predefined
criteria.

*

*

384. The Court and international criminal tribunals have recognized that genocidal intent may
be established either through direct evidence or indirectly by relying on inferences drawn from
circumstantial evidence (see 2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), pp. 196-197, para. 373;
2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 65, para. 143; Nahimana et al., ICTR-99-52-A,
Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 28 November 2007, para. 524; Tolimir, IT-05-88/2-T, Trial
Chamber II, Judgement of 12 December 2012, para. 34). In the absence of direct evidence of an
intent to commit genocide, The Gambia must therefore rely on circumstantial evidence to establish
that Myanmar acted with genocidal intent.
385. With respect to The Gambia’s argument regarding “particular circumstances” (see
paragraph 382 above), in the Court’s view, that term, as used in paragraph 373 of the 2007 Bosnia
Judgment, cannot be understood as a ground for inferring genocidal intent based on conduct carried
out at only a small number of specific locations. Such an approach would conflict with the Court’s
finding that the part of the group that was targeted by Myanmar was substantial for the purposes of
the Genocide Convention. It would not be appropriate for the Court to consider that the entire
Rohingya population of northern Rakhine State was targeted by Myanmar’s actions (see
paragraph 379 above), while simultaneously inferring genocidal intent solely from evidence confined
to a limited number of locations.
386. The Court now turns to Myanmar’s argument that the inference of genocidal intent from
circumstantial evidence is only possible through a pattern of conduct. In the 2015 Croatia Judgment,
the Court defined a pattern of conduct as “a consistent series of acts carried out over a specific period
of time” (I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 151, para. 510). It follows from the often systematic nature of
acts of genocide that allegations concerning a pattern of conduct are commonly put forward in
disputes under the Genocide Convention, and the factors based on which such a pattern is identified
frequently form the basis of the inquiry into genocidal intent.
387. The jurisprudence of the Court and of international criminal tribunals indicates, however,
that the inquiry into genocidal intent is not confined to determining whether certain factors reveal a
pattern of conduct from which such intent may be inferred. Rather, genocidal intent may be inferred
from an array of different factors, without necessarily requiring a prior finding that a pattern of
conduct is established (Tolimir, IT-05-88/2-T, Trial Chamber II, Judgement of 12 December 2012,
para. 1161; Gacumbitsi, ICTR-01-64-T, Trial Chamber III, Judgement of 17 June 2004,
paras. 252-253; Gacumbitsi, ICTR-01-64-A, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 7 July 2006, para. 40;
see also 2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 122, para. 418). The Court further
observes that the circumstantial evidence that it may consider in identifying the existence of
genocidal intent is not limited to conduct that constitutes the material acts under Article II of the
Genocide Convention (Karadžić, MICT-13-55-A, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 20 March 2019,
para. 728).

- 120 388. In Tolimir, the ICTY described the approach of inferring intent from “all of the evidence,
taken together” as an approach that is most “in line with the fluid concept of intent” (IT-05-88/2-T,
Trial Chamber II, Judgement of 12 December 2012, para. 772; IT-05-88/2-A, Appeals Chamber,
Judgement of 8 April 2015, para. 247). The Court in the 2015 Croatia Judgment characterized the
ICTY’s reasoning in Tolimir as “in substance identical” to its own (I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 67,
para. 148). The Court will thus assess the evidence holistically in order to establish whether genocidal
intent may be inferred from Myanmar’s conduct.
389. In cases such as the present one, in which the Court draws inferences from circumstantial
evidence, genocidal intent must be the only reasonable inference from the facts (2015 Croatia
Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 122, para. 417; Tolimir, IT-05-88/2-T, Trial Chamber II,
Judgement of 12 December 2012, para. 34; Nahimana et al., ICTR-99-52-A, Appeals Chamber,
Judgement of 28 November 2007, para. 524). The Court will consider below whether the evidence
supports a reasonable inference that Myanmar’s security forces acted with genocidal intent (see
paragraphs 390-437 below) and whether this is the only reasonable inference that can be drawn from
the totality of the facts (see paragraphs 438-467 below).
3. Factors relevant to establishing an inference of genocidal intent in the present case
390. The Gambia identifies a series of what it terms “indicators” of genocidal intent relevant
to this case. The language of indicators may however suggest a fixed category of signs of genocidal
intent, rather than a context-specific set of factors from which genocidal intent may be inferred. For
this reason, the Court will use the term “factors” in the following discussion.
391. The Gambia identifies the following factors as indicative of genocidal intent in this case:
(1) Myanmar’s laws and policies, characterized as discriminatory; (2) Myanmar’s anti-Rohingya
hate speech and propaganda; (3) the “extreme brutality” of Myanmar’s clearance operations;
(4) sexual and gender-based violence; (5) the targeting of children; (6) Myanmar’s destruction and
concealment of evidence of crimes committed against the Rohingya; and (7) the impunity enjoyed
by Myanmar’s security forces (see FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 1419-1433).
The Gambia also relies on a set of factors that was identified by the ICTR in the Muvunyi case:
“(a) the general context; (b) the perpetration of other culpable acts systematically
directed against that same group, whether these acts were committed by the same
offender or by others; (c) the scale of atrocities committed; (d) their general nature;
(e) their execution in a region or a country; (f) the fact that the victims were deliberately
and systematically chosen on account of their membership in a particular group; (g) the
exclusion, in this regard, of members of other groups; (h) the political doctrine which
gave rise to the acts referred to; (i) the repetition of destructive and discriminatory acts;
and (j) the perpetration of acts which violate the very foundation of the group or are
considered as such by their perpetrators” (ICTR-00-55A-T, Trial Chamber III,
Judgement of 11 February 2010, para. 29).
392. Myanmar contends that The Gambia must first prove the existence of a pattern of conduct
on the basis of four factors that the Court identified as “most important” in the 2015 Croatia

- 121 Judgment (see paragraph 393 below). Only then, in Myanmar’s view, can the Court consider whether
genocidal intent may be inferred from a pattern of conduct. Myanmar also argues that many of the
factors that The Gambia points to as indicating genocidal intent, such as hate speech, are not relevant
to establishing a pattern of conduct or are otherwise irrelevant in assessing the existence of a specific
intent to commit genocide. Myanmar further contends that no such pattern of conduct is established.

*

*

393. The Court recalls that, in the 2007 Bosnia Judgment and the 2015 Croatia Judgment, the
Court’s analysis of the existence of genocidal intent was informed to a large extent by prior findings
of the ICTY (I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 155, para. 277, and pp. 163-166, paras. 292-297; I.C.J.
Reports 2015 (I), p. 128, para. 440). In the Croatia case, Croatia proposed “a series of 17 factors
which it believe[d], individually or taken together, could lead the Court to conclude that there was a
systematic policy of targeting Croats with a view to their elimination from the regions concerned”
(I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 119, para. 408). The Court found that, of these factors,
“the most important are those that concern the scale and allegedly systematic nature of
the attacks, the fact that those attacks are said to have caused casualties and damage far
in excess of what was justified by military necessity, the specific targeting of Croats and
the nature, extent and degree of the injuries caused to the Croat population” (ibid.,
p. 121, para. 413).
Based on these factors and the relevant factual findings of the ICTY, the Court was able to “establish
the existence of a pattern of conduct that consisted, from August 1991, in widespread attacks by the
JNA [Yugoslav People’s Army] and Serb forces on localities with Croat populations in various
regions of Croatia, according to a generally similar modus operandi” (ibid., p. 122, para. 416).
394. The Court also considered two further factors proposed by Croatia to support the
argument that Serbia acted with genocidal intent. These factors were “the context in which those acts
were committed” and “the opportunity which the JNA and Serb forces had of destroying the Croat
population” (2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 122, para. 418). The Court found
that neither these factors nor “the series of 17 factors invoked by Croatia” supported Croatia’s claim
that the relevant acts were committed with genocidal intent (ibid., pp. 125-126, paras. 429-430;
p. 127, para. 437; and p. 128, para. 439) and thus concluded that such an intent was not the only
reasonable inference that could be drawn from the pattern of conduct that Croatia relied upon (ibid.,
p. 128, para. 440).
395. Reliance on factors of this type to determine genocidal intent in the absence of direct
evidence is well established in the jurisprudence of international criminal tribunals. In Tolimir, for
example, the ICTY Appeals Chamber stated that,
“in the absence of direct evidence, genocidal intent may be inferred from a number of
facts and circumstances, such as the general context, the perpetration of other culpable
acts systematically directed against the same group, the scale of atrocities committed,
the systematic targeting of victims on account of their membership in a particular group,

- 122 the repetition of destructive and discriminatory acts, or the existence of a plan or policy”
(IT-05-88/2-A, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 8 April 2015, para. 246; see also
Karadžić, IT-95-5/18-AR98bis.1, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 11 July 2013,
para. 57).
396. As the Court has observed above, drawing inferences from a pattern of conduct is not the
only method to infer genocidal intent from circumstantial evidence (see paragraph 387). Nor does
the 2015 Croatia Judgment suggest that the factors that the Court described as “the most important”
in that case constitute an exhaustive or fixed list. Indeed, international criminal tribunals have relied
on an array of different factors in inferring genocidal intent (see paragraphs 387, 391 and 395 above).
In identifying the factors it considers relevant to this case, the Court will take into account the factual
background as well as the jurisprudence of the Court and of international criminal tribunals.
397. Accordingly, the Court will base its assessment of the existence of genocidal intent in the
present case on the following factors: (a) the general context, including the perpetration of other
culpable acts systematically directed against the Rohingya; (b) the selective targeting of the
Rohingya; (c) the perpetration of acts that violate the foundations of the Rohingya group; and (d) the
systematic nature and the scale of Myanmar’s actions.
(a) General context, including the perpetration of other culpable acts systematically directed
against the group
398. This Court and international criminal tribunals have recognized that inferences
concerning the existence of genocidal intent may be drawn from the general context, including the
perpetration of “other culpable acts” systematically directed against the same group (2015 Croatia
Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 122, para. 419; Tolimir, IT-05-88/2-T, Trial Chamber II,
Judgement of 12 December 2012, para. 745; Gacumbitsi, ICTR-01-64-T, Trial Chamber III,
Judgement of 17 June 2004, para. 252; see also Simba, ICTR-01-76-T, Trial Chamber I, Judgement
of 13 December 2005, para. 413; Rutaganda, ICTR-96-3-A, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of
26 May 2003, para. 528). While international criminal tribunals have used the expression “other
culpable acts” in connection with the criminal responsibility of individuals, the Court does not use
the term “culpable” as a criminal legal qualification of the conduct in question. The expression is
intended only to describe acts of a particularly serious nature.
399. There are four distinct elements of Myanmar’s conduct that are particularly pertinent in
this regard. They are (i) the long-standing legislative and other forms of discrimination against the
Rohingya; (ii) the prevalence of anti-Rohingya hate speech; (iii) military operations targeting the
Rohingya prior to 2017; and (iv) the forcible displacement of the Rohingya population during and
after the 2017 clearance operations. The Court considers these elements to constitute both part of the
relevant general context and other culpable acts that were systematically directed against the
Rohingya. While the Court does not consider these elements individually to constitute evidence of
Myanmar’s genocidal intent, taken together, they are important aspects of the broader contextual
framework of the case from which inferences concerning the existence of genocidal intent might be
drawn. The Court will consider each element in turn.

- 123 (i) Legislative and other forms of discrimination against the Rohingya
400. The Court has already described the long-standing discrimination against the Rohingya
population in Myanmar (see paragraphs 134-152). The Court recalls its finding that the
1982 Citizenship Law as implemented denied the Rohingya access to citizenship based on race and
that the onerous verification requirements imposed by that law resulted in a denial of citizenship even
to members of the Rohingya who would otherwise have been entitled to it (see paragraphs 137-138
above). The denationalization of Rohingya through the 1982 Citizenship Law has left approximately
one million Rohingya stateless (see paragraph 140 above). The successive identification régimes that
were introduced in Myanmar for the Rohingya carried with them a number of other restrictions on
the Rohingya as a group, including restrictions on access to employment and other economic
activities, such as fishing, and to higher education. Restrictions on freedom of movement also
affected the Rohingya’s access to, inter alia, food, essential healthcare and humanitarian aid (see
paragraphs 148-150 above). These regulatory systems were discriminatory both by design and in
their implementation, rendering the Rohingya marginalized and particularly vulnerable (see
paragraphs 137-140 and 142 above).
401. Myanmar’s imposition of policies aimed at restricting the rights of the Rohingya to marry
and to have children (see paragraphs 143-146 above) is an important element of the general context.
Regional Order 1/2005, in force in Maungdaw Township between 2005 and 2018, required prior
permission for Islamic marriages and sought to limit the number of children born into those
marriages, intending coercively to slow the rate of growth of the Rohingya population
(see paragraphs 357-371 above).
(ii) Anti-Rohingya hate speech
402. The Court recalls its earlier findings on anti-Rohingya hate speech in Myanmar, focusing
on a series of speeches made by Senior General Min Aung Hlaing during and after the clearance
operations, and on the campaign disseminating hate speech on Facebook organized by the Tatmadaw
(see paragraphs 153-168 above). For example, Senior General Hlaing reportedly stated, while the
2017 clearance operations were under way, that “[t]he Bengali problem was a longstanding one
which has become an unfinished job despite the efforts of the previous governments to solve it” (see
paragraph 157 above). According to Myanmar, this statement and other such passages have been
“distorted” by being taken out of context by The Gambia. In the Court’s view, however, the
derogatory and discriminatory meaning of these statements is not altered substantially when read in
their full context (see paragraphs 157-159 above).
403. The Court has described above the Facebook campaign organized by the Tatmadaw
against the Rohingya (see paragraphs 161-168). The IIMM described the depiction of the Rohingya
in the posts as that of a group posing “an existential threat to Myanmar through violence, terrorism
or ‘Islamisation’”, as well as a “threat to Burmese racial purity” (see paragraph 164 above). The
Court has found that the Myanmar military actively disseminated and promoted such speech in a
systematic and co-ordinated way during the 2017 clearance operations (see paragraphs 162-163
above).
404. Utterances of hate speech may be relevant in assessing the genocidal intent of the State,
especially in cases where the person disseminating hate speech is a high-ranking government official
or where the dissemination of hate speech is organized by governmental authorities (Akayesu,
ICTR-96-4-T, Trial Chamber I, Judgement of 2 September 1998, para. 524; Kayishema and

- 124 Ruzindana, ICTR-95-1-T, Trial Chamber II, Judgement of 21 May 1999, paras. 93 and 527; Popović
et al., IT-05-88-T, Trial Chamber II, Judgement of 10 June 2010, para. 1177). As the Appeals
Chamber of the International Residual Mechanism for Criminal Tribunals (IRMCT) observed in
Karadžić, “[u]tterances that fall short of expressly calling for a group’s physical destruction might
constitute evidence of genocidal intent but a perpetrator’s statements must be understood and
assessed in their proper context” (MICT-13-55-A, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 20 March 2019,
para. 745). In this regard, the Court notes the temporal overlap between the anti-Rohingya hate
speech campaign and the clearance operations (see paragraphs 156-159 and 162 above). The Court
recalls that the hate speech organized and disseminated by the Myanmar military in the lead-up to
and during the clearance operations portrayed the Rohingya both as subhuman and as a violent threat,
thus contributing to a climate conducive to targeting a group for destruction (see paragraph 167
above).
(iii) Previous military operations targeting the Rohingya
405. There had been military campaigns against the Rohingya before the 2017 clearance
operations, which had caused large-scale displacement (see paragraph 73 above). The Tatmadaw
conducted a registration operation in 1978 and a similar operation between 1991 and 1992, both of
which were large-scale military campaigns that targeted the Rohingya and resulted in hundreds of
thousands of Rohingya fleeing to Bangladesh. In both cases, the great majority of the displaced
Rohingya population was ultimately repatriated to Myanmar.
406. In October 2016, the Myanmar military undertook clearance operations against the
Rohingya following attacks by ARSA on police posts. The resulting violence caused approximately
87,000 Rohingya to flee to Bangladesh (see paragraph 175 above); those who fled have not since
been repatriated. The Court considers that these military operations constitute other culpable acts
systematically directed against the same group.
(iv) Forcible displacement of the Rohingya population
407. Forcible displacement cannot “as such be designated as genocide” (2007 Bosnia
Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 123, para. 190 (emphasis in original)). This does not however
preclude reliance on it as a potential basis for inferring genocidal intent (Tolimir, IT-05-88/2-A,
Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 8 April 2015, para. 254). As the Court stated in the 2007 Bosnia
Judgment, “it is clear that acts of ‘ethnic cleansing’ may occur in parallel to acts prohibited by
Article II of the Convention, and may be significant as indicative of the presence of a specific intent
(dolus specialis) inspiring those acts” (I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 123, para. 190). Forcible
displacement has been considered to fall within the category of “other culpable acts systematically
directed against the same group” for the purpose of determining genocidal intent (Krstić, IT-98-33-A,
Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 19 April 2004, para. 33).
408. International criminal tribunals have characterized forcible displacement as potentially
constituting “additional means” by which to ensure the physical destruction of a group by
“eliminating even the residual possibility” that the protected group in the area could reconstitute itself
(Krstić, IT-98-33-A, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 19 April 2004, para. 31). In Blagojević and

- 125 Jokić, the ICTY found that the manner in which the forcible transfer of Bosnian Muslim women,
children and elderly persons from Srebrenica occurred indicated an intent to clear the enclave of its
Bosnian Muslim population. The Trial Chamber observed that
“[t]he manner in which the transfer was carried out ⎯ through force and coercion, by
not registering those who were transferred, by burning the houses of some of the people,
sending the clear message that they had nothing to return to, and significantly, through
its targeting of literally the entire Bosnian Muslim population of Srebrenica, including
the elderly and children ⎯ clearly indicates that it was a means to eradicate the Bosnian
Muslim population from the territory where they had lived” (IT-02-60-T, Trial
Chamber I, Section A, Judgement of 17 January 2005, para. 675).
The forcible displacement of the Rohingya from northern Rakhine State by Myanmar’s security
forces bears similar hallmarks.
409. The Court has described above the forcible displacement of the Rohingya in the context
of the actus reus analysis (see paragraphs 328-330). The journey to Bangladesh was dangerous, with
evidence also establishing incidents of Myanmar security forces attacking Rohingya attempting to
escape the violence and raping women and girls as they fled to Bangladesh (see paragraphs 266-268,
270 and 332 above). The campaign of violence against the Rohingya during the 2017 clearance
operations destroyed any semblance of community security, dispersing families and village
populations in a chaotic manner. Moreover, the burning of houses in Rohingya villages, further
discussed below (see paragraphs 413-415), signalled that the Rohingya had no place to return to,
completing the strategy of purging the Rohingya population from the territory where they had lived
(cf. Blagojević and Jokić, IT-02-60-T, Trial Chamber I, Section A, Judgement of 17 January 2005,
para. 675).
(b) Selective targeting of the Rohingya
410. In the 2015 Croatia Judgment, the Court considered the specific targeting of a group as
an important factor in the process of establishing genocidal intent (I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 121,
para. 413). The ICTR has similarly identified “the systematic targeting of victims on account of their
membership in a particular group” as one of the factors from which genocidal intent may be inferred
(Simba, ICTR-01-76-T, Trial Chamber I, Judgement of 13 December 2005, para. 413). The selective
targeting of the Rohingya was manifested in two distinct ways: (i) through the selective targeting of
Rohingya villages and individuals during the clearance operations; and (ii) through the selective
burning and razing of Rohingya villages during and after the clearance operations, combined with
the construction of other infrastructure and the organized resettlement of non-Rohingya communities
in areas where Rohingya had formerly lived.
(i) Selective targeting of Rohingya villages and individuals
411. Evidence before the Court indicates that, in numerous locations in northern Rakhine
State, only Rohingya settlements were affected by the clearance operations, indicating selective
targeting, while non-Rohingya areas in the same locations remained untouched (see paragraph 272
above and, for example, paragraph 198 (Min Gyi), paragraph 211 (Chut Pyin), paragraph 233 (Inn
Din), paragraph 239 (Koe Tan Kauk and Chein Khar Li) and paragraph 247 (Myin Hlut)). The FFM

- 126 found that nearby Rakhine villages were generally left intact in at least 22 of the locations the FFM
analysed (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 972 and 1063). These operations were
conducted in the region of Myanmar with the highest Rohingya population. The record also contains
extensive evidence of direct and intentional targeting of Rohingya civilians, including women,
children, the disabled and the elderly, as well as of executions of Rohingya men and boys (see
paragraphs 250-253 and 318 above). For example, a video posted on the Facebook military network
during the clearance operations showed a soldier explaining to a group of non-Rohingya civilians
how the military would “clear the villages where those animals [the Rohingya] live”. He stated: “We
have marked the places. In order for them not to overflow into the ethnic villages, there will be a
group blocking [the Rohingya]. There will be another group doing the clearing. They [the soldiers]
will be doing the clearing systematically.” (IIMM, “Anti-Rohingya hate speech on Facebook:
Content and network analysis” (27 March 2024), para. 320; see also paragraph 165 above.) The
selective targeting of the Rohingya is further evidenced by the testimony of a person self-identifying
as a former Tatmadaw soldier who participated in the clearance operations:
“They ordered [us] to do for clearance operation, for example, ordered to shoot
and clear the villages. When (we are) sent along with police to enter the village, (we are
ordered) to clear. Ordered to enter the village and clear like that. Clearance means, for
example, surrounding the villages according to race.” (Interview of Zaw Naing Tun
(15 August 2020), p. 6.)
412. Myanmar claims that the reason for its selective targeting was its intention to conduct
counter-terrorism operations against ARSA. This claim will be considered below (see
paragraphs 444-446 and 451-453).
(ii) Burning and razing of Rohingya villages combined with the organized resettlement of
non-Rohingya communities in formerly Rohingya areas
413. The Court has established above that approximately 392 villages ⎯ representing
approximately 40 per cent of the 993 villages in the 3 townships of northern Rakhine State ⎯ were
partially or totally destroyed during and after the 2017 clearance operations (see paragraph 272).
Bulldozing and large-scale terrain clearance often followed the destruction of Rohingya villages; in
turn rapid construction took place on the cleared sites, including of BGP bases and other security
installations, new villages and houses, resettlement camps and other civilian infrastructure (see
paragraphs 277-279 above).
414. Myanmar does not dispute that the burning took place, but it either denies that it was
solely responsible for this or suggests that it cannot be known who burned the villages. The totality
of the evidence indicates, however, that it was primarily Myanmar’s security forces that burned the
Rohingya villages during the clearance operations (see paragraphs 276 above and 551 below).
415. The burning and razing of the villages are relevant to the question of genocidal intent.
First, the fact that it was only Rohingya villages that were destroyed supports the inference that the
Rohingya were selectively targeted by Myanmar. Second, as the ICTY emphasized in Blagojević

- 127 and Jokić, “burning the houses of some of the people” “sen[t] the clear message that they had nothing
to return to” (IT-02-60-T, Trial Chamber I, Section A, Judgement of 17 January 2005, para. 675).
The intention to purge Rohingya from northern Rakhine State is also evidenced by Myanmar’s
co-ordinated policy of construction of new infrastructure and resettlement of non-Rohingya groups
in formerly Rohingya areas (IIMM, “The destruction and dispossession of Rohingya land and
property during the 2017 clearance operations — public summary” (29 September 2025), para. 61).
(c) Perpetration of acts that violate the foundations of the Rohingya group
416. The perpetration of acts that violate the foundations of the group has been considered
relevant to inferring genocidal intent (Muvunyi, ICTR-00-55A-T, Trial Chamber III, Judgement of
11 February 2010, para. 29; see also Kalimanzira, ICTR-05-88-T, Trial Chamber III, Judgement of
22 June 2009, para. 731). This factor includes the examination of matters relating to “the nature,
extent and degree of the injuries caused” to the protected population — also considered by the Court
in the 2015 Croatia Judgment (I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 121, para. 413). Under this category, the
Court identifies two attributes of the conduct of Myanmar’s security forces that warrant particular
emphasis: (i) the extent and manner of the sexual violence committed against the Rohingya; and
(ii) the targeting of children.
(i) Sexual violence
417. Sexual violence has, in some cases, been associated with genocidal intent, including in
the ICTR’s Akayesu judgment (ICTR-96-4-T, Trial Chamber I, Judgement of 2 September 1998).
The particular features of sexual violence against Tutsi women in that case included mutilation,
women being raped several times, gang and mass rape, rape and sexual violence accompanied by
ethnic hate speech, sexual violence in public places and in front of other members of the community,
and sexual violence accompanied by threats to kill (ibid., paras. 731-733). As the Trial Chamber
observed in that case, sexual violence not only physically and mentally harms the victims, it can also
be a step in the process of degrading and destroying a human group by causing the “destruction of
the spirit, of the will to live, and of life itself” (ibid., para. 732).
418. The Court has discussed the nature, extent and degree of the sexual violence that was
committed against the Rohingya both as part of the established facts (see paragraphs 254-258 and
260 above) and in the context of its findings on actus reus (see paragraphs 326, 329, 350 and 370
above). It has observed that rape and other forms of sexual violence contributed to lasting mental
harm suffered by the survivors, as well as by family members who witnessed such acts (see
paragraphs 331 and 335 above). While the actus reus and the mens rea elements of genocide are
analytically distinct, they are nevertheless linked. As the Court has stated, “[t]he determination of
actus reus can require an inquiry into intent. In addition, the characterization of the acts and their
mutual relationship can contribute to an inference of intent” (2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports
2015 (I), p. 62, para. 130; see also Tolimir, IT-05-88/2-A, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 8 April
2015, para. 253).

- 128 419. The Gambia argues that the sexual violence in this case is comparable to that in Akayesu,
pointing to the following common features: the raping of women while killing their children; rape
accompanied by threats to kill; public and humiliating sexual violence designed to degrade the group
as a whole; deliberate targeting of women and girls of reproductive age, including pregnant women;
and scarring, such as distinctive bite marks that “brand” the survivors and rupture social bonds.
420. An estimated 1,834 individuals were raped during the clearance operations in 2017 (see
paragraph 258 above). Mass or gang rapes occurred in at least ten village tracts across northern
Rakhine State (see paragraph 254 above), including in, inter alia, Min Gyi, Kyein Chaung,
Kyauk Pan Du, Nga Yant Chaung, Gu Dar Pyin and Chut Pyin (FFM, Report of the detailed findings
(2018), para. 921, fn. 2055). The Court has observed the pervasive and systemic character of the
sexual violence and that acts of sexual violence were often exacerbated by acts of physical brutality
(see paragraph 255 above). Incidents of mutilation, scarring or branding accompanied sexual
violence on some occasions (see paragraph 256 above). For these reasons, the Court considers that
Myanmar’s security forces committed sexual violence against members of the Rohingya population
in a way that degraded the victims and their communities to an extreme degree.
(ii) Targeting of children
421. The Court has recognized that the forcible transfer of children within the meaning of
Article II (e) of the Genocide Convention can have “consequences for the group’s capacity to renew
itself, and hence to ensure its long-term survival” (2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I),
p. 63, para. 136). While the Applicant does not assert that there was a breach of Article II (e) in this
case, similar considerations apply to the targeting of children as a potential factor supporting the
inference of genocidal intent. As children are vital to the continued existence of any human group,
acts targeting them will directly affect the resilience and ultimate survival of the group. Moreover,
surviving children are likely to be gravely affected by their experience of being targeted, impairing
their development and capacity to contribute to the group’s survival and prosperity in the future.
422. There are credible estimates that at least 730 and up to 2,109 Rohingya children under
the age of five died as a result of violence during the clearance operations (see paragraph 259 above).
The killing of children was often carried out with extreme cruelty. Children were also the victims of
sexual violence (see paragraph 260 above). The Court considers that it is difficult to explain the
targeting of children on any basis other than their membership in the protected group.
(d) Systematic nature and scale of the relevant conduct
(i) Systematic nature of the relevant conduct
423. The Court considered the systematic nature of the relevant conduct an important factor
in its 2015 Croatia Judgment (I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 121, para. 413). International criminal
tribunals have also characterized the “systematic targeting” of the protected group as relevant to
inferring genocidal intent (Gacumbitsi, ICTR-01-64-A, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 7 July
2006, para. 41). Systematic actions involve planning and organization in order to direct operations

- 129 at a particular group and geographic area. They therefore suggest deliberation and purpose and, as
such, may allow inferences of genocidal intent to be drawn, also taking into consideration other
elements of the evidentiary record.
424. The Gambia alleges that systematic elements indicative of genocidal intent emerged
across multiple locations during the clearance operations. Relying on its expert’s oral testimony,
The Gambia argues that “the various elements of the genocidal modus operandi” were present in at
least 54 Rohingya villages. The Gambia’s expert drew on the FFM’s reports to describe a “consistent
pattern of attacks” during the 2017 clearance operations, involving a number of elements, including:
the encirclement of Rohingya villages; surprise attacks; attacks from multiple angles; lack of efforts
to conduct a cordon and search operation; indiscriminate shooting at civilians; the segregation of
men and boys; mass rape of women; executions “often [conducted] in a gruesome way”; shooting at
fleeing refugees; burning houses or areas; and bulldozing houses and villages. According to the
expert, this modus operandi was “indicative of an intentional, co-ordinated series of attacks directed
against [the Rohingya] population”. The Gambia’s expert further described the Tatmadaw’s modus
operandi during the 2017 clearance operations as “systematic”, asserting that the actions were
“preplanned both at a macro and a micro level”. In the expert’s view, based on his assessment of the
FFM’s reports, it was “a military impossibility that that pattern of conduct consistently replicated
across so many places by so many different units could have been merely coincidence”.
425. Myanmar contends that a modus operandi of attacks does not itself establish a pattern of
conduct. It argues that, even accepting The Gambia’s evidence, no modus operandi can be
established. Myanmar maintains that the assertion of The Gambia’s expert that the “entire pattern
replicates itself in over 54 documented instances” is incorrect. In particular, Myanmar argues that,
even on the basis of the findings of the FFM reports, there is no single location where all the elements
that were identified by The Gambia’s expert were present: most of the elements were allegedly
present in one location, the village of Min Gyi, but the “large majority” of locations “only ha[d] one
or two elements allegedly present”.
426. The evidence before the Court establishes that many of the elements of the modus
operandi identified by The Gambia occurred in a large number of locations. There were incidents in
numerous places of indiscriminate shooting and of Myanmar security forces shooting at fleeing
Rohingya (see paragraphs 251 and 264 above). Widespread mass or gang rape of Rohingya occurred
in many locations in northern Rakhine State (see paragraph 254 above). The record also establishes
the burning of all or parts of 392 villages, most of which took place during the clearance operations,
and the subsequent large-scale bulldozing of 78 villages (see paragraphs 271-282 above). For actions
to be considered as systematic, it is not necessary for every identified element of a given conduct to
be exhibited at each specific location. In its 2015 Croatia Judgment, the Court spoke of a “generally
similar modus operandi” (I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 122, para. 416). The Court thus considers that
the conduct of Myanmar’s security forces during the clearance operations can be considered as
systematic.

- 130 (ii) Scale of the relevant conduct
427. The Court identified the scale of the attacks as a relevant factor in its assessment of the
existence of genocidal intent in the 2015 Croatia Judgment (I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 121,
para. 413). In that case, the Court considered the scale of the destruction as follows:
“Croatia put forward a figure of 12,500 Croat deaths, which is contested by
Serbia. The Court notes that, even assuming that this figure is correct ⎯ an issue on
which it will make no ruling ⎯ the number of victims alleged by Croatia is small in
relation to the size of the targeted part of the group.” (Ibid., p. 127, para. 437.)
The “relative proportionate scale” of the actual or attempted destruction of the group has also been
considered as “strong evidence to prove the necessary intent to destroy a group in whole or in part”
by international criminal tribunals (Kayishema and Ruzindana, ICTR-95-1-T, Trial Chamber II,
Judgement of 21 May 1999, para. 93; see also Simba, ICTR-01-76-T, Trial Chamber I, Judgement
of 13 December 2005, para. 413).
428. Accordingly, the apparently small scale of the Croatian deaths contributed to the Court’s
finding in the 2015 Croatia Judgment that there was no specific intent to destroy the protected group
(I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 127, para. 437, and p. 128, para. 440). In this case Myanmar maintains
that the relative proportion of the number of victims excludes any genocidal intent on its part.
Myanmar argues that, even if the Court accepts The Gambia’s allegation of 10,000 deaths, this
number is “small in relation to the size of the targeted part of the group”. Myanmar compares the
number of alleged fatalities to the population of approximately 1.4 million Rohingya in Myanmar at
the time. It points out that 10,000 fatalities would constitute 0.7 per cent of the total Rohingya
population, which is, according to Myanmar, slightly lower than the corresponding percentage in the
Croatia case.
429. The Court observes that, while a high relative number of deaths may well be an important
factor from which to infer genocidal intent, a comparatively lower percentage of deaths does not, by
itself, necessarily preclude a finding of genocidal intent. Furthermore, the relevant number to be used
as a basis of comparison is the size of the targeted group. As the Court has found above, the group
that was targeted in this case was the Rohingya population of northern Rakhine State, numbering
approximately 815,000 (see paragraph 379). Thus, the estimated 10,000 Rohingya that were killed
(see paragraph 253 above) constituted 1.2 per cent of the targeted population.
430. Moreover, killing is not the only material act that is prohibited under the Convention. As
the joint intervening States emphasize, focusing on deaths alone can “obscure how killings and other
underlying acts can be waged together in a coordinated strategy aimed at destroying a protected
group”. The victims to be taken into account as part of an assessment of the scale of the destruction
include not only those who were killed, but also all those who were subjected to any of the acts listed
in Article II of the Genocide Convention (Kayishema and Ruzindana, ICTR-95-1-T, Trial
Chamber II, Judgement of 21 May 1999, para. 93). Therefore, the Court must also take account of
the victims of Myanmar’s acts under Article II (b) and (d) of the Genocide Convention, which
increases the relative proportionate scale of the destruction of the protected group (see
paragraphs 329, 335-336 and 370 above).

- 131 431. The Court now turns to Myanmar’s argument that its “security forces had, and did not
avail themselves of, opportunities to destroy the Bengali population”. Myanmar maintains that the
following facts are inconsistent with an intention to destroy the Rohingya population in northern
Rakhine State as such: hundreds of thousands of members of this population are still living in towns
and villages in Rakhine State; hundreds of thousands of members of this population are living in
camps in Bangladesh; and some 120,000 Rohingya already living in IDP camps were unharmed.
According to Myanmar, its security forces “time and time again eschewed numerous opportunities
for . . . destruction while many [Rohingya] made their way to Bangladesh in huge numbers”.
432. The Gambia responds that Myanmar’s alleged restraint is irrelevant, since Rohingya
living in the camps were not part of the group that was targeted during the clearance operations.
The Gambia further adds that Myanmar is wrong to suggest that “specific examples of perpetrators
refraining from killing members of the group” are sufficient to preclude an inference that Myanmar
acted with genocidal intent.
433. The Court has previously considered the relevance of the opportunity available to the
alleged perpetrators of genocide to the possibility of inferring genocidal intent (2015 Croatia
Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), pp. 126-127, paras. 431-437). The IRMCT has also suggested that
the fact that “more members of a targeted group could have been, for example, killed, but were not,
may indicate a lack of the dolus specialis required to prove such intent” (Karadžić, MICT-13-55-A,
Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 20 March 2019, para. 727; see also Stakić, IT-97-24-A, Appeals
Chamber, Judgement of 22 March 2006, para. 42).
434. In the Court’s view, a reasonable inference of genocidal intent does not depend on
evidence that the perpetrators made every effort to maximize the level of harm that they caused to
the protected group. In Krstić, the ICTY Appeals Chamber recognized that in cases where such
destruction is incomplete, “this ineffectiveness alone does not preclude a finding of genocidal intent”.
The Appeals Chamber also observed that those committing genocide are often “[c]onstrained by the
circumstances” and thus adopt a “method which would allow them to implement the genocidal design
while minimizing the risk of retribution” (IT-98-33-A, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 19 April
2004, para. 32). The Krstić Appeals Chamber considered the issue of the Serbs’ sparing of Bosnian
Muslim women and children in Srebrenica in these terms:
“The decision not to kill the women or children may be explained by the Bosnian
Serbs’ sensitivity to public opinion. In contrast to the killing of the captured military
men, such an action could not easily be kept secret, or disguised as a military operation,
and so carried an increased risk of attracting international censure.” (Ibid., para. 31.)
435. In the context of this case, the Court observes that the claimed purpose of Myanmar’s
actions was to undertake counter-insurgency operations in response to ARSA attacks, which imposed
geographic and other constraints on the opportunities that were available to Myanmar. In particular,
Myanmar does not claim that ARSA attacks occurred in the IDP camps and therefore, by its own
account, it had no basis to carry out counter-insurgency operations there. The Court notes that the
part of the Rohingya population living in IDP camps is not included in the targeted part of the group

- 132 for establishing genocidal intent; therefore, Myanmar’s opportunity to target them does not bear upon
the Court’s analysis. In any event, the Court observes that the total set of victims, and not just the
victims of intentional killings, must be considered for the purposes of the assessment of the
opportunity available to the perpetrator (see paragraph 430 above). In this case, the Court finds that
the fact that Myanmar did not take every opportunity it had to destroy members of the Rohingya
group does not preclude an inference of genocidal intent.

*

436. The Court’s examination of the above factors establishes that the context surrounding the
2017 clearance operations, including decades of discriminatory legislation and a military-sponsored
hate speech campaign against the Rohingya, created an atmosphere that was conducive to the
violence perpetrated during those clearance operations by Myanmar’s security forces and formed the
basis of Myanmar’s intention to destroy part of the Rohingya group. The manner in which the forcible
displacement of the Rohingya was conducted, together with the selective targeting of the Rohingya
population for violence and the burning of their homes, which sent a clear message that they had
nothing to return to, supports the inference of an intention to destroy the group. The evidence of
brutal acts of sexual violence and the targeting of children for violence in Rohingya communities
also supports an inference that there was a desire to destroy the foundations of the group. The
systematic nature of these actions further indicates a reasonable inference that Myanmar acted with
genocidal intent.
437. The Court recalls that, when drawing inferences concerning the existence of genocidal
intent, it is necessary to assess “all of the evidence, taken together” (Tolimir, IT-05-88/2-T, Trial
Chamber II, Judgement of 12 December 2012, para. 772; Tolimir, IT-05-88/2-A, Appeals Chamber,
Judgement of 8 April 2015, para. 247). The Court concludes that all the evidence before it, taken
together, supports a reasonable inference that Myanmar’s security forces acted with genocidal intent.
4. “Only reasonable inference” test
438. The Court may only establish genocidal intent by inference from circumstantial evidence
