if it is the only reasonable inference that can be drawn from the evidence (2007 Bosnia Judgment,
I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), pp. 196-197, para. 373; 2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I),
p. 67, para. 148). The Court must now consider whether Myanmar’s conduct can be explained by
other, alternative, reasonable inferences.
439. The Gambia understands this test to mean that other “possible or plausible” inferences
will only prevent a finding of genocidal intent if those inferences are reasonable. For The Gambia,
intent to destroy the group may be the “only reasonable inference” even if the evidence demonstrates
that there was forcible displacement, provided that the evidence also demonstrates that forcible
displacement was carried out in circumstances calculated to physically destroy the group. Similarly,
The Gambia argues that the Court may still find genocidal intent to be the only reasonable inference
if genocide is an interim goal alongside ultimate military objectives.

- 133 440. Myanmar contends that genocidal intent must be the only inference that can reasonably
be drawn from the acts in question. Myanmar characterizes The Gambia’s approach as potentially
turning the “only reasonable inference” test into a “one possible inference” test. Myanmar argues
that, if any other intent may be reasonably inferred from the acts in question, then genocidal intent
cannot be the “only reasonable inference”. It recognizes that genocide can be committed in the
context of counter‑terrorism operations, as long as genocidal intent is the only inference that could
reasonably be drawn from the evidence as a whole.
441. Intervening States emphasize different aspects of the “only reasonable inference” test.
Ireland, for example, contends that the fact
“[t]hat the pattern of conduct could only point to the existence of such intent is not to
say that it could point to such intent only . . . [I]t is perfectly possible that a pattern of
conduct, upon examination, could point to two separate intentions, only one of which is
genocidal . . . The co-existence of two or more intents does not rule out, exclude or
displace either intent.” (Emphasis in original.)
Slovenia, for its part, argues that the object and purpose of the Convention would be undermined if
the Court had to eliminate “every other conceivable inference”, while the joint intervening States and
the DRC highlight that the test applies only between alternative explanations that have been found
to be reasonably supported by the evidence.

*

*

442. The Court has explained that, “in order to infer the existence of dolus specialis from a
pattern of conduct, it is necessary and sufficient that this is the only inference that could reasonably
be drawn from the acts in question” (2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 67,
para. 148). The “only reasonable inference” standard does not derive from the Genocide Convention
itself but is a general test under international criminal law, applying whenever inferences are drawn
from circumstantial evidence (Tolimir, IT-05-88/2-T, Trial Chamber II, Judgement of 12 December
2012, para. 34; Stakić, IT-97-24-A, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 22 March 2006, para. 219; see
also Corfu Channel (United Kingdom v. Albania), Merits, Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 1949, p. 18).
443. In this regard, both the Court and international criminal tribunals have recognized that
genocidal intent may exist alongside other intentions, provided that such intentions are not
incompatible with the existence of genocidal intent (see 2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports
2007 (I), p. 123, para. 190). The only reasonable inference standard means that the inference drawn
should be capable of explaining all of the evidence, taken together (see Tolimir, IT-05-88/2-T, Trial
Chamber II, Judgement of 12 December 2012, para. 745). When some elements of the evidentiary
record may allow additional intentions to be inferred, but the totality of the evidence can only be
explained by the perpetrator’s genocidal intent, the only reasonable inference standard is met.

- 134 5. Whether other inferences explain Myanmar’s conduct
444. Myanmar argues that an inference of genocidal intent cannot reasonably be drawn from
the evidence. It further maintains that it is not required to prove that any other intent could be
reasonably inferred. Nevertheless, Myanmar claims that its actions during the clearance operations
qualified as “counter-terrorism” measures “undertaken in order to counter violent terrorist attacks by
ARSA and its supporters”. It contends that this intention constitutes an alternative reasonable
inference that precludes a finding of genocidal intent.
445. According to Myanmar, the clearance operations were temporally and geographically
limited to those Rohingya villages where ARSA was present. Myanmar recognizes that if the civilian
population of Rakhine State had been subjected to the treatment described in the FFM reports,
“Myanmar’s forces acted in a manner not compliant with [counter-insurgency] doctrine”. However,
in Myanmar’s view, this is not enough to establish a genocidal intent.
446. The Gambia contends that the totality of the evidence precludes a reasonable inference
that Myanmar’s actions were intended as counter-terrorism operations. It identifies actions that were
inconsistent with counter-terrorism, such as the indiscriminate shooting of civilians; instances of
extreme brutality, including setting fire to people’s homes while people were still inside them; rape,
gang rape and sexual mutilation; attacks on young children; the razing of villages; and “egregious[]”
violations of Myanmar’s rules of engagement. Moreover, The Gambia argues that the clearance
operations specifically targeted the Rohingya and their homes and not the ethnic Rakhine, despite
the co-existing significant terrorist threat from other alleged terrorist organizations, such as the ethnic
Rakhine Arakan Army (see paragraph 170 above).
447. Myanmar alludes to other possible reasonable inferences that it maintains could be drawn
from the evidence before the Court, most prominently that of forcible displacement or “ethnic
cleansing”. While Myanmar denies that it intended to commit ethnic cleansing, it suggests that the
Court could nevertheless find that this intention was a reasonable inference from the evidence, such
that genocidal intent would not be the only reasonable inference. As to evidence allowing the
alternative inference of an intention to forcibly displace the Rohingya, Myanmar argues, inter alia,
that The Gambia’s witnesses themselves stated that Myanmar’s aim was to “force [them] out of the
country”.
448. The Gambia, for its part, argues that the forcible displacement of the Rohingya group was
carried out in circumstances that support an inference of genocidal intent. The Gambia contends that
there is a tension between Myanmar’s argument that the movement of the Rohingya “was not the
product of deliberate policy or intent to systematically displace the population” and its argument that
the Court could nevertheless reasonably infer that Myanmar acted with an intent to forcibly displace
the Rohingya population. The Gambia emphasizes that the fact that the security forces were shooting
at fleeing refugees and killing children further precludes alternative inferences such as forcible
displacement.
449. Myanmar also suggests, invoking the Court’s finding in the 2015 Croatia Judgment, that
a further possible alternative reasonable inference to be drawn from the evidence is an intention to

- 135 punish the civilian population. Myanmar notes that The Gambia’s expert concluded in his written
reports that Myanmar’s actions “warrant the inference that the government’s objective is not to defeat
an insurgency, but to punish or destroy the civilian population in whole or in part”.

*

*

450. The Court recalls that The Gambia bears the burden of establishing the alleged breaches
of the Genocide Convention. This entails establishing that genocidal intent is the only reasonable
inference that can be drawn from the evidence. While Myanmar carries the burden of establishing
the facts upon which it relies in support of its own contentions, it is not required to establish, to a
fully conclusive standard, the existence of an alternative reasonable inference. Whether genocidal
intent is the only reasonable inference to be drawn from the evidence is ultimately a matter for the
Court’s assessment on the basis of the evidence before it. In making that determination, the Court
will consider the Parties’ competing interpretations of the evidence, addressing possible alternative
inferences.
451. The Court turns first to Myanmar’s claim that the intent behind its actions was to carry
out counter-insurgency operations. The Court observes at the outset that Myanmar’s assessment of
ARSA as a terrorist threat does not preclude the possibility that Myanmar acted with genocidal intent
(cf. Stakić, IT-97-24-A, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 22 March 2006, para. 226), a point that
Myanmar acknowledges (see paragraph 297 above). The Court notes that the ICOE, established by
Myanmar, defined clearance operations as “operations against insurgents or terrorists who have
intruded into and attacked a designated area”, and as “clear[ing] the area of weapons, mines, and
terrorists with a view to restoring peace and stability in the affected areas” (Republic of the Union of
Myanmar, ICOE, “Executive Summary” (21 January 2020), p. 4).
452. The specific locations examined by the Court where Myanmar conducted clearance
operations all correspond to areas where the evidence establishes that ARSA committed attacks, or
where Myanmar contends that such attacks occurred (see paragraphs 181-183, 187, 202, 214-216,
226, 230, 234, 240, 242 and 245 above). Thus, there is a geographical and temporal correlation
between ARSA attacks and the clearance operations that followed.
453. Many of the characteristics of the clearance operations, however, are inconsistent with an
intent to carry out counter-insurgency operations. For example, there is no evidence before the Court
that, in the conduct of the clearance operations, Myanmar distinguished between civilians and alleged
ARSA fighters. Myanmar’s military operation reports and Ministry of Defence records themselves
do not distinguish between alleged fighters and civilians. The fact that “attacks are said to have
caused casualties and damage far in excess of what was justified by military necessity” was
considered relevant by the Court in the 2015 Croatia Judgment in examining the question of
genocidal intent (I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 121, para. 413). In the context of the present case, the
Court views this factor as weighing against the inference that Myanmar’s intent was to carry out

- 136 counter-insurgency operations (see paragraph 304 above). Further, the execution of Rohingya men
and boys in some locations, the widespread sexual violence, including gang and mass rape, the
targeting of children and babies, the methodical burning of villages and attacks on fleeing civilians
are not consistent with an intent to conduct counter-insurgency operations. On this basis, the Court
concludes that the intention of conducting genuine counter-insurgency operations does not constitute
an alternative reasonable inference.
454. The Court now turns to Myanmar’s suggestion that it is reasonable to infer from the
evidence that Myanmar’s intent in carrying out the clearance operations was the forcible
displacement of the Rohingya. The Court, in accordance with the jurisprudence of international
criminal tribunals, understands the forcible displacement of a group to mean displacement where the
members of a group did not have a genuine choice to remain in the territory where they were present
(Blagojević and Jokić, IT-02-60-T, Trial Chamber I, Section A, Judgement of 17 January 2005,
para. 596). The Court recalls that, in the 2015 Croatia Judgment, it held that genocidal intent could
not be inferred from the evidence in that case; rather, the acts committed were designed to force the
population to leave, not to destroy it systematically (I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), pp. 126-127, para. 435).
455. The Court and international criminal tribunals have observed that forcible displacement
and genocide may occur in parallel. In the 2007 Bosnia Judgment, the Court found that the killing of
military-age men together with the forcible transfer of thousands of Bosnian Muslim women,
children and the elderly constituted genocide in Srebrenica (I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 165,
para. 294). As the ICTY explained in Blagojević and Jokić:
“The Trial Chamber has no doubt that all these acts constituted a single operation
executed with the intent to destroy the Bosnian Muslim population of Srebrenica. The
Trial Chamber finds that the Bosnian Serb forces not only knew that the combination of
the killings of the men with the forcible transfer of the women, children and elderly,
would inevitably result in the physical disappearance of the Bosnian Muslim population
of Srebrenica, but clearly intended through these acts to physically destroy this group.”
(IT-02-60-T, Trial Chamber I, Section A, Judgement of 17 January 2005, para. 677; see
also Stakić, IT-97-24-T, Trial Chamber II, Judgement of 31 July 2003, para. 557.)
456. The intent to commit forcible displacement and the intent to commit genocide may thus
co-exist. The Court has observed above that the only reasonable inference standard is met even if
some elements of the evidentiary record allow additional intentions to be inferred, as long as the
totality of the evidence can be explained only by the perpetrator’s genocidal intent (see
paragraph 443). While, as Myanmar contends, a State accused of genocide is not required to
incriminate itself by pleading other criminal acts, the question that the Court has to resolve is whether,
on the totality of the evidence, Myanmar’s actions can be reasonably explained by inferring that it
acted with an intention to forcibly displace the Rohingya population.
457. In its prior application of the Genocide Convention, the Court has at times treated
evidence of an intent to displace the population as supporting a conclusion that genocidal intent had

- 137 not been established. In the 2007 Bosnia Judgment, the Court emphasized that the respondent’s aim
of creating a larger Serb State “did not necessarily require the destruction of the Bosnian Muslims
and other communities, but [rather] their expulsion”, and that this aim was “capable of being
achieved by the displacement of the population and by territory being acquired” (I.C.J. Reports
2007 (I), p. 196, para. 372). In the 2015 Croatia Judgment, the Court found that there was evidence
of an intent to forcibly displace the Croat population but not evidence of an intent to commit genocide
(see I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), pp. 126-127, paras. 435-436, and pp. 148-150, paras. 504-507).
458. The Court observes that both earlier cases occurred in the context of a policy of ethnic
cleansing (see 2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), pp. 122-123, para. 190; 2015 Croatia
Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), pp. 126-127, para. 435), and evidence before the Court established
an affirmative practice of transfer and evacuation of the relevant population (see, for example,
2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 84, para. 223; p. 121, para. 414; and p. 127,
para. 436; 2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), pp. 181-182, para. 334). Similarly, the
Trial Chamber in Stakić concluded that the defendant’s aim in that case was the deportation of the
group, but not its physical destruction. In making that assessment, the ICTY took into account the
defendant’s organization of transport to transfer the population, as well as his contemporaneous
expression of a willingness for part of the population to return after the war (IT-97-24-T, Trial
Chamber II, Judgement of 31 July 2003, paras. 403 and 553-557).
459. By contrast, in the present case, there is no evidence in the record of any effort or plan by
the Myanmar Government to transfer or evacuate the Rohingya population, and Myanmar’s actions
also do not indicate such an intention. Indeed, in response to a question by a judge about the
protection of the civilian population, Myanmar referred predominantly to examples of its military
protecting ethnic Rakhine and Hindu civilians from ARSA and made no mention of offering
protection to the Rohingya. While there is evidence that a number of fleeing Rohingya were allowed
to pass into Bangladesh unharmed, the evidentiary record also contains many instances of
Myanmar’s security forces attacking and killing civilians, including children, both when they were
fleeing from their villages during the clearance operations (see paragraph 264 above) and when they
were travelling to Bangladesh (see paragraphs 266-268 above). In some instances, security forces
ordered the Rohingya to remain in Myanmar or to return to their point of origin, despite their villages
having been destroyed by burning (see paragraph 269 above).
460. The record indicates that Myanmar security forces attacked groups of fleeing Rohingya,
including by firing on them and sinking or overturning boats they were in (see paragraphs 266-268
above). Myanmar had also planted landmines along certain roads and at some crossing points on the
border with Bangladesh (see paragraph 267 above). Moreover, there is convincing evidence before
the Court that security forces raped, tortured and killed women as they were fleeing to Bangladesh
(see paragraphs 266-268 above). Médecins sans Frontières estimated that 13.4 per cent of violent
deaths among the Rohingya occurred during the period between displacement from their village and
arrival in Bangladesh (see paragraph 268 above). Security forces obstructed river crossings, and
many Rohingya took arduous routes in order to avoid encountering security forces (see
paragraphs 265 and 267 above).

- 138 461. The testimony on which Myanmar relies (see paragraph 447 above) does not support
forcible displacement as an alternative reasonable inference either. While the statements of
Witnesses MN and NJ that the security forces sought to force the Rohingya out of the country record
what those witnesses understood at the time, the Court’s assessment of alternative reasonable
inferences of intent must be based on Myanmar’s conduct as a whole. The same witnesses’ accounts
of what members of Myanmar’s security forces did to them and to their families — the execution of
Witness MN’s father and eight-year-old nephew at Maung Nu (see paragraph 220 above); the killing
of Witness NJ’s child, her own rape, and the disappearance of her husband and father-in-law at
Chut Pyin (see paragraphs 205-206 and 208 above) — are inconsistent with an intent to forcibly
displace.
462. The Court thus considers that an intent to forcibly displace the population does not explain
the totality of the evidence before the Court. A number of aspects of the clearance operations,
including the brutal manner in which they were conducted, attacks by the security forces on the
Rohingya as they fled to Bangladesh and the widespread attacks on young children, are not consistent
with an intent to forcibly displace the population. For this reason, in the Court’s view, an intent to
forcibly displace the population does not constitute an alternative reasonable inference.
463. The Court finally turns to Myanmar’s suggestion that an intention to punish the Rohingya
population could constitute an alternative reasonable inference in this case (see paragraph 449
above). While Myanmar does not elaborate on what it means by an intent to punish or offer an
interpretation of the evidence to support its contention, the question of whether punishment
constitutes an alternative reasonable inference is “ultimately a matter for the Court’s assessment”
(see paragraph 450 above). The Court considers, in this regard, that Myanmar is suggesting that its
security forces might have intended to punish the Rohingya civilian population in retaliation for the
activities of ARSA.
464. The Court recalls that genocidal intent may co-exist with other intentions, as long as such
intentions are not incompatible with the existence of genocidal intent (see paragraph 443 above). In
the Court’s view, genocidal intent is likely to be accompanied by an intent to punish, particularly in
the context of collective punishment. Genocidal and punitive intentions may often be entwined in
the sense that the destruction of a protected group is an extreme form of punishment, and the two
intentions may not always be capable of meaningful separation for the purpose of drawing alternative
inferences.
465. Myanmar relies on the Court’s finding in the 2015 Croatia Judgment that Serbia’s attack
on the civilian population during the siege of Vukovar and its ill-treatment of Croatian prisoners in
the Ovčara camp following the fall of Vukovar were intended to punish rather than to destroy the
group (I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), pp. 125-126, paras. 429-430). The Court observes that this finding
was based on a substantially distinct set of facts from those in the present case. In that case, the Court
relied upon the prior determinations of the ICTY in the Mrkšić et al. case to conclude that the purpose
of the attack on the city of Vukovar was to punish Croats in that location for Croatia’s declaration of
independence (ibid., p. 125, para. 429). Moreover, the individual victims of mistreatment in that case
were prisoners of war, who were “specifically identified and selected” by the Serb forces for
punishment on the basis that “they were known, or were believed, to have been active in the Croatian
forces in Vukovar, and thus in Serb eyes were war crimes suspects” (Mrkšić et al., IT-95-13/1-T,
Trial Chamber II, Judgement of 27 September 2007, para. 480). In that context, the ICTY concluded

- 139 that the mistreatment of the prisoners of war in Vukovar reflected “hatred and the desire to punish
the enemy forces” (2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), p. 125, para. 430, quoting
Mrkšić et al., IT-95-13/1-T, Trial Chamber II, Judgement of 27 September 2007, para. 535).
466. The Court considers that, in the present case, the scale and nature of Myanmar’s actions
and the methods it employed went well beyond what would be required to punish the population and
are instead characteristic of an intent to destroy a substantial part of the group. Features of such intent
include the large numbers of persons killed, injured or displaced and the widespread brutal attacks
on women and children. In light of the reasons set out above, the Court finds that punishment of the
Rohingya population does not constitute an alternative reasonable inference on the evidence before
it.
467. Accordingly, the Court cannot conclude, from all the evidence taken together, that an
intent to carry out counter-insurgency operations, an intent to forcibly displace or an intent to punish
the Rohingya population are reasonable inferences to be drawn. In the Court’s view, the only
reasonable inference that can explain Myanmar’s actions is an intention to destroy a substantial part
of the protected group, namely the Rohingya population in northern Rakhine State.

*

468. For the reasons above, the Court is fully convinced that Myanmar committed acts falling
under Article II (a), (b) and (d) of the Genocide Convention with an intent to destroy a substantial
part of the Rohingya population of Myanmar.
D. Conclusion
469. The Court finds that Myanmar breached its obligations under Article I and Article III (a)
of the Convention.
VI. RESPONSIBILITY FOR BREACHES OF OTHER OBLIGATIONS
UNDER THE GENOCIDE CONVENTION
470. In its final submissions, The Gambia requests the Court to find that Myanmar, in addition
to committing genocide, is responsible for four breaches of obligations arising under the Convention:
failure to prevent genocide, in violation of Article I; direct and public incitement of genocide, in
violation of Article III (c); failure to punish acts punishable under Article III, in violation of
Articles I, IV and VI; and failure to enact necessary legislation, in violation of Article V (see
paragraph 63 above). With respect to these alleged breaches, the Court recalls that claims of breach
of the obligations to prevent and to punish genocide require “proof at a high level of certainty
appropriate to the seriousness of the allegation” (see paragraph 80 above). The Court will address
each of the above claims in turn.

- 140 A. Obligation to prevent genocide
471. The Gambia alleges that Myanmar failed to prevent genocide pursuant to Article I of the
Convention. In paragraph (1) of its final submissions, The Gambia asks the Court to find that
Myanmar is responsible for genocide “perpetrated by its security forces and individuals acting under
their instruction, direction, or control” (see paragraph 63 above). In paragraph (2), however, with
respect to the obligation to prevent genocide, The Gambia requests the Court to find only that
Myanmar has failed to prevent acts of genocide “perpetrated by individuals alongside its security
forces” (ibid.).
472. In the view of the Court, while paragraph (1) of the final submissions refers to conduct
attributable to Myanmar, including that of “individuals acting under [its] instruction, direction, or
control”, the reference in paragraph (2) to acts perpetrated by “individuals alongside its security
forces” shows that the claim regarding the duty to prevent only concerns acts committed by persons
whose conduct is not attributable to Myanmar.

*

473. In support of its claim, The Gambia contends that ethnic Rakhine individuals and other
ethnic minorities assisted the Tatmadaw during the clearance operations. It argues that the Tatmadaw
recruited, armed and trained ethnic Rakhine to serve as police and in militias during those operations.
The Gambia further asserts that
“genocidal acts indisputably occurred in the immediate vicinity where the Tatmadaw
was deployed and Myanmar had . . . all the means necessary within its power to bring
to an immediate end any genocidal act perpetrated by individuals, assuming those acts
were not committed under its instructions, direction or effective control”.
474. Myanmar submits that since The Gambia has not established that genocide has been
committed, there can be no breach of the obligation to prevent genocide. With respect to the facts,
the Respondent acknowledges that ethnic Rakhine and other civilians participated in the clearance
operations. It confirms that following the 2016 events, government authorities publicly recruited
“non-Bengali citizens to a new ‘regional’ force”, though it denies having armed ethnic Rakhine
groups or militia “for any nefarious purpose”. The Respondent also attempts to attribute burnings
both during and after the clearance operations to persons, including ethnic Rakhine and other
civilians, not acting under its authority or control.
475. Among the interveners, the DRC contends that the duty to prevent applies to the entirety
of the obligations under Article III, and that a State may incur responsibility for breach of the
obligation to prevent irrespective of whether a violation of that Article has been established. The
DRC emphasizes that the duty to prevent is an obligation of conduct and of due diligence, with an
autonomous and distinct character from other obligations under the Convention. It further notes that

- 141 the Court has recognized that, because the obligation to prevent is an obligation of conduct, a State
may not evade responsibility for its breach by claiming that genocide would have been committed
regardless of whether or not it fulfilled the obligation to prevent (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J.
Reports 2007 (I), p. 221, para. 430). The DRC states that the obligation to prevent follows the same
logic as the prohibition on incitement to commit genocide, which is punishable even when genocide
has not been committed. Conditioning the breach of the obligation to prevent on the prior
establishment of a violation of Article III would likely lead to results that are manifestly absurd and
unreasonable, and be contrary to the object and purpose of the Convention.

*

*

476. The Court recalls that its interpretation of the scope of the obligation to prevent under
Article I of the Genocide Convention does not purport to establish jurisprudence applicable to other
international instruments setting forth an obligation to prevent certain acts (2007 Bosnia Judgment,
I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 220, para. 429).
477. The prevention of genocide, together with its punishment, is the stated object and purpose
of the Genocide Convention and constitutes the means by which States parties to the Convention
seek, according to its preamble, “to liberate mankind from [this] odious scourge”.
478. The Court recalls that the obligation to prevent genocide set out in Article I of the
Convention
“is one of conduct and not one of result, in the sense that a State cannot be under an
obligation to succeed, whatever the circumstances, in preventing the commission of
genocide: the obligation of States parties is rather to employ all means reasonably
available to them, so as to prevent genocide so far as possible. A State does not incur
responsibility simply because the desired result is not achieved; responsibility is
however incurred if the State manifestly failed to take all measures to prevent genocide
which were within its power, and which might have contributed to preventing the
genocide. In this area the notion of ‘due diligence’, which calls for an assessment
in concreto, is of critical importance.” (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I),
p. 221, para. 430.)
With respect to the circumstances giving rise to the obligation, the Court has explained that
“a State’s obligation to prevent, and the corresponding duty to act, arise at the instant
that the State learns of, or should normally have learned of, the existence of a serious
risk that genocide will be committed. From that moment onwards, if the State has
available to it means likely to have a deterrent effect on those suspected of preparing
genocide, or reasonably suspected of harbouring specific intent (dolus specialis), it is
under a duty to make such use of these means as the circumstances permit.” (Ibid.,
p. 222, para. 431.)
As a result, in the Court’s view,
“a State may be found to have violated its obligation to prevent even though it had no
certainty, at the time when it should have acted, but failed to do so, that genocide was

- 142 about to be committed or was under way; for it to incur responsibility on this basis it is
enough that the State was aware, or should normally have been aware, of the serious
danger that acts of genocide would be committed” (ibid., p. 223, para. 432).
479. The Court has recognized that the obligation to prevent under Article I applies to any act
punishable under Article III of the Convention (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I),
pp. 221-222, para. 431). The Court observes in this regard that the assessment of a “serious risk”
with respect to the obligation to prevent involves a determination on both the material and mental
elements of genocide, as well as any other elements of the relevant provisions of Article III. In
particular, absent a serious risk of the presence of genocidal intent, the obligation to prevent under
Article I of the Convention does not arise.
480. The Court has held that if a State itself is found responsible for genocide, then that State
has necessarily breached the obligation to prevent those same acts. As it explained, “logic dictates
that a State cannot have satisfied an obligation to prevent genocide in which it actively participated”
(2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 201, para. 382). Under such circumstances, there
is no need for the Court to make a separate determination that the obligation to prevent those same
acts pursuant to Article I of the Convention has been violated. The same reasoning applies to the
responsibility of a State for the other offences enumerated in Article III of the Convention, to which
the duty to prevent equally applies (ibid.).
481. In the present case, however, The Gambia contends that Myanmar failed to prevent
genocide committed by persons acting alongside its security forces and not under its instruction,
direction or control. This claim is not subsumed by the finding that Myanmar itself is responsible for
committing genocide.
482. Evidence before the Court establishes that non-Rohingya civilians entered villages with
the Myanmar security forces and participated alongside them in acts such as killings and burning
houses, including at the locations specifically examined by the Court (see paragraphs 188, 203, 207,
227, 231, 235 and 241 above; Affidavit of Witness NJ, para. 46; FFM, Report of the detailed findings
(2018), paras. 761, 777, 782, 797, 821, 822, 830, 837, 873, 874 and 920; PILPG, Factual findings &
legal analysis report, pp. 30-31; Fortify Rights, “‘They gave them long swords’: Preparations for
genocide and crimes against humanity against Rohingya Muslims in Rakhine State, Myanmar” (July
2018), pp. 55-57). Myanmar’s ICOE also found that “local vigilantes” participated in the violence
alongside the security forces during the clearance operations (Republic of the Union of Myanmar,
ICOE, “Executive Summary” (21 January 2020), pp. 4, 6 and 10; see paragraphs 126 above and 539
below).
483. The evidence does not clarify, however, whether such civilians were acting under
Myanmar’s command or control. The Court recalls that the conduct of non-State actors may be
attributable to a State for the purposes of international responsibility if they were acting in accordance
with a State’s instructions or under its “effective control” (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports
2007 (I), p. 208, para. 399, citing Military and Paramilitary Activities in and against Nicaragua

- 143 (Nicaragua v. United States of America), I.C.J. Reports 1986, p. 65, para. 115). Such effective
control must be exercised, or instructions given, “in respect of each operation in which the alleged
violations occurred, not generally in respect of the overall actions taken by the persons or groups of
persons having committed the violations” (ibid., p. 208, para. 400). Moreover, attribution to a State
of conduct by actors that are neither de jure nor de facto organs of that State cannot be presumed
(ibid., p. 215, paras. 414-415).
484. Based on the record before it, the Court cannot conclude that all acts committed by
non-Rohingya civilians during the clearance operations were taken in accordance with Myanmar’s
instructions or under its effective control, such that their conduct is attributable to the State. The
Court therefore considers that at least some of the above acts of civilians during the clearance
operations must be considered to be those of non-State actors whose conduct is not attributable to
the Respondent.
485. In light of the evidence regarding the nature of the conduct of non-Rohingya civilians
(see paragraph 482 above), the Court considers that the participation of such civilians in the clearance
operations alongside the Myanmar security forces posed a serious risk of genocide of which the State
should normally have been aware (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 223, para. 432).
Myanmar does not contend that it was unaware of the participation of non-Rohingya civilians or that
it lacked the capacity to take action to address it. The record contains no evidence, however, that
Myanmar authorities made any attempt to discourage non-Rohingya civilians from participating in
the attacks on the Rohingya or otherwise sought “to employ all means reasonably available to them
so as to prevent genocide so far as possible” (ibid., p. 221, para. 430). To the contrary, Myanmar
acknowledges that it recruited some non-Rohingya civilians (see paragraph 474 above). Moreover,
the Court has found that, prior to and during the clearance operations, Myanmar’s military broadly
spread hatred of the Rohingya through the Facebook military network (see paragraphs 164-167
above), and at least one video posted on that platform showed a soldier encouraging non-Rohingya
residents to join the fight (see paragraph 165 above). Under the circumstances, the Court is convinced
that Myanmar had means available to it that might have contributed to preventing acts likely to
constitute genocide by persons acting alongside its security forces, and failed to avail itself of them
(2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 221, para. 430, and p. 225, para. 438).
486. Given the limited evidence before it on this question, the Court is not in a position to
conclusively determine whether the non-Rohingya civilians participating in the clearance operations,
whose conduct is not attributable to the State, themselves acted with genocidal intent. This finding
does not, however, affect the conclusion that such civilians participated in the clearance operations
where acts of genocide were committed by Myanmar’s security forces and that the acts of civilians
posed a serious risk of genocide.
487. In this regard, the Court recalls that the instant that a State learns of, or should normally
have learned of, the existence of a serious risk that genocide will be committed, the due diligence
obligation to prevent genocide under Article I of the Convention arises (2007 Bosnia Judgment,
I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 222, para. 431). This duty is breached and the responsibility of a State is
engaged when a State should normally have been aware of a serious risk that genocide would be
committed and “manifestly failed to take all measures to prevent genocide which were within its

- 144 power” (ibid., p. 221, para. 430; see paragraph 478 above). As the Court has emphasized, the
obligation to prevent is not one of result; a State does not incur responsibility simply because its
efforts do not succeed (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 221, para. 430). On the
other hand, the obligation to prevent genocide does not allow a State that is in a position to act in the
face of a serious risk of genocide to remain idle and gamble that the risk might not ultimately
materialize. Like other obligations in the Convention with a preventive character, including the
prohibition of incitement to commit genocide (Article III (c)) (see paragraph 496 below), the
obligation to adopt legislation to give effect to the Convention (Article V) (see paragraphs 518 and
561 below) and the obligation to investigate acts of genocide under the duty to punish (see
paragraph 514 below), international responsibility for breach of the duty to prevent genocide is not
conditioned on a finding that genocide has occurred. Such an interpretation serves the protective
humanizing and civilizing purpose of the Convention (see Reservations to the Convention on the
Prevention and Punishment of the Crime of Genocide, Advisory Opinion, I.C.J. Reports 1951, p. 23).
488. In the present case, the Court concludes that Myanmar knew or should have known of a
serious risk of acts of genocide being committed by non-Rohingya civilians alongside its security
forces during the 2017 clearance operations, that it had the means to take action to prevent such acts
and that it manifestly refrained from using them. Myanmar thus violated its obligation to prevent
genocide pursuant to Article I of the Convention.
B. Direct and public incitement to commit genocide
489. The Court now turns to The Gambia’s claims that Myanmar has directly and publicly
incited genocide, in violation of Article III (c) of the Genocide Convention. In this regard,
The Gambia contends that “senior officers of the Tatmadaw and the civilian government openly and
explicitly exhorted the military and others in Rakhine State to commit genocide against the
Rohingya”.
490. As with the duty to prevent genocide, the Court has held that where a State has been
found to be responsible for genocide pursuant to Article III (a), it is unnecessary to determine
whether it may also have incurred responsibility for direct and public incitement to commit genocide
under Article III (c) with respect to those same acts (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I),
p. 200, para. 380). The responsibility of the State under Article III (c) for incitement of its own agents
or organs to commit genocide is established and absorbed by the finding that the State itself has
committed genocide (ibid.). Thus, the Court need not separately address The Gambia’s contention
that members of the Tatmadaw and the civilian government “exhorted the military” to commit
genocide.
491. The Court observes, however, that the prohibition of direct and public incitement to
commit genocide under Article III (c) is not limited to incitement, by the State, of genocide
committed by State agents or organs. The provision also prohibits incitement, by the State, of
genocide committed by private actors, namely persons and entities whose conduct is not attributable
to the State. Thus, the Court’s finding that Myanmar itself committed genocide, in violation of

- 145 Article III (a), does not address The Gambia’s contention that Myanmar may have incited private
actors to commit genocide. The Court accordingly will examine this question.

*

*

492. The Parties offer somewhat differing views regarding what constitutes direct and public
incitement to commit genocide. The Gambia maintains that a State is responsible for incitement if a
State organ speaks, publishes or displays in public words that encourage or provoke the commission
of genocide. It emphasizes that incitement may be implicit, and that the words must be considered in
their context and as they would be understood by their intended audience. The Gambia submits that
there is considerable overlap between hate speech and incitement, since incitement can be preceded
or accompanied by hate speech, and hate speech affects how statements are likely to be received by
the intended audience. The Applicant adds, however, that incitement always involves the additional
requirement that the speech is understood by its intended audience as a call to violence or action
against the targeted group.
493. With respect to the facts, The Gambia contends that Myanmar led a co-ordinated and
prolonged hate campaign against the Rohingya, indoctrinated soldiers and the people of Myanmar to
believe that the Rohingya posed an existential threat to the country, its racial purity and Buddhism
generally, and incited violence and genocide against them. It submits that the climate of hatred left
ethnic Rakhine civilians who joined the Tatmadaw’s genocidal efforts susceptible to incitement to
genocide. According to the Applicant, the examples of hate speech in the record would have been
understood in context by their intended audience as calls to genocidal violence. The Gambia also
maintains that Myanmar disseminated speech by private actors inciting genocide, particularly speech
by prominent Buddhist monks, which is either attributable to Myanmar or which Myanmar is
responsible for failing to suppress.

*

494. For Myanmar, in order to constitute direct and public incitement, speech must contain a
direct and unambiguous call to commit one of the acts referred to in Article II of the Convention, and
must be based on precise and incontrovertible evidence. It maintains that statements found to
constitute incitement by the ICTR explicitly advocated killing, often accompanied by a direct
expression of genocidal intent. The Respondent additionally submits that incitement requires proof
of a causal link between the incitement and the commission of acts of genocide.
495. Myanmar argues that messages referred to by The Gambia do not constitute direct and
public incitement to genocide within the meaning of Article III (c). For the Respondent, the examples
of speech presented by The Gambia cannot be regarded as advocacy or encouragement of hate, even
less a call to commit genocide. Nor has The Gambia established that the messages were delivered
with genocidal intent or, in many cases, even identified the author. The Applicant has thus failed to

- 146 furnish “precise and incontrovertible evidence” of such speech on the part of the authorities. The
Respondent further maintains that the statements of private actors, particularly certain Buddhist
monks, cannot be attributable to the State, regardless of whether they constituted incitement to
commit genocide.

*

*

496. The Court observes that the prohibition on incitement to commit genocide has a
preventative purpose (Nahimana et al., ICTR-99-52-A, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of
28 November 2007, para. 678, fn. 1614). It is well established that incitement can be committed
irrespective of whether genocide itself takes place (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I),
p. 119, para. 180; Akayesu, ICTR-96-4-T, Trial Chamber I, Judgement of 2 September 1998,
para. 562). Thus, “the crime . . . is completed as soon as the discourse in question is uttered”
(Nahimana et al., ICTR-99-52-A, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 28 November 2007, para. 723;
see also Nzabonimana, ICTR-98-44D-T, Trial Chamber III, Judgement and Sentence of 31 May
2012, para. 1752). Any other interpretation would deprive the prohibition of incitement to commit
genocide of its preventative character.
497. A State is responsible for direct and public incitement to commit genocide pursuant to
Article III (c) of the Genocide Convention when a person or organ whose conduct is attributable to
the State communicates in public messages that directly prompt, encourage or provoke the
commission of genocide (Akayesu, ICTR-96-4-T, Trial Chamber I, Judgement of 2 September 1998,
paras. 555-560). The mental element of incitement encompasses the intent to directly and publicly
incite other actors to commit genocide and presupposes a genocidal intent on the part of the speaker
(2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), pp. 128-129, para. 441, and p. 152, para. 517;
Akayesu, ICTR-96-4-T, Trial Chamber I, Judgement of 2 September 1998, para. 560; Nahimana et
al., ICTR-99-52-A, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 28 November 2007, para. 677).
498. The appeal must be public, in the sense that it is made available to an indeterminate group
of persons. It must also be direct, in that it directly calls for the commission of one or more material
acts with genocidal intent (Nahimana et al., ICTR-99-52-A, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of
28 November 2007, paras. 692-693). In other words, it should “specifically provoke another to
engage in” such acts (Akayesu, ICTR-96-4-T, Trial Chamber I, Judgement of 2 September 1998,
para. 557). As the ICTR Appeals Chamber has explained,
“there is a difference between hate speech in general (or inciting discrimination or
violence) and direct and public incitement to commit genocide. Direct incitement to
commit genocide assumes that the speech is a direct appeal to commit an act referred to
in Article 2 . . . [i.e. genocide]; it has to be more than a mere vague or indirect
suggestion.” (Nahimana et al., ICTR-99-52-A, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of
28 November 2007, para. 692.)
499. The Court considers that incitement to commit genocide under the Convention does not
require a call for killing or extermination. The speaker may seek to provoke the audience to commit
any material act set forth in Article II with genocidal intent against the protected group. Nevertheless,
the discourse must call on the audience to “take action” in this manner. Thus, a media article that

- 147 was “brimming with ethnic hatred but [that] did not call on readers to take action against the Tutsi
population” was not held to constitute direct incitement (Nahimana et al., ICTR-99-52-A, Appeals
Chamber, Judgement of 28 November 2007, para. 696, quoting Nahimana et al., ICTR-99-52-T,
Trial Chamber I, Judgement and Sentence of 3 December 2003, para. 1037).
500. Incitement may “be direct, and nonetheless implicit” (Akayesu, ICTR-96-4-T, Trial
Chamber I, Judgement of 2 September 1998, para. 557). Messages may be delivered through
euphemism or code. Interpretation of the message must take into account the particular context,
including the language and culture, the identity of the audience and the political and community
affiliations of the inciter (Kalimanzira, ICTR-05-88-T, Trial Chamber III, Judgement of 22 June
2009, para. 514; Nahimana et al., ICTR-99-52-A, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 28 November
2007, para. 698). The inquiry must consider how the speech would be understood by its intended
audience, and whether the audience would have immediately grasped its implication (Akayesu,
ICTR-96-4-T, Trial Chamber I, Judgement of 2 September 1998, para. 558; Nahimana et al.,
ICTR-99-52-A, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 28 November 2007, paras. 698 and 700). Thus, a
speech containing no explicit appeal to commit genocide, or which appears ambiguous, can still
constitute direct incitement; if, however, the message remains ambiguous when considered in
context, it cannot constitute incitement (Nahimana et al., ICTR-99-52-A, Appeals Chamber,
Judgement of 28 November 2007, paras. 701 and 703).
501. In considering the evidence before it, the Court has found that the clearance operations
were accompanied by speeches by Senior General Min Aung Hlaing fuelling hatred against the
Rohingya, as well as widespread hate speech on official sites of the Myanmar military and the
Facebook military network, among other sources (see paragraphs 155-168 above). For hate speech
to constitute incitement, however, it must involve a call to commit genocide when viewed in context.
The Gambia highlights in particular three pieces of evidence in this regard: speeches of
Senior General Hlaing, as well as the video of a soldier addressing non-Rohingya civilians and a
message calling on ethnic Rakhine to “sharpen” their knives, both posted on the Facebook military
network. The Court will consider this and other evidence in the record.
502. With respect to the speeches delivered by Senior General Hlaing in September 2017 (see
paragraphs 157-159 above), the Court recalls that these speeches were delivered in the midst or
immediate aftermath of widespread violence against the Rohingya; their purpose was to raise funds
for and honour the security forces conducting those operations; the speaker was the commander of
those same security forces and he spoke, inter alia, of their “brilliant efforts” and of “solving” the
“unfinished job” of the “Bengali problem”. In the Court’s view, these messages appear to have been
directed primarily at the Myanmar security forces and other State agents and organs, rather than
constituting an effort to directly incite genocide by civilians. Any question of incitement to commit
genocide by State actors or organs themselves is absorbed by the Court’s finding of genocide, as
already noted (see paragraph 490). The Court accordingly considers that the speeches do not
constitute direct calls to private actors to engage in genocide.
503. The Court has found that the Facebook military network reached a broad civilian audience
(see paragraph 161 above) and that numerous posts on that network dehumanized the Rohingya,

- 148 portrayed them as violent and existential threats to the purity and existence of other ethnic groups
and to Buddhism, and at times encouraged and legitimized violence against them (see
paragraphs 156-168 above). The video posted during the clearance operations, for example, shows a
soldier telling non-Rohingya civilians, “if you can carry a sword, then carry a sword . . . Carry
whatever you can and bravely face these animals.” (See paragraph 165 above.) Another message
called on “Myanmar people” to “sharpen even the knives at home that you do not use” to protect
themselves against an alleged Muslim plan for mass murder (see paragraph 166 above). Other posts
proclaimed to Rohingya “you can get a six-foot pit [a grave]” and called for emulating India’s alleged
“shoot-to-kill policy” for “Bengalis” at the border (see paragraph 164 above). The message of hate
was also received by the viewership. An official Tatmadaw post simply describing an alleged
“Bengali” attack the day before the August 2017 ARSA attacks unleashed numerous calls for the
Rohingya and/or Muslims to be killed, exterminated or permanently removed from Myanmar (see
paragraph 168 above).
504. The Gambia additionally points to statements by Buddhist monks, politicians and others
which, it contends, constituted incitement to commit genocide that was either attributable to
Myanmar or that Myanmar failed to suppress (see FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018),
paras. 1303, 1315-1316, 1328 and 1423; Tin Aung Kyaw, “Buddhist monk Wirathu leads violent
national campaign against Myanmar’s Muslims”, Global Post (21 June 2013)). In the view of the
Court, the evidence in the record is not sufficient to conclusively establish that the acts of the monks
and politicians are attributable to Myanmar for the purposes of The Gambia’s claim regarding
incitement. The responsibility of Myanmar to punish potential acts of incitement to commit genocide
by private actors will be considered below (see paragraph 550).
505. In the view of the Court, the evidence establishes that the Facebook military network
posts were made by persons and organs whose conduct is attributable to Myanmar (see
paragraphs 162-163 above), that the posts were broadly and publicly disseminated to the civilian
population and that the messages targeted the Rohingya as a group. A number of the messages sought
to present the Rohingya as a violent threat, to be violently defended against. The Court considers that
some of the Facebook military network messages sought to encourage the non-Rohingya population
to engage in violence against the Rohingya as a group. The Court is not persuaded, however, that the
evidence before it conclusively establishes that the posts, even when taken in context, were made
with the specific intent to destroy the group in whole or in part or constituted a direct appeal for the
commission of genocide, or that the intended audience would have understood the messages as such,
as required by Article III (c).
506. The Court is therefore not fully convinced that The Gambia has established that Myanmar
violated its obligation under Article III (c) of the Convention by directly and publicly inciting
genocide against the Rohingya by private actors. The Gambia’s submission that Myanmar failed to
investigate and punish acts of incitement will be addressed in the next section (see paragraph 550
below).

- 149 C. Obligation to punish acts enumerated in Article III
507. The Court now turns to The Gambia’s claim that Myanmar has failed to punish acts
prohibited under Article III of the Convention (see paragraph 63 above). The Gambia contends that
Myanmar has failed to effectively investigate genocide or to identify, prosecute and punish persons
reasonably suspected of engaging in acts of genocide, including members and commanders of the
Tatmadaw. Myanmar, for its part, maintains that genocide did not occur and that The Gambia has
not identified plausible or credible evidence sufficient to prompt an investigation for genocide. Thus,
the duty to punish under the Convention does not arise.
508. The obligation to punish the crime of genocide is set out in Article I of the Genocide
Convention, and Article III sets out the acts that “shall be punishable”. The obligation is further
elaborated in Articles IV, V and VI. Article IV provides that persons committing any of the acts
enumerated in Article III “shall be punished, whether they are constitutionally responsible rulers,
public officials or private individuals”. Article V obliges States parties to adopt “legislation to give
effect to” the Convention and in particular “to provide effective penalties” for breaches of Article III,
while Article VI provides that persons charged with violations of Article III “shall be tried by a
competent tribunal” of the territorial State “or by such international penal tribunal as may have
jurisdiction”. This suite of duties together constitutes the obligation of States parties to punish
genocide under the Convention. Collectively, their purpose is to enable proceedings to be brought
against suspects and to achieve the object and purpose of the Convention by avoiding impunity for
the perpetrators (see Questions relating to the Obligation to Prosecute or Extradite (Belgium v.
Senegal), Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2012 (II), p. 451, para. 74).
509. The obligation to punish arises whenever any of the offences enumerated in Article III
are perpetrated, and not exclusively when genocide itself has been committed. Furthermore, the
obligation to punish extends to acts committed by private actors whose conduct is not attributable to
the State and does not require a showing of genocidal intent on the part of the State. Nor is the duty
to punish subsumed by a finding that a State has committed genocide. As the Court has noted, “[i]t
is perfectly possible for a State to incur responsibility at once for an act of genocide . . . and for the
breach by the State of its obligation to punish the perpetrator of the act: these are two distinct
internationally wrongful acts” (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 201, para. 383).
510. The Court will now consider the Parties’ arguments regarding the applicable law
concerning the duties to investigate (1) and to prosecute and punish (2), before examining the
application of this law in the present case (3).
1. Duty to investigate
511. Both Parties accept that investigation is a logical prerequisite to the imposition of
punishment under the Convention. The Gambia embraces the position of the Maldives, as intervener,
that the duty to investigate arises as soon as there are reasonable grounds to believe that acts of
genocide have been committed on a State’s territory, and that such investigations must be carried out
in good faith and must be prompt, effective and thorough, independent and impartial.

- 150 512. Myanmar stresses that the duty to investigate “cannot be triggered without the persons
suspected” of acts of genocide “having been precisely identified”, or unless the State receives
information that is “sufficiently plausible and credible to suggest” that the actus reus and mens rea
of genocide are established.
513. The Court observes that the Genocide Convention expressly provides that persons
committing genocide “shall be” “charged”, “tried” and “punished” with “effective penalties”
(Articles IV, V and VI). The Convention thus imposes on States an obligation to prosecute and
punish. The Court considers that, ordinarily, an investigation is a necessary prerequisite to the
fulfilment of these obligations, particularly in light of the Convention’s object and purpose of
ensuring accountability for the crimes enumerated therein.
514. While the obligation to impose punishment on a perpetrator cannot arise unless a violation
of Article III has been established (2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2015 (I), pp. 128-129,
para. 441), in the Court’s view, the obligation to investigate is necessarily antecedent to any
determination of a violation. As the Court has observed, “it is precisely the purpose of an
investigation to uncover the facts necessary to determine whether a criminal offence has been
committed” (Application of the International Convention for the Suppression of the Financing of
Terrorism and of the International Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Racial
Discrimination (Ukraine v. Russian Federation), Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2024 (I), p. 133,
para. 103; see also Questions relating to the Obligation to Prosecute or Extradite (Belgium v.
Senegal), Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2012 (II), p. 455, para. 91). Like the duty to adopt legislation
under Article V of the Convention (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 109, para. 159;
see paragraph 560 below), the duty to investigate may give rise to international responsibility under
the Convention independent of any prior determination of a violation of Article III.
515. The Court considers that the obligation to investigate arises as soon as there are
reasonable grounds to suspect that acts of genocide or other acts punishable under Article III of the
Convention have been committed in a State’s territorial jurisdiction (see Application of the
International Convention for the Suppression of the Financing of Terrorism and of the International
Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Racial Discrimination (Ukraine v. Russian
Federation), Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2024 (I), p. 133, para. 103, and p. 166, para. 219; see also
Questions relating to the Obligation to Prosecute or Extradite (Belgium v. Senegal), Judgment, I.C.J.
Reports 2012 (II), p. 454, para. 88). This includes an assessment of the presence of genocidal intent.
The Court cannot accept Myanmar’s contention that the obligation to investigate is not implicated
until a State has received information “precisely identif[ying]” a suspect or “specifically inform[ing]”
the State of the possibility of genocide. The obligation to investigate is one of due diligence. Once a
State knows or should know that there are reasonable grounds to suspect that violations of Article III
have been committed, “steps must be taken” to proceed with an effective investigation (see Questions
relating to the Obligation to Prosecute or Extradite (Belgium v. Senegal), Judgment, I.C.J. Reports
2012 (II), p. 454, para. 86).
516. The Court considers that investigations must be conducted in good faith, in a manner
designed to achieve their purpose of identifying violations and bringing persons responsible to
justice. Investigations must be prompt, thorough, effective, independent and impartial, and capable
of grounding a fair trial.

- 151 2. Duty to prosecute and punish
517. Articles IV and VI of the Convention further oblige a State to prosecute and punish any
individuals who may be responsible for committing acts proscribed by Article III within its territorial
jurisdiction, if there is sufficient evidence to do so. The Gambia contends that failure by a State to
prosecute and punish such acts constitutes a breach of the obligation to punish, regardless of any
justification a State may offer. Myanmar counters that the absence of prosecutions or convictions
does not necessarily entail a breach of the duty to punish.
518. The Court has recognized that national authorities have some discretion in determining
whether prosecution is warranted. Such decisions, however, must be based on the available evidence
and applicable legal rules, and taken in the same manner as in the case of other grave offences under
the law of that State (Application of the International Convention for the Suppression of the
Financing of Terrorism and of the International Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of
Racial Discrimination (Ukraine v. Russian Federation), Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2024 (I), p. 138,
para. 118; Questions relating to the Obligation to Prosecute or Extradite (Belgium v. Senegal),
Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2012 (II), p. 455, para. 90). Such discretion must also be exercised
independently, impartially and in good faith, in a manner consistent with the obligation under the
Convention to ensure that “persons guilty of genocide” “shall be” tried and punished (Articles IV, V
and VI). This includes prosecuting “without delay” (see Questions relating to the Obligation to
Prosecute or Extradite (Belgium v. Senegal), Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2012 (II), p. 460, para. 115).
The requirement of a trial by a competent tribunal in Article VI also necessarily implies a proceeding
that comports with fair trial guarantees, including independence and impartiality. The investigation,
prosecution and trial must be sufficiently transparent for the integrity of the process to be assessed.
Finally, as the Court has recognized in the context of Article V, one of the most effective ways of
preventing criminal acts is to provide penalties and impose them effectively on those who commit
the criminal acts (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 219, para. 426). It follows that,
to be effective, penalties must be proportionate to the gravity of the crime of genocide and sufficient
to prevent such acts in the future.
519. Myanmar emphasizes that the duty to punish genocide does not require punishment of
crimes other than genocide, and that The Gambia must therefore “prove a failure to punish genocide”
and not other crimes. It also maintains that the duty to punish does not require a State to prosecute
and punish a crime specifically called “genocide” and that States parties may satisfy the obligation
to punish under the Convention by relying on general provisions in their criminal law such as murder,
grievous bodily harm or rape.
520. The Court observes that the obligation to investigate, prosecute and punish genocide
applies only to acts falling within Articles II and III of the Convention. The Convention does not
require investigations into other crimes. On the other hand, as the Court concludes with regard to the
duty to adopt legislation (see paragraph 569 below), unless a crime provided for under a State’s
domestic criminal law encompasses genocidal intent as well as the other elements of the crime of
genocide, its enforcement will not fulfil the obligation to punish genocide. Accordingly, prosecution
and punishment of crimes that do not capture all the constituent elements of the offences enumerated
in Article III do not satisfy the duty to punish under the Genocide Convention.

- 152 521. The Parties also differ with respect to whether the Convention obliges States parties to
punish commanders for the acts of their subordinates. Myanmar argues that Article IV of the
Convention addresses only “[p]ersons committing genocide or any of the other acts enumerated in
Article III”, and that this language does not include the responsibility of commanders who have not
themselves “committed” genocide.
522. The Court observes that the principle of superior responsibility recognizes that superiors
incur responsibility for offences committed by their subordinates if they knew, or should have
known, that the subordinates committed, or were likely to commit, such acts, and if the superior
failed to take the necessary and reasonable measures to prevent or punish those offences (see
Karemera and Ngirumpatse, ICTR-98-44-A, Appeals Chamber, Judgement of 29 September 2014,
para. 119; Brđanin, IT-99-36-T, Trial Chamber II, Judgement of 1 September 2004, para. 715). The
Court further notes that Article IV of the Convention specifically provides that all persons
committing any of the acts enumerated in Article III “shall be punished, whether they are
constitutionally responsible rulers, public officials or private individuals”. The Commentary to the
United Nations Secretariat’s draft convention also noted that leaders bore “[t]he heaviest
responsibility”, as their “duty . . . is to abstain from organizing genocide personally and from
provoking it and to prevent its commission by others” (United Nations, Secretary-General, “Draft
Convention on the Crime of Genocide”, UN doc. E/447 (26 June 1947), p. 35). The Court considers
that the specific recognition of punishment of constitutional rulers and other high-level officials in
Article IV, particularly when read in light of the Convention’s object and purpose, encompasses the
principle of superior responsibility.
523. Under Myanmar’s Constitution, criminal responsibility for members of the military can
generally be imposed only within the military justice system. The Gambia contends that this régime
is non-transparent and under the direct command of Senior General Min Aung Hlaing, who was the
Commander-in-Chief of all Myanmar’s armed forces during the clearance operations, and his deputy,
both of whom were accused by the FFM of genocide. According to The Gambia, impunity is thus
“built into the legal framework”. Myanmar, in turn, submits that the obligation to punish under the
Convention cannot require a State to alter its constitutional arrangements or reform its justice system.
524. In this respect, the Court observes that the Genocide Convention does not mandate any
particular domestic legal arrangements for a State party, so long as those arrangements do not
preclude effective punishment as mandated by the Convention (see paragraph 563 below). A State
cannot, however, invoke its domestic legal system to justify failure to comply with the Convention
(Certain Questions of Mutual Assistance in Criminal Matters (Djibouti v. France), Judgment, I.C.J.
Reports 2008, p. 222, para. 124).

*

525. In sum, the obligation to punish pursuant to Articles I, IV, V and VI of the Genocide
Convention obliges a State party to effectively investigate, prosecute and punish persons, including
commanders, having committed offences under Article III in a competent tribunal and to impose
effective penalties commensurate with the severity of the crime, in a manner consistent with the
considerations set forth above.

- 153 3. Application of the obligation to punish in the present case
526. The Court now turns to the question of Myanmar’s compliance with the obligation to
punish in this case. The Parties disagree as to whether the duty to investigate was engaged, whether
Myanmar conducted adequate and effective investigations and prosecutions and imposed adequate
punishment, and whether Myanmar obstructed investigations and destroyed evidence.
527. The Gambia submits that following the 2017 clearance operations, United Nations
entities and a number of States voiced concerns that atrocities including genocide had taken place
and called for investigations. The Gambia points in particular to the September 2018 report of the
FFM, which recommended that “named senior generals of the Myanmar military should be
investigated and prosecuted . . . for genocide”, including Senior General Hlaing (FFM, Report of the
detailed findings (2018), p. 1 and paras. 1553-1555). The Applicant also notes General Assembly
resolution 73/264 of 22 December 2018, which expressed “grave concern at the findings of the
[FFM] that there [was] sufficient information to warrant investigation and prosecution . . . for
genocide”, and called for “a full and independent investigation of the human rights violations and
abuses committed, including against the Rohingya Muslims” (United Nations General Assembly,
resolution 73/264, “Situation of human rights in Myanmar”, adopted on 22 December 2018,
UN doc. A/RES/73/264 (22 January 2019), paras. 1 and 3).
528. The Gambia submits that such calls engaged Myanmar’s obligation to carry out effective
investigations in good faith. It maintains that Myanmar instead diverted attention through the creation
of several “sham” investigative commissions that neither identified any member of the Tatmadaw
responsible for crimes against the Rohingya nor recommended prosecution of any military personnel,
and that the few penalties imposed were inadequate. Adequate and effective investigations and
prosecutions have therefore not taken place.
529. With respect to the alleged destruction of evidence, The Gambia points in particular to
the military’s disposal of the remains of Rohingya victims through means such as burning and burial
in mass graves, as well as acts of bulldozing, clearance and reconstruction across large parts of the
Maungdaw, Buthidaung and Rathedaung Townships. Additionally, The Gambia contends that
Myanmar’s failure to investigate and prosecute is evidenced by Myanmar’s blocking of independent
investigations.
530. Myanmar maintains that it has not received any sufficiently plausible or credible
information establishing that acts constituting genocide have been committed with the intent to
physically or biologically destroy the Rohingya group in whole or in part, or identifying specific
individuals who may have committed such acts. The obligation to investigate and punish under the
Convention accordingly has not been engaged. In any event, Myanmar notes that investigations have
been conducted and prosecutions have occurred.
531. Myanmar does not dispute that its security forces burned, buried or otherwise disposed
of the remains of dead Rohingya, though it contends that the casualties were ARSA terrorists, and

- 154 that “the burial of bodies without delay, including in mass graves, is not unusual” in a warm climate.
As for the destruction of buildings, the Respondent asserts that The Gambia has not established that
all damage and destruction of all buildings was caused deliberately by Myanmar’s security forces
and for the purpose of destroying evidence. As noted above, Myanmar also contends that it is not
obliged under the Genocide Convention to co-operate with United Nations investigative bodies (see
paragraph 129).

*

*

532. The Court will first examine the facts relating to Myanmar’s efforts to investigate,
prosecute and punish allegations of crimes of genocide arising from the clearance operations. It will
then consider The Gambia’s allegations regarding the destruction of evidence and lack of
co-operation with independent United Nations investigations.
533. The Court observes that Myanmar established various commissions in relation to the
situation in Rakhine State, a number of which predated the 2017 clearance operations. For example,
the Advisory Commission on Rakhine State was not mandated to investigate alleged crimes or make
findings as to individual responsibility and issued its final report on 24 August 2017 (see
paragraph 140 above). Although the final report called for an “independent and impartial
investigation” given that the security forces had been accused of serious human rights violations
(Final report of the Advisory Commission on Rakhine State (2017), p. 55, para. 71), the committee
appointed to implement the recommendations did not have any investigative powers either. These
mechanisms were thus not capable of fulfilling Myanmar’s investigative obligation with respect to
the 2017 clearance operations.
534. Regarding the entities that had an investigative mandate in relation to events surrounding
the 2017 clearance operations, the Parties discuss the Inquiry Board established by the Tatmadaw,
the ICOE (see paragraph 126 above) and the Criminal Investigation and Prosecution Body. The Court
will consider each of these entities in turn.
535. The Tatmadaw Inquiry Board was established in October 2017 and composed exclusively
of members of the military. It claimed to have conducted interviews with 3,217 villagers in 26 days.
The Board’s report issued in November 2017, one month after it was established, rejected all
allegations of any deaths of innocent people, sexual violence or destruction of property, and
concluded that the security forces “did not perform the use of excessive force” (“Information released
by the Tatmadaw True News Information Team on the findings of the Investigation Team in
connection with the performances of the security troops during the terrorist attacks in
Maungtaw region, Rakhine State”, The Global New Light of Myanmar (14 November 2017), para. 5).
The Inquiry Board later issued a statement acknowledging the killing of ten men at Inn Din disclosed
by Reuters journalists (see paragraph 232 above).
536. The ICOE was established on 30 July 2018 to “investigate allegations of human rights
violations and related issues following the terrorist attacks by [ARSA] in Rakhine State with a view

- 155 to seeking accountability and formulating recommendations on steps to be taken to ensure peace and
stability in Rakhine State” (see paragraph 126 above) (Republic of the Union of Myanmar, ICOE,
“Mandate”; Republic of the Union of Myanmar, ICOE, “Executive Summary” (21 January 2020),
p. 1). There is limited information before the Court regarding the mandate, methodology and scope
of the investigation conducted by the ICOE, and its terms of reference were not entered into evidence.
537. The Executive Summary of the ICOE final report (see paragraph 539 below) indicated
that the Commission had conducted over 1,300 witness interviews, including 29 members of the
defence forces, 20 police officers and 1,017 residents of 13 locations, encompassing more than
60 villages in northern Rakhine State, where “major incidents” were said to have occurred (Republic
of the Union of Myanmar, ICOE, “Executive Summary” (21 January 2020), pp. 5 and 7). The
Executive Summary stated that priority was given to obtaining statements from “Muslim and ethnic
national witnesses”, and that interviews paid particular attention to the “six most serious allegations”:
mass killing, rape, property destruction (arson), looting, torture and forced displacement (ibid., p. 5).
The Executive Summary gave no information, however, regarding the number of Muslim witnesses,
which members of the security forces were interviewed, how witnesses were selected or the
qualifications and methodology of the interviewers.
538. The Parties disagree regarding the independence and impartiality of the Commission’s
domestic members and its methods. The Commission comprised four members, two from Myanmar
and two international experts, who served in their personal capacity with secretarial support from the
Office of the President. The record indicates that ICOE field interviews were conducted in the
presence of members of the security forces, although Myanmar contends that such forces merely
provided security for the witnesses and ICOE members and did not disturb or threaten witnesses.
The Government of Bangladesh granted permission for the ICOE to conduct interviews in
Cox’s Bazar, where the vast majority of the Rohingya survivors of the 2017 clearance operations are
located, but the necessary logistics could not be organized before the Commission submitted its final
report (Republic of the Union of Myanmar, ICOE, “Executive Summary” (21 January 2020),
pp. 7-8). During the oral proceedings, counsel for Myanmar emphasized that the work of the ICOE
was “incomplete” and “a basis for further investigations”, and that its conclusions were “preliminary
non-established facts”.
539. The ICOE issued a final report on 20 January 2020, of which only the Executive
Summary was annexed to the written pleadings (see paragraph 126 above). The ICOE concluded that
“war crimes, serious human rights violations, and violations of domestic law took place during the
security operations between 25 August and 5 September 2017” and that there were “reasonable
grounds to believe that members of Myanmar’s security forces were involved” (Republic of the
Union of Myanmar, ICOE, “Executive Summary” (21 January 2020), p. 12). The Commission
elaborated:
“[A]ccording to statements collected by the ICOE, many civilians were killed by
members of Myanmar’s security forces, directly or collaterally during the security
operations; and many village houses were burned down by members of the security
forces and local vigilantes during and after the security operations. The ICOE has also
found that members of Myanmar’s security forces failed in several instances to prevent
village destruction, and thus came to abet the demolition of property.” (Ibid., p. 10.)

- 156 The ICOE indicated that it had received “no credible statements on allegations of gang rape
committed by Myanmar’s security forces” and that all references to rape were “second-hand
