information” (ibid., p. 6). The Commission found that individual members of the security forces and
“vigilantes” used excessive force, but did not find evidence that the “killings or acts of displacement
were committed pursuant to an intent or plan to destroy the Muslim . . . community in northern
Rakhine State” (ibid., pp. 9-10).
540. The ICOE observed that “there may be cases for which the perpetrators need to be
investigated and brought to justice” (Republic of the Union of Myanmar, ICOE, “Executive
Summary” (21 January 2020), p. 10). According to Myanmar’s first provisional measures report, the
ICOE identified “139 cases of crimes”, including 87 by “members of the ARSA and their
collaborators”, 29 by “local people” and 23 by the BGP (Myanmar’s First report on the
implementation of provisional measures, paras. 98-101). Myanmar emphasized, however, that “the
investigation and prosecution of crimes is the role of authorities other than the ICOE”.
541. The ICOE did not identify any members of Myanmar’s military who might be responsible
for crimes. It instead recommended that “Myanmar’s Office of the Judge Advocate General must
expeditiously conduct the necessary investigations and seek accountability for responsible military
personnel throughout the Chain of Command” (Republic of the Union of Myanmar, ICOE,
“Executive Summary” (21 January 2020), p. 12).
542. On 24 January 2020, Myanmar established a Criminal Investigation and Prosecution
Body (hereinafter the “CIPB”), reportedly charged with undertaking “criminal investigations and
prosecution of those suspected of committing crimes” during the conflict with ARSA in Rakhine
State (Myanmar’s First report on the implementation of provisional measures, para. 96). The CIPB
is composed of civilian law enforcement and prosecution authorities and does not have jurisdiction
over members of the Tatmadaw. The CIPB examined the cases identified by the ICOE. Of these, 2 of
the 23 cases that concerned the BGP led to disciplinary sanctions of reduction in pensionable service;
the others were either closed or suspended (Myanmar’s Second report on the implementation of
provisional measures, para. 48; Myanmar’s Fourth report on the implementation of provisional
measures, paras. 42-44).
543. Although Myanmar claims that the CIPB is “continuing its work”, it acknowledges that
since 2021, “no other prosecutions have been initiated” and no new convictions have occurred.
Myanmar gives “several reasons why the work of this body is currently hindered”, including “[t]he
lack of evidence and formal complaints” and the security situation in Rakhine State.
544. The Court observes that none of the above measures involved investigation or prosecution
of individual members of the Tatmadaw. With respect to prosecutions within the military justice
system, following the Reuters report regarding Inn Din, four officers and three soldiers were
convicted by court martial for the killing of ten alleged ARSA members. These individuals were
sentenced to ten years imprisonment, but were all pardoned by Senior General Hlaing after serving
eight months in prison. Following an Associated Press report documenting mass graves in

- 157 Gu Dar Pyin (see paragraph 228 above), two officers were sentenced to one year’s imprisonment —
one for breaching the rules of engagement, one for failure in supervisory duties — with a third officer
receiving a one-year reduction in pensionable service for failure in supervisory duties. Three military
personnel were convicted for events at Maung Nu: one officer received a reduction in pensionable
service for negligence and two subordinates were convicted to one and two years’ imprisonment with
hard labour, respectively, for theft and criminal use of force against women.
545. The Court recalls that the FFM was established by the United Nations specifically to
establish the facts and circumstances of alleged abuses by the Myanmar security forces, particularly
in Rakhine State, “with a view to ensuring full accountability for perpetrators and justice for victims”
(see paragraph 101 above). The FFM’s September 2018 report contained extensive information
regarding the conduct of the clearance operations across northern Rakhine State and found sufficient
information to warrant initiation of proceedings for genocide, including with respect to named
individuals (FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), paras. 1 and 1555). The Court therefore
considers that at least as of that date, Myanmar had both reasonable grounds to suspect that genocide
may have been committed in its territory and sufficient evidence to commence an investigation. Its
duty to investigate offences under the Convention was therefore engaged. The Court considers that,
at that point, it was incumbent upon Myanmar to conduct a prompt, effective, thorough, independent
and impartial investigation, with a view to ensuring the identification, prosecution and punishment
of all persons responsible for acts of genocide.
546. It is clear from the record that Myanmar did not take such action, despite having the
capacity to do so. For example, Myanmar authorities promptly exhumed the mass graves of Hindu
civilians attacked on 25 August 2017 at Kha Maung Seik, one month after the killings occurred (see
paragraph 177 above; FFM, Report of the detailed findings (2018), para. 1059).
547. Myanmar acknowledges that the ICOE investigation was incomplete and preliminary (see
paragraph 538 above). The ICOE also found some evidence consistent with that before the Court
regarding the conduct of the 2017 clearance operations (see paragraph 539 above). This information
should have prompted further investigations, and prosecutions as appropriate, of persons potentially
responsible for acts punishable under the Genocide Convention. The record establishes, however,
that the CIPB’s subsequent investigations and prosecutions of the BGP have been very limited, with
minimal penalties imposed, and that the CIPB had no jurisdiction to investigate the military (see
paragraphs 542-543 above). In addition, the record does not establish that any further efforts were
made to interview Rohingya survivors of the 2017 clearance operations in Bangladesh, or that any
further investigations were pursued regarding the conduct of the BGP.
548. With respect to criminal proceedings involving the military, the Court considers that
Myanmar has not genuinely investigated, or, if warranted, prosecuted its soldiers and commanders
and punished them with penalties that could be considered commensurate with the crime of genocide.
Despite the recommendation of the ICOE that Myanmar “must expeditiously conduct the necessary

- 158 investigations and seek accountability for responsible military personnel throughout the Chain of
Command” (see paragraph 541 above), the evidence establishes that there has been no systematic
investigation into any crimes committed by the Myanmar military during the 2017 events, including
any investigation into the role of the military command. Myanmar has pointed to court martial
proceedings regarding three locations, each of which involved a limited number of defendants, none
of whom served sentences of more than a year. The Respondent’s immediate reaction to Reuters’
exposure of the killings at Inn Din was to arrest and prosecute the journalists, who were jailed for
more than twice as long as the perpetrators (see paragraph 544 above; FFM, Detailed findings (2019),
para. 232; “Key facts about Reuters reporters Wa Lone and Kyaw Soe Oo”, Reuters (7 May 2019)).
With respect to allegations of rape and other forms of sexual violence during the clearance operations,
the IIMM similarly concluded that “no credible investigations” have occurred (IIMM, “Efforts to
investigate and punish sexual and gender-based crimes committed against Rohingya: Evidence
analysis” (27 March 2024), paras. 100-102).
549. The Court further observes that, although the ICOE stated that it had investigated the
presence of genocidal intent (see paragraph 539 above), Myanmar did not criminalize genocide in
any form until 2021 (see paragraphs 562 and 569 below). There was thus no offence of genocide
under Myanmar’s domestic law for the ICOE to investigate. Moreover, there were no further
investigations into the acts punishable under the Convention after that date. Myanmar has not
identified, charged, prosecuted or punished any individual — whether military or civilian — for any
of the offences set forth in Article III of the Convention, including direct and public incitement to
commit genocide. To the contrary, Myanmar continues to maintain that it is unaware of any plausible
or credible evidence that would warrant investigation into genocide (see paragraph 530 above).
Based on the evidence before the Court, there appears to have been no progress in any investigations
or prosecutions by the CIPB or otherwise relating to the 2017 clearance operations since 2021. While
the security situation in northern Rakhine State may have hindered further on-site investigations, this
does not explain why Myanmar has not been able to interview its own military personnel or Rohingya
survivors in Bangladesh.
550. With respect to the punishment of direct and public incitement to commit genocide, while
the Court found insufficient evidence in the record to determine that Myanmar itself is responsible
for inciting private actors to commit genocide (see paragraph 506 above), the obligation to
investigate acts of incitement is engaged once a State knows or should know that there is a reasonable
basis for suspecting that a violation of Article III (c) of the Convention may have occurred (see
paragraph 515 above). The Court considers that the record contains sufficient information to
establish such a reasonable basis, thus engaging Myanmar’s duty, at a minimum, to investigate
potential acts of incitement by both State and non-State actors. Although Myanmar contends that
some penalties have been imposed on certain Buddhist monks and entities relating to “political”
activities, this does not establish that Myanmar has met its obligation to investigate, prosecute and
punish acts of direct and public incitement to commit genocide.
551. Turning to The Gambia’s allegations regarding the destruction of evidence, the evidence
in the record establishes that security forces engaged in widespread burning or otherwise disposed of
bodies during the clearance operations (see, for example, paragraphs 222 and 228-229 above;
Affidavit of Witness MS, paras. 28-30). These practices were confirmed by Myanmar’s written

- 159 witness testimony. The Court has also found that Myanmar was primarily responsible for the burning
of several hundred Rohingya villages during and after the clearance operations, as well as for the
subsequent bulldozing, clearing of and construction on sites where genocide was committed (see
paragraphs 271-282 above). At least seven Rohingya villages that were destroyed during the 2017
clearance operations have been replaced by BGP battalion bases (see paragraph 279 above). The
IIMM concluded that such activity has “permanently concealed much evidence of what occurred
during the 2017 clearance operations” (IIMM, “The destruction and dispossession of Rohingya land
and property during the 2017 clearance operations — public summary” (29 September 2025),
para. 59). As the IIMM emphasized,
“[t]hese actions have eliminated forensic evidence such as mass graves, burned
structures, victims’ remains and personal effects that could demonstrate patterns of
persecution, methods of killing, and the identities of victims and perpetrators. Such
destruction may also eradicate ballistic, blast pattern and weapons evidence, as well as
documentation showing property ownership, victim targeting, or links between military
leadership and field operations.” (Ibid.; see also paragraph 281 above.)
552. Contrary to Myanmar’s contention (see paragraph 531 above), it is not necessary for the
Court to determine that all fires were set by the Myanmar security forces (see paragraphs 271 and
276 above) or that all of these acts occurred at sites where clearance operations took place. Moreover,
in the view of the Court, it is irrelevant whether these actions were intentionally carried out for the
purposes of destruction of evidence, so long as they objectively achieved that effect. Once Myanmar
had reasonable grounds to suspect that genocide may have occurred — a point that the Court has
found arose no later than September 2018 — government authorities had a responsibility to ensure
the preservation of evidence in order to allow a thorough and effective investigation to be conducted.
The Court considers that Myanmar engaged in extensive destruction of evidence by burning or
otherwise disposing of bodies, burning and bulldozing sites where clearance operations took place
and erecting new infrastructure, thus materially obstructing the ability of Myanmar or any other entity
to effectively investigate the 2017 events.
553. With respect to Myanmar’s co-operation with United Nations investigative entities, the
Court observes that Article VI of the Convention places an obligation on Myanmar either to itself
conduct adequate and effective investigations, prosecutions and punishments, or to co-operate with
such international penal tribunal as may have jurisdiction (2007 Bosnia Judgment, Judgment, I.C.J.
Reports 2007 (I), p. 227, para. 443). In the present case, Myanmar has done neither. In the Court’s
view, Myanmar created obstacles to effective investigation and prosecution by either a competent
domestic or international tribunal. It also limited the evidence available to this Court, through its
destruction of evidence, denial of access to United Nations investigative entities and failure itself to
adequately investigate the possibility of genocide during the clearance operations.
554. In light of all the above, the Court considers that Myanmar has failed to investigate and,
if warranted, to prosecute and punish persons, including commanders, committing offences under
Article III in a competent tribunal, and to impose effective penalties commensurate with the severity

- 160 of the crime. It therefore concludes that Myanmar has failed to punish genocide under Articles I, IV
and VI of the Genocide Convention.
D. Obligation to enact necessary legislation
555. Article V of the Convention requires parties to “enact, in accordance with their respective
Constitutions, the necessary legislation to give effect to the provisions of the present Convention,
and, in particular, to provide effective penalties for persons guilty of genocide or any of the other
acts enumerated in article III”. The Gambia requests the Court to adjudge and declare that Myanmar
is in breach of this obligation.
556. The Gambia submits that Myanmar’s Penal Code had no provisions on the crime of
genocide until 2021, when Myanmar introduced an offence of genocide under Sections 311A and
311B. The Applicant also contends that the 2021 amendment fails to satisfy the requirements of
Article V because it does not apply to members of the Tatmadaw and because, under the Myanmar
Constitution, military personnel may be pardoned or potentially protected from prosecution. In
addition, The Gambia notes that the new law defines the actus reus of genocide as including only
measures intended to prevent births within the group that are “not in accordance with any existing
laws”. This restriction, according to The Gambia, insulates measures, such as those introduced to
restrict births in the Rohingya community, from prosecution.
557. Myanmar, on the other hand, maintains that The Gambia’s claim regarding legislation
improperly asks the Court to intrude into the sphere of constitutional autonomy by examining the
implementation of a treaty in a State’s domestic law. The Respondent also denies that its legislation
is inconsistent with the Genocide Convention. In its view, Article V affords States broad legislative
discretion and does not oblige States parties to enact the text of the Convention into their domestic
law or to create a domestic criminal offence of “genocide”. Thus, a State may discharge its
obligations under Article V by relying on its ordinary criminal laws.
558. With respect to the 2021 amendment, Myanmar submits that the new crime and the
penalties thereunder apply to members of the military. As for pardons, Myanmar points out that it is
not the only country in which the Head of State or Commander-in-Chief has the power to issue
pardons. The Respondent also maintains that the domestic laws of other States parties define
genocide more narrowly than the Convention and that, in any event, its measures regarding births
are not contrary to the Convention. The enactment of the 2021 provisions therefore cured, and
rendered moot, any claims regarding the pre-2021 legislation.
559. The Maldives, as intervener, submits that the legislation enacted by a State party pursuant
to Article V must allow for the prosecution of all acts committed in that State’s territory that are
punishable under the Convention and ensure that all potential perpetrators of acts of genocide
committed in that same territory can be prosecuted, in accordance with Article IV, with the
imposition of effective penalties.

*

*

- 161 560. It is well established that “the provisions requiring States to punish acts of genocide . . .
are necessarily linked to the obligation (in Article V) for each State party to enact legislation for the
purpose of giving effect to the provisions of the Convention” (2015 Croatia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports
2015 (I), p. 50, para. 96). As the Court has recognized, a State’s adoption of legislation enacting a
criminal offence that is mandated by a treaty usually precedes the performance of the obligation to
prosecute (Questions relating to the Obligation to Prosecute or Extradite (Belgium v. Senegal),
Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2012 (II), p. 455, para. 91, and p. 461, para. 119). The adoption of
legislation necessary to give effect to the obligations under the Convention is therefore an essential
prerequisite to the obligation to investigate, prosecute and punish acts of genocide and to provide
effective penalties for those responsible.
561. The Court recalls that Article V is “plainly” among the provisions of the Convention that
“impose obligations on States in respect of which they may, in the event of breach, incur
responsibility” (2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 109, para. 159). It is an
autonomous obligation, which gives rise to international responsibility irrespective of any finding of
a violation of Article III. A State that fails to adopt adequate domestic measures to prevent and punish
genocide also breaches the broader duties to prevent and punish genocide set forth in Article I (ibid.,
p. 219, para. 426).
562. Prior to 2021, Myanmar had no legislation specifically criminalizing genocide. The Court
will, accordingly, first consider whether Myanmar could nevertheless fulfil the obligation to enact
the necessary legislation set forth in Article V through reliance on its general domestic criminal law,
such as provisions addressing murder, assault or rape.
563. The Court observes that Article V does not require contracting parties to adopt legislation
that mirrors the wording of Articles II and III of the Convention. Nevertheless, Article V obliges
States parties to enact legislation “necessary . . . to give effect to the provisions” of the Convention,
including “to provide effective penalties for persons guilty of genocide or any of the other acts
enumerated in article III”. Article III of the Convention further provides that the acts thereunder
“shall be punishable”. These provisions require a State to give full legislative effect to the acts
prohibited under Articles II and III of the Convention.
564. As previously noted, the specific intent to destroy the group, as such, in whole or in part is
the defining element that distinguishes genocide from other crimes, including those involving mass
harm (see paragraph 288). The Convention defines and penalizes a crime, not just against individuals,
but against a group “as such”, thereby criminalizing a qualitatively distinct wrong of international
concern. The Court observes in this regard that the ICTR refused to refer a prosecution for genocide
to a State whose domestic law lacked a specific crime of genocide, noting that the legal values
protected by the “ordinary crime” of homicide and those protected by genocide are different, in that
“[t]he penalization of genocide protects specifically defined groups” (Bagaragaza,
ICTR-05-86-AR11bis, Appeals Chamber, Decision on Rule 11bis Appeal, 30 August 2006,
paras. 14 and 17).
565. Domestic criminal law provisions may encompass aspects of the material elements of
genocide set forth in Article II; the offence of murder, for example, includes intentional killing.

- 162 Absent the additional requirement of genocidal intent, however, such provisions do not capture the
specific concern of the crime of genocide with the protection of groups. The Court further observes
that ordinary criminal legislation may not capture the full range of the material acts encompassed by
Article II of the Convention, such as the deliberate infliction of conditions of life calculated to bring
about the physical destruction of the group or measures intended to prevent births within the group.
The offences set forth in Article III (b) to (e) of the Convention may also not be reflected in a State’s
domestic criminal law, such as direct and public incitement to commit genocide. Finally, the penalties
imposed for genocide pursuant to Article V must reflect the extreme gravity of this offence.
566. The Respondent submits that 57 States (including Myanmar) have not enacted specific
legislation prohibiting genocide, and that this constitutes subsequent State practice within the
meaning of Article 31, paragraph (3) (b), of the Vienna Convention on the Law of Treaties. The
Court observes, however, that only 35 of the States referenced by Myanmar are parties to the
Genocide Convention, and that the other 118 States parties have criminalized genocide. The fact that
some States parties may not have enacted such legislation does not constitute subsequent State
practice within the meaning of Article 31, paragraph (3) (b), of the Vienna Convention or exonerate
Myanmar of its obligation to legislate.
567. The negotiating history indicates that the position advanced by Myanmar was not
accepted by the drafters of the Convention. During the debates of the Ad Hoc Committee on
Genocide, some States noted that “the facts constituting genocide are already dealt with by domestic
criminal laws (murder, etc.)” (Report of the Ad Hoc Committee on Genocide, UN doc. E/794
(24 May 1948), p. 27; see also p. 28). In response, two States observed that the introduction into
national legislation of specific laws for the suppression and prevention of genocide was “an
elementary condition” for the suppression of the crime of genocide (ibid., p. 28). A proposal to
include the terms “as may be necessary” to the requirement to enact legislation was similarly
withdrawn, following opposition by several States on the grounds that the text “weakened the
compulsory nature of the Article by making it possible for any State wishing to evade the obligation
to take the legislative measures provided for in the Article, to declare that it did not consider them
necessary” (Ad Hoc Committee on Genocide, Summary record of the 19th meeting,
UN doc. E/AC.25/SR.19 (27 April 1948), p. 6). This view was reinforced in the Sixth Committee of
the General Assembly, where several States noted that Article V obligated States to introduce the
definition of genocide and the penalties envisaged for it into their own penal codes (United Nations
General Assembly, Sixth Committee, 3rd Session, 93rd meeting, UN doc. A/C.6/SR.93
(6 November 1948), p. 325).
568. The Court further observes that other treaties that mandate the domestic incorporation of
particular criminal offences have been interpreted to require adoption of legislation prohibiting the
relevant crime in all its elements. These include the Convention against Torture and Other Cruel,
Inhuman or Degrading Treatment or Punishment of 10 December 1984 (United Nations, Committee
against Torture, General Comment No. 2: Implementation of article 2 by States Parties,
UN doc. CAT/C/GC/2 (24 January 2008), para. 8) and the International Convention for the
Protection of All Persons from Enforced Disappearances of 20 December 2006 (see United Nations,
Committee on Enforced Disappearances, Concluding observations on the additional information
submitted by Spain under article 29 (4) of the Convention, adopted on 22 September 2021,
UN doc. CED/C/ESP/OAI/1 (4 November 2021), para. 6). As the Committee against Torture has

- 163 observed, defining torture as a distinct crime promotes the Convention’s aim by emphasizing “the
special gravity of the crime”, while serious discrepancies between the Convention definition and
domestic law create the potential for impunity (United Nations, Committee against Torture, General
Comment No. 2: Implementation of article 2 by States Parties, UN doc. CAT/C/GC/2 (24 January
2008), paras. 9 and 11). Similar concerns are present here.
569. A central purpose of the Genocide Convention is to recognize genocide as a particularly
grave and distinct crime, and to secure accountability for its breach. The Court considers that the
ordinary meaning of Article V, understood in light of the Convention’s context, object and purpose,
and taking into account the negotiating history, requires that States parties adopt legislation
criminalizing genocide as a distinct offence which encompasses all the elements of Articles II and III
of the Convention. This includes, in particular, incorporating the element of genocidal intent. In the
Court’s view, failure to enact legislation that prohibits acts of genocide as set forth in these Articles,
or adopting legislation that addresses only some of those acts or applies only to some individuals,
breaches Article V of the Genocide Convention. Genocide cannot be punished merely through the
sanction of murder, rape or other crimes, which do not comprise all the elements of genocide. Prior
to the enactment of the 2021 amendment to Myanmar’s criminal code, therefore, Myanmar was in
breach of its obligation to enact necessary legislation under Article V.
570. The Court now turns to the question whether Myanmar’s enactment of legislation in 2021
satisfied its obligation under Article V. Section 311A of the Penal Code enacts a crime qualified as
“genocide”, which is defined in terms of the five material acts under Article II of the Convention,
together “with the intent to destroy, in whole or in part, a national, ethnical, racial or religious group”
(Myanmar’s Fourth report on the implementation of provisional measures, Annex 3). The provision
is articulated in terms generally comparable to the definition in Article II of the Genocide
Convention. Section 311B of the Penal Code imposes the death penalty and a fine for acts of genocide
involving killing of members of the group, and life imprisonment and a fine for acts of genocide
involving the other material acts (ibid.).
571. Although The Gambia argued that the new legislation does not apply to members of the
Myanmar military, the Respondent indicated to the satisfaction of the Court that the crime of
genocide in Section 311A applies to members of the military by virtue of Sections 3 (e), 71-72 and
128-129 of the Defence Services Act 1959. While the Commander-in-Chief’s power to grant pardons
under the Myanmar Constitution could be exercised in a manner contrary to the duty to punish under
the Convention, the Court is of the view that the existence of that possibility, in itself, does not breach
the duty to enact legislation.
572. The Court considers, however, that a material disparity arises between the new law and
Article II (d) of the Convention. Section 311A defines this actus reus of genocide as applying to
whoever “[i]mposes measures, not in accordance with any existing laws, intended to prevent births
within the group” (Myanmar’s Fourth report on the implementation of provisional measures,
Annex 3 (emphasis added)). This limitation allows Myanmar to impose the very measures which
Article II (d) prohibits and creates the possibility that conduct classified as genocide under the
Convention escapes punishment under Myanmar’s domestic law.

- 164 573. The Court further observes that the duty to enact legislation obliges a State to give effect
to the entirety of Articles II and III, as noted above. Section 311A of the Penal Code addresses only
the crime of genocide itself; it does not, on its face, implement the provisions of Article III (b) to (e)
of the Convention, including, for example, conspiracy and the prohibition of direct and public
incitement to commit genocide. Although in some national jurisdictions the application of general
criminal provisions on offences ancillary to a domestic crime of genocide may be sufficient to meet
a State’s obligations under Article III (b) to (e) of the Genocide Convention — provided they
encompass all the elements of those offences — no evidence before the Court suggests that
Myanmar’s domestic legislation is in conformity with these provisions of Article III.
574. The Court considers that the above characteristics of Section 311A of the Penal Code fail
to meet Myanmar’s obligations under Article V to enact “the necessary legislation to give effect to
the provisions of the present Convention, and, in particular, to provide effective penalties for persons
guilty of genocide or any of the other acts enumerated in article III”. Myanmar is therefore in breach
of the obligation to adopt necessary legislation under Article V of the Genocide Convention.
VII. LEGAL CONSEQUENCES AND REMEDIES
575. Having concluded that Myanmar has breached obligations under the Genocide
Convention, it is for the Court now to determine, in light of The Gambia’s final submissions, what
consequences flow from these internationally wrongful acts that give rise to Myanmar’s international
responsibility.
576. The Gambia submits that, as a State party to the Genocide Convention, it is entitled to
seek from the Court a declaration that Myanmar has violated its obligations under the Convention,
and to request cessation of these violations, assurances and guarantees of non-repetition, and
reparation. Myanmar challenges both the jurisdiction of the Court to order reparation and
The Gambia’s standing to request remedies beyond declaratory relief and an order to cease alleged
ongoing violations of the Convention. The Court will first address the preliminary questions of
jurisdiction (Section A) and standing (Section B) raised by Myanmar.
A. Jurisdiction to order reparation
577. Myanmar argues that jurisdiction under Article IX of the Genocide Convention does not
encompass claims for reparation in a case where genocide is alleged to have been committed by a
State party against its own population. It submits that such a situation does not involve inter-State
responsibility, to which the Court’s jurisdiction is limited, and that such remedies are not recognized
in customary international law. In Myanmar’s view, the fact that the drafters of the Convention
considered and rejected a provision that would have expressly provided for redress determined by
the United Nations for victims of genocide committed by their own government indicates that such
redress is not otherwise available under the Genocide Convention (United Nations,

- 165 Secretary-General, “Draft Convention on the Crime of Genocide”, UN doc. E/447 (26 June 1947),
pp. 47-49). It further maintains that the Court’s 2022 Judgment on preliminary objections (see
paragraph 17 above) established only the existence of jurisdiction, not its scope, and poses no
res judicata bar to the Court’s consideration of this challenge.
578. The Gambia counters that the 2022 Judgment establishing jurisdiction necessarily
extends to the remedies sought and has the force of res judicata. The Gambia emphasizes that the
2022 Judgment did not exclude from the merits phase any claims contained in the Application,
including its claims for remedies. Reparation for injury resulting from a breach of an international
obligation, it maintains, falls squarely within the concept of Myanmar’s “responsibility” under
international law, which the Court is called upon to decide. The Applicant further argues that the
absence of an explicit provision on reparation in the Convention cannot be read as excluding such an
obligation. According to The Gambia, the obligation to make reparation in favour of victims of
genocide, irrespective of their nationality, is well established as a matter of customary international
law.

*

*

579. Article IX of the Convention confers jurisdiction on the Court over disputes between
contracting parties relating to the “interpretation, application or fulfilment” of the Convention,
“including those relating to the responsibility of a State for genocide or for any of the other acts
enumerated in article III”. As the Court confirmed in the 2007 Bosnia Judgment, where jurisdiction
is founded on Article IX of the Convention, the Court may determine not only whether a party
breached its obligations under the Convention, but also the legal consequences that would flow from
such breaches under the rules on the responsibility of States for internationally wrongful acts (I.C.J.
Reports 2007 (I), p. 105, para. 149; see also LaGrand (Germany v. United States of America),
Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2001, p. 485, para. 48).
580. The Court recalls that its 2022 Judgment on preliminary objections confirmed that it had
jurisdiction, on the basis of Article IX of the Convention, to entertain the Application filed by
The Gambia. In that Application, The Gambia requested the Court to adjudge and declare that
Myanmar, inter alia, must perform the obligation of reparation in the interest of the victims of
genocide and provide assurances and guarantees of non-repetition (Application of The Gambia,
para. 112). Accordingly, the Court considers that its jurisdiction encompasses all aspects of
The Gambia’s final submissions, including its claims for remedies.
B. The Gambia’s standing to request remedies
581. The Court now turns to the question whether The Gambia has standing to claim remedies
for Myanmar’s violations of the Genocide Convention. At the outset, the Court notes that the Parties
agree that the Applicant may seek declaratory relief and an order of cessation (see paragraph 576
above). Myanmar challenges only the legal basis for The Gambia’s claims for reparation and
guarantees of non-repetition, arguing that only injured States may claim these remedies.

- 166 582. The Parties disagree as to whether the entitlement of a State to invoke responsibility for
violations of obligations erga omnes partes also entails a right to seek assurances and guarantees of
non-repetition, as well as reparation on behalf of beneficiaries of the breached obligation. They point
to Article 48, paragraph 2, of the ILC Articles on Responsibility of States for Internationally
Wrongful Acts (hereinafter the “ILC Articles on State Responsibility”), which addresses a State’s
invocation of responsibility for violations of obligations erga omnes as follows:
“Any State entitled to invoke responsibility under paragraph 1 may claim from
the responsible State:
(a) cessation of the internationally wrongful act, and assurances and guarantees of
non-repetition in accordance with article 30; and
(b) performance of the obligation of reparation in accordance with the preceding
articles, in the interest of the injured State or of the beneficiaries of the obligation
breached.” (ILC, Responsibility of States for Internationally Wrongful Acts,
Yearbook of the International Law Commission, 2001, Vol. II, Part Two.)
583. Myanmar maintains that Article 48, paragraph 2, does not reflect customary international
law but is an example of progressive development of international law. With respect to reparations,
it further argues that individuals are not entitled to reparation under international law outside special
treaty régimes and that no such provision exists under the Genocide Convention. Accordingly,
Myanmar contends that even if Article 48, paragraph 2, were to reflect customary international law,
The Gambia cannot claim reparation where the ultimate beneficiaries themselves lack such
entitlement under the Convention. Finally, the Respondent submits that The Gambia has not
demonstrated that it is acting in the interest of the beneficiaries.
584. The Gambia counters that the ability to seek assurances and guarantees of non-repetition,
as well as performance of the obligation of reparation, is reflected in Article 48, paragraph 2, and
logically follows from the entitlement of States to invoke responsibility for breaches of obligations
erga omnes partes. It maintains that this principle reflects customary international law and is
acknowledged in the Court’s Advisory Opinion on the Obligations of States in respect of Climate
Change of 23 July 2025. The Gambia notes that assurances and guarantees of non-repetition have
been defined by the ILC as an obligation of the responsible State, and can be claimed by a non-injured
State. The Applicant also denies that it is precluded from seeking performance of the obligation of
reparation on the ground that the beneficiaries cannot themselves claim reparation. According to
The Gambia, a State with an interest in compliance with the primary obligation necessarily has an
interest in seeking assurances and guarantees of non-repetition and ensuring performance of the
secondary obligation of reparation in favour of the legal subjects concerned. Finally, The Gambia
submits that it is acting both in its own interest and in that of the Rohingya victims of Myanmar’s
alleged acts of genocide, for whose sole and direct benefit the requested measures are sought.

*

*

- 167 585. The Court stated in its 2022 Judgment on preliminary objections that
“any State party to the Genocide Convention may invoke the responsibility of another
State party, including through the institution of proceedings before the Court, with a
view to determining the alleged failure to comply with its obligations erga omnes partes
under the Convention and to bringing that failure to an end” (I.C.J. Reports 2022 (II),
p. 517, para. 112).
586. The Court explained that this entitlement derives from the common interest of all States
parties to the Convention in ensuring compliance by each State party with its obligations thereunder
(2022 Judgment on preliminary objections, I.C.J. Reports 2022 (II), p. 516, para. 109). Thus, the
Court recognized the existence of a legal interest of any State party in compliance by all other States
parties with obligations erga omnes partes. States holding such a legal interest may bring claims
against a State in breach of a collective obligation, even if they are not themselves injured.
587. With respect to the question of remedies, the Court has clarified that the position of
non-injured and injured States differs:
“While a non-injured State may pursue a claim against a State in breach of a
collective obligation, it may not claim reparation for itself. Rather, it may only make a
claim for cessation of the wrongful act and assurances and guarantees of non-repetition,
as well as for the performance of the obligation of reparation in the interest of the injured
State or of the beneficiaries of the obligation breached.” (Obligations of States in respect
of Climate Change, Advisory Opinion of 23 July 2025, para. 443.)
The Court thus recognized that States seeking to protect obligations erga omnes partes may request
assurances and guarantees of non-repetition, as well as the performance of the obligation of
reparation, “in the interest of . . . the beneficiaries of the obligation breached”.
588. In this regard, with respect to reparation, the Court notes that it has long been established
that where obligations involve the protection of human rights, the individuals concerned should be
regarded as the ultimate beneficiaries (see ILC Articles on State Responsibility, Yearbook of the
International Law Commission, 2001, Vol. II, Part Two, p. 95, paragraph 3 of the commentary on
Article 33). The Court has previously recognized the responsibility of States to provide reparation
for damage caused to all natural and legal persons. In its Advisory Opinion on the Legal
Consequences of the Construction of a Wall in the Occupied Palestinian Territory, the Court stated
that
“Israel is accordingly under an obligation to return the land, orchards, olive
groves and other immovable property seized from any natural or legal person for
purposes of construction of the wall in the Occupied Palestinian Territory. In the event
that such restitution should prove to be materially impossible, Israel has an obligation
to compensate the persons in question for the damage suffered. The Court considers that
Israel also has an obligation to compensate, in accordance with the applicable rules of
international law, all natural or legal persons having suffered any form of material
damage as a result of the wall’s construction.” (Advisory Opinion, I.C.J. Reports
2004 (I), p. 198, para. 153.)

- 168 The Court similarly recognized an obligation to provide full reparation for damage caused “to all
natural or legal persons” in its Advisory Opinion on the Legal Consequences arising from the
Policies and Practices of Israel in the Occupied Palestinian Territory, including East Jerusalem
(I.C.J. Reports 2024 (III), p. 846, para. 269).
589. While the Genocide Convention protects certain groups, like other human rights treaties,
individuals are also beneficiaries of the protected right — in this case, not to be subject to genocide.
As the Court has affirmed, the Genocide Convention “was manifestly adopted for a purely
humanitarian and civilizing purpose”, and “its object on the one hand is to safeguard the very
existence of certain human groups and on the other to confirm and endorse the most elementary
principles of morality” (Reservations to the Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of the
Crime of Genocide, Advisory Opinion, I.C.J. Reports 1951, p. 23). The Court accordingly considers
that remedies in the interest of the protected group and the individual victims of genocide serve the
object and purpose of the Convention and advance the collective interest of the States parties. Indeed,
to allow the harm inflicted on a protected group by acts of genocide to go unaddressed would
undermine the raison d’être of the Convention. The Gambia accordingly may seek the performance
of the obligation of reparation, in the interest of the beneficiaries of the obligation breached,
consistent with its standing to secure compliance with obligations erga omnes partes.
590. In the Court’s view, therefore, in light of its legal interest in protecting the rights enshrined
in the Genocide Convention, The Gambia is entitled to invoke Myanmar’s obligation to offer
assurances and guarantees of non-repetition, and to make reparation in the interest of the Rohingya,
as a group and as individuals, who were harmed by acts in violation of the Genocide Convention for
which Myanmar is responsible.
C. Appropriate remedies
591. Having concluded that it has jurisdiction to entertain The Gambia’s claim for remedies
and that The Gambia is entitled to make such requests, the Court now turns to the remedies sought.
In its final submissions, The Gambia requests the Court to order Myanmar to cease ongoing
internationally wrongful acts, to provide assurances and guarantees of non-repetition, and to provide
reparation for the victims of genocide and other punishable acts, including restitution and
compensation (see paragraph 63 above). The Court will address each of these requests in turn.
1. Cessation
592. A State responsible for an internationally wrongful act is under an obligation to cease that
act if it is continuing and the obligation breached remains in force. Both Parties accept that cessation
is an appropriate remedy when responsibility is invoked by a State seeking to secure the compliance
of another State with its obligations erga omnes partes. In such circumstances, “[t]he common
interest in compliance with the relevant obligations under the Convention . . . implies the entitlement
of each State party to the Convention to make a claim concerning the cessation of an alleged breach
by another State party” (Questions relating to the Obligation to Prosecute or Extradite (Belgium v.
Senegal), Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2012 (II), p. 450, para. 69).

- 169 593. In its final submissions, The Gambia submits that Myanmar must cease any
internationally wrongful act under Articles I, III (c), IV, V and VI of the Convention (see
paragraph 63 above). In particular, the Applicant requests that the Court order the Respondent to
“(i) take immediate and effective steps to suppress and prevent any direct and
public incitement to commit genocide;
(ii) take immediate and effective steps to enact specific and complete genocide
criminal legislation applicable to all individuals, addressing all the prohibited
acts under Article III of the Convention and providing for adequate and severe
penalties;
(iii) take immediate and effective steps to submit to trial before an independent and
effective tribunal, including before an international penal tribunal, those
members of its armed forces, police and other security forces or any other
persons within its jurisdiction who are suspected on probable grounds of
having committed acts of genocide referred to in paragraphs 1 and 2 and
punishable under Article III”.
594. Myanmar denies that it is in breach of any of its obligations under the Genocide
Convention. It therefore maintains that there cannot be any question of cessation of a violation.

*

*

595. Cessation may be ordered only in respect of breaches of Myanmar’s obligations under
the Convention that are continuing. With respect to The Gambia’s request for cessation regarding
direct and public incitement to commit genocide, the Court observes that its finding of genocide is
limited to the period around the 2017 clearance operations and that Myanmar’s violation of the
prohibition on incitement is subsumed in that finding of genocide. Since neither the genocide nor the
related incitement has been found to be ongoing, the Court cannot order cessation in this regard. The
Court observes that this finding is made only with respect to the cessation of breaches of obligations
found by the Court. It does not impact the Respondent’s continuing duty as a State party to the
Convention to perform its obligations to prevent and punish acts of direct and public incitement to
commit genocide pursuant to Articles I and III of the Genocide Convention.
596. Myanmar’s failure to enact legislation to give full effect to its obligations under the
Convention and its failure to investigate, prosecute and punish persons who committed acts
punishable under Article III of the Convention constitute ongoing breaches of the Genocide
Convention (see 2007 Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 235, para. 465, and p. 238,
para. 471 (6); see also mutatis mutandis, Questions relating to the Obligation to Prosecute or
Extradite (Belgium v. Senegal), Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2012 (II), p. 461, paras. 119-121; Armed
Activities on the Territory of the Congo (Democratic Republic of the Congo v. Uganda), Reparations,
Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2022 (I), p. 133, para. 390). The Court therefore considers it appropriate to
order the cessation of these continuing breaches. Myanmar is accordingly obliged to enact legislation

- 170 giving full effect to its obligations under the Genocide Convention, and to take immediate and
effective steps to investigate all persons reasonably suspected of having committed acts prohibited
by Article III of the Convention in connection with the 2017 clearance operations, and, if warranted,
to prosecute and punish them.
2. Assurances and guarantees of non-repetition
597. The Gambia maintains that assurances and guarantees of non-repetition are appropriate
remedies under Article 48, paragraph 2 (a), of the ILC Articles on State Responsibility when a State
is seeking to enforce obligations erga omnes partes (see paragraph 584 above) and that the
circumstances before the Court warrant such measures. In this regard, The Gambia notes, inter alia,
the gravity of the obligations at issue and the severity of the breach, Myanmar’s refusal to
acknowledge the facts or its responsibility, its lack of co-operation with United Nations investigative
mechanisms and destruction of evidence, and its ongoing refusal to recognize the Rohingya as such,
as well as the overthrow, during the proceedings, of the constitutional order by the military junta
responsible for the clearance operations.
598. The Applicant asks the Court to order Myanmar to offer assurances and guarantees of
non-repetition by “providing full and equal citizenship to all members of the Rohingya group who
are present in Myanmar or have been displaced due to the events for which Myanmar bears
responsibility under the Convention” (see paragraph 63 above). It notes that it would welcome any
additional assurance and guarantee of non-repetition that the Court deems appropriate, and highlights
that the United Nations General Assembly has considered that “[e]nsuring effective civilian control
of military and security forces” is an appropriate guarantee of non-repetition for a State responsible
for gross violations of international human rights law (United Nations General Assembly,
resolution 60/147, “Basic Principles and Guidelines on the Right to a Remedy and Reparation for
Victims of Gross Violations of International Human Rights Law and serious Violations of
International Humanitarian Law”, adopted on 16 December 2005, UN doc. A/RES/60/147
(21 March 2006) (hereinafter “Basic Principles and Guidelines”), para. 23).
599. As noted above, Myanmar maintains that Article 48, paragraph 2 (a), of the ILC Articles
on State Responsibility does not reflect customary international law (see paragraph 583). It further
argues that, in any event, breaches of obligations under the Genocide Convention do not
automatically constitute special circumstances warranting assurances and guarantees of
non-repetition and that no such circumstances are present here. In this regard, Myanmar highlights
its compliance with the Court’s Order on provisional measures and its conduct throughout the
proceedings. Myanmar also contends that the particular measures requested by The Gambia are not
“appropriate” within the meaning of Article 30 (b) of the ILC Articles on State Responsibility and
would go beyond what is required to avoid repetition of any alleged breach.

*

*

600. The Court recalls that The Gambia, in light of its legal interest in protecting the rights
enshrined in the Genocide Convention, is entitled to invoke Myanmar’s obligation to offer assurances

- 171 and guarantees of non-repetition (see paragraphs 587 and 590 above). In this regard, the Court has
recognized that “in appropriate circumstances, a responsible State could be required to offer
appropriate assurances and guarantees of non-repetition” (Obligations of States in respect of Climate
Change, Advisory Opinion of 23 July 2025, para. 448; Dispute regarding Navigational and Related
Rights (Costa Rica v. Nicaragua), Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2009, p. 267, para. 150). These remedies
may involve “preventive measures to be taken by the responsible State designed to avoid the
repetition of the breach” (see ILC Articles on State Responsibility, Yearbook of the International
Law Commission, 2001, Vol. II, Part Two, p. 90, paragraph 12 of the commentary on Article 30).
The obligation to offer appropriate assurances and guarantees of non-repetition generally depends on
the nature of the obligation breached, the gravity of the wrongful act and the risk of repetition.
601. With respect to the nature of the obligation breached and the gravity of the wrongful act,
the Court observes that obligations under the Genocide Convention are erga omnes partes and that
the prohibition of genocide has a peremptory character under international law (2007 Bosnia
Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 111, para. 161). In the present case, it is the State itself that has
been found responsible for genocide. The Court considers that violations of the Genocide Convention
are of exceptional gravity and that, in the present case, they also arose in a context of long-standing
and deeply entrenched hostility towards the Rohingya as a group.
602. Turning to the likelihood of repetition, the Court considers particularly important the fact
that Myanmar has failed to investigate and, if warranted, prosecute and punish persons suspected of
having committed acts prohibited by Article III of the Convention, has provided no remedies to the
victims, including the large number who remain refugees in Bangladesh and who in Myanmar remain
subject to continuing and structural exclusion, and continues to deny that the harms at issue — which
the Court has found result from violations of the Genocide Convention — ever occurred. Moreover,
Myanmar has not presented to the Court concrete measures aimed specifically at recognizing and
ensuring the right of the Rohingya to exist as a protected group under the Genocide Convention, as
the Court previously underscored in its Order of 23 January 2020 indicating provisional measures
(I.C.J. Reports 2020, p. 27, para. 73). This situation is in contrast to that in LaGrand, where the
respondent State acknowledged and apologized for the breach, and presented the Court with
measures that it was undertaking in order to secure compliance (LaGrand (Germany v. United States
of America), Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2001, p. 513, para. 124). The likelihood of repetition also
differs from that in the 2007 Bosnia Judgment (I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 235, para. 466). The Court
considers that, in view of the circumstances before it, it is appropriate to require Myanmar to provide
assurances and guarantees of non-repetition, and that Myanmar is under an obligation to prevent
similar violations from occurring in the future.
603. The Court turns to the question of what guarantees are appropriate. The Gambia requests
that Myanmar be ordered to provide full and equal citizenship to all members of the Rohingya group.
The Court has concluded that denial of citizenship for the Rohingya has been central to the overall
pattern of discrimination and vulnerability that it has identified (see, for example, paragraphs 142
and 147-149 above) and, as such, is a consideration in the determination of genocidal intent (see
paragraphs 400-401 above). The Court considers, however, that ordering the provision of citizenship
for all members of the Rohingya group is beyond the scope of appropriate assurances and guarantees
of non-repetition for acts of genocide in the circumstances before it.

- 172 604. The Court observes that other remedies ordered by the Court in the present case, including
the measures of cessation requiring Myanmar to enact legislation giving full effect to its obligations
under the Convention and to pursue genuine and effective investigations and prosecutions and
impose effective penalties (see paragraph 596 above), and the restitution measure to ensure the
protection of the Rohingya against discrimination and persecution, including with respect to access
to citizenship (see paragraphs 615-616 below), also constitute appropriate guarantees of
non-repetition. The Court considers, however, that, beyond these measures, Myanmar is under an
obligation to prevent similar violations from occurring in the future. In this regard, additional
appropriate guarantees may include, inter alia, legal and institutional reform to ensure compliance
with relevant international human rights law, international humanitarian law and international
criminal law obligations, including adequate avenues for redress; training of security forces
personnel in these obligations; and ensuring effective civilian control of the security forces (Basic
Principles and Guidelines, para. 23). The Court considers that beyond the other measures ordered by
it, the choice of additional means must be left to Myanmar.
3. Reparation
605. With respect to the question of reparation, The Gambia submits that, as a consequence of
Myanmar’s responsibility for violations of the Genocide Convention, Myanmar is obliged to provide
full reparation to all Rohingya victims of its punishable acts. The Gambia maintains that full
reparation, including restitution and compensation, is indispensable to effectively repairing the
trauma suffered both individually and collectively by surviving Rohingya.
606. The Court has already determined that a request for reparation falls within the scope of
its jurisdiction under Article IX of the Convention and that The Gambia has standing to request the
performance of Myanmar’s obligation of reparation in the interest of the Rohingya (see
paragraph 590). The Court will now analyse whether such a request should be granted and, if so,
what form it should take.
607. The Court recalls that it is well established in international law that “the breach of an
engagement involves an obligation to make reparation in an adequate form” (Factory at Chorzów,
Jurisdiction, Judgment No. 8, 1927, P.C.I.J., Series A, No. 9, p. 21) and that “reparation must, as far
as possible, wipe out all the consequences of the illegal act and reestablish the situation which would,
in all probability, have existed if that act had not been committed” (Factory at Chorzów, Merits,
Judgment No. 13, 1928, P.C.I.J., Series A, No. 17, p. 47; see also Article 31 of the ILC Articles on
State Responsibility). To the extent that restitution proves to be materially impossible, reparation
may take “the form of compensation or satisfaction, or even both” (Pulp Mills on the River Uruguay
(Argentina v. Uruguay), Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2010 (I), pp. 103-104, para. 273; see also Certain
Activities Carried Out by Nicaragua in the Border Area (Costa Rica v. Nicaragua), Compensation,
Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2018 (I), p. 26, para. 31). Thus, full reparation for the injury caused by the
internationally wrongful act shall take the form of restitution, compensation and satisfaction, either
singly or in combination (Obligations of States in respect of Climate Change, Advisory Opinion of
23 July 2025, para. 450; Article 34 of the ILC Articles on State Responsibility).
608. For a request for reparation to be granted, the Court must ascertain whether, and to what
extent, there is “a sufficiently direct and certain causal nexus between the wrongful act . . . and the
injury suffered by the Applicant, consisting of all damage of any type, material or moral” (2007
Bosnia Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 234, para. 462). Therefore, reparation requires that a

- 173 causal relationship be established between the wrongful act of a State and particular damage suffered
by the injured State, group or individuals (see Obligations of States in respect of Climate Change,
Advisory Opinion of 23 July 2025, para. 449). The Court has found that the internationally wrongful
acts for which Myanmar has been found responsible resulted in injury to the Rohingya, as a group
and as individuals, and that this injury was caused by Myanmar. The Court thus finds that Myanmar
has an obligation to make reparation.
609. The Court recalls that because reparation constitutes a remedy for a harm suffered, States
seeking to enforce a common interest in compliance with obligations erga omnes or
erga omnes partes, such as The Gambia, are in a different situation than a directly injured or a
specially affected State claiming reparation. While a non-injured or non-affected State may pursue a
claim against another State for breach of a collective obligation, it may not claim reparation for itself.
Rather, it may only “make a claim for . . . the performance of the obligation of reparation in the
interest of the injured State or of the beneficiaries of the obligation breached” (Obligations of States
in respect of Climate Change, Advisory Opinion of 23 July 2025, para. 443; see Article 48,
paragraph 2 (b), of the ILC Articles on State Responsibility; see also paragraphs 586-587 above). As
noted, the Court has recognized that the obligation to provide full reparation may extend beyond the
obligation to provide reparation to injured States to encompass individuals harmed by a State’s
wrongful acts, including those subject to the State’s own jurisdiction (see Legal Consequences
arising from the Policies and Practices of Israel in the Occupied Palestinian Territory, including
East Jerusalem, Advisory Opinion, I.C.J. Reports 2024 (III), p. 846, para. 269; Legal Consequences
of the Construction of a Wall in the Occupied Palestinian Territory, Advisory Opinion, I.C.J. Reports
2004 (I), p. 198, para. 152; see paragraph 588 above).
610. As a result, in the Court’s view, while The Gambia cannot seek reparation for itself, it
may make a claim for the performance of the obligation of full reparation, including restitution,
compensation and satisfaction, by Myanmar, in the interest of the Rohingya, as a group and as
individual victims, who are the beneficiaries of the obligation breached (see Armed Activities on the
Territory of the Congo (Democratic Republic of the Congo v. Uganda), Reparations, Judgment,
I.C.J. Reports 2022 (I), p. 137, para. 408; see paragraph 590 above). Reparation should be
proportional to the gravity of the violations and the harm suffered. The Gambia accordingly may
seek, and Myanmar is obliged to provide, adequate, effective and prompt reparation for the harms
suffered by the Rohingya population as a group and as individuals as a result of Myanmar’s wrongful
acts. The Court thus turns to the question of what measures of reparation may be appropriate.
(a) Restitution
611. The Gambia submits that reparation should primarily take the form of restitution, and that
Myanmar should be ordered to compensate and satisfy the harm, loss and injury suffered by the
Rohingya only to the extent that it is not capable of redress by full restitution. It requests the following
measures by way of restitution:
“(1) allow the safe and dignified return to their place of residence of displaced
members of the Rohingya group, whether they are displaced within Myanmar
or abroad;
(2) return to the Rohingya their individual and collective property, including their
land, houses, places of worship and communal life, fields, livestock and
crops, or replace them in kind;

- 174 (3) allow and facilitate the safe and dignified reunification of families;
(4) provide for the rehabilitation of the physically or mentally injured members
of the Rohingya group; such rehabilitation must include adequate medical and
psychological care as well as legal and social services;
(5) facilitate the search for the disappeared and assist in the recovery,
identification and reburial of the bodies of those killed in accordance with the
expressed or presumed wishes of the victims and in accordance with the
cultural and religious practices of the Rohingya;
(6) ensure the protection of the Rohingya against discrimination and persecution;
(7) ensure the right of the Rohingya to identify as such;
(8) ensure the liberty and freedom of movement of the Rohingya within
Myanmar and remove any restriction on their place of residence; and
(9) remove any restriction or discrimination on the employment or access to
livelihoods of the Rohingya.” (See paragraph 63 above.)
612. Myanmar maintains that these and other remedies are not relevant to breaches of the
Genocide Convention.

*

*

613. Restitution involves the re-establishment of the situation that existed before the wrongful
act was committed (Factory at Chorzów, Merits, Judgment No. 13, 1928, P.C.I.J., Series A, No. 17,
p. 47). As the Court noted in its Advisory Opinion on the Legal Consequences arising from the
Policies and Practices of Israel in the Occupied Palestinian Territory, including East Jerusalem, in
light of the large-scale displacement of a population and dispossession of their property, appropriate
restitution may include allowing the displaced population to return to their original place of
residence, and the return of land, other immovable property and seized assets (I.C.J. Reports
2024 (III), p. 846, para. 270; see also paragraph 588 above).
614. The Court has found that the internationally wrongful acts for which Myanmar has been
found responsible resulted in injury to the Rohingya as a group and as individuals and that this injury
was caused by Myanmar. The Court recalls that the harms to the Rohingya that resulted from the
Respondent’s wrongful acts include numerous killings (see paragraphs 250-253 above), including of
persons whose fate or whereabouts remains unknown to family members, as well as the large-scale
displacement of the Rohingya population to Bangladesh, extensive burning and destruction of homes
and other movable and immovable property, and large-scale sexual and other violence against the
Rohingya population (see paragraphs 320-337 and 366-371 above).
615. In light of these harms, which resulted from the Respondent’s breach of its obligations
under the Genocide Convention, and considering the arguments of the Parties and the entirety of the

- 175 evidence before the Court, the Court determines that the following measures of restitution by
Myanmar are appropriate in this case:
(1) allow and facilitate the safe, voluntary and dignified return to their place of residence and
reunification of families for the Rohingya who were displaced as a result of the Respondent’s
wrongful acts, whether they were displaced within Myanmar or abroad;
(2) return or replace in kind all individual and collective property of the Rohingya that has been lost
or damaged as a result of the Respondent’s wrongful acts;
(3) facilitate the search for the disappeared and assist in the recovery, identification and reburial with
dignity, in accordance with Rohingya traditions, of the bodies of the Rohingya who were killed
as a result of the Respondent’s wrongful acts;
(4) provide for the physical and psychological rehabilitation of the Rohingya who were physically
or mentally injured as a result of the Respondent’s wrongful acts;
(5) ensure the protection of the Rohingya from discrimination and persecution.
616. The Court considers these measures necessary to adequately redress the harms suffered
by the protected group. The first three measures seek to redress killings and disappearances inflicted
on the Rohingya and the displacement of persons, including through the destruction of their property.
The fourth measure regarding physical and psychological rehabilitation is also appropriate in light
of the large-scale serious bodily and mental harm inflicted on the protected group, including as a
result of rape and other forms of sexual violence. Finally, the Court considers that it is necessary to
ensure protection of the Rohingya in Myanmar from persecution and discrimination, including
protection from discrimination in accessing citizenship and with respect to freedom of movement.
This measure also constitutes a form of assurances and guarantees of non-repetition (see
paragraph 604 above).
617. The Gambia requests the additional specific measures of ensuring the right of the
Rohingya to identify as such, ensuring the liberty and freedom of movement of the Rohingya within
Myanmar, and removing any restriction or discrimination on employment or access to livelihoods.
The Court considers that to the extent that these measures directly address the harms resulting from
the Respondent’s wrongful acts and provide appropriate assurances and guarantees of non-repetition,
notably with respect to protection from discrimination and persecution, they are encompassed by the
measures indicated above.
(b) Compensation and full reparation
618. As a further measure of reparation, The Gambia requests the Court to adjudge and declare
that, as a consequence of its responsibility for violations of the Convention, Myanmar “shall . . .
compensate, and provide any additional forms of reparation, for any harm, loss or injury suffered by
the Rohingya victims that is not capable of full reparation by restitution” (see paragraph 63 above).
The Gambia submits that, at this stage, the Court is called upon to pronounce only on the principle
of compensation, while the quantum and mechanisms for distribution to victims would be determined
at a subsequent phase, should the Parties fail to agree. During the oral proceedings, The Gambia also

- 176 suggested that the Court could order Myanmar to transmit to it, within a reasonable time frame,
details of the restitution and compensation measures it intends to take or has taken, so as to ensure
its fulfilment of the obligation of reparation at a later stage of the proceedings.
619. With respect to compensation, Myanmar reiterated its objection to The Gambia’s
standing to seek reparation, including compensation, in the interest of the beneficiaries. It also
contended that allowing a State to demand that reparation be made directly to the beneficiaries of the
obligation “would raise further questions of to whom and how compensation payments [c]ould be
distributed”.
620. The Court recalls that, like restitution, compensation is a form of reparation.
Compensable losses, which correspond to financially assessable damage, are those suffered not only
by an injured State but also by the beneficiaries of the obligation breached. Indeed, the Court has
observed that any reparation is intended, as far as possible, to benefit all those who suffered injury
resulting from internationally wrongful acts (see Ahmadou Sadio Diallo (Republic of Guinea v.
Democratic Republic of the Congo), Compensation, Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2012 (I), p. 344,
para. 57; Armed Activities on the Territory of the Congo (Democratic Republic of the Congo v.
Uganda), Reparations, Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2022 (I), p. 137, para. 408). Thus, when restitution
is materially impossible, a State may have an obligation to compensate natural or legal persons or
populations that have suffered material damage (Legal Consequences arising from the Policies and
Practices of Israel in the Occupied Palestinian Territory, including East Jerusalem, Advisory
Opinion, I.C.J. Reports 2024 (III), p. 847, para. 271; see paragraph 607 above).
621. The Court has already determined that The Gambia may seek performance of the
obligation to provide compensation, as a form of reparation, in the interest of the injured Rohingya
group and its members (see paragraph 610). The Gambia seeks compensation to the extent that
fulfilling the measures of restitution ordered by the Court above may prove materially impossible.
Like the measures of restitution themselves, the compensation sought by The Gambia is not in its
own interest, but in the common interest of States parties to the Convention, and is intended to secure
full reparation in the interest of the beneficiaries of the obligation breached. In the Court’s view, the
Rohingya are entitled to adequate compensation for the injury caused by the violations of Myanmar
that have been determined by the Court, in so far as such damage is not made good by restitution.
Accordingly, in the circumstances of the present case, the Court concludes that, to the extent that full
reparation by restitution is materially impossible, the Respondent is obliged to provide adequate
compensation, in accordance with the applicable rules of international law, to all of the Rohingya,
individually and as a group, having suffered any form of material damage as a result of Myanmar’s
wrongful acts, in an amount adequate to fully repair the injuries caused.
622. The Court is not requested, at this stage of the proceedings, to indicate the specific amount
of compensation or the modalities pursuant to which such compensation must be made to the injured
Rohingya. Moreover, the amount of compensation must be determined in light of the adoption by
the Respondent of the measures of restitution which the Court has deemed to be appropriate in this
case (see paragraph 615 above).
623. In its final submissions, The Gambia requests that “failing agreement between the Parties
on the amount of compensation and any additional forms of reparation . . . the question will be

- 177 decided by the Court in a subsequent phase of the proceedings in this case” (see paragraph 63 above).
Myanmar maintains that The Gambia cannot seek reparation in the present case (see paragraph 619
above).
624. The Court has already determined that The Gambia is entitled to seek Myanmar’s
compliance with its obligation to provide reparation, including compensation, in the interest of the
Rohingya group and its members, for whose direct benefit the measures of reparation are sought (see
paragraphs 590, 610 and 621). This entitlement derives from The Gambia’s legal interest in
protecting the rights enshrined in the Convention, without requiring a mandate to represent the
protected group or the individual victims (ibid.). In the present case, the Court has determined that
measures of restitution are appropriate (see paragraph 615 above). Were the Court unable to
determine compensation where restitution proves impossible or insufficient, the entitlement to seek
reparation would, in practice, fail to give full effect to the principle that reparation must, so far as
possible, wipe out the consequences of the internationally wrongful act (see paragraph 607 above).
Accordingly, the Court sees no obstacle to proceeding to a determination of compensation or other
appropriate reparation in the present case.
625. Under these circumstances, the Court considers that the question of the amount and
modalities of compensation or other appropriate reparation should be determined in a subsequent
phase of the proceedings. To that end, Myanmar shall, within a period of two years of the delivery
of the present Judgment, transmit to the Court a report setting out the measures of restitution that it
has adopted and, to the extent that full reparation by restitution is materially impossible, specifying
the amount and modalities of compensation. Thereafter, and after ascertaining the views of the
Parties, the Court will make such orders as may be required for the further conduct of the
proceedings, with a view to determining the nature, form and amount of compensation or other
appropriate reparation in a manner consistent with the interests of the protected group and its
members. It is recalled that in the phase of the proceedings devoted to reparation, neither Party may
call into question such findings in the present Judgment as have become res judicata (see Armed
Activities on the Territory of the Congo (Democratic Republic of the Congo v. Uganda), Judgment,
I.C.J. Reports 2005, p. 257, para. 260, citing Military and Paramilitary Activities in and against
Nicaragua (Nicaragua v. United States of America), Merits, Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 1986, p. 143,
para. 284).

*

626. Genocide “shocks the conscience of mankind . . . and is contrary to moral law and to the
spirit and aims of the United Nations” (United Nations General Assembly, resolution 96 (I), “The
Crime of Genocide”, adopted on 11 December 1946, UN doc. A/RES/96 (I); Reservations to the
Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of the Crime of Genocide, Advisory Opinion, I.C.J.
Reports 1951, p. 23). Therefore, all States parties to the Convention have a common interest to
ensure, in view of their shared values, “the accomplishment of those high purposes which are the
raison d’être of the Convention” (Reservations to the Convention on the Prevention and Punishment
of the Crime of Genocide, Advisory Opinion, I.C.J. Reports 1951, p. 23).

- 178 VIII. MYANMAR’S COMPLIANCE WITH THE COURT’S
ORDER INDICATING PROVISIONAL MEASURES
627. In its seventh final submission, The Gambia requested the Court to adjudge and declare
that
“[t]he Republic of the Union of Myanmar has failed to fully and adequately implement
the Provisional Measures Order and must:
(a) by way of restitution as referred to in paragraph (6) (b) (i) above, make good any
bodily or mental injury, including death, suffered by members of the Rohingya
group, or any material injury caused to their property, as a result of Myanmar’s
violations of paragraph 86 (1), (2) or (3) of the Order;
(b) compensate, and provide any additional forms of reparation, for any harm, loss or
injury referred to in paragraph (7) (a) above that is not capable of full reparation by
restitution”.
628. In its Order of 23 January 2020, the Court indicated four provisional measures, the fourth
of which required Myanmar to periodically submit reports to the Court on the implementation of the
first three measures (see paragraph 8 above). The Court’s orders on provisional measures under
Article 41 of its Statute “have binding effect” (LaGrand (Germany v. United States of America),
Judgment, I.C.J. Reports 2001, p. 506, para. 109).
629. The Gambia alleges that Myanmar has breached the Order of 23 January 2020 in respect
of the first three measures. The Court will consider these measures in turn.
A. First provisional measure
630. In its first provisional measure, the Court ordered Myanmar to “take all measures within
its power to prevent the commission of all acts within the scope of Article II of the Convention”.
631. The Gambia contends that members of the Rohingya group have been killed or injured
as a result of Myanmar’s military activities in northern Rakhine State since the date of the Court’s
Order. The Gambia highlights atrocities against Rohingya in the context of police killings, landmine
incidents and armed conflict, forced conscription, significant discrimination and the burning of a
World Food Programme warehouse in June 2024. The Gambia points outs that the United Nations
Special Rapporteur on the situation of human rights in Myanmar recorded at least 33 Rohingya
civilians killed and 39 others injured “as a result of the conflict” in the year following the Court’s
Order (United Nations Human Rights Council, “Report of the Special Rapporteur on the situation of
human rights in Myanmar”, UN doc. A/HRC/46/56 (29 September 2021), Annex III, “The situation
of human rights in Myanmar in 2020 and up to the coup d’état”, paras. 31 and 34).
632. Myanmar disputes these allegations, arguing that The Gambia’s claims have, inter alia,
failed to precisely articulate the material elements of responsibility for the violation of the provisional
measures and failed to substantiate the claims with evidence. In particular, Myanmar points to its
own official records, which state that Rohingya deaths between May to November 2020 were caused
by the Arakan Army rather than Myanmar’s security forces. Official documents also record that there
were two incidents between the Tatmadaw and ARSA in February and March 2021 respectively, but

- 179 the number of casualties remained to be investigated (Myanmar’s Second report on the
implementation of provisional measures, Appendix 1).

*

*

633. The Court is unable to find that, on the evidence, the acts alleged by The Gambia
