occurring after the Order of 23 January 2020 fall within the scope of the actus reus of Article II.
Unlike the evidence before the Court regarding the clearance operations in 2017, the reports that
The Gambia relies on do not provide sufficient details of the events during which Rohingya were
killed or seriously injured, the location where those events took place or the perpetrators responsible
for those acts, and the Court has been unable to corroborate these incidents with other evidence in
the record. Moreover, the evidentiary record does not support an assertion that Myanmar’s acts after
23 January 2020 were carried out with genocidal intent or that Myanmar failed to take measures
within its power to prevent the commission of such acts after the Order. The Court finds therefore
that Myanmar has not breached its obligations in respect of the first provisional measure.
B. Second provisional measure
634. In its second provisional measure, the Court ordered Myanmar to “ensure that its military,
as well as any irregular armed units which may be directed or supported by it and any organizations
and persons which may be subject to its control, direction or influence”, did not commit acts
described in Article II of the Convention or any acts of “conspiracy to commit genocide, of direct
and public incitement to commit genocide, of attempt to commit genocide, or of complicity in
genocide”, which are prohibited by Article III of the Convention.
635. The Gambia maintains that, since 23 January 2020, Myanmar’s military has continued to
engage in anti-Rohingya propaganda that “could amount to conspiracy or incitement to commit
genocide”. In this context, The Gambia refers to Facebook’s removal of a large number of accounts
during 2020 and 2021 that were linked to the members of the Tatmadaw (see The Gambia’s
Observations on Myanmar’s Second report on the implementation of provisional measures
(7 December 2020), paras. 20-23; The Gambia’s Observations on Myanmar’s Third report on the
implementation of provisional measures (16 August 2021), para. 30; The Gambia’s Observations on
Myanmar’s Fourth report on the implementation of provisional measures (7 December 2021),
paras. 35-36). The Gambia also argues that Myanmar engaged in the dissemination of anti-Rohingya
propaganda through pamphlets and other media platforms.
636. According to Myanmar, the acts alleged by The Gambia do not fall within the personal
or the subject-matter scope of the Genocide Convention, and, in any event, the evidence cited by
The Gambia to support the allegations is insufficient.

*

*

- 180 637. The Court recalls that the evidence has not conclusively established that Myanmar’s
Facebook military network messages were made with the specific intent to destroy the group in whole
or in part (see paragraph 505 above). While the materials disseminated by Myanmar since 23 January
2020 may have fuelled religious tension and contempt towards the Rohingya group, the Court is not
persuaded that the evidence shows that the dissemination occurred with a specific intent to destroy
the group in whole or in part, or that it constituted a direct appeal for the commission of genocide
and that the intended audience would have understood the messages as such. The Court therefore
finds that Myanmar has not breached its obligations in respect of the second provisional measure.
C. Third provisional measure
638. In its third provisional measure, the Court ordered Myanmar to “take effective measures
to prevent the destruction and ensure the preservation of evidence related to allegations of acts within
the scope of Article II of the Convention”.
639. The Gambia accepts that most of the burning and destruction of Rohingya villages
occurred in 2017 and 2018, before the obligation under the Court’s Order took effect. Nevertheless,
according to The Gambia, in the period following 23 January 2020, Myanmar destroyed Rohingya
villages, including with bulldozers, and, in some instances, built BGP facilities on top of those areas,
in breach of Myanmar’s obligations under the Order of 23 January 2020. The Gambia argues that
Myanmar cannot be understood to have complied in good faith with the Order given the combination
of its alleged destruction of evidence and its denial of access to such evidence by international
monitoring mechanisms.
640. Myanmar rejects these allegations and claims that there are no incidents of destruction of
evidence that would fall within the scope of the Court’s Order. It points to statements by local
authorities that such incidents did not occur. Myanmar highlights a Presidential Directive of 2020
titled “Preservation of evidence and property in areas of northern Rakhine State”, which prohibited
State officials from destroying property that may provide evidence of genocide (Myanmar’s First
report on the implementation of provisional measures, paras. 89 and 91). Myanmar also points to
Rathedaung Township’s issuance of a local order in January 2022 that prohibited residents from
occupying property formerly inhabited by persons displaced by what it described as the “ARSA
terrorist attacks” in 2016 and 2017 (Myanmar’s Fifth report on the implementation of provisional
measures, paras. 72-73). Myanmar claims that there have been no complaints concerning the
confiscation of lands formerly inhabited by displaced Rohingya (Myanmar’s Eighth report on the
implementation of provisional measures, para. 53).

*

*

641. The Court recalls its findings concerning the destruction of evidence by Myanmar in the
context of Myanmar’s compliance with its duty to punish (see paragraphs 551-552 above). The Court
further recalls that in September 2025 the IIMM published a public summary of a report on
Myanmar’s destruction and dispossession of Rohingya land and property (see paragraph 279 above).

- 181 The IIMM documented that construction of the No. 7 BGP battalion base on land where Done Paik
and Ree Dar villages once stood continued until 21 April 2021, and documented construction of the
No. 9 BGP battalion base in Myin Hlut village as of 2021 (IIMM, “The destruction and dispossession
of Rohingya land and property during the 2017 clearance operations — public summary”
(29 September 2025), paras. 24, 32 and 53). The Court also notes that the United Nations High
Commissioner for Human Rights reported that in May 2020 the Tatmadaw “burned large swathes of
Buthidaung township where between five and more than a dozen Rohingya villages once stood”
(United Nations Human Rights Council, “Situation of human rights of Rohingya Muslims and other
minorities in Myanmar: Report of the United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights”,
UN doc. A/HRC/45/5 (3 September 2020), para. 29). Those conclusions were primarily based on
witness testimony verified and corroborated by satellite imagery and government reports.
642. In the Court’s view, the report of the IIMM provides convincing evidence that Myanmar
completed the construction of at least one government base in 2021 on the sites of former Rohingya
villages in Maungdaw Township, where clearance operations were conducted in 2017 (IIMM, “The
destruction and dispossession of Rohingya land and property during the 2017 clearance operations —
public summary” (29 September 2025), para. 24; see also para. 32). Similarly, the report of the
United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights offers convincing evidence that the remnants
of at least five Rohingya villages in Buthidaung Township were burned and destroyed in May 2020.
The Court finds that, through these acts, Myanmar breached its obligation under the Court’s third
provisional measure to take effective measures to prevent the destruction and ensure the preservation
of evidence related to allegations of acts within the scope of Article II of the Genocide Convention.

*

643. The Court notes that, under the fourth provisional measure, Myanmar is obliged to submit
six-monthly reports to the Court on all measures taken to give effect to the Order, until a final
decision on the case is rendered by the Court (see paragraph 8 above). Myanmar has complied with
this obligation, duly submitting thirteen reports. As the present Judgment qualifies as a final decision
for the purposes of the fourth provisional measure, Myanmar is no longer required to submit such
reports to the Court.
D. Remedies
644. In addition to claiming declaratory relief, The Gambia has also claimed reparation for
Myanmar’s breach of the Court’s Order “[t]o the extent that reparation has not already been made
for these injuries through the measures of restitution and compensation”. The Gambia sought to
reserve the precise determination of the reparation owed by Myanmar for a subsequent phase of the
proceedings.
645. In the 2007 Bosnia Judgment, the Court considered that “for purposes of reparation, the
Respondent’s non-compliance with the provisional measures ordered is an aspect of, or merges with,
its breaches of the substantive obligations of prevention and punishment laid upon it by the
Convention” (I.C.J. Reports 2007 (I), p. 236, para. 469). As a result, the Court awarded declaratory

- 182 relief in that case but did not find it appropriate to grant Bosnia and Herzegovina’s request for an
order for symbolic compensation. The Court notes that its order that Myanmar cease the continuing
breach of the obligation to punish is necessarily applicable in the context of Myanmar’s breach of its
obligation under the Court’s third provisional measure. For these reasons, the Court finds declaratory
relief to be an appropriate remedy in the present case, and that no further form of remedy is required.

*
*

*

646. For these reasons,
THE COURT,
(1) By nine votes to six,
Finds that the Republic of the Union of Myanmar has committed genocide against the
Rohingya group in northern Rakhine State, in violation of its obligations under the Convention on
the Prevention and Punishment of the Crime of Genocide;
IN FAVOUR: President Iwasawa; Judges Charlesworth, Brant, Gómez Robledo, Cleveland,

Aurescu, Tladi, Hmoud; Judge ad hoc Pillay;

Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of the Crime of Genocide, to prevent the commission
of genocide by individuals alongside its security forces;
IN FAVOUR: President Iwasawa; Judges Charlesworth, Brant, Gómez Robledo, Cleveland,

AGAINST: Vice-President Sebutinde; Judges Tomka, Abraham, Xue, Nolte; Judge ad hoc Kress;

(3) By fourteen votes to one,
Finds that the Republic of the Union of Myanmar has not directly and publicly incited the
commission of genocide against the Rohingya group by private persons in northern Rakhine State in
violation of its obligation under the Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of the Crime of
Genocide;
IN FAVOUR: President Iwasawa; Vice-President Sebutinde; Judges Tomka, Abraham, Xue,

the Crime of Genocide;

IN FAVOUR: President Iwasawa; Vice-President Sebutinde; Judges Tomka, Abraham, Nolte,

Decides that the Republic of the Union of Myanmar must take immediate and effective steps
to investigate all persons reasonably suspected of having committed acts contrary to Article III of the
Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of the Crime of Genocide in relation to the 2017
clearance operations and, if warranted, to prosecute and punish them;
IN

President Iwasawa; Judges Tomka, Xue, Nolte, Charlesworth, Brant,

- 184 (8) By nine votes to six,
Decides that the Republic of the Union of Myanmar must provide assurances and guarantees
of non-repetition of the violations of its obligations under the Convention on the Prevention and
Punishment of the Crime of Genocide and prevent similar violations from occurring in the future;
IN FAVOUR: President Iwasawa; Judges Charlesworth, Brant, Gómez Robledo, Cleveland,

IN FAVOUR:

is not made good by restitution;
IN FAVOUR:

President Iwasawa; Judges Tomka, Charlesworth, Brant, Gómez Robledo,

(11) By ten votes to five,
Decides that the question of the amount and modalities of compensation or other reparation
due to the Rohingya group and its members shall be settled by the Court, and that, to this end, the
Republic of the Union of Myanmar shall, within a period of two years of the delivery of the present
Judgment, transmit to the Court the report described in paragraph 625; and reserves for this purpose
the subsequent procedure in the case;
IN FAVOUR: Judges Tomka, Abraham, Charlesworth, Brant, Gómez Robledo, Cleveland,

Tladi, Hmoud; Judges ad hoc Pillay, Kress;
AGAINST: President Iwasawa; Vice-President Sebutinde; Judges Xue, Nolte, Aurescu;

- 185 (12) By eleven votes to four,
Finds that the Republic of the Union of Myanmar has violated its obligation under
paragraph 86 (3) of the Order of 23 January 2020 indicating provisional measures to take effective
measures to prevent the destruction and ensure the preservation of evidence related to allegations of
acts within the scope of Article II of the Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of the Crime
of Genocide;
IN FAVOUR:

President Iwasawa; Judges Tomka, Charlesworth, Brant, Gómez Robledo,
Cleveland, Aurescu, Tladi, Hmoud; Judges ad hoc Pillay, Kress;

AGAINST: Vice-President Sebutinde; Judges Abraham, Xue, Nolte;

(13) Unanimously,
Rejects all other submissions of the Republic of The Gambia.

Done in English and in French, the English text being authoritative, at the Peace Palace,
The Hague, this nineteenth day of November, two thousand and twenty-six, in fourteen copies, one
of which will be placed in the archives of the Court and the others transmitted to the Government of
the Republic of The Gambia, the Government of the Republic of the Union of Myanmar, and the
Governments of the Kingdom of Belgium, Canada, the Democratic Republic of the Congo, the
Kingdom of Denmark, the French Republic, the Federal Republic of Germany, Ireland, the Republic
of Maldives, the Kingdom of the Netherlands, the Republic of Slovenia and the United Kingdom of
Great Britain and Northern Ireland, respectively.

IWASAWA Yuji,
